import math
# Python: both tuning curves are single-line bell shapes with the math module.
def ct_rate(velocity_cm_s, temp_c, recent_strokes=0):
    """Impulses per second from one human C-tactile afferent."""
    PEAK_V, V_WIDTH = 3.0, 0.55      # cm/s optimum, width in log10 units
    PEAK_T, T_WIDTH = 32.0, 8.0      # deg C optimum and width
    PEAK_RATE, FATIGUE = 50.0, 0.85  # Hz at optimum, decay per repeat
    if velocity_cm_s <= 0:
        return 0.0                   # a still finger evokes almost nothing
    v = math.exp(-((math.log10(velocity_cm_s) - math.log10(PEAK_V)) ** 2)
                 / (2 * V_WIDTH ** 2))
    t = math.exp(-((temp_c - PEAK_T) ** 2) / (2 * T_WIDTH ** 2))
    return PEAK_RATE * v * t * (FATIGUE ** recent_strokes)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    for speed in (0.3, 1.0, 3.0, 10.0, 30.0):
        print(speed, "cm/s ->", round(ct_rate(speed, 32.0), 1), "Hz")
    for temp in (18.0, 32.0, 42.0):
        print(temp, "C    ->", round(ct_rate(3.0, temp), 1), "Hz")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other afferent fires best to a slow, skin-warm caress and worse to anything faster, colder or hotter.

CHAPTER 246. NOCICEPTORS (NON-COG)

Touch a hot kettle and your hand is already moving before you know why, because this cell has shouted.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A nociceptor is a sensory neuron with a deliberately high threshold that fires only to stimuli strong enough to threaten tissue, whether burning heat, crushing force, freezing cold or irritant chemicals.

It is the alarm layer of the body, and the word covers a family rather than one cell, so this entry is the umbrella whose members are specified separately.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is pseudounipolar, a round soma on a forked stalk with no dendrites, and its skin ending is a free nerve ending, meaning a bare branching tip with no capsule or accessory structure.

Those tips climb between skin cells as intraepidermal nerve fibres, the fibres counted in a skin biopsy when a doctor tests for small fibre neuropathy.

SIZE:

Somata are small to medium, about 15 to 45 micrometres across, with heat-sensing TRPV1-positive human neurons averaging 44 plus or minus 7 micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The main divisions are peptidergic and non-peptidergic, C fibre and A-delta fibre, polymodal and single-modality, plus silent nociceptors that are mechanically insensitive until inflamed and make up roughly 15 to 25 per cent of human C nociceptors.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Somata fill the dorsal root ganglia at every level from the second cervical to the sacral, the border post between peripheral and central nervous systems.

Endings sit in epidermis, dermis, joint capsule, muscle, bone periosteum, tooth pulp and viscera, and central terminals arborise in lamina I, outer lamina II and lamina V of the dorsal horn.

Two interfaces matter: the dermo-epidermal junction, which its endings cross to sit among skin cells, and the dorsal root entry zone, where Schwann cell territory becomes oligodendrocyte territory.

NUMBER:

About 6 million nociceptors, roughly 60 per cent of the approximately 10 million human dorsal root ganglion neurons, with a defensible range of 5 to 7 million.

The peptidergic, non-peptidergic and A-delta entries that follow divide this same 6 million rather than adding to it, and against 86 billion neurons overall nociceptors are about seven cells in every hundred thousand.

Confidence is moderate, resting on fibre counts and molecular marker studies rather than a direct human tally.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage is near minus 55 millivolts and the action potential is broad with a hump on its falling edge, because the upstroke uses the slow, tetrodotoxin-resistant sodium channels Nav1.8 and Nav1.9.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The soma carries no synapses; central terminals excite lamina I projection neurons bearing the neurokinin 1 receptor and lamina II interneurons, and they receive axoaxonic inhibition using gamma-aminobutyric acid.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Unmyelinated C nociceptors conduct at 0.5 to 2 metres per second and thinly myelinated A-delta nociceptors at 5 to 30, which is why a stubbed toe delivers a sharp first pain and then a slower burning second pain.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

All release glutamate, packaged by vesicular glutamate transporter 2, and many also release the peptides calcitonin gene-related peptide and substance P, along with adenosine triphosphate and brain-derived neurotrophic factor.

MYELINATION:

Mixed by definition, since C nociceptors are unmyelinated and travel in Remak bundles while A-delta nociceptors carry a thin sheath of a few wraps.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The dominant cost is running ion pumps along up to a metre of thin axon, and thin axons have a high surface to volume ratio, so per millimetre they are expensive.

PLASTICITY:

Central sensitisation then amplifies the message in the cord, and after axon damage the neuron switches on a repair transcription programme marked by the gene ATF3 and redistributes its sodium channels, which can leave it firing without any stimulus at all.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

In the skin the specialised partner is the nociceptive Schwann cell, a mesh-like glial cell whose own processes can initiate pain signalling, and keratinocytes act as accessory transducers by releasing adenosine triphosphate onto the bare ending.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove nociceptors and the skin becomes a numb map: pressure, stroking and warmth are all still felt, but nothing is ever urgent.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The axon reflex is the signature trick: activity travelling back down side branches makes the ending secrete peptides that widen blood vessels and leak plasma, producing the red flare around a scratch, so this neuron controls local blood flow and inflammation.

It also talks to immune cells and glia, changes its own gene expression within hours of injury, synthesises proteins in its distal axon, and can be silenced for months by high-dose capsaicin, which retracts the endings before they regrow.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: temperature in degrees Celsius; mechanical force in millinewtons; proton concentration as pH; inflammatory mediator concentration as a scalar 0 to 1; descending and segmental inhibition 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: spikes releasing glutamate onto lamina I and lamina II neurons; peripheral release of calcitonin gene-related peptide and substance P onto vessels and immune cells.

• STATE: membrane voltage; TRPV1 open fraction; Nav1.8 availability; sensitisation level; transcriptional injury state.

• PARAMETERS: heat threshold 42 to 45 degrees Celsius before sensitisation; mechanical threshold above 5 millinewtons; conduction 0.5 to 30 metres per second; maximum rate about 50 impulses per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: transduction 1 to 10 milliseconds; spike 2 to 5 milliseconds; peripheral sensitisation seconds to minutes; injury transcription hours to days; endings regrow over weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: loss-of-function in the gene SCN9A abolishes pain entirely, gain-of-function causes the burning attacks of inherited erythromelalgia, and die-back of these endings in diabetes or chemotherapy gives painful small fibre neuropathy.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide when a nociceptor fires and how inflammation lowers its threshold over time.
Create a number variable called base_threshold with initial value 43.
Create a number variable called sensitisation with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called spike_counts with initial value empty list.
For each sample in stimulus_trace, perform the following operations.
    Create a number variable called threshold with initial value 0.
    Set threshold to the difference of base_threshold and sensitisation.
    Create a number variable called spikes with initial value 0.
    If the temperature of sample is greater than threshold, then perform the following actions.
        Set spikes to the product of 2 and the difference of the temperature of sample and threshold.
    End the conditional block.
    If the inflammation of sample is greater than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set sensitisation to the sum of sensitisation and the product of 0.5 and the inflammation of sample.
    End the conditional block.
    If sensitisation is greater than 10, then perform the following actions.
        Set sensitisation to 10.
    End the conditional block.
    Set sensitisation to the product of sensitisation and 0.99.
    Add spikes to the end of spike_counts.
End the loop.
Return spike_counts to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: the sensitisation loop is the whole story, and it reads as plain text.
def nociceptor_trace(samples, base_threshold=43.0):
    """samples: list of (skin temperature in C, inflammation from 0 to 1)."""
    MAX_SENS, GAIN, DECAY, RATE = 10.0, 0.5, 0.99, 2.0
    sensitisation, out = 0.0, []
    for temp_c, inflammation in samples:
        threshold = base_threshold - sensitisation   # inflamed skin hurts sooner
        spikes = RATE * (temp_c - threshold) if temp_c > threshold else 0.0
        sensitisation = min(MAX_SENS, sensitisation + GAIN * inflammation)
        sensitisation *= DECAY                       # sensitisation fades slowly
        out.append(round(spikes, 2))
    return out

if __name__ == "__main__":
    warm_only = [(40.0, 0.0)] * 5                    # warm, not painful
    inflamed = [(40.0, 1.0)] * 10                    # same warmth, inflamed skin
    print("healthy skin:", nociceptor_trace(warm_only))
    print("inflamed skin:", nociceptor_trace(inflamed))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only afferent family that stays silent through everything harmless and then fires to heat above about 43 degrees Celsius, to crushing force, or to a drop of capsaicin.

CHAPTER 247. PEPTIDERGIC NOCICEPTORS (Swelling-Driven Pain Neuron) (NON-COG)

Scratch your arm and watch a red streak bloom beside the scratch: that flare is this cell spraying chemicals out of its own skin ending.

Peptidergic is a chemical barcode naming the cargo, calcitonin gene related peptide and substance P, without saying what that cargo does.

Swelling-Driven names the cell's unique second job, because these endings do not only carry the pain signal inward but also dump their peptides outward at the injury site, and calcitonin gene related peptide released from perivascular endings widens local blood vessels to produce the visible red flare of the Lewis triple response.

Pain names the primary faculty, since these are polymodal detectors driven by damaging heat, damaging pressure and irritant chemicals, and are the capsaicin-sensitive population.

Neuron marks it as the afferent whose two-way traffic makes an injured patch of skin turn red and tender on its own.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A peptidergic nociceptor is a pain-sensing neuron that stores peptides, meaning short chains of amino acids, and releases them at both ends of itself.

Peptide is Greek for digested, and the two that matter here are calcitonin gene-related peptide and substance P, which widen blood vessels, leak plasma into tissue and excite the spinal cord, so this cell does not merely report damage, it inflames the tissue it reports on.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is pseudounipolar with free nerve endings, and its distinguishing feature is cargo: large dense-core vesicles, spherical packets with a dark core seen under the electron microscope, which carry peptides rather than the clear vesicles that carry glutamate.

Its endings reach the upper epidermis, wrap blood vessels and hair follicles, and supply deep tissue such as joint, muscle and bone.

SIZE:

Somata are small to medium at about 20 to 45 micrometres, dense-core vesicles are roughly 80 to 120 nanometres across, and terminal branches are well under a micrometre.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Human ganglion sequencing separates several peptidergic clusters, including a heat-sensing TRPV1-rich set, a TRPA1-rich chemical-sensing set, an enkephalin-expressing set and an itch-related pruritogen receptor set, and in women that itch-related set carries markedly more calcitonin gene-related peptide messenger RNA than in men.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Somata sit in the dorsal root ganglia at every level, again at the peripheral to central interface, with their facial counterparts in the trigeminal ganglion.

Endings occupy skin, cornea, joint, periosteum, tooth pulp and viscera, and their central terminals concentrate in lamina I and outer lamina II of the dorsal horn, with some reaching lamina V.

NUMBER:

About 3 to 3.5 million cells, roughly 55 per cent of the 6 million nociceptors and about a third of all 10 million dorsal root ganglion neurons, drawn from the nociceptor pool rather than added to it.

Confidence is moderate: calcitonin gene-related peptide messenger RNA is reported in 50 to 70 per cent of human ganglion neurons, a wide bracket, and in humans about a third of neurons carry both peptidergic and non-peptidergic markers, so the boundary is genuinely blurred.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage near minus 55 millivolts, a broad shouldered spike carried by Nav1.8 and Nav1.9, and threshold set by Nav1.7.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Centrally it drives lamina I projection neurons carrying the neurokinin 1 receptor, the receptor for substance P, which is the main ascending pain route to brainstem and thalamus.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Most are unmyelinated C fibres conducting at 0.5 to 2 metres per second, with a thinly myelinated A-delta minority; peptides are made in the soma and carried the whole way out by motor proteins, so a terminal cannot restock quickly.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate for fast signalling through vesicular glutamate transporter 2, plus calcitonin gene-related peptide and substance P, and the receptor that defines the class is TrkA, the receptor for nerve growth factor.

MYELINATION:

Mostly none, since the majority are C fibres in Remak bundles, with the A-delta minority carrying a few wraps of Schwann cell myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Peptide manufacture is costly, because peptides cannot be recycled at the terminal the way glutamate is, so the soma must transcribe, package and ship every molecule.

PLASTICITY:

Chronic inflammation makes these fibres sprout into damaged tissue, and repeated migraine attacks depend on calcitonin gene-related peptide release from the trigeminal counterparts of these cells.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

In tissue the crucial partners are mast cells and macrophages, which supply nerve growth factor and are in turn degranulated by substance P, a two-way loop absent from most of the nervous system.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this cell and skin still hurts when cut, but it stops turning red, stops swelling and stops becoming tender around an injury.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This is the clearest example in the body of a neuron acting as a secretory gland, releasing peptides from peripheral endings to control blood vessel diameter, plasma leak and immune cell behaviour.

It performs volume transmission in the dorsal horn, where peptides diffuse beyond the synapse to reach receptors on distant cells, it changes its own gene expression under nerve growth factor control, and its axon reflex spreads the alarm sideways into skin that was never touched.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: temperature above 42 degrees Celsius; force above about 5 millinewtons; pH below 6; nerve growth factor and bradykinin concentrations; opioid and gamma-aminobutyric acid inhibition 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate onto lamina I neurokinin 1 receptor projection neurons; substance P and calcitonin gene-related peptide into spinal tissue and into skin, gut and joint.

• STATE: membrane voltage; TRPV1 sensitisation; internal calcium; readily releasable peptide pool; transcriptional state under nerve growth factor.

• PARAMETERS: heat threshold 42 to 45 degrees Celsius; peptide release requires bursts above about 5 impulses per second; conduction 0.5 to 2 metres per second; peptide pool refill limited by axonal transport.

• TIME CONSTANTS: spike 2 to 5 milliseconds; TRPV1 sensitisation seconds to minutes; flare develops over 20 to 60 seconds; peptide resupply hours; transcriptional change hours to days.

• FAILURE MODE: losing the TrkA gene NTRK1 causes hereditary sensory and autonomic neuropathy type four, in which children feel no pain and cannot sweat; overactivity of the same peptides drives migraine, and blocking calcitonin gene-related peptide prevents attacks.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to model a peptidergic nociceptor that fires to heat and, when firing hard enough, empties peptide into the tissue around it.
Create a number variable called heat_threshold with initial value 43.
Create a number variable called peptide_pool with initial value 100.
Create a number variable called flare with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called report with initial value empty list.
For each sample in stimulus_trace, perform the following operations.
    Create a number variable called firing_rate with initial value 0.
    If sample is greater than heat_threshold, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing_rate to the product of 3 and the difference of sample and heat_threshold.
    End the conditional block.
    Create a number variable called release with initial value 0.
    If both firing_rate is greater than 5 and peptide_pool is greater than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set release to the quotient of firing_rate divided by 10.
        Set peptide_pool to the difference of peptide_pool and release.
        Set flare to the sum of flare and release.
        Set heat_threshold to the difference of heat_threshold and 0.05.
    End the conditional block.
    Set flare to the product of flare and 0.98.
    Add firing_rate to the end of report.
End the loop.
Display flare to the console.
Return report to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: one loop shows firing, peptide depletion, flare and self-sensitisation.
def peptidergic_nociceptor(temps, threshold=43.0, pool=100.0):
    """temps: skin temperature in C, one value per second."""
    BURST, DRAIN, SENS, FADE = 5.0, 10.0, 0.05, 0.98
    flare, rates = 0.0, []
    for temp_c in temps:
        rate = 3.0 * (temp_c - threshold) if temp_c > threshold else 0.0
        if rate > BURST and pool > 0:          # only bursts empty dense-core vesicles
            released = rate / DRAIN
            pool -= released
            flare += released                  # the visible red flare in skin
            threshold -= SENS                  # the ending sensitises itself
        flare *= FADE
        rates.append(round(rate, 2))
    return rates, round(flare, 2), round(pool, 2)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    rates, flare, pool = peptidergic_nociceptor([46.0] * 20)
    print("firing rates:", rates[:5], "...")
    print("flare:", flare, " peptide left:", pool)

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only sensory neuron whose activity turns the skin red, because no other cell squirts calcitonin gene-related peptide out of its own sensory ending.

CHAPTER 248. NON-PEPTIDERGIC NOCICEPTORS (Bare-Skin Injury Neuron) (NON-COG)

These pain fibres carry no chemical weapons for the tissue; they simply deliver the bad news and get out of the way.

Non-peptidergic defines the cell by a chemical it lacks, which is the least informative naming strategy available.

Bare-Skin names its exclusive territory, because roughly seventy-five percent of this population carries the Mrgprd marker and those cells innervate skin and nothing else, ending in the most superficial living layer of the epidermis, the stratum granulosum, above where their peptide-carrying cousins terminate.

Injury names the faculty, since removing these cells leaves animals selectively blunted to damaging mechanical stimulation while other pain responses survive.

Neuron marks it as the afferent teaching that the outer surface of the body has its own dedicated damage line, separate from the one that inflames tissue.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A non-peptidergic nociceptor is a pain-sensing neuron that lacks the peptide cargo of its peptidergic cousin and therefore does not inflame the tissue it monitors.

It is polymodal, answering pinch, noxious heat and irritant chemicals, and in mice the same cells signal the itch caused by beta-alanine, a food supplement amino acid.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is pseudounipolar with bare free nerve endings that climb higher into the epidermis than most, reaching the granular layer just beneath the dead surface cells.

It carries clear synaptic vesicles rather than dense-core peptide vesicles, and its central terminals form the core of glomeruli in the inner half of lamina II.

SIZE:

Somata are small at roughly 15 to 30 micrometres across, among the smallest neurons in the body, with terminal branches well under a micrometre wide.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

They divide by receptor: a large MrgprD-expressing mechanical and itch class, a TRPA1-rich chemical-sensing class, and smaller classes tuned to cold or to specific irritants, with about 80 per cent of small neurons responding to TRPA1 agonists binding the marker lectin.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Somata occupy the dorsal root ganglia at every spinal level, the interface between peripheral and central nervous systems.

Endings are concentrated in skin rather than deep tissue, and central terminals occupy the inner part of lamina II, just above the low-threshold mechanoreceptor zone and just below the peptidergic band in lamina I and outer lamina II.

NUMBER:

About 1.8 to 2 million cells, roughly 30 per cent of the 6 million nociceptors and about 20 per cent of the 10 million dorsal root ganglion neurons, taken from the nociceptor pool rather than added to it.

Confidence is low to moderate, because the defining markers behave differently in people: the adenosine triphosphate-gated channel P2X3 is found in virtually all human ganglion neurons, and roughly a third of human neurons carry peptidergic and non-peptidergic markers together.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage near minus 55 millivolts, a broad shouldered spike carried by tetrodotoxin-resistant Nav1.8, and a strong adenosine triphosphate-gated inward current through P2X3 that makes damaged, leaking tissue an effective stimulus.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No synapses on the soma; centrally it excites inner lamina II interneurons, including vertical cells that relay onward to lamina I, and it receives axoaxonic inhibition using gamma-aminobutyric acid.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Unmyelinated C fibres conducting at 0.5 to 2 metres per second, branching profusely so that one neuron tiles a patch of skin with hundreds of tips.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate through vesicular glutamate transporter 2 and nothing else of consequence, which is precisely the point of the name.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable in the sense of a sheath, because these are unmyelinated C fibres; they travel bundled inside Remak Schwann cells, which support them metabolically without insulating them.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Running pumps along a metre of very thin, heavily branched axon is the whole expense, and the branching means an unusually large membrane area per cell.

PLASTICITY:

After nerve injury they contribute strongly to mechanical allodynia, and their spinal terminals change which interneurons they can drive.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Remak Schwann cells supply the axons, and specialised nociceptive Schwann cells contribute at the ending.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Kill this population selectively in animals and pinching hurts less while burning still hurts, and inflamed tissue remains inflamed because no peptides were being released here anyway.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

It reads the chemistry of broken cells directly, since adenosine triphosphate spilling from damaged tissue is its natural agonist, making it a damage detector as much as a force detector.

It also occupies the inner lamina II glomeruli where afferent terminals contact one another, undergoes a developmental identity switch controlled by the transcription factor Runx1, and maintains hundreds of branch points by local protein synthesis.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: mechanical force above about 5 millinewtons; temperature above 43 degrees Celsius; extracellular adenosine triphosphate in micromolar amounts; irritant chemicals acting on TRPA1; segmental inhibition 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate onto inner lamina II interneurons and, through them, lamina I projection neurons.

• STATE: membrane voltage; P2X3 desensitisation; Nav1.8 availability; branch-level local excitation.

• PARAMETERS: mechanical threshold 5 to 20 millinewtons; heat threshold about 43 degrees Celsius; conduction 0.5 to 2 metres per second; maximum rate about 20 impulses per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: P2X3 activation 10 to 50 milliseconds with desensitisation over seconds; spike 2 to 5 milliseconds; developmental identity switch over the first postnatal weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: these endings are lost early in diabetic and chemotherapy-induced small fibre neuropathy, and their overactivity after nerve injury contributes to allodynia and to chronic itch.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to combine force, heat and spilled adenosine triphosphate into the firing of a non-peptidergic nociceptor, including desensitisation of its chemical receptor.
Create a number variable called desensitisation with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called rates with initial value empty list.
For each sample in stimulus_trace, perform the following operations.
    Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
    If the force of sample is greater than 5, then perform the following actions.
        Set drive to the sum of drive and the quotient of the difference of the force of sample and 5 divided by 5.
    End the conditional block.
    If the temperature of sample is greater than 43, then perform the following actions.
        Set drive to the sum of drive and the difference of the temperature of sample and 43.
    End the conditional block.
    Create a number variable called chemical with initial value 0.
    Set chemical to the product of the atp of sample and the difference of 1 and desensitisation.
    Set drive to the sum of drive and chemical.
    Set desensitisation to the sum of desensitisation and the product of 0.2 and chemical.
    If desensitisation is greater than 0.9, then perform the following actions.
        Set desensitisation to 0.9.
    End the conditional block.
    Set desensitisation to the product of desensitisation and 0.95.
    Add drive to the end of rates.
End the loop.
Return rates to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: three input channels summed in one loop, with P2X3 desensitisation.
def non_peptidergic(samples):
    """samples: list of (force in mN, temperature in C, ATP level 0 to 1)."""
    F_THRESH, T_THRESH, DESENS_GAIN, RECOVERY = 5.0, 43.0, 0.2, 0.95
    desens, rates = 0.0, []
    for force, temp_c, atp in samples:
        drive = 0.0
        if force > F_THRESH:
            drive += (force - F_THRESH) / 5.0        # graded mechanical pain
        if temp_c > T_THRESH:
            drive += temp_c - T_THRESH               # noxious heat adds on top
        chemical = atp * (1.0 - desens)              # ATP from broken cells
        drive += chemical
        desens = min(0.9, desens + DESENS_GAIN * chemical)
        desens *= RECOVERY                           # the receptor recovers
        rates.append(round(drive, 2))
    return rates

if __name__ == "__main__":
    pinch = [(20.0, 32.0, 0.0)] * 3                  # pure pinch
    wound = [(20.0, 32.0, 1.0)] * 5                  # pinch on damaged skin
    print("pinch:", non_peptidergic(pinch))
    print("wound:", non_peptidergic(wound))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the pain fibre that binds the plant lectin isolectin B4, carries MrgprD and P2X3, answers to glial cell line-derived neurotrophic factor rather than nerve growth factor, and holds no peptides at all.

CHAPTER 249. A-DELTA NOCICEPTORS (First-Pain Pricking Neuron) (NON-COG)

The bright sting that makes you snatch your hand back travels on these wires, and it beats the slow burn to your brain by about a second.

The existing label is an alphanumeric fibre-class barcode, since the letter A and the Greek letter delta describe axon calibre rather than what the cell does for a person.

First-Pain names the moment the cell owns, because a single sharp injury produces a double sensation and this cell delivers the opening half of it.

Pricking names the quality of that half, the bright well-localised sting that makes a hand withdraw before anything is felt as burning.

Neuron marks it as a thinly myelinated afferent conducting at roughly five to forty metres per second, about fifteen on average, so its message arrives at the spinal cord roughly a second earlier than the unmyelinated traffic behind it.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An A-delta nociceptor is a thinly insulated pain fibre that carries first pain, the sharp, well-located prick that arrives before the dull ache.

It exists because speed matters: a fast warning can move a limb out of danger while a slow one only teaches a lesson.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is pseudounipolar with free nerve endings, but unlike C fibres its axon carries a thin myelin sheath that stops short of the terminal, leaving a bare sensing tip.

Its central terminals concentrate in lamina I and lamina V of the dorsal horn, the two layers that hold the main ascending pain projection neurons.

SIZE:

The soma is medium at about 30 to 50 micrometres, the axon 1 to 5 micrometres wide including myelin, and one cell covers a skin field of a few square millimetres to a couple of square centimetres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Type one has a very high heat threshold above about 53 degrees Celsius with a low mechanical threshold, and sensitises sharply after a burn.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Somata sit in the dorsal root ganglia at all levels, at the peripheral to central interface, with axons crossing the dorsal root entry zone where Schwann cell myelin gives way to oligodendrocyte myelin.

Endings are found in skin, joint capsule, muscle, periosteum and cornea, with type two units concentrated in hairy skin, and central terminals in lamina I and lamina V.

NUMBER:

About 1 million cells, roughly 10 per cent of the 10 million dorsal root ganglion neurons and about 15 to 20 per cent of the 6 million nociceptors, drawn from that same nociceptor pool.

Confidence is moderate, based on fibre counts in peripheral nerve and microneurography sampling rather than on a direct human census, giving roughly one cell per hundred thousand neurons in the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage near minus 60 millivolts, a spike narrower than a C fibre spike but still carrying a Nav1.8 component, and threshold set by Nav1.7.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No synapses on the soma; centrally it excites lamina I projection neurons bearing the neurokinin 1 receptor and lamina V wide-dynamic-range neurons, and it drives spinal motor circuits directly enough to produce the withdrawal reflex before the brain is informed.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Conduction is 5 to 30 metres per second, roughly ten to thirty times faster than a C fibre, so from the foot the sharp warning arrives in about 40 milliseconds while the burn takes a full second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate through vesicular glutamate transporter 2 is the main transmitter, with some units also carrying calcitonin gene-related peptide and substance P.

MYELINATION:

Thin, only a few wraps of Schwann cell membrane, giving nodes of Ranvier spaced a few hundred micrometres apart where the impulse jumps.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Myelin makes conduction cheap per millimetre by confining current to the nodes, but it must be built and maintained by Schwann cells, and the sodium pumps at each node are heavy consumers during a burst.

PLASTICITY:

After nerve injury, demyelination and sodium channel clustering can make these axons fire spontaneously, generating shooting, electric pain.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Satellite glia surround the soma, and at the tip the bare ending sits within the mesh of nociceptive Schwann cells and among keratinocytes.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove A-delta nociceptors and injury still hurts, but the hurt loses its edge and its address, arriving as a slow spreading ache with no sharp beginning.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its speed lets the spinal cord run a protective reflex before conscious pain exists, so the same spike drives behaviour and perception on two different timescales.

Type one units display a striking use-dependent sensitisation after burn injury, its thin myelin makes it selectively vulnerable to pressure and to demyelinating disease, and its terminals in lamina I sit on the projection neurons that carry pain to the brainstem and thalamus.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: temperature in degrees Celsius, with type one responding above 53 and type two above 46; mechanical force in millinewtons, low threshold in type one and very high in type two; inflammatory mediators 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate onto lamina I neurokinin 1 receptor neurons and lamina V wide-dynamic-range neurons, plus reflex drive to spinal motor circuits.

• STATE: membrane voltage; nodal sodium channel availability; heat sensitisation offset; burn-induced threshold shift.

• PARAMETERS: conduction 5 to 30 metres per second; maximum firing about 100 impulses per second; type one heat threshold above 53 degrees Celsius; type two heat threshold about 46 degrees Celsius.

• TIME CONSTANTS: transduction 1 to 5 milliseconds; spike about 1 millisecond; foot to cord delay 30 to 60 milliseconds; burn sensitisation minutes to hours.

• FAILURE MODE: pressure and demyelinating neuropathy silence these fibres first, removing sharp pain and slowing withdrawal, while after nerve injury the same fibres fire spontaneously and produce shooting electric pain.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to model the two kinds of A-delta nociceptor and the first pain they deliver ahead of the C fibre signal.
Create a number variable called heat_threshold with initial value 53.
Create a number variable called force_threshold with initial value 5.
Create a number variable called burn_shift with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called rates with initial value empty list.
If unit_type is equal to two, then perform the following actions.
    Set heat_threshold to 46.
    Set force_threshold to 200.
End the conditional block.
For each sample in stimulus_trace, perform the following operations.
    Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
    Create a number variable called effective_heat with initial value 0.
    Set effective_heat to the difference of heat_threshold and burn_shift.
    If the temperature of sample is greater than effective_heat, then perform the following actions.
        Set drive to the sum of drive and the product of 8 and the difference of the temperature of sample and effective_heat.
        Set burn_shift to the sum of burn_shift and 0.3.
    End the conditional block.
    If the force of sample is greater than force_threshold, then perform the following actions.
        Set drive to the sum of drive and the quotient of the difference of the force of sample and force_threshold divided by 2.
    End the conditional block.
    If drive is greater than 100, then perform the following actions.
        Set drive to 100.
    End the conditional block.
    Add drive to the end of rates.
End the loop.
Return rates to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: one function covers both A-delta types by swapping two thresholds.
def a_delta_nociceptor(samples, unit_type=1):
    """samples: list of (temperature in C, force in mN). Returns spikes/s."""
    heat_thr = 53.0 if unit_type == 1 else 46.0      # type I is heat-resistant
    force_thr = 5.0 if unit_type == 1 else 200.0     # type II ignores pressure
    MAX_RATE, BURN_STEP = 100.0, 0.3
    burn_shift, rates = 0.0, []
    for temp_c, force in samples:
        effective = heat_thr - burn_shift            # a burn lowers the bar
        drive = 0.0
        if temp_c > effective:
            drive += 8.0 * (temp_c - effective)
            burn_shift += BURN_STEP                  # sensitisation after burn
        if force > force_thr:
            drive += (force - force_thr) / 2.0
        rates.append(round(min(drive, MAX_RATE), 1))
    return rates

if __name__ == "__main__":
    burn = [(55.0, 0.0)] * 5
    pinch = [(32.0, 50.0)] * 3
    print("type I burn: ", a_delta_nociceptor(burn, 1))
    print("type II burn:", a_delta_nociceptor(burn, 2))
    print("type I pinch:", a_delta_nociceptor(pinch, 1))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only pain fibre fast enough to beat its own C fibre partner to the spinal cord, and the only one silenced first when you sit on your leg.

CHAPTER 250. C-FIBRE POLYMODAL NOCICEPTORS (Second-Pain Burning Neuron) (NON-COG)

Touch a hot pan and a sharp jab comes first, but the deep burning that floods in a second later belongs to this cell, the slowest alarm wire in the body.

C-fibre is a conduction-class letter and polymodal is technical shorthand for responding to more than one kind of stimulus, so the old label teaches classification rather than experience.

Second-Pain names the cell's slot in the sequence, the delayed wave that follows the sharp opening sting of the same injury.

Burning names its quality, the dull spreading heat that is poorly localised because these endings are unmyelinated and conduct at roughly one metre per second, less than two in all.

Neuron marks it as the larger population, since C-fibres make up around seventy percent of all fibres carrying noxious input, and the name keeps the pairing with the first-pain cell visible, which is the whole point.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

It is a sensory neuron of the dorsal root ganglion (DRG), the bead of nerve cell bodies in the bony gap beside each vertebra.

Polymodal means many modes, and it answers crushing pressure, burning heat and irritant chemicals alike, carrying the slow ache that outlasts an injury.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is pseudounipolar, meaning one stalk leaves the round cell body and splits like a letter T, one branch to skin and one into the spinal cord.

Its ending is a free nerve ending, a bare spray of axon climbing between the cells of the epidermis, the outermost skin layer.

SIZE:

The cell body is 15 to 30 micrometres across, about a third the width of a hair.

The axon is 0.2 to 1.5 micrometres wide, conducts at 0.5 to 2 metres per second, and can be a metre long, so a toe signal needs about a second to arrive.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• The peptidergic subtype carries calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP), substance P and the receptor TrkA.

• The non-peptidergic subtype binds isolectin B4, carries the Mrgprd receptor and depends on the receptor Ret.

• Functionally they grade from CMH units answering force and heat to CMHC units that add cold.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies fill dorsal root ganglia at all 31 spinal levels on both sides, and endings reach epidermis, dermis, fascia, periosteum, joint capsule and tooth pulp.

Central terminals stop in laminae I and II outer of the dorsal horn, so the cell spans two interfaces: the epidermal border with the outside world, and the dorsal root entry zone where Schwann cell territory meets central nervous tissue.

NUMBER:

About 900,000 cells, roughly 9 per cent of the 10 million neurons in human dorsal root ganglia and about 0.001 per cent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the nervous system.

The fraction comes from rat lumbar recordings, where these units were 9.8 per cent of all neurons and 19.4 per cent of nociceptors.

Confidence is moderate for the fraction and low for the human total, since nobody has counted them directly.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage is near minus 55 millivolts and the spike is unusually wide at 2 to 5 milliseconds with a shoulder on its falling phase, because it runs on Nav1.8, a sodium channel that ignores the pufferfish poison tetrodotoxin.

An afterhyperpolarisation of 50 to 150 milliseconds holds firing to 0.5 to 20 spikes per second.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It excites lamina I projection neurons carrying the neurokinin 1 receptor, which relay to parabrachial nucleus and thalamus, plus local lamina II interneurons.

Its terminals carry mu-opioid, GABA-B and cannabinoid CB1 receptors, so morphine silences this cell before it speaks.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The bare axon rides in a Remak bundle, up to about 20 axons in the grooves of one Schwann cell, and repeated firing progressively slows it, a fingerprint used to identify it in living volunteers.

Spikes can also run backwards into neighbouring branches, the axon reflex that spreads the red flare around a scratch.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, loaded by VGLUT2, is the fast transmitter centrally, while CGRP and substance P are released both centrally and into skin, where they widen arterioles and make mast cells dump histamine.

MYELINATION:

None, which defines a C fibre and explains its slowness, although its Schwann cells still feed it lactate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

One small cell body must supply a metre of axon and a whole terminal tree, which is why these fibres die back from the toes first in diabetes, the stocking and glove pattern of small fibre neuropathy.

PLASTICITY:

Within minutes, prostaglandin E2 and bradykinin switch on protein kinases A and C, which alter the heat sensor TRPV1 and drop its trigger from about 43 degrees Celsius to below body temperature, so warm bathwater hurts.

Over days, nerve growth factor carried back to the nucleus raises TRPV1 and Nav1.8 production.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Each cell body is sealed in a complete satellite glial jacket only 20 nanometres away, an arrangement found nowhere else in the nervous system.

Those glia buffer potassium through Kir4.1 channels, and after injury they lose Kir4.1 and let neighbouring cell bodies excite one another.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete it and skin keeps touch, cold and the first sharp prick but loses the burning ache and the red flare, so wounds redden less, heal slower, and lose the alarm that pulls blood and immune cells into damaged tissue.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most striking act is at the far tip, where the axon reflex dumps CGRP and substance P into tissue with no message sent to the brain at all.

It also makes proteins inside its own axon, ships signalling endosomes to the nucleus over days, tunes blood flow, and steers immune cells, since animals lacking these neurons fight infection differently.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: terminal temperature in degrees Celsius, force in millinewtons, and protons, bradykinin, prostaglandin E2 and nerve growth factor within about 1 square millimetre.

• OUTPUTS: 0 to 20 hertz onto lamina I and II outer neurons, plus peripheral CGRP and substance P onto arterioles and mast cells.

• STATE: generator potential, TRPV1 sensitisation, afterhyperpolarisation timer, conduction slowing index.

• PARAMETERS: heat threshold 43 degrees Celsius, falling to 32 when inflamed, mechanical threshold about 90 millinewtons, conduction 0.5 to 2 metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 milliseconds, afterhyperpolarisation 50 to 150 milliseconds, sensitisation minutes, transcription 1 to 3 days.

• FAILURE MODE: loss-of-function mutations in SCN9A, the gene for Nav1.7, cause congenital insensitivity to pain, while gain-of-function mutations cause inherited erythromelalgia with burning red hands and feet.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn heat and inflammation into the slow burning discharge of a C-fibre polymodal nociceptor.
Create a number variable called threshold with initial value 43.
Create a number variable called membrane with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spikeCount with initial value 0.
For each sample in skinSamples, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the difference of 43 and the inflammation of sample.
    Set threshold to the result.
    Calculate the difference of the temperature of sample and threshold.
    Calculate the sum of membrane and the result.
    Set membrane to the result.
    Check if membrane is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if membrane is greater than 12.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of spikeCount and 1.
        Set spikeCount to the result.
        Set membrane to 0.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display spikeCount to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because this model needs no libraries and reads almost like the English above.

# Polymodal nociceptor: heat, force and inflammation feed one generator potential.
def polymodal_nociceptor(samples, dt=0.001):
    """samples: (temperature_C, force_mN, inflammation 0..1). Returns spike times."""
    v, refractory, spikes = 0.0, 0.0, []
    for i, (temp, force, infl) in enumerate(samples):
        threshold = 43.0 - 8.0 * infl        # inflammation lowers the TRPV1 threshold
        drive = 2.0 * infl                   # chemical arm
        if temp > threshold:
            drive += (temp - threshold) * 1.2
        if force > 90.0:
            drive += (force - 90.0) / 60.0   # mechanical arm, 90 mN threshold
        v = v * 0.85 + drive                 # leak plus generator current
        if refractory > 0.0:
            refractory -= dt
        elif v > 12.0:
            spikes.append(round(i * dt, 4))
            v, refractory = 0.0, 0.05        # 50 ms afterhyperpolarisation
    return spikes

trace = [(32.0, 0.0, 0.0)] * 2000 + [(48.0, 0.0, 0.3)] * 2000   # 2 s calm, 2 s burn
out = polymodal_nociceptor(trace)
print(len(out), "spikes, first at", out[0], "s")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Nothing else combines a bare ending in the epidermis, a spike wide enough to have a shoulder, a speed slower than walking, and the power to redden skin from its own tip.

CHAPTER 251. A-BETA NOCICEPTORS AND MODERATE PRESSURE RECEPTORS (Ultrafast Bruise-Signalling Neuron) (NON-COG)

For a century the textbooks said thick fast fibres carry touch and thin slow fibres carry pain, and this cell quietly breaks the rule by carrying pain at motorway speed.

The old label is two fibre-class codes joined by an and, which no one can say aloud and which hides the surprise it contains, that some of the thickest, fastest axons in the nerve, usually thought of as touch lines, carry pain.

Ultrafast records the solid measured fact, a conduction velocity of roughly thirty-three to seventy-five metres per second, faster than any other pain-carrying afferent in the body.

Bruise-Signalling names the stimulus and the purpose together, a firm damaging indentation rather than a light stroke, which is exactly what the old phrase moderate pressure receptor was groping at, and electrical stimulation of single such fibres in humans evokes a painful percept and drives fast protective withdrawal.

Neuron marks it as an afferent of which about half of rat lumbar cells with superficial receptive fields fall into that moderate-threshold group.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are dorsal root ganglion (DRG) neurons, from the bead of cell bodies beside each vertebra, with thick fast axons that nonetheless report damage rather than gentle touch.

The A-beta nociceptor needs tissue-threatening pressure, while the moderate pressure receptor has a far lower trigger yet still fires steadily throughout a squeeze rather than only at first contact.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is pseudounipolar, one stalk splitting into a peripheral and a central branch, with a large pale cell body.

Its ending is free and unencapsulated, which is what separates it from the corpuscle-wrapped touch fibres of the same size.

SIZE:

The cell body is 40 to 70 micrometres across, two to three times a C-fibre cell body.

The axon is 6 to 12 micrometres wide and conducts at 30 to 100 metres per second, with human hairy-skin recordings putting fast high-threshold units near 48.5 metres per second.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Moderate pressure receptors have superficial fields and thresholds in the tens of millinewtons, and about half of superficial A-beta nociceptors belong to this group.

• Classic high-threshold A-beta nociceptors need roughly 90 millinewtons or more and sit deeper, in dermis, fascia and joint.

• A minority carries calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) and substance P at low levels, with low TrkA, the nerve growth factor receptor.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies lie in dorsal root ganglia at all spinal levels, with endings in hairy and glabrous skin, joint capsule, periosteum and deep fascia.

Centrally the thick axon dives ventrally and arborises in laminae I, III, IV and V, so it straddles the interface between the deep touch layers and the superficial pain layers.

NUMBER:

About 600,000 cells, roughly 6 per cent of the 10 million neurons in human dorsal root ganglia and about 0.0007 per cent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the nervous system.

Rat lumbar recordings put moderate pressure receptors at 1.6 per cent of ganglion neurons and A-beta nociceptors overall at a looser 5 to 9 per cent.

Confidence is low to moderate, because electrodes favour either very large or very small cells.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Its spike is 0.8 to 2 milliseconds, narrower than a C fibre's but wider than a same-sized touch fibre's, often with a small shoulder.

Firing is slowly adapting, starting in the tens to low hundreds of spikes per second and settling to a level set by the force still applied.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It excites lamina I projection neurons directly and, through its deep arbor, wide dynamic range neurons in lamina V that pool touch and pain.

Its terminals receive presynaptic inhibition from GABAergic interneurons, the brake believed to fail in mechanical allodynia, where light touch hurts.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thick and myelinated, with nodes of Ranvier about 1 millimetre apart, so the spike jumps node to node and a foot pinprick registers within about 20 milliseconds.

Unlike C fibres it shows little activity-dependent slowing, another way experimenters tell them apart.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, loaded by VGLUT1 in this large myelinated population, with a minority co-releasing CGRP and substance P at levels too low to redden skin.

MYELINATION:

Heavily myelinated, with many tens of membrane wraps per internode, which makes it fast but also the first casualty of demyelination, and a bared stretch can fire spontaneously.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Insulation confines sodium entry to the nodes and slashes the pumping bill, but Schwann cells must feed the axon lactate through monocarboxylate transporters, so it depends metabolically on its own sheath.

PLASTICITY:

After nerve injury these fibres can start making substance P and brain-derived neurotrophic factor, a switch that makes a touch-speed fibre behave chemically like a pain fibre.

That switch, plus lost spinal inhibition, is a leading explanation for why brushing the skin hurts after shingles.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Its satellite glial jacket is thinner and made of fewer cells than the dense sheath around a small dark C-fibre neuron, and along the axon one Schwann cell makes exactly one internode, unlike the shared Remak cradles of C fibres.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and pain loses its speed and its map: skin would keep slow burning pain and light touch but lose the instant, precisely placed mechanical pain that snatches your hand back.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its terminal sums force across a patch of tissue, and a central arbor spanning laminae I to V lets one cell drive discriminative and emotional pain circuits at once.

It even instructs its own insulation, since neuregulin 1 type III on its surface tells the Schwann cell how thick to build the myelin.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: indentation force in millinewtons at the ending, plus a local inflammatory sensitisation index from 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: a slowly adapting train of 0 to about 200 hertz onto lamina I, III, IV and V neurons, with a collateral toward the gracile and cuneate nuclei.

• STATE: accumulated impulse, slow adaptation variable, refractory timer, injury-switch flag.

• PARAMETERS: threshold about 20 millinewtons for a moderate pressure receptor and about 90 for a high-threshold unit, conduction 30 to 100 metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 4 milliseconds, adaptation 600 milliseconds, refractory 5 milliseconds, phenotypic switch 3 to 14 days.

• FAILURE MODE: after injury or demyelination it fires ectopically into pain circuits, producing mechanical allodynia in which a light brush is agony.

◦ In large fibre neuropathies it is lost early, leaving only slow burning pain with no sense of where the damage is.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert sustained pressure into the slowly adapting fast discharge of an A-beta nociceptor or moderate pressure receptor.
Create a number variable called adaptation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called impulse with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spikeCount with initial value 0.
For each force in forceSamples, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the difference of force and 20.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.3.
    Set drive to the result.
    Check if drive is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set drive to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of drive and 0.6.
    Calculate the difference of the result and adaptation.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.000833.
    Calculate the sum of adaptation and the result.
    Set adaptation to the result.
    Calculate the difference of drive and adaptation.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.0005.
    Calculate the sum of impulse and the result.
    Set impulse to the result.
    Check if impulse is greater than or equal to 1.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set impulse to 0.
        Calculate the sum of spikeCount and 1.
        Set spikeCount to the result.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because the fastest fibre in the pain system belongs in a tight allocation-free loop a whole-body simulator can run millions of times per second.

/* A-beta nociceptor and moderate pressure receptor: slowly adapting force coding. */
#include <stdio.h>
#define DT 0.0005    /* 0.5 ms step */

typedef struct { double impulse, adapt, refrac; } AbetaNociceptor;

/* mpr = 1 gives a moderate pressure receptor near 20 mN, 0 a high threshold unit near 90 mN. */
static int abeta_step(AbetaNociceptor *n, double force_mN, int mpr)
{
    double threshold = mpr ? 20.0 : 90.0, drive = 0.0, net;
    int fired = 0;
    if (force_mN > threshold)
        drive = (force_mN - threshold) * (mpr ? 0.30 : 0.18);
    n->adapt += (0.6 * drive - n->adapt) * (DT / 0.60);   /* 600 ms adaptation */
    net = drive - n->adapt;
    if (net < 0.0) net = 0.0;
    n->impulse += net * DT;   /* a threshold of 1.0 makes net a rate in hertz */
    if (n->refrac > 0.0) n->refrac -= DT;
    else if (n->impulse >= 1.0) { n->impulse -= 1.0; n->refrac = 0.005; fired = 1; }
    return fired;
}

int main(void)
{
    AbetaNociceptor n = {0.0, 0.0, 0.0};
    int i, spikes = 0;
    for (i = 0; i < 4000; i++)   /* 2 s of a steady 150 mN squeeze */
        spikes += abeta_step(&n, 150.0, 1);
    printf("moderate pressure receptor fired %d times in 2 s\n", spikes);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the contradiction of the ganglion, a fat fast myelinated axon of the kind that should report a feather, ending in a bare terminal that only speaks when something is being crushed.

CHAPTER 252. SILENT OR SLEEPING NOCICEPTORS (Inflammation-Woken Ache Neuron) (NON-COG)

Press healthy skin with three quarters of a newton, hard enough to leave a dent, and this fibre says nothing; inflame the same patch for a day and it wakes up screaming.

Silent or sleeping is an evocative half-name spoiled by the alternation and by describing only what the cell fails to do in health.

Inflammation-Woken names the condition under which it starts working, since these endings ignore even crushing mechanical force in normal tissue and acquire mechanical sensitivity only once tissue is inflamed, a switch that depends on inflammation raising the level of the membrane protein TMEM100 in them.

Ache names what their recruitment feels like, the deep sustained soreness of an inflamed joint or organ rather than a sharp sting.

Neuron marks it as a large hidden reserve, roughly thirty percent of all C-fibre afferents in viscera and joints and about fifteen to twenty percent in human skin, so un-silencing them adds a substantial new stream of input to the spinal cord.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

It is a dorsal root ganglion (DRG) neuron, from the bead of nerve cell bodies beside each vertebra, that is born mechanically deaf and fires at no squeeze, poke or stretch in healthy tissue.

It is the reserve alarm, switched on by injury so that damaged tissue becomes far more sensitive than healthy tissue.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is pseudounipolar, and its free ending has an unusually wide branching tree covering several centimetres of skin, which is why these fibres, not ordinary ones, spread the red flare that fans out well beyond a sting.

SIZE:

The cell body is 20 to 35 micrometres across, and the bare axon of 0.2 to 1.5 micrometres conducts at 0.5 to 1 metre per second, the slow end even for a C fibre.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Cutaneous mechano-insensitive units feel no force, though many respond to heat and are then called CH units.

• A rarer group answers nothing natural at all and betrays itself only when capsaicin, the chilli chemical, is injected.

• Visceral and deep silent nociceptors carry CHRNA3, the alpha 3 subunit of the nicotinic acetylcholine receptor, and are roughly half of all peptide-carrying afferents supplying organs and deep tissue.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies sit in dorsal root ganglia at all levels, most densely at thoracolumbar and lumbosacral levels, with endings in skin, joint capsule and synovium, bone, bladder wall and gut wall.

Central branches run in Lissauer's tract, the interface strip between the dorsal root entry zone and the dorsal horn, then terminate across several segments in laminae I and II outer.

NUMBER:

About 700,000 cells, roughly 7 per cent of the 10 million neurons in human dorsal root ganglia and about 0.0008 per cent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the nervous system.

Human skin recordings make mechanically insensitive units 15 to 20 per cent of C fibres, rising to about 30 per cent of C afferents in joints and viscera.

Confidence is moderate for skin and lower for deep tissue.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Its electrical threshold is high, its spike wide at 3 to 5 milliseconds, and it shows the most extreme activity-dependent slowing of any fibre, shedding more than 30 per cent of its speed during 2 hertz stimulation.

Once awake it fires slowly and irregularly, 0.1 to 5 spikes per second.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It excites lamina I projection neurons and lamina II interneurons, but because one fibre branches across several segments it recruits a far wider spinal territory than a normal nociceptor.

That spread is the accepted explanation for secondary hyperalgesia, where undamaged skin around an injury becomes painful to touch.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Unmyelinated and carried in Remak bundles, the grooved cradles of non-myelinating Schwann cells, with an unusually powerful axon reflex that dumps peptides across a large area of skin.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate is the fast transmitter centrally, and nearly all these cells are peptidergic, releasing calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) and substance P, which is why waking them reddens skin over a broad zone.

MYELINATION:

None, and that bareness is part of why it is so slow and so prone to slowing further when it works.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Almost nothing at rest, because a cell that never fires spends almost nothing pumping ions back, but demand climbs steeply once inflammation wakes it and it must build new sensor proteins.

PLASTICITY:

Plasticity is not a modifier here; it is the entire point of the cell.

Nerve growth factor from inflamed tissue raises production of TMEM100, a small membrane protein that is both required and sufficient to unsilence the fibre over hours, reversing over days once inflammation clears.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Its satellite glia look ordinary until inflammation arrives, when local glia and immune cells become the main source of the nerve growth factor that wakes it, so uniquely among sensory neurons this cell depends on its neighbours for its functional identity rather than merely for support.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete it and acute pain is untouched, but inflamed tissue stops being tender, so a sprained ankle would hurt when injured yet feel normal to press the next day and the protective limp that lets tissue heal would never appear.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its defining computation is a slow biochemical switch, a gene expression change that turns a deaf ending into a hair trigger over hours.

It spreads CGRP by volume transmission across centimetres, controls local blood flow, and signals to immune cells that feed nerve growth factor back to it, closing a loop between nervous and immune systems.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: force in millinewtons, tissue temperature in degrees Celsius, and nerve growth factor, bradykinin and proton levels at the ending.

• OUTPUTS: 0 to 5 hertz onto lamina I and II outer neurons across three or more spinal segments, plus wide-area peptide release into skin.

• STATE: TMEM100 level from 0 to 1, current mechanical threshold, conduction slowing index.

• PARAMETERS: resting threshold above 750 millinewtons, awakened threshold near 30 to 60 millinewtons, conduction 0.5 to 1 metre per second, field up to several square centimetres.

• TIME CONSTANTS: unsilencing rises with a time constant near 6 hours and decays over about 24 hours.

• FAILURE MODE: chronic inflammation keeps these fibres permanently awake, a major driver of pain in arthritis, interstitial cystitis and inflammatory bowel disease.

◦ Silencing them is an attractive drug target precisely because protective acute pain would survive.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to model how inflammation unsilences a sleeping nociceptor by lowering its mechanical threshold over hours.
Create a number variable called unsilencingProtein with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called mechanicalThreshold with initial value 750.
Create a number variable called firingRate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called elapsedHours with initial value 0.
While elapsedHours is less than 24, repeat the following steps.
    Calculate the difference of 1 and unsilencingProtein.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.0167.
    Calculate the sum of unsilencingProtein and the result.
    Set unsilencingProtein to the result.
    Calculate the product of unsilencingProtein and 700.
    Calculate the difference of 750 and the result.
    Set mechanicalThreshold to the result.
    Calculate the difference of appliedForce and mechanicalThreshold.
    Set firingRate to the result.
    Check if firingRate is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firingRate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Display firingRate to the console.
    Calculate the sum of elapsedHours and 0.1.
    Set elapsedHours to the result.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the interesting behaviour is a slow state change over a whole day, which reads best as plain sequential code.

# Silent nociceptor: nerve growth factor raises TMEM100, which drops the threshold.
RISE_H, FALL_H = 6.0, 24.0       # hours: un-silencing is fast, re-silencing is slow

def silent_nociceptor(force_mN, hours=48.0, dt=0.1, inflamed_until=24.0):
    """Returns a log of (hours, mechanical threshold in mN, firing rate in Hz)."""
    tmem100 = 0.0                # un-silencing protein level, 0 to 1
    log = []
    for i in range(int(hours / dt)):
        t = i * dt
        if t < inflamed_until:
            tmem100 += (1.0 - tmem100) * dt / RISE_H     # nerve growth factor present
        else:
            tmem100 -= tmem100 * dt / FALL_H             # inflammation has cleared
        threshold = 750.0 - 700.0 * tmem100  # 750 mN asleep, about 50 mN fully awake
        rate = max(0.0, (force_mN - threshold) / 25.0)
        log.append((round(t, 1), round(threshold, 1), round(rate, 2)))
    return log

for row in silent_nociceptor(200.0)[::120]:      # a steady 200 mN press, sampled 12-hourly
    print("t=%.1f h  threshold=%.1f mN  rate=%.2f Hz" % row)

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only sensory neuron identified by what it does not do, findable only by proving it exists with capsaicin or an electric shock after every natural stimulus has failed.

CHAPTER 253. THERMORECEPTORS (NON-COG)

Right now, with nothing touching your skin, hundreds of thousands of these fibres are quietly ticking, and a room feeling cool is nothing more than that ticking speeding up.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are dorsal root ganglion (DRG) neurons, from the bead of cell bodies beside each vertebra, reporting temperatures that are not yet damaging.

Cool fibres speed up as skin cools and warm fibres do the opposite, and because both fire steadily at normal skin temperature they can report change in either direction.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Each is pseudounipolar with a free unencapsulated ending, and cool endings sit remarkably shallow, within about 0.2 millimetres of the surface, serving a spot about 1 millimetre across, which is why you can map cold spots on your forearm with a chilled pin.

SIZE:

Cool cell bodies are 20 to 40 micrometres across and warm ones 15 to 30.

Cool fibres are mostly thinly myelinated A-delta axons of 1 to 5 micrometres at 5 to 30 metres per second, while warm fibres are unmyelinated C fibres at 0.5 to 2, which is why warmth feels more sluggish than cold.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Cool thermoreceptors carry TRPM8, the menthol receptor and principal cold detector, which opens as temperature falls below about 28 degrees Celsius.

• Warm thermoreceptors depend on TRPM2 and TRPV1 together, and deleting either blunts warmth without abolishing it, so no single warm sensor has been found.

• A separate population carrying TRPA1 contributes mainly in the noxious cold range below about 15 degrees Celsius.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies lie in dorsal root ganglia at all spinal levels, with face and cornea served by the trigeminal ganglion, and endings in the epidermis of hairy and glabrous skin and in the sclera of the eye.

Central terminals reach lamina I, the interface layer where the signal splits between a conscious route to thalamus and insula and an unconscious route through the parabrachial nucleus to the preoptic hypothalamus that runs body temperature.

NUMBER:

About 450,000 cells, roughly 4.5 per cent of the 10 million neurons in human dorsal root ganglia and about 0.0005 per cent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the nervous system.

Cool fibres outnumber warm ones in skin recordings by something like five or ten to one, so perhaps 380,000 cool and 70,000 warm.

Confidence is low, because warm fibres are hard to find with an electrode and are probably undercounted.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Cool fibres tick regularly at 2 to 10 spikes per second at a neutral skin temperature of 33 to 34 degrees Celsius, with static firing peaking near 27 to 29 degrees and the regular tick breaking into rhythmic bursts below about 30.

Cooling also causes a large transient overshoot, so a 9 degree step briefly drives firing far above its settled level.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Both types excite thermospecific lamina I neurons, which answer temperature but not pinch, and warm and cool channels inhibit each other through spinal interneurons, which is why interlaced warm and cool bars feel painfully hot in the thermal grill illusion.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Cool A-delta axons carry thin myelin with nodes about 0.2 to 0.5 millimetres apart, while warm C axons are bare and ride in Remak bundles, so warmth arrives at the brain roughly a second after cold from the same patch of skin.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate at the spinal terminal for both types, and cool fibres are largely non-peptidergic, so unlike nociceptors they do not flood skin with calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP), one reason cold alone does not redden it.

MYELINATION:

Cool fibres carry only a few wraps of Schwann cell membrane, intermediate between the bare C fibre and the thick touch fibre, while warm fibres carry none, and that mismatch is the direct cause of the difference in how quickly you feel cold and warmth.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Unusually high for a small neuron, because these cells never stop firing and must run their ion pumps continuously even when nothing at all is happening.

PLASTICITY:

TRPM8 is tuned by protein kinase C, so inflammation blunts cold sensitivity, and by the membrane lipid PIP2, so its set point drifts with membrane chemistry.

Chronic cold exposure and repeated menthol shift the population set point, part of how people acclimatise to a cold climate.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Nothing about the satellite glia around these cell bodies is known to be special, and the honest answer is that they behave like satellite glia elsewhere in the ganglion.

The genuinely unusual partner is the keratinocyte, because a cool ending sits so shallow that surrounding skin cells act both as thermal buffer and as a source of adenosine triphosphate that modulates firing.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and skin goes thermally blind while still feeling touch and burn, so you would notice a scalding kettle only once it hurt, would not feel a draught, and would lose the reflex shivering and sweating that holds core temperature within a degree of 37 all day.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Information rides in the pattern as well as the rate, since below 30 degrees Celsius the discharge organises into bursts and the spikes per burst encode temperature independently of overall rate.

Because it never falls silent, the brain reads a ratio between warm and cool populations rather than a threshold crossing, and the same output drives sweating and shivering through the hypothalamus without ever reaching consciousness.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: skin temperature in degrees Celsius at the ending, plus its rate of change in degrees per second.

• OUTPUTS: an ongoing 0 to about 40 hertz train onto thermospecific lamina I neurons, feeding the insula for sensation and the preoptic hypothalamus for thermoregulation.

• STATE: an adapting reference temperature, a burst phase variable, and the current static rate.

• PARAMETERS: cool peak near 27 degrees Celsius, background 2 to 10 hertz at 34, silence above about 40, warm range 30 to 45 with a peak near 42.

• TIME CONSTANTS: the adapting reference follows skin with a time constant near 1.5 seconds, bursts recur every 0.2 to 1 second, acclimatisation takes days.

• FAILURE MODE: the chemotherapy drug oxaliplatin makes cold agonising within hours by altering sodium channels in these fibres, so patients cannot hold a cold glass.

◦ In small fibre neuropathy they die back and thermal thresholds widen, the basis of quantitative sensory testing.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to produce the ongoing and dynamic discharge of a cool thermoreceptor from a stream of skin temperatures.
Create a number variable called referenceTemperature with initial value 34.
Create a number variable called staticRate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called dynamicRate with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called rateTrace with initial value empty list.
For each temperature in temperatureSamples, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the difference of temperature and 27.
    Calculate the product of the result and the result.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.045.
    Calculate the difference of 8 and the result.
    Set staticRate to the result.
    Check if staticRate is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set staticRate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of referenceTemperature and temperature.
    Calculate the product of the result and 6.
    Set dynamicRate to the result.
    Check if dynamicRate is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set dynamicRate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of staticRate and dynamicRate.
    Add the result to the end of rateTrace.
    Calculate the difference of temperature and referenceTemperature.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.033.
    Calculate the sum of referenceTemperature and the result.
    Set referenceTemperature to the result.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

JavaScript is chosen because the dynamic overshoot is a filtering problem a short array pipeline shows plainly, and it runs in any browser console with no installation.

// Cool thermoreceptor: a bell-shaped static rate plus a transient response to cooling.
function coolFibre(tempTrace, dt = 0.05) {
  const staticRate = t => Math.max(0, 8 - 0.045 * Math.pow(t - 27, 2));  // peak near 27 C
  let reference = tempTrace[0];        // slowly adapting reference temperature
  const out = [];
  for (const t of tempTrace) {
    const dynamic = Math.max(0, (reference - t) * 6);   // fires on the fall, not the level
    out.push(Number((staticRate(t) + dynamic).toFixed(2)));
    reference += (t - reference) * dt / 1.5;            // 1.5 s adaptation
  }
  return out;
}

const trace = Array(40).fill(34).concat(Array(60).fill(25));  // 2 s at 34 C, then 25 C
const rates = coolFibre(trace);
console.log("neutral skin   :", rates[30], "Hz");
console.log("peak on cooling:", Math.max(...rates).toFixed(2), "Hz");
console.log("3 s after step :", rates[99], "Hz");

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the sensory cell that never shuts up, firing steadily into a nervous system nobody is stimulating, so that silence itself becomes a signal.

CHAPTER 254. PRURICEPTORS (Itch-Sensing Scratch-Urge Neuron) (NON-COG)

Itch is the only sensation with a built-in reflex answer, and the fibre that creates the urge to scratch is the same fibre that scratching switches off.

Pruriceptor is built from the Latin prurire, to itch, so it teaches nothing to a reader who does not already know Latin, unlike the plain word itch.

Itch-Sensing names the input, the chemical signals such as histamine and chloroquine that these endings detect at the skin.

Scratch-Urge names the output and the reason the cell exists, because stimulating the skin terminals of this population in mice produces scratching and not pain behaviour, which is the evidence behind treating them as a dedicated line for itch.

Neuron marks it as a genuine minority only partly separable from pain lines, since between fifteen and forty percent of ganglion cells respond to histamine but only the few that respond with sustained discharge appear to signal itch.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

It is a dorsal root ganglion (DRG) neuron, from the bead of cell bodies beside each vertebra, tuned to chemicals signalling something crawling, stinging or wrong on the skin.

Its firing produces itch rather than pain, and itch produces scratching rather than withdrawal, a completely different behaviour from a nearly identical looking cell.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is pseudounipolar, with a free ending rising high into the epidermis, the outermost skin layer, close to the keratinocytes and immune cells that make itch chemicals.

Histamine-sensitive units have extraordinarily wide trees, with receptive fields measured in human volunteers up to about 85 millimetres across, which is why one bite makes a whole forearm itch.

SIZE:

The cell body is 15 to 30 micrometres across, among the smallest in the ganglion, and the bare axon conducts at about 0.5 to 1 metre per second, which is why itch builds slowly instead of arriving in an instant.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• The histamine line, called NP2, carries the histamine H1 receptor with MrgprA3 in mouse or MRGPRX1 in human, the receptor for the antimalarial drug chloroquine that makes people itch.

• The cytokine line, NP3, carries the interleukin 31 receptor, leukotriene and serotonin receptors and the peptide natriuretic peptide B, and drives the deep chronic itch of eczema.

• A third line, NP1, carries Mrgprd and answers beta-alanine, while the human receptor MRGPRX4 detects bile acids and explains the relentless itch of liver disease.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies sit in dorsal root ganglia at all spinal levels, with face and scalp served by the trigeminal ganglion, and endings concentrate at the dermal-epidermal junction, the interface where mast cells and nerve endings sit within micrometres of one another.

Central terminals reach laminae I and II outer, onto a dedicated relay of neurons carrying the gastrin-releasing peptide receptor.

NUMBER:
