About 250,000 cells, roughly 2.5 per cent of the 10 million neurons in human dorsal root ganglia and about 0.0003 per cent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the nervous system.

Mouse single-cell studies put the NP2 and NP3 groups near 10 per cent of ganglion neurons, but many of those also serve pain, so only the itch-dedicated share is counted here to avoid double counting with the nociceptor entries.

Confidence is low, and the honest bound is between 150,000 and 700,000.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Firing rates are strikingly low, 0.1 to 5 spikes per second even at maximum itch, so the brain reads sustained low chatter rather than a burst.

Histamine-sensitive units are mechanically insensitive, which is why itch and touch feel so different.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Central terminals release glutamate with natriuretic peptide B onto lamina I interneurons, which then release gastrin-releasing peptide onto the relay cells carrying itch upward.

Inhibitory interneurons defined by the transcription factor Bhlhb5 sit across that pathway and are what scratching recruits, which is why a scratch buys about half a minute of relief.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Unmyelinated, riding in Remak bundles, the grooved cradles of non-myelinating Schwann cells, and slowing markedly with use, while wide terminal trees let one axon gather chemical news from a large territory.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate is the fast transmitter and natriuretic peptide B the itch-selective co-transmitter that opens the labelled itch line in the cord.

Many also carry somatostatin, which disinhibits spinal itch circuits, and the peptidergic subset releases calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) into skin.

MYELINATION:

None, and that bareness is part of why itch is slow, spreading and poorly localised compared with a pinprick.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low, because these cells fire slowly, but sustained for weeks in chronic itch while terminals continuously renew receptors in an inflamed environment.

PLASTICITY:

In eczema, interleukin 31 from immune cells makes these fibres sprout, so itchy skin becomes physically more densely innervated over weeks.

Sensitised MrgprA3 neurons recruit the mechanical sensor Piezo2, turning light touch into itch, the everyday phenomenon called alloknesis in which a shirt collar suddenly itches.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The distinctive partners here are not glia but mast cells and keratinocytes, sitting within micrometres of the ending and supplying histamine, tryptase, interleukin 31 and leukotrienes on demand.

Satellite glia around the cell body behave conventionally, though in chronic itch they release interleukin 6 and help sustain firing after the skin trigger has gone.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and skin still feels pain, touch and temperature, but a mosquito bite would give a brief sting and then nothing, and the region would lose its chemical surveillance layer for parasites, plant irritants and allergens too small and too slow to hurt.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

It runs a labelled line, meaning its identity rather than its firing pattern sets the sensation, a design principle a simulator must encode explicitly.

It holds a two-way chemical conversation with immune cells, and it is subject to a behavioural feedback loop unlike any other sensory neuron, since the animal's own scratching recruits spinal inhibition that shuts the pathway down for tens of seconds.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: local histamine, interleukin 31, chloroquine-like ligands, bile acids, leukotriene C4 and serotonin, each as a 0 to 1 occupancy, plus a scratch boolean from the motor system.

• OUTPUTS: 0 to 5 hertz onto lamina I itch interneurons, with natriuretic peptide B released alongside glutamate.

• STATE: histamine pathway level, cytokine pathway level, scratch-driven inhibition, terminal sprouting density.

• PARAMETERS: peak firing about 15 hertz, conduction 0.5 to 1 metre per second, field up to 85 millimetres across, scratch inhibition depth about 85 per cent.

• TIME CONSTANTS: histamine response about 20 seconds, cytokine response about 2 minutes, scratch relief about 8 seconds, sprouting weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: in atopic dermatitis and prurigo nodularis these fibres fire for months, and the itch-scratch cycle damages skin, releasing more cytokines and driving more itch.

◦ Two drugs prove the mechanism: nemolizumab, blocking the interleukin 31 receptor, approved in 2024, and difelikefalin, a peripheral kappa opioid agonist approved in 2021 for the itch of dialysis.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to combine a fast histamine itch line and a slow cytokine itch line and then gate the result by scratching.
Create a number variable called histamineDrive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called cytokineDrive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called scratchBrake with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called itchRate with initial value 0.
For each sample in skinChemistrySamples, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the difference of the histamine of sample and histamineDrive.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.005.
    Calculate the sum of histamineDrive and the result.
    Set histamineDrive to the result.
    Calculate the difference of the cytokine of sample and cytokineDrive.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.00083.
    Calculate the sum of cytokineDrive and the result.
    Set cytokineDrive to the result.
    Calculate the difference of the scratching of sample and scratchBrake.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.0125.
    Calculate the sum of scratchBrake and the result.
    Set scratchBrake to the result.
    Calculate the product of histamineDrive and 9.
    Set itchRate to the result.
    Calculate the product of cytokineDrive and 6.
    Calculate the sum of itchRate and the result.
    Set itchRate to the result.
    Calculate the product of scratchBrake and 0.85.
    Calculate the difference of 1 and the result.
    Calculate the product of itchRate and the result.
    Set itchRate to the result.
    Display itchRate to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because itch is a race between two chemical lines and one behavioural brake, and small pure functions make that race easy to read and test.

# Pruriceptor: a fast histamine line, a slow cytokine line, and a scratch brake.
HIST_TAU, CYTO_TAU, SCRATCH_TAU = 20.0, 120.0, 8.0    # seconds

def pruriceptor(histamine, il31, scratching, dt=0.1, seconds=600):
    """Each argument is a function of time in seconds. Returns a trace in hertz."""
    h = c = brake = 0.0
    out = []
    for i in range(int(seconds / dt)):
        t = i * dt
        h += (histamine(t) - h) * dt / HIST_TAU       # H1 receptor onto TRPV1
        c += (il31(t) - c) * dt / CYTO_TAU            # IL-31 receptor onto TRPA1
        brake += ((1.0 if scratching(t) else 0.0) - brake) * dt / SCRATCH_TAU
        out.append(max(0.0, (9.0 * h + 6.0 * c) * (1.0 - 0.85 * brake)))
    return out

bite = lambda t: 1.0 if 30 <= t < 90 else 0.0     # a mosquito bite, 60 s
eczema = lambda t: 1.0 if t >= 30 else 0.0        # a flare that does not stop
scratch = lambda t: 300 <= t < 340                # 40 s of scratching
trace = pruriceptor(bite, eczema, scratch)
print("peak histamine itch %.2f Hz" % max(trace[:2000]))
print("while scratching    %.2f Hz" % trace[3200])
print("after scratching    %.2f Hz" % trace[4500])

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only sensory neuron whose output triggers a behaviour that silences it, and the only one that can be deaf to a hard poke while screaming at a molecule.

CHAPTER 255. VISCERAL AFFERENTS (Inner-Organ Stretch-Reporting Neuron) (NON-COG)

A heart attack is often felt in the left arm, because the nerve reporting from your heart shares a spinal address with the nerve reporting from your skin, and the brain guesses wrong.

Visceral afferent is an address plus a direction-of-travel term, saying where the wire starts and which way the signal runs but nothing about what is being reported.

Inner-Organ replaces the Latin viscera with the plain English a reader already owns.

Stretch-Reporting names the stimulus these endings actually care about, since most afferents supplying hollow organs are mechanosensitive and it is over-distension of a muscular wall, not cutting or burning, that hurts.

Neuron marks it as a thin minority of the ganglion, about seven percent of cell bodies for the gut and under ten percent in the mouse lower lumbar ganglion, which quietly explains why such pain feels vague and hard to point at.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are dorsal root ganglion (DRG) neurons, from the bead of cell bodies beside each vertebra, whose endings lie inside internal organs and report stretch, chemical irritation and damage as fullness, urgency, nausea, cramp and deep aching pain.

They are separate from the vagal afferents of the nodose ganglion, which carry unconscious housekeeping data to the brainstem; these spinal ones carry the discomfort.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Each is pseudounipolar, with free endings in several shapes: flat branching arrays in the mucosa, long fibres running with muscle bundles, and knotty endings on the serosal surface and in the mesentery, the fatty sheet suspending the gut.

The central branch is remarkable for how far it travels, dividing in Lissauer's tract and running up and down across many spinal segments before terminating.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 20 to 45 micrometres across, slightly larger on average than skin C-fibre cell bodies.

Roughly 80 to 90 per cent of the axons are unmyelinated C fibres at 0.5 to 2 metres per second, the rest thinly myelinated A-delta at 5 to 20.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Mucosal afferents have the lowest threshold of all, answering a 10 milligram stroke, and report the texture and chemistry of the contents.

• Muscular and muscular-mucosal afferents answer low-intensity stretch and encode how full an organ is.

• Serosal and mesenteric afferents are high-threshold nociceptors answering only strong distension or traction, and a further mechanically insensitive group stays silent until inflammation wakes it.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies occupy two clusters, thoracolumbar ganglia from about T5 to L2 whose axons run in splanchnic nerves and sacral ganglia at S2 to S4 whose axons run in the pelvic nerve, with endings in oesophagus, stomach, intestine, bladder, ureter, uterus, heart, great vessels and mesentery.

Central branches enter at the dorsal root entry zone, travel in Lissauer's tract and terminate in laminae I, II outer, V and X around the central canal, converging in laminae I and V onto the very neurons that receive skin input, which is the anatomical basis of referred pain.

NUMBER:

About 200,000 cells, roughly 2 per cent of the 10 million neurons in human dorsal root ganglia and about 0.0002 per cent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the nervous system.

Even in the ganglion supplying the colon most heavily, colorectal afferents are fewer than 10 per cent of the neurons present, and the share falls sharply in neighbouring ganglia.

Confidence is low to moderate, which is startling given that these cells report on the organs keeping you alive.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Spikes are wide at 2 to 5 milliseconds, carried by tetrodotoxin-resistant channels including Nav1.8 and Nav1.9.

Muscular afferents fire slowly in proportion to organ volume, while serosal units stay silent until distension becomes uncomfortable and then climb steeply to 20 or 40 spikes per second.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

They excite lamina I projection neurons and lamina V wide dynamic range neurons, and because one afferent branches across up to six segments, a single organ event floods a long stretch of cord.

Their terminals are strongly inhibited by mu-opioid receptors, which is why opioids relieve visceral pain so well and constipate the gut at the same time.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Mostly unmyelinated and grouped in Remak bundles, with a long indirect peripheral course through prevertebral ganglia such as the coeliac and inferior mesenteric ganglia, where sympathetic neurons can modulate these axons before they reach the cord.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate is the fast transmitter, with calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) and substance P as co-transmitters in most of these cells.

Peripherally they carry TRPV1 in high proportion, 69 per cent of bladder-projecting ganglion neurons in the rat, along with the P2X3 receptor that detects adenosine triphosphate released by the stretched lining.

MYELINATION:

Predominantly absent, and the thin or missing sheath is one reason visceral sensation is slow, dull and hard to place.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate and continuous, because low-threshold visceral afferents report organ filling all day, unlike skin nociceptors that spend most of their lives silent.

PLASTICITY:

Sensitisation here is dramatic and well measured: an inflammatory mixture applied to a stretch-sensitive colonic ending drops its threshold from about 91 millinewtons to about 31, so ordinary filling now hurts.

After gastroenteritis that change can persist for months, the leading explanation for post-infectious irritable bowel syndrome.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

At the far end the specialised partners are not glia at all but enteric glia and the enterochromaffin cells of the gut lining, which release serotonin directly onto the ending, making the gut wall itself part of the sensor.

Around the cell body the satellite jacket is conventional, though in colitis these glia proliferate and couple more strongly, sustaining pain after the gut has healed.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and you would not know your bladder was full, would not feel hunger, fullness, nausea or the cramp that signals obstruction, and a heart attack would be silent, which is exactly what happens to some people with long-standing diabetes.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most consequential property is architectural rather than electrical, because massive divergence in the cord plus convergence with skin inputs means the brain cannot localise the source, producing referred pain in a predictable map.

It senses chemistry through a second cell, since serotonin from enterochromaffin cells and adenosine triphosphate from stretched lining cells are its real stimuli, and it drives gut motility reflexes through prevertebral ganglia in a loop that never reaches the brain.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: wall tension in millinewtons, luminal pH and osmolarity, and local serotonin, adenosine triphosphate and inflammatory mediator levels, with an inflammation index from 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: 0 to about 40 hertz onto laminae I, II outer, V and X neurons across up to six spinal segments, plus peptide release driving local reflexes.

• STATE: current threshold, sensitisation level, adaptation variable, peptide stores.

• PARAMETERS: mucosal threshold near 0.1 millinewtons, muscular in the low tens, serosal about 91 millinewtons falling to about 31 when inflamed, saturating rate near 40 hertz.

• TIME CONSTANTS: mechanical adaptation seconds, acute sensitisation minutes, post-infectious sensitisation months.

• FAILURE MODE: in irritable bowel syndrome and bladder pain syndrome these afferents fire at normal organ volumes, so ordinary filling is agony with no damage present.

◦ The drug linaclotide acts on guanylate cyclase C in the gut lining to raise cyclic guanosine monophosphate, which quietens these endings directly.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert hollow organ wall tension into the saturating discharge of a visceral afferent whose threshold falls with inflammation.
Create a number variable called restingThreshold with initial value 91.
Create a number variable called workingThreshold with initial value 91.
Create a number variable called excess with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called saturationTerm with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firingRate with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called rateTrace with initial value empty list.
For each sample in organSamples, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the difference of restingThreshold and 31.
    Calculate the product of the result and the inflammation of sample.
    Calculate the difference of restingThreshold and the result.
    Set workingThreshold to the result.
    Calculate the difference of the tension of sample and workingThreshold.
    Set excess to the result.
    Check if excess is less than or equal to 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firingRate to 0.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of excess and 60.
        Set saturationTerm to the result.
        Calculate the quotient of excess divided by saturationTerm.
        Calculate the product of the result and 40.
        Set firingRate to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    Add firingRate to the end of rateTrace.
End the loop.
Display firingRate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because a gut simulation runs thousands of afferents against a moving wall model on every time step, and a plain struct with no allocation keeps that affordable.

/* Visceral afferent: high threshold stretch coding that sensitises with inflammation. */
#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    double threshold_mN;   /* resting mechanical threshold at the ending */
    double sens;           /* 0 = healthy, 1 = fully sensitised */
    double rate_Hz;        /* most recent output */
} ColonAfferent;

/* Inflammation drops a serosal threshold from about 91 mN to about 31 mN. */
static double visceral_rate(ColonAfferent *a, double tension_mN)
{
    double thr = a->threshold_mN - (a->threshold_mN - 31.0) * a->sens;
    double x = tension_mN - thr;
    /* saturating curve: climbs steeply, then plateaus near 40 Hz */
    a->rate_Hz = (x <= 0.0) ? 0.0 : 40.0 * x / (x + 60.0);
    return a->rate_Hz;
}

int main(void)
{
    ColonAfferent healthy = {91.0, 0.0, 0.0}, inflamed = {91.0, 1.0, 0.0};
    double p;
    printf("tension_mN   healthy_Hz   inflamed_Hz\n");
    for (p = 20.0; p <= 200.0; p += 45.0)
        printf("%9.0f %12.2f %12.2f\n", p,
               visceral_rate(&healthy, p), visceral_rate(&inflamed, p));
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the sensory neuron that cannot tell you where it is, because its central branch spreads across half a dozen spinal segments and lands on the same cells that serve a patch of skin.


PART SIXTEEN: AUTONOMIC GANGLION NEURONS

CHAPTER 256. SYMPATHETIC POSTGANGLIONIC NEURONS (NON-COG)

String a necklace of nerve knots down each side of the spine, hang a few more around the great arteries of the belly, and you have the relay station that turns a spinal whisper into a whole body command.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The sympathetic postganglionic neuron is the second and final cell in the two cell chain that carries sympathetic commands to the body.

It receives acetylcholine from a preganglionic axon and passes the message on to a target organ, almost always by releasing noradrenaline, the chemical cousin of adrenaline.

This entry describes the archetype, the principal ganglion cell.

The three entries that follow describe its specialised working versions, and those are drawn from this same population rather than added to it.

Its job is amplification and distribution: a few hundred thousand preganglionic cells hand off to several million of these, and each of these then spreads its output over centimetres of blood vessel, gland or muscle.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A multipolar cell, meaning several dendrites radiate from the cell body in different directions, with a single axon leaving from one pole.

Unlike neurons in the brain, its dendrites are short, thick and few, typically four to twelve main branches forming a loose ball around the cell body rather than a sprawling tree.

The whole cell is wrapped in a jacket of satellite glial cells, and the incoming axon pushes through a gap in that jacket to make a small cluster of contacts directly on the cell body or the first stretch of a dendrite.

The axon is the striking part: thin, unmyelinated and enormously long relative to the cell, ending not in a single terminal but in a string of thousands of swellings called varicosities, threaded through the target tissue like beads on a wire.

SIZE:

In the human superior cervical ganglion the cell bodies average about 25 micrometres across, with a mean cross sectional area of 509 to 528 square micrometres.

Eighty five per cent fall in a small class of 15 to 30 micrometres and most of the rest in a medium class of 30 to 40 micrometres.

The axon is 0.2 to 1.5 micrometres wide and may run 30 centimetres or more.

A single axon supplying a leg artery can carry ten thousand or more varicosities spread over a terminal arbour tens of centimetres long.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Paravertebral principal neurons in the sympathetic chain, small, supplying skin, skeletal muscle vessels, sweat glands, hair muscles and the heart.

• Prevertebral principal neurons in the coeliac, superior mesenteric and inferior mesenteric ganglia, larger, with long dendrites, and unique in receiving direct input from sensory neurons of the gut wall so that they can run reflexes without consulting the spinal cord at all.

• Small intensely fluorescent cells, tiny dopamine containing interneurons scattered between the principal cells, which modulate rather than relay.

• By chemical coat: noradrenaline plus neuropeptide Y, noradrenaline plus somatostatin, noradrenaline plus calcitonin gene related peptide, and the cholinergic class described in the next entry.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The paravertebral sympathetic chain, a string of 20 to 24 paired ganglia running from the base of the skull to the tailbone, where the two chains meet at the single ganglion impar.

Named members include the superior cervical ganglion behind the carotid artery, the middle cervical ganglion, the stellate ganglion where the inferior cervical and first thoracic ganglia fuse, and eleven or twelve thoracic, four lumbar and four or five sacral ganglia.

The prevertebral ganglia sit on the front of the abdominal aorta at the coeliac, superior mesenteric and inferior mesenteric arteries, with the aorticorenal ganglion as an offshoot.

A third home is the terminal or short ganglion embedded in the wall of a pelvic organ.

Two interfaces are explicit.

The chain ganglia sit at the junction between spinal nerve and blood vessel, joined to the spinal nerves by white rami carrying traffic in and grey rami carrying traffic back out.

The prevertebral ganglia sit at the interface between the central autonomic system and the enteric nervous system of the gut, and are the only place where the two hold a two way conversation.

NUMBER:

Several million in an adult human, with a defensible range of about 3 million to 6 million and a working central estimate near 4 million.

Confidence is moderate: individual ganglia have been counted in animals and sampled in humans, but nobody has counted every ganglion in one body.

That figure sits inside the roughly 20 million neuron budget for all autonomic ganglia, and it already includes the sudomotor, vasoconstrictor, pilomotor and cardiomotor cells detailed in the next two entries.

It is about four or five neurons in every hundred thousand in the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage about minus 55 to minus 60 millivolts, with threshold about 15 millivolts above that.

Two personalities exist and can be told apart in a slice of living tissue.

Phasic cells fire one or two spikes at the start of a long current step and then fall silent, held back by a potassium current called the M current, while tonic cells fire steadily throughout.

The M current is the key to the whole ganglion: it is a slow potassium leak that keeps the cell hyperpolarised, and shutting it off is how the ganglion changes gear.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Each cell receives, on average, a handful of preganglionic axons.

Usually one is strong enough on its own to force a spike every time, the so called primary or dominant input, and the rest are weak secondaries that only matter when they add together.

Modelling of human recordings suggests an average of about two effective preganglionic inputs per cell.

Three postsynaptic events follow, on three timescales.

A fast excitatory potential lasting tens of milliseconds comes through nicotinic receptors.

A slow excitatory potential begins after about one second and lasts 30 to 60 seconds, produced by acetylcholine acting on M1 muscarinic receptors that close the M current channels built from the Kv7.2 and Kv7.3 proteins.

A late slow potential lasting minutes is produced by peptides such as substance P and luteinising hormone releasing hormone.

On the output side there are no classical synapses at all: transmitter is released from varicosities into a gap 50 to 100 nanometres wide in small vessels and up to 2 micrometres wide in large ones.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Unmyelinated C fibres, 0.2 to 1.5 micrometres in diameter, conducting slowly at about 0.5 to 2 metres per second.

They re enter the spinal nerves through the grey rami communicantes, which exist at every spinal level, and then travel with the nerve to skin and muscle, or they wrap around arteries and travel with them as a perivascular plexus.

An impulse launched at the neck can take half a second to reach a toe.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Noradrenaline is the headline, built from the amino acid tyrosine by a chain of enzymes and loaded into small dense cored vesicles.

It is never released alone.

Adenosine triphosphate, the cell's own energy molecule, is co stored in the same vesicles and acts on P2X1 purinergic receptors to produce the fast, sharp first phase of vessel narrowing, while noradrenaline on alpha1 adrenoceptors produces the slower, sustained phase.

Neuropeptide Y sits in separate large vesicles, is released only during intense or bursting activity, and acts both to strengthen the constriction at the muscle and to feed back on the nerve terminal and suppress further release of the other two.

The cholinergic sudomotor class is the great exception and gets its own entry.

MYELINATION:

Essentially none.

The postganglionic axon is bundled with dozens of others inside a single Schwann cell in what is called a Remak bundle, each axon lying in its own shallow groove without a spiral wrap.

This is why sympathetic responses are slow, why grey rami look grey, and why sympathetic outflow is a diffuse wash rather than a precise pulse.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high for the axon and modest for the cell body.

Manufacturing and pumping noradrenaline into thousands of varicosities, recovering roughly 80 per cent of it afterwards through the noradrenaline transporter, and running vesicle traffic along tens of centimetres of unmyelinated axon costs far more energy than the occasional spike.

Because the axon is unmyelinated, every square micrometre of membrane must repolarise itself, so sodium pump costs scale with an enormous surface area.

PLASTICITY:

These neurons never stop remodelling their terminals.

Blood vessel innervation density tracks vessel use across the lifespan, and terminals sprout in high blood pressure and retract in heart failure.

Nerve growth factor released by the target tissue is the currency: more target activity means more nerve growth factor, which means more sprouting.

The cell body can also change which transmitter it makes, which is one of the most remarkable facts in neuroscience and is described in the next entry.

After spinal cord injury, thoracic chain neurons rearrange their inputs even though the neurons themselves were never touched.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

There are no astrocytes and no oligodendrocytes out here.

Satellite glial cells form a complete envelope around each cell body, leaving only small windows for synapses, and they do something distinctive: in the tight extracellular space of a ganglion they mop up potassium and transmitter, and after injury they multiply and release inflammatory signals that make the neurons more excitable.

Schwann cells bundle the axons without myelinating them and also regulate how much noradrenaline survives in the junction.

Resident macrophages, not microglia, do the immune policing.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

The sympathetic chain without its principal neurons would be a bundle of passing fibres and nothing else, an empty relay.

Remove the cell and every artery in the body loses its background tone, blood pressure falls, and the person cannot stand without fainting.

The skin goes warm, pink and dry, the pupil stays small, the eyelid droops and the face flushes on one side only if the loss is one sided, the picture called Horner syndrome.

The whole notion of a body that can prepare for effort in advance disappears.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The ganglion is a gain controller, not a wire.

Muscarinic closure of the M current does not make the cell fire by itself; it makes previously useless weak inputs suddenly effective, multiplying the ganglion's throughput without adding a single new synapse.

Neuropeptide Y released from the same terminals acts back on the terminal to suppress further release, a self limiting brake.

Transmitter escapes the junction and acts at a distance on nearby smooth muscle, a form of volume transmission.

Nitric oxide from neighbouring nerves and from the vessel lining diffuses back and damps release.

The terminals are also immune organs: noradrenaline released into lymph nodes and spleen changes how lymphocytes behave.

And the cell reads its target chemically, switching which genes it expresses according to whatever tissue its axon happens to reach.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Nicotinic drive from 1 to 8 preganglionic axons, one usually strong enough to fire the cell alone, the others worth 3 to 8 millivolts each.

◦ Muscarinic slow drive from the same axons at firing rates above about 5 impulses per second.

◦ For prevertebral cells only, direct cholinergic input from intestinofugal neurons of the gut wall.

◦ Nerve growth factor from the target tissue, setting terminal density over days.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Noradrenaline and adenosine triphosphate from varicosities onto alpha1, alpha2 and P2X1 receptors of smooth muscle.

◦ Neuropeptide Y at high firing rates only.

◦ Noradrenaline into lymphoid tissue as an immune signal.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage.

◦ M current activation, on a scale from 0 to 1.

◦ Vesicle pool size and separate neuropeptide Y pool size.

◦ Terminal arbour density.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting voltage minus 58 millivolts and threshold minus 45 millivolts.

◦ Conduction velocity 0.5 to 2 metres per second.

◦ Varicosities per axon 10 thousand to 30 thousand.

◦ Noradrenaline reuptake fraction about 0.8.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Fast nicotinic potential 5 to 50 milliseconds.

◦ Slow muscarinic potential, onset 1 second and duration 30 to 60 seconds.

◦ Late slow peptide potential 1 to 5 minutes.

◦ Terminal remodelling days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Autoimmune autonomic ganglionopathy, in which antibodies against the alpha3 subunit of the ganglionic nicotinic receptor drag the receptor inside the cell and break transmission; such antibodies are found in about half of cases and cause fainting, dry mouth, dry eyes and a paralysed gut.

◦ Deposits of the protein alpha synuclein in these neurons in pure autonomic failure and Parkinson disease, causing a fall in blood pressure on standing.

◦ Surgical or chemical loss produces Horner syndrome, or, if both sides are lost, catastrophic postural hypotension.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to work out whether weak nicotinic inputs to a sympathetic postganglionic neuron reach threshold, given the state of the muscarinic slow excitatory potential.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value negative fifty eight.
Create a number variable called threshold with initial value negative forty five.
Create a number variable called m current activation with initial value one.
Create a number variable called weak input count with initial value four.
Create a number variable called weak input size with initial value six.
Create a boolean variable called muscarinic input present with initial value true.
Create a number variable called summed input with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called m current hyperpolarisation with initial value zero.
Create a boolean variable called the neuron fired with initial value false.
Check if muscarinic input present is equal to true.
If the result of that check is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set m current activation to the product of m current activation and zero point one.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Set m current activation to one.
End the conditional block.
Set m current hyperpolarisation to the product of m current activation and fifteen.
Set summed input to the product of weak input count and weak input size.
Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and summed input.
Set membrane voltage to the difference of membrane voltage and m current hyperpolarisation.
Check if membrane voltage is greater than threshold.
If the result of that check is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set the neuron fired to true.
    Display the text value a spike leaves for the blood vessel to the console.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Set the neuron fired to false.
    Display the text value the weak inputs died inside the ganglion to the console.
End the conditional block.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because a whole body simulation needs millions of these cells stepped at sub millisecond resolution, and this is the form the inner loop will actually take.

#include <stdio.h>

/* One paravertebral sympathetic postganglionic neuron. Weak secondary
   nicotinic inputs summate; whether they reach threshold depends on the
   muscarinic slow excitatory potential, which works by closing the M type
   potassium current. */

#define V_REST  -60.0   /* millivolts */
#define E_K     -90.0   /* millivolts, potassium reversal */
#define V_TH    -45.0   /* millivolts, spike threshold */
#define TAU_MEM  20.0   /* milliseconds */
#define TAU_KM  150.0   /* milliseconds, M current relaxation */
#define TAU_SYN   5.0   /* milliseconds, nicotinic decay */
#define G_KM      0.5   /* M current strength */

typedef struct { double v; double m; double g; } Cell;

static int step(Cell *c, int weak_spikes, int muscarinic_on, double dt)
{
    double i_km = G_KM * c->m * (c->v - E_K);
    c->g += 6.0 * weak_spikes;                    /* 6 mV per weak input */
    c->v += dt * ((V_REST - c->v) - i_km + c->g) / TAU_MEM;
    c->g -= dt * c->g / TAU_SYN;
    c->m += dt * ((muscarinic_on ? 0.0 : 1.0) - c->m) / TAU_KM;
    if (c->v >= V_TH) { c->v = V_REST - 5.0; return 1; }
    return 0;
}

static int run(int muscarinic_on)
{
    Cell c; int t, n = 0;
    c.v = V_REST; c.m = 1.0; c.g = 0.0;
    for (t = 0; t < 5000; t++)          /* 500 ms at dt = 0.1 ms */
        n += step(&c, (t % 30 == 0) ? 2 : 0, muscarinic_on, 0.1);
    return n;
}

int main(void)
{
    printf("weak inputs alone:          %d spikes in 500 ms\n", run(0));
    printf("weak inputs plus slow EPSP: %d spikes in 500 ms\n", run(1));
    return 0;
}

The same weak inputs that die silently in the first run drive a train of spikes in the second, and that difference is the entire point of having a ganglion.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A small multipolar cell outside the central nervous system, jacketed in satellite glia, receiving a cholinergic input and sending out an unmyelinated axon that releases noradrenaline from thousands of beads rather than from one endpoint.

Stain a tissue for tyrosine hydroxylase and dopamine beta hydroxylase, the two enzymes on the road to noradrenaline, and these cells and their beaded fibres light up while nothing else in the peripheral nervous system does.

CHAPTER 257. SUDOMOTOR SYMPATHETIC NEURONS (Sweat-Releasing Cooling Neuron) (NON-COG)

This is the traitor of the sympathetic nervous system: a cell born to make noradrenaline that is talked out of it by the very gland it grows towards, and spends the rest of its life making acetylcholine instead.

Sudomotor is Latin for sweat-mover and teaches a first-year student nothing, so the name says sweat in English.

Sweat-Releasing names the act, because this is the one sympathetic output cell that switches its chemistry, abandoning noradrenaline for acetylcholine acting on muscarinic receptors on the eccrine gland, a switch instructed by the gland itself during development.

Cooling names the purpose the sweat serves, evaporative heat loss, which is why this pathway fires with body temperature rather than with fear alone.

Neuron marks it as a postganglionic sympathetic cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Sudomotor neurons are the sympathetic postganglionic cells that make you sweat.

Each one sits in a sympathetic chain ganglion, sends an unmyelinated axon out to the skin, and wraps its terminals around eccrine sweat glands, the coiled tubes that produce watery sweat all over the human body.

When it fires it releases acetylcholine, which is startling, because every other sympathetic postganglionic neuron releases noradrenaline.

The gland responds by pushing salty water up onto the skin surface, where evaporation carries heat away.

These cells are therefore the final common path for the only cooling system a human being has, and they are also the cells that make your palms damp when you are frightened.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Structurally they look like ordinary principal ganglion cells: a small multipolar body with a few short stubby dendrites, wrapped in satellite glia.

The difference is chemical, and it is visible under a microscope.

They carry the vesicular acetylcholine transporter, the pump that loads acetylcholine into vesicles, and in humans and other primates many of them keep tyrosine hydroxylase, the first enzyme of noradrenaline manufacture, at the same time.

Their terminals form dense baskets around the secretory coil of the sweat gland, and each axon supplies many glands.

SIZE:

Cell body 20 to 30 micrometres across, at the smaller end of the ganglionic range.

Axon 0.2 to 1 micrometre wide and unmyelinated, running up to a metre from a lumbar ganglion to the sole of the foot.

The target is smaller still: a human eccrine gland is a coiled tube about 3 to 5 millimetres long packed into a ball roughly 0.5 millimetres across.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Thermoregulatory sudomotor neurons, supplying general body skin, driven by core and skin temperature.

• Emotional sudomotor neurons, supplying the palms, soles and armpits, driven by arousal and almost unresponsive to heat.

• Cholinergic only cells and cells co expressing the cholinergic and noradrenergic machinery, the latter being characteristic of primates including humans.

• By peptide content: sudomotor neurons contain vasoactive intestinal peptide and calcitonin gene related peptide, and consistently lack the calcium binding protein calbindin, which is how they are told apart from vasoconstrictor cells in the same ganglion.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies lie in the paravertebral sympathetic chain ganglia only, never in prevertebral ganglia.

The hand is served mainly from the second to the eighth thoracic ganglia and the stellate ganglion at the root of the neck; the face from the superior cervical ganglion; the trunk from the matching thoracic ganglia; and the foot from the lumbar ganglia.

Axons leave through the grey rami communicantes and travel inside ordinary peripheral nerves, which places the interface of interest at the sweat gland itself, where a sympathetic terminal meets an epithelial secretory cell across a gap of a few hundred nanometres.

NUMBER:

Best estimate 300 thousand to 700 thousand in an adult human, with a working central value near 500 thousand, at low confidence.

These are drawn from the several million sympathetic postganglionic neurons already counted in the previous entry and must not be added to that total.

The estimate is anchored by the fact that humans carry roughly 2 to 4 million eccrine sweat glands, and each sudomotor axon supplies several glands.

They are about six neurons in every million in the whole nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Recorded one axon at a time in awake humans by microneurography, a technique in which a fine tungsten needle is pushed into a nerve, a single sudomotor neuron fires at only about 0.5 impulses per second and takes part in only about 30 per cent of the bursts passing along the nerve.

Crucially, its bursts are not locked to the heartbeat, unlike the muscle vasoconstrictor neurons lying beside it in the same nerve.

That single observation is how a physiologist knows which kind of neuron is on the electrode: sudomotor traffic follows a deep breath, a sudden noise or a rise in temperature, not the pulse.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input is nicotinic, from preganglionic neurons in the intermediolateral column that are themselves driven by the median preoptic nucleus of the hypothalamus, the body's thermostat, relayed through the rostral medullary raphe.

Emotional sweating is driven instead from the limbic system through the same final path, which is why the two kinds of sweating can be dissociated by disease.

Output is a chemical spray onto muscarinic M3 receptors on the clear cells of the gland's secretory coil, which drives chloride and then water out of the cell.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Unmyelinated C fibres conducting at about 1 metre per second.

They branch extensively in the dermis, and that branching is clinically exploited: put acetylcholine on the skin and the impulse travels backwards up one branch and forwards down another to make a neighbouring patch of skin sweat, a trick called the axon reflex and the basis of the quantitative sudomotor axon reflex test used to diagnose small fibre nerve damage.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine, on muscarinic M3 receptors, which is why the anti sweating drug glycopyrrolate and the poison atropine both dry you out, and why botulinum toxin injected into an armpit stops sweating for months by blocking acetylcholine release.

Co released with it are vasoactive intestinal peptide, which widens the local blood vessels so the gland has water to work with, and calcitonin gene related peptide.

The remarkable part is the history: these neurons leave the neural crest making noradrenaline, and when their growing tips reach the developing sweat gland the gland releases a cholinergic differentiation factor that rewrites the neuron's chemistry.

Blocking that signal keeps the neuron noradrenergic and the sweat gland never works properly, which means the target chooses the transmitter, not the neuron.

MYELINATION:

None.

Sudomotor axons run in Remak bundles inside the peripheral nerve, sharing a Schwann cell with vasoconstrictor and sensory C fibres.

Their lack of myelin is why they are among the first fibres lost in diabetes and why a dry, cracked foot is often the earliest visible sign of diabetic nerve damage.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low at rest and high in the heat.

The neuron itself is cheap, but sustaining maximal sweating means firing continuously for hours while the gland moves up to two litres of fluid per hour across the whole body, so the terminal must resynthesise acetylcholine as fast as acetylcholinesterase destroys it.

PLASTICITY:

The transmitter switch is the headline, and it is not only a developmental event: transplant a sympathetic neuron to a new target in adulthood and its chemistry follows the target.

Sudomotor function also adapts to training and climate, with heat acclimatisation over ten to fourteen days raising sweat rate and lowering its salt content, changes that involve both the gland and its nerve supply.

After nerve injury, sudomotor axons regenerate and often reinnervate the wrong glands, producing patches of sweating in odd places.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia around the terminal are the interesting ones.

Terminal Schwann cells around the sweat gland do not simply insulate; they sit at the junction, take up transmitter and, during development, help hold the growing sympathetic tip at the gland until the switching signal has done its work.

In the ganglion, satellite glia behave as they do for any other principal cell.

The unusual support element is not a glial cell at all: it is the sweat gland, which acts as a chemical tutor to its own nerve supply for the life of the animal.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove sudomotor neurons and the skin becomes a dead surface for heat.

A person in a warm room would overheat within hours, and exercise on a summer day would be lethal.

Palms would stay dry, so grip on a smooth object would fail, and the skin conductance response, the tiny drop in electrical resistance that a polygraph measures, would vanish, because that signal is nothing but sudomotor firing.

The peculiar human quality of a body that betrays its own emotions through its skin would be gone.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This neuron is the classic demonstration that a target tissue can rewrite a neuron's identity, an idea that overturned the assumption that a neuron's transmitter is fixed at birth.

The axon reflex means the terminal arbour computes locally, spreading excitation sideways without any spike ever returning to the cell body.

Co released vasoactive intestinal peptide couples the neuron's firing directly to local blood flow, so the cell commands its own supply line.

Sweat itself is an immune organ, carrying the antimicrobial peptide dermcidin onto the skin, so a sudomotor neuron is also an indirect commander of skin defence.

And in Ross syndrome the same neurons accumulate the protein alpha synuclein in their terminals, placing this humble cell inside the same disease family as Parkinson disease.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Nicotinic drive from thermoregulatory preganglionic neurons, 0 to 10 impulses per second, scaled by core temperature error.

◦ Nicotinic drive from emotional and arousal pathways, arriving as brief irregular volleys.

◦ Local skin temperature, which modulates gland sensitivity independently of the nerve.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Acetylcholine onto M3 muscarinic receptors of the eccrine gland, producing 0 to 2 nanolitres per gland per minute.

◦ Vasoactive intestinal peptide onto local arterioles, raising skin blood flow.

◦ Calcitonin gene related peptide as a slower trophic and vasodilator signal.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage.

◦ Acetylcholine concentration in the junction.

◦ Gland fill and duct reabsorption state.

◦ Cholinergic phenotype strength, a slow variable set by the target.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Mean firing rate 0.5 impulses per second at rest, up to about 8 during maximal heat stress.

◦ Burst participation probability about 0.3.

◦ Glands supplied per axon roughly 4 to 10.

◦ Whole body eccrine gland count 2 million to 4 million.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Acetylcholine hydrolysis under 100 milliseconds.

◦ Sweat onset after a burst 1 to 3 seconds.

◦ Thermoregulatory feedback loop tens of seconds to minutes.

◦ Heat acclimatisation 10 to 14 days.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Ross syndrome, where these cholinergic neurons degenerate segmentally, leaving patches of skin that cannot sweat next to patches that sweat far too much to compensate.

◦ Primary focal hyperhidrosis, in which drenching palms are treated by cutting the second to fourth thoracic sympathetic chain, a procedure whose commonest complication is compensatory sweating elsewhere.

◦ Early diabetic small fibre neuropathy, in which sudomotor loss precedes any measurable sensory loss.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to run the sudomotor thermoregulatory loop, turning a core temperature error into a firing rate, then into acetylcholine, then into sweat, then into cooling.
Create a number variable called core temperature with initial value thirty seven.
Create a number variable called set point with initial value thirty seven.
Create a number variable called gain with initial value twenty.
Create a number variable called maximum firing rate with initial value eight.
Create a number variable called acetylcholine with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called sweat rate with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called temperature error with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called firing rate with initial value zero.
Repeat the following steps exactly six hundred times.
    Set temperature error to the difference of core temperature and set point.
    Set firing rate to the product of gain and temperature error.
    Check if firing rate is less than zero.
    If the result of that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to zero.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if firing rate is greater than maximum firing rate.
    If the result of that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to maximum firing rate.
    End the conditional block.
    Set acetylcholine to the sum of acetylcholine and firing rate.
    Set acetylcholine to the product of acetylcholine and zero point five.
    Set sweat rate to the product of acetylcholine and three point four.
    Set core temperature to the sum of core temperature and zero point zero zero four.
    Set core temperature to the difference of core temperature and the product of sweat rate and zero point zero zero zero one seven.
    Display core temperature to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because this is a closed control loop rather than a spiking model, and readable arithmetic matters more than speed.

# One lumped sudomotor channel: core temperature error -> firing rate ->
# acetylcholine at the gland -> sweat rate -> evaporative cooling -> temperature.
DT = 0.1               # seconds per step
SET_POINT = 37.0       # degrees Celsius
GAIN = 20.0            # impulses per second per degree of error
MAX_RATE = 8.0         # impulses per second, saturation
ACH_PER_SPIKE = 0.9    # arbitrary units of acetylcholine per impulse
TAU_ACH = 2.0          # seconds, destruction by acetylcholinesterase
SWEAT_PER_ACH = 1.7    # millilitres per minute per unit of acetylcholine
EVAP_COOLING = 0.010   # degrees Celsius lost per millilitre evaporated
HEAT_LOAD = 0.004      # degrees Celsius gained per second

def simulate(minutes):
    temp, ach, total_sweat = 37.0, 0.0, 0.0
    for step in range(int(minutes * 60 / DT)):
        rate = min(MAX_RATE, max(0.0, GAIN * (temp - SET_POINT)))
        ach += rate * ACH_PER_SPIKE * DT
        ach -= DT * ach / TAU_ACH
        sweat = SWEAT_PER_ACH * ach                  # millilitres per minute
        total_sweat += sweat * DT / 60.0
        temp += HEAT_LOAD * DT - EVAP_COOLING * sweat * DT / 60.0
        if step % 3000 == 0:
            print("minute %4.1f  core %.3f C  firing %.2f Hz  sweat %5.1f mL/min"
                  % (step * DT / 60.0, temp, rate, sweat))
    return total_sweat

print("total sweat over the run: %.0f mL" % simulate(30))

The loop settles about four tenths of a degree above the set point, which is the honest behaviour of a proportional controller and the reason a working human runs slightly warm in the heat rather than exactly at 37 degrees.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only sympathetic postganglionic neuron that releases acetylcholine onto its target, and the only neuron anywhere that in humans routinely carries the machinery for two opposite transmitters at once.

A cell body in a chain ganglion staining for both the vesicular acetylcholine transporter and tyrosine hydroxylase, containing vasoactive intestinal peptide and lacking calbindin, with terminals forming a basket around a coiled sweat gland, can be nothing else.

CHAPTER 258. VASOCONSTRICTOR, VASODILATOR, PILOMOTOR, AND CARDIOMOTOR SYMPATHETIC NEURONS (Blood-Flow-Setting Readiness Neuron) (NON-COG)

Four crews work the same nerve trunk: one squeezes your arteries, one opens the skin to dump heat, one raises the hair on your arms, and one leans on the accelerator of your heart.

The existing label is a committee minute that strings four Latin job titles together and no one can say it aloud.

Blood-Flow-Setting names what unites three of the four, since narrowing and widening vessels and driving the heart are all one problem, deciding where blood goes and how fast.

Readiness names the shared purpose, the fight-or-flight preparation that also raises the hairs.

Neuron marks it honestly as a set of separate labelled lines rather than one cell, since vasoconstrictor endings carry calcitonin-gene-related-peptide markers while vasodilator endings carry substance-P markers.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are the four great functional classes of sympathetic postganglionic neuron defined by what they act on rather than by what they look like.

Vasoconstrictor neurons narrow blood vessels, and they are the single most numerous sympathetic class in the body, holding arteries partly shut all day so that blood pressure exists at all.

Vasodilator neurons widen vessels, and in humans the important ones are the cutaneous active vasodilators that flood the skin with blood during heat stress.

Pilomotor neurons contract the arrector pili, the tiny muscle that tilts a hair upright, giving goosebumps.

Cardiomotor neurons speed the heart, strengthen each beat and shorten the pause between beats.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

All four are multipolar ganglion cells with short dendrites and a long unmyelinated axon ending in beaded varicosities, but the shape of the terminal arbour differs sharply with the job.

A muscle vasoconstrictor terminal forms a fine mesh over the outer coat of an arteriole, standing 50 to 100 nanometres from the smooth muscle.

A pilomotor terminal makes a compact cluster on one small bundle of muscle fibres.

A cardiomotor terminal spreads a wide, loose plexus over the sinoatrial node and out across the ventricular wall, with gaps of up to a micrometre between nerve and muscle so that transmitter must diffuse.

SIZE:

Cell bodies 20 to 40 micrometres, with cardiomotor and prevertebral vasoconstrictor cells at the larger end and pilomotor cells at the smaller.

Axons 0.2 to 1.5 micrometres wide.

A single muscle vasoconstrictor axon can control a vascular bed several centimetres across, and a cardiomotor axon from the stellate ganglion may run 15 to 20 centimetres to reach the ventricle.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Muscle vasoconstrictor neurons, baroreflex controlled, firing in bursts locked to the heartbeat.

• Cutaneous vasoconstrictor neurons, temperature and arousal controlled, rich in neuropeptide Y, and not locked to the heartbeat.

• Visceral and renal vasoconstrictor neurons, from prevertebral ganglia, which also control renin release from the kidney.

• Cutaneous active vasodilator neurons, which are cholinergic rather than noradrenergic and are recruited only above a core temperature threshold.

• Pilomotor neurons, noradrenergic, acting on alpha1 adrenoceptors of the arrector pili muscle.

• Cardiomotor neurons, noradrenergic, from the stellate and middle cervical ganglia, subdivided into sinoatrial rate neurons, atrioventricular conduction neurons and ventricular contractility neurons.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Muscle and skin vasoconstrictor and pilomotor cell bodies lie throughout the paravertebral chain, matched segment by segment to the skin and muscle they supply.

Visceral vasoconstrictor cells lie in the coeliac, superior mesenteric, inferior mesenteric and aorticorenal ganglia.

Cutaneous active vasodilator cells lie in the chain ganglia beside the sudomotor cells and supply only hairy skin, not the palms and soles.

Cardiomotor cells lie mainly in the stellate ganglion and the middle cervical ganglion, and their axons enter the heart alongside the vagus at the interface known as the cardiac plexus, where sympathetic and parasympathetic fibres and the heart's own intrinsic ganglia meet in one tangle at the base of the great vessels.

NUMBER:

Vasoconstrictor neurons are the largest class, plausibly half to two thirds of the sympathetic postganglionic pool, giving roughly 2 million to 3 million.

Cutaneous active vasodilators and pilomotor neurons together plausibly account for a few hundred thousand, and cardiomotor neurons for the order of tens of thousands, since the entire cardiac sympathetic supply arises from a handful of neck and upper chest ganglia.

Confidence is low for every one of these splits, because they rest on the proportions of axon types found in microneurography and animal tracing rather than on human counts.

All of them are drawn from the several million already counted under sympathetic postganglionic neurons, not added to it, and together they represent roughly two to three neurons in every hundred thousand in the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The muscle vasoconstrictor neuron has the best documented firing pattern of any neuron in the awake human body.

It fires at about 0.5 impulses per second, takes part in only about 30 per cent of the bursts travelling down the nerve, usually fires just once per heartbeat, and only rarely fires four or more times in a burst.

Even in severe heart failure or untreated high blood pressure, when the whole nerve is roaring, individual neurons seldom exceed 1 impulse per second; the system recruits more neurons rather than driving each one harder.

Cutaneous vasoconstrictor and pilomotor neurons fire irregularly and are not locked to the pulse, and cardiomotor neurons fire tonically at 1 to 4 impulses per second at rest.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Each neuron is driven by, on average, about two effective preganglionic inputs, which is why the pattern of firing in the ganglion so closely copies the pattern in the spinal cord.

Muscle vasoconstrictor cells receive preganglionic drive that has already been gated by the baroreceptors, the stretch sensors in the carotid artery and aortic arch, so their bursts appear in the trough between pressure peaks.

Cutaneous vasoconstrictor cells receive drive shaped by skin and core temperature and by arousal, with no baroreceptor gating at all.

Cardiomotor cells receive drive from the rostral ventrolateral medulla and, uniquely, are also modulated locally inside the heart by the intrinsic cardiac ganglia they run past.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Unmyelinated, 0.5 to 2 metres per second, running to skin and muscle in the ordinary peripheral nerves and to viscera along the arteries.

Cardiac sympathetic axons form a subepicardial plexus and dive into the muscle, and left sided and right sided cardiac axons have measurably different jobs: right stellate stimulation mostly speeds the heart, left stellate stimulation mostly increases the force of contraction and is the more dangerous for triggering rhythm disturbances.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Vasoconstrictor, pilomotor and cardiomotor neurons all release noradrenaline together with adenosine triphosphate and, at high firing rates, neuropeptide Y.

Adenosine triphosphate acting on P2X1 receptors produces the fast initial squeeze; noradrenaline on alpha1 adrenoceptors produces the sustained squeeze; neuropeptide Y makes both stronger and lasts for many minutes.

At the heart, noradrenaline acts on beta1 adrenoceptors to raise the rate of the sinoatrial node and the force of every cell it reaches.

Cutaneous active vasodilator neurons are the exception again, using acetylcholine together with vasoactive intestinal peptide and driving nitric oxide production in the vessel lining.

MYELINATION:

None in any of the four classes.

The consequence is worth stating plainly: the fastest reflex the sympathetic system can manage takes about a second from stimulus to vessel response, whereas a somatic reflex takes 30 milliseconds.

The autonomic nervous system trades speed for reach.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Continuous and dominated by transmitter handling.

A vasoconstrictor terminal recycles noradrenaline through its transporter thousands of times a minute; a cardiomotor terminal supplying a heart beating a hundred thousand times a day never stops.

Cardiac sympathetic nerve terminals are exceptionally vulnerable to loss of oxygen and are damaged in the first minutes of a heart attack, which is why the border of a healed infarct is a patchwork of denervated and hyperinnervated muscle and why it generates dangerous rhythms.

PLASTICITY:

After a heart attack, surviving cardiac sympathetic axons sprout wildly into the scar border, creating uneven innervation that is a major cause of sudden death.

In high blood pressure, vasoconstrictor terminals thicken; in heart failure the terminals lose their noradrenaline transporter, so transmitter lingers in the junction and the receptors switch themselves off.

Pilomotor and vasoconstrictor innervation density in skin tracks with climate and use across the seasons.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Beyond the standard satellite glia in the ganglion, the specialised support is at the terminal.

Perivascular Schwann cells sit between the nerve varicosity and the smooth muscle and take up noradrenaline, effectively setting how far a transmitter molecule can travel.

At the heart, the terminal shares its space with the intrinsic cardiac ganglia and with the endothelium of the coronary vessels, which releases nitric oxide that damps sympathetic release directly.

In the skin, the arrector pili muscle and its sympathetic nerve together form a niche that instructs the hair follicle stem cell, a partnership shown in mice in which the nerve is not just a motor supply but a growth signal.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take vasoconstrictor neurons out of a limb and it turns warm, pink and swollen, and the person faints when they stand because their arteries can no longer be told to tighten.

Take out the cutaneous vasodilators and the same person cannot dump heat, so their core temperature climbs during exercise even though they are sweating.

Take out the pilomotor neurons and the skin loses its power to bristle, which in a human costs almost nothing and in a cat costs it its bluff.

Take out the cardiomotor neurons and the heart still beats, driven by its own pacemaker, but it cannot accelerate for exercise or fright, so the maximum heart rate collapses towards the intrinsic rate of about 100 beats per minute and exercise capacity falls by half.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

These neurons are the clearest case in the body of population coding in the periphery: the message to a blood vessel is carried by how many neurons fire in a burst, not by how fast any one of them fires.

Neuropeptide Y released only in bursts turns the same nerve into a slow, long acting hormone system layered on top of the fast one.

Vasoconstrictor terminals control local blood flow and therefore control the oxygen supply to every other cell in the tissue, including their own.

Cardiac sympathetic terminals talk to the intrinsic cardiac ganglia and to circulating immune cells, and after injury they release signals that recruit inflammation.

Pilomotor nerves instruct stem cells.

And all of them are subject to prejunctional autoreceptors: noradrenaline that has just been released binds alpha2 receptors on the terminal that made it and shuts the next release down, a negative feedback loop entirely outside the cell body's knowledge.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Nicotinic drive from about two effective preganglionic axons per neuron, class specific in timing.

◦ Baroreceptor phase signal, 0.8 to 2 hertz, for muscle vasoconstrictor cells only.

◦ Core and skin temperature signals for cutaneous vasoconstrictor, vasodilator and pilomotor cells.

◦ Circulating adrenaline, which binds prejunctional beta2 receptors and increases release.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Noradrenaline and adenosine triphosphate onto alpha1 and P2X1 receptors of vascular smooth muscle and arrector pili.

◦ Noradrenaline onto beta1 receptors of sinoatrial node, atrioventricular node and ventricular myocytes.

◦ Acetylcholine and vasoactive intestinal peptide from cutaneous active vasodilators.

◦ Neuropeptide Y above about 2 impulses per second in bursts.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage and refractory state.

◦ Junctional noradrenaline concentration.

◦ Vesicle pool and separate neuropeptide Y pool.

◦ Prejunctional alpha2 autoreceptor occupancy.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Mean single unit firing rate 0.5 impulses per second, ceiling near 1 in disease.

◦ Burst participation probability 0.2 to 0.5.

◦ Junctional cleft width 50 nanometres at arterioles, up to 2 micrometres at the heart.

◦ Noradrenaline reuptake fraction about 0.8, falling towards 0.4 in heart failure.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Vessel narrowing onset 1 to 2 seconds, peak 10 to 20 seconds.

◦ Heart rate response to a sympathetic burst 3 to 5 seconds to onset, 20 to 30 seconds to peak.

◦ Neuropeptide Y action 5 to 20 minutes.

◦ Terminal sprouting after injury days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Overactivity of muscle vasoconstrictor neurons is a core mechanism of high blood pressure, heart failure and obstructive sleep apnoea, and is measurable at the bedside as an increased burst count.

◦ Uneven cardiac sympathetic sprouting after a heart attack causes ventricular arrhythmia, which is why blocking or removing the left stellate ganglion is used to stop electrical storm and to treat inherited arrhythmia syndromes.

◦ Loss of cutaneous vasodilators produces heat intolerance with intact sweating, a dissociation seen in some autonomic neuropathies.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether a muscle vasoconstrictor neuron fires during one heartbeat, given that baroreceptors silence it except in a short window and that even then it usually stays quiet.
Create a number variable called mean arterial pressure with initial value ninety.
Create a number variable called firing probability with initial value zero point three.
Create a number variable called baroreflex gain with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called adjusted probability with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called random draw with initial value zero point four.
Create a number variable called spikes this beat with initial value zero.
Create a boolean variable called inside the permitted window with initial value true.
Set baroreflex gain to the difference of mean arterial pressure and ninety.
Set baroreflex gain to the quotient of baroreflex gain divided by sixty.
Set baroreflex gain to the difference of one and baroreflex gain.
Check if baroreflex gain is less than zero.
If the result of that check is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set baroreflex gain to zero.
End the conditional block.
Set adjusted probability to the product of firing probability and baroreflex gain.
Check if inside the permitted window is equal to true.
If the result of that check is true, then perform the following actions.
    Check if random draw is less than adjusted probability.
    If the result of that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set spikes this beat to one.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set spikes this beat to zero.
    End the conditional block.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Set spikes this beat to zero.
End the conditional block.
Display spikes this beat to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

JavaScript is chosen because this model is most useful as a live bedside teaching tool that runs in any browser with nothing installed.

// One human muscle vasoconstrictor neuron, sixty seconds of resting life.
// Baroreceptors silence it around the systolic pressure peak, so it can only
// fire in a window locked to the cardiac cycle, and even there it usually does not.

const HEART_RATE_HZ = 1.0;      // 60 beats per minute
const DT = 0.001;               // one millisecond steps
const BASE_PROBABILITY = 0.30;  // chance of firing in a permitted window
const WINDOW_START = 0.55;      // fraction of the cardiac cycle
const WINDOW_END   = 0.75;

function simulate(seconds, meanArterialPressure) {
  // Falling pressure lifts baroreceptor inhibition and raises the odds.
  const gain = Math.min(2, Math.max(0, 1 - (meanArterialPressure - 90) / 60));
  let spikes = 0, bursts = 0, armed = false;
  for (let t = 0; t < seconds; t += DT) {
    const phase = (t * HEART_RATE_HZ) % 1;
    const inWindow = phase >= WINDOW_START && phase < WINDOW_END;
    if (inWindow && !armed) {            // one decision per heartbeat
      armed = true;
      if (Math.random() < BASE_PROBABILITY * gain) {
        bursts += 1;
        spikes += (Math.random() < 0.35) ? 2 : 1;   // occasionally a doublet
      }
    }
    if (!inWindow) armed = false;
  }
  return { bursts, spikes, rateHz: +(spikes / seconds).toFixed(2) };
}

console.log("normal pressure:", simulate(60, 90));
console.log("head up tilt:   ", simulate(60, 65));

The neuron ends up firing about 0.4 impulses per second at normal pressure and participating in roughly a third of heartbeats, which is what a microneurography electrode actually records from a resting human.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Nothing else in the human body fires at half an impulse per second, in bursts timed to the gaps between your heartbeats, and controls your blood pressure by voting rather than by shouting.

Put a needle in the peroneal nerve of a resting volunteer and the pulse synchronous crackle you hear is these neurons and only these neurons; ask the volunteer to take a sharp breath and the sudomotor and skin vasoconstrictor units answer instead, out of step with the pulse.

CHAPTER 259. PARASYMPATHETIC PREGANGLIONIC NEURONS (NON-COG)

Your heart wants to beat about a hundred times a minute, and the only reason it does not is that a few thousand cells in your brainstem are quietly holding it back, all day, every day, for your whole life.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The parasympathetic preganglionic neuron is the rest and digest counterpart of the sympathetic preganglionic cell.

Its cell body sits in the brainstem or in the lower spinal cord, and its axon leaves the central nervous system inside a cranial nerve or a pelvic nerve and travels a long way, often most of the distance to the organ itself, before ending in a ganglion.

There it releases acetylcholine onto nicotinic receptors, exactly as its sympathetic cousin does.

The geometry is the reverse of the sympathetic system: here the first axon is long and the second is short, so the decision point sits on or inside the target organ rather than beside the spine.

That single architectural fact is why parasympathetic commands can be aimed at one organ at a time while sympathetic commands tend to spread.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Small to medium multipolar cells, generally more compact than the somatic motor neurons that lie a few hundred micrometres away in the same brainstem.

In the dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus the cells form a long, thin column with dendrites that spread sideways into the neighbouring nucleus of the solitary tract, the brainstem's sensory receiving station, so that visceral sensation and visceral command are separated by almost nothing.

In the nucleus ambiguus the parasympathetic cardiac cells sit just outside the compact somatic motor group, in a looser region called the external formation.

SIZE:

Cell bodies mostly 15 to 35 micrometres across, notably smaller than the 50 to 70 micrometre somatic motor neurons of the same brainstem.

The axon is where the size lies: a preganglionic vagal axon to the stomach may be 40 centimetres long, and a pelvic preganglionic axon to the bladder 20 centimetres, which for a cell body this small is an extraordinary logistical burden.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Edinger Westphal preganglionic neurons, driving pupil constriction and lens focusing, in the midbrain.

• Superior salivatory neurons, driving the tear glands, the nasal glands and the submandibular and sublingual salivary glands.

• Inferior salivatory neurons, driving the parotid salivary gland.

• Dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus neurons, driving the gut, pancreas, liver, airways and ventricular muscle, with slow unmyelinated axons.

• Nucleus ambiguus cardiac vagal neurons, driving the sinoatrial node with fast myelinated axons, respiratory modulated, and responsible for beat to beat heart rate control.

• Sacral preganglionic neurons in the second to fourth sacral segments, driving the bladder, rectum and erectile tissue.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Four brainstem homes and one spinal home, and each deserves naming.

The Edinger Westphal preganglionic population lies in the midbrain just dorsal to the oculomotor nucleus, distinct from the neighbouring urocortin containing cells that project into the brain rather than out of it.

The superior salivatory nucleus lies in the pontine tegmentum near the facial nerve, and the inferior salivatory nucleus lies in the rostral medulla near the glossopharyngeal nerve.

The dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus lies in the floor of the fourth ventricle, and the nucleus ambiguus lies deeper in the ventrolateral medulla.

The sacral parasympathetic nucleus occupies lamina VII of segments S2 to S4.

Two interfaces matter.

The dorsal motor nucleus sits immediately against the nucleus of the solitary tract, forming a sensory to motor interface only a few cell widths thick, the anatomical basis of the vagovagal reflex.

The dorsal motor nucleus also lies against the floor of the fourth ventricle at the area postrema, one of the few places where the blood brain barrier is absent, so these neurons are exposed to circulating hormones and toxins that the rest of the brain never sees.

NUMBER:

The best human count is for the dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus: 16,826 plus or minus 967 neurons, of which 13,802 plus or minus 844 are vagal motor neurons and the rest are local interneurons.

Doubling for both sides and adding the nucleus ambiguus cardiac group, the two salivatory nuclei, the Edinger Westphal preganglionic cells and the sacral parasympathetic nucleus gives a total on the order of 100 thousand, with a defensible range of 50 thousand to 200 thousand.

Confidence is moderate for the vagal figure and low for the rest.

These cell bodies lie in the central nervous system and so do not draw on the roughly 20 million neuron budget for the autonomic ganglia.

They are about one neuron in every million in the whole nervous system, which makes them one of the smallest populations in this book with one of the largest jobs.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Dorsal motor nucleus neurons are pacemakers: they fire slowly and regularly at about 0.5 to 2 impulses per second even when every synapse onto them is blocked, driven by a persistent sodium current and shaped by a fast A type potassium current that sets the interval.

Nucleus ambiguus cardiac vagal neurons are the opposite: they are almost silent at rest and are held silent during inspiration by rhythmic inhibitory input using the transmitters gamma aminobutyric acid and glycine, then released during expiration.

That inspiratory gate is the whole mechanism of respiratory sinus arrhythmia, the familiar quickening of the pulse on breathing in and slowing on breathing out.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The dominant input is from the nucleus of the solitary tract, carrying signals from baroreceptors, chemoreceptors, lung stretch receptors and the gut, mostly using glutamate.

Further input arrives from the hypothalamic paraventricular nucleus, the central nucleus of the amygdala, the insular cortex and the medial prefrontal cortex, which is how an anxious thought slows or quickens a heart.

Local inhibitory interneurons within the dorsal motor nucleus, about three thousand per side in the human, provide fine control.

Output is nicotinic, onto the principal cells of cranial, cardiac, airway, pelvic and enteric ganglia.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Two distinct populations, and the difference is functional.

Nucleus ambiguus cardiac axons are myelinated B fibres conducting at 3 to 15 metres per second, fast enough to deliver a beat by beat command.

Dorsal motor nucleus axons are unmyelinated C fibres conducting at under 2 metres per second, slow, and suited to the sluggish business of digestion and to setting ventricular excitability rather than timing individual beats.

Roughly a fifth of cardiac vagal neurons come from the dorsal motor nucleus and the rest from the nucleus ambiguus.

It is worth noting that in the whole vagus nerve only about a fifth of the fibres are efferent at all; the vagus is overwhelmingly a sensory cable pointing the other way.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine at nicotinic receptors, again built largely from alpha3 and beta4 subunits.

Co transmitters include nitric oxide, vasoactive intestinal peptide and enkephalin, and the sacral preganglionic neurons are notably rich in nitric oxide synthase, the enzyme that makes nitric oxide.

MYELINATION:

Mixed, and the mix is the point.

Cardiac vagal axons from the nucleus ambiguus carry a thin myelin sheath, made by oligodendrocytes inside the brainstem and by Schwann cells beyond the exit zone, which buys the speed that beat by beat control requires.

Digestive vagal axons carry none, because a stomach does not need millisecond timing.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate at the cell body and severe at the axon.

Keeping a 40 centimetre unmyelinated axon supplied with vesicles and mitochondria from a cell body 25 micrometres across is one of the harder logistical problems in the nervous system, and it is the reason these axons are so vulnerable in diabetes and in nutritional deficiency.
