Because the dorsal motor nucleus sits at a leaky part of the blood brain barrier, its neurons are also directly exposed to circulating glucose, leptin and cholecystokinin, and they change their firing in response, which makes them metabolic sensors as well as metabolic controllers.

PLASTICITY:

Vagal tone is trainable.

Endurance training raises resting vagal control of the heart within weeks, and slow paced breathing raises it within minutes, both measurable as increased beat to beat variability.

After vagal nerve injury the surviving preganglionic axons sprout into denervated ganglia.

Long term stimulation of the vagus, used to treat epilepsy and depression, produces lasting changes in the strength of these synapses.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes in the dorsal motor nucleus do something unusual: they are themselves glucose sensors, and they signal to the neurons using adenosine triphosphate and lactate, so the local glia participate directly in the metabolic reflex rather than merely supporting it.

The specialised ependymal cells called tanycytes near the floor of the fourth ventricle regulate what reaches the nucleus from the cerebrospinal fluid.

Microglia in this nucleus are activated early in Parkinson disease, before the movement centres are affected at all.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

The medulla without these cells would still breathe, still sense, still control blood pressure through the sympathetic side, and still be a competent brainstem, but the body below it would run permanently in emergency mode.

The heart would settle at its intrinsic rate near 100 beats a minute and would lose almost all beat to beat variability, so a doctor looking at a rhythm strip would see an eerily regular trace, the same sign that predicts death after a heart attack.

The stomach would not empty, the pupil would not constrict to light, the eye could not focus on a page, and the mouth would be dry.

The floor of the fourth ventricle would lose the thin dark column that anatomists use as a landmark.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

These cells are intrinsic pacemakers, generating their own rhythm without input, so silence in their inputs does not mean silence in their output.

They are chemosensors, changing firing in response to blood glucose, leptin and gut hormones diffusing in through a leaky barrier.

They release nitric oxide, a gas that acts on neighbours without any synapse.

They form the efferent arm of the inflammatory reflex, in which vagal activity ultimately suppresses the release of inflammatory signalling molecules from the spleen, making these neurons part of the immune system.

And they are the earliest known site of Parkinson disease pathology: deposits of alpha synuclein appear in the dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus years or decades before any tremor, which has led to the still debated proposal that the disease begins in the gut and climbs the vagus into the brain.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Glutamate from the nucleus of the solitary tract carrying baroreceptor, chemoreceptor, lung stretch and gut signals, 0 to 50 impulses per second.

◦ Rhythmic inhibition using gamma aminobutyric acid and glycine, phase locked to inspiration, for nucleus ambiguus cells.

◦ Descending input from hypothalamus, amygdala and insular cortex.

◦ Circulating glucose, leptin and cholecystokinin sensed directly.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Acetylcholine onto nicotinic receptors of cranial, cardiac, airway, pelvic and enteric ganglion neurons.

◦ Nitric oxide and vasoactive intestinal peptide as slow modulators inside the ganglion.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage.

◦ Persistent sodium and A type potassium activation, which set the pacemaker interval.

◦ Respiratory phase, held as an external gate.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Intrinsic pacemaker rate 0.5 to 2 impulses per second for dorsal motor nucleus cells.

◦ Cardiac vagal firing during expiration up to 15 impulses per second.

◦ Conduction velocity 3 to 15 metres per second for myelinated cardiac axons, under 2 for unmyelinated.

◦ Human dorsal motor nucleus neuron count 16,826 plus or minus 967 per nucleus.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Vagal effect on the heart begins within 200 milliseconds and is over within 1 second, far faster than any sympathetic effect.

◦ Respiratory gate cycles every 3 to 5 seconds.

◦ Gastric effects build over minutes.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Diabetic vagal neuropathy, producing a fixed fast pulse, gastroparesis and a doubling of cardiac risk.

◦ Alpha synuclein deposition in the dorsal motor nucleus as the earliest stage of Parkinson disease, presenting as constipation years before tremor.

◦ Loss of nitric oxide releasing preganglionic and ganglionic pathways in achalasia, in which the lower end of the oesophagus will not relax.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to generate respiratory sinus arrhythmia by gating a cardiac vagal preganglionic neuron with the breathing cycle and converting its output into a heart period.
Create a number variable called intrinsic heart period with initial value six hundred.
Create a number variable called acetylcholine with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called firing rate with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called expiratory firing rate with initial value twelve.
Create a number variable called heart period with initial value six hundred.
Create a list variable called recorded periods with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called breath step with initial value zero.
Repeat the following steps exactly one hundred times.
    Set breath step to the remainder of breath step divided by ten.
    Check if breath step is less than four.
    If the result of that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to zero.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to expiratory firing rate.
    End the conditional block.
    Set acetylcholine to the sum of acetylcholine and firing rate.
    Set acetylcholine to the product of acetylcholine and zero point five.
    Set heart period to the product of acetylcholine and eight.
    Set heart period to the sum of heart period and intrinsic heart period.
    Add heart period to the end of recorded periods.
    Set breath step to the sum of breath step and one.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in recorded periods.
Display heart period to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the interesting output is a list of heart periods that a reader can immediately compare with their own smartwatch data.

# A cardiac vagal preganglionic neuron in the nucleus ambiguus.
# Inspiration gates it off, expiration lets it fire, and each pulse of
# acetylcholine at the sinoatrial node lengthens the next heart beat.
# The result is respiratory sinus arrhythmia.
DT = 0.005                    # seconds per step
RESP_HZ = 0.25                # 15 breaths per minute
INSP_FRACTION = 0.4           # fraction of the breath spent breathing in
BASE_RATE = 12.0              # impulses per second during expiration
TAU_ACH = 0.35                # seconds, acetylcholine clearance at the node
MS_PER_ACH = 50.0             # milliseconds of extra heart period per unit
INTRINSIC_PERIOD_MS = 600.0   # a denervated human sinus node, about 100 bpm

def simulate(seconds):
    ach, t, next_beat, periods = 0.0, 0.0, 0.0, []
    while t < seconds:
        phase = (t * RESP_HZ) % 1.0
        rate = 0.0 if phase < INSP_FRACTION else BASE_RATE
        ach += rate * DT
        ach -= DT * ach / TAU_ACH
        if t >= next_beat:
            period = INTRINSIC_PERIOD_MS + MS_PER_ACH * ach
            periods.append(period)
            next_beat = t + period / 1000.0
        t += DT
    return periods

p = simulate(60.0)
print("beats in 60 s        : %d" % len(p))
print("shortest heart period: %.0f ms" % min(p))
print("longest heart period : %.0f ms" % max(p))
print("mean heart rate      : %.1f beats per minute" % (60000.0 / (sum(p) / len(p))))

The printed spread between the shortest and longest beat is respiratory sinus arrhythmia, and its size is one of the best single predictors of long term survival that medicine has.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron whose cell body sits in a brainstem or sacral autonomic nucleus, whose axon runs most of the way to a distant organ before making its first synapse, and which then releases acetylcholine onto a nicotinic receptor in a ganglion lying on or inside that organ.

Long first leg, short second leg, cholinergic at both ends: no other neuron in the body has that signature.

CHAPTER 260. PARASYMPATHETIC POSTGANGLIONIC NEURONS (NON-COG)

These are the shortest workers in the nervous system: cells whose entire axon may be no longer than a grain of rice, parked directly on the organ they command.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The parasympathetic postganglionic neuron is the last cell in the rest and digest chain.

It sits either in a small named ganglion near its target or actually buried in the wall of the organ itself, receives acetylcholine from a long preganglionic axon, and then releases acetylcholine again onto the organ.

The second release lands on muscarinic receptors, a slower family of receptors that work through internal messengers rather than by opening a pore directly, which is why parasympathetic effects build over hundreds of milliseconds rather than instantly.

Its jobs are the quiet ones: constricting the pupil, focusing the lens, making tears and saliva, slowing the heart, narrowing the airways, emptying the bladder, and producing an erection.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Multipolar cells with short dendrites, wrapped like all peripheral neurons in a jacket of satellite glia.

They are, on average, simpler than sympathetic ganglion cells, with fewer dendrites and often just a single dominant preganglionic input, because they do not need to integrate; they need to relay faithfully.

The axon is remarkably short, from a few hundred micrometres for a cell inside a heart wall to two or three centimetres for a ciliary ganglion cell reaching the iris.

The terminals again form varicosities, but the target is usually within a micrometre or two.

SIZE:

Human ciliary ganglion neurons are 20 to 35 micrometres across.

Cells in the intramural ganglia of the heart, airways and bladder are similar or a little larger.

The ganglia themselves are tiny: the human ciliary ganglion is about 2 millimetres long and sits loose in the orbital fat behind the eye, and many intramural ganglia contain only a handful of cells and are invisible without a microscope.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Ciliary ganglion neurons, subdivided into the large majority that drive the ciliary muscle for focusing and a small minority that drive the sphincter of the iris; textbooks commonly quote roughly 95 per cent to 5 per cent for that split.

• Pterygopalatine ganglion neurons, driving the tear gland, the nasal glands and the blood vessels of the nasal lining and the choroid of the eye.

• Submandibular ganglion neurons, driving the submandibular and sublingual salivary glands.

• Otic ganglion neurons, driving the parotid gland.

• Intramural cardiac, airway, bladder and glandular neurons, which include cells that inhibit rather than excite, using nitric oxide.

• Nitrergic subtypes throughout, which relax smooth muscle instead of contracting it, and which are the reason an erection is possible at all.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Four named cranial ganglia and an uncountable scatter of intramural ones.

The ciliary ganglion sits in the orbit between the optic nerve and the lateral rectus muscle; the pterygopalatine ganglion in the pterygopalatine fossa below the maxillary nerve; the submandibular ganglion hangs from the lingual nerve above the submandibular gland; and the otic ganglion sits just below the foramen ovale against the mandibular nerve.

The intramural ganglia lie in the fat pads on the surface of the heart, in the walls of the airways, in the wall of the bladder and in the pelvic plexus.

The interface to name explicitly is the wall of the organ itself: these neurons sit inside the tissue they govern, between the muscle and the connective tissue, and in the heart they sit at the boundary between the epicardial fat and the atrial muscle, where sympathetic axons, vagal axons, sensory endings and their own dendrites all mingle in one plexus.

NUMBER:

The human ciliary ganglion contains about 2,500 neurons, with some sources reporting closer to 3,000, and the other three cranial ganglia are of broadly similar size, so the named cranial ganglia together hold on the order of 20 thousand to 50 thousand cells.

The intramural populations are much larger and much less well counted: the human heart carries something like 100 thousand neurons in 700 to 1,560 small ganglia, and the pelvic ganglia hold tens of thousands, both of which are itemised separately elsewhere in this section.

Adding the uncounted airway, bladder and glandular ganglia, the total for parasympathetic postganglionic neurons outside the gut is plausibly 1 million to 2 million, at low confidence, comfortably inside the roughly 20 million neuron budget for all autonomic ganglia and excluding the several hundred million neurons of the enteric nervous system, which are counted in their own section.

They are on the order of one or two neurons in every hundred thousand in the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage about minus 55 millivolts.

Most are phasic, firing one or two spikes at the start of a depolarising step and then falling silent, held in check by the same M type potassium current found in sympathetic ganglia.

The defining feature is a very high safety factor at the synapse: in the ciliary ganglion one preganglionic input is usually enough to fire the cell every single time, so the ganglion is a faithful relay rather than a computer.

Intramural cardiac and airway neurons break this rule and behave like a local network, with many showing spontaneous activity and long lasting afterhyperpolarisations.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Convergence is low, often one to three preganglionic axons per cell and sometimes exactly one, in stark contrast to the fan out of the sympathetic system.

The same three timescales of response exist as in sympathetic ganglia: a fast nicotinic potential, a slow muscarinic potential produced by closing the M current, and a late peptide mediated potential.

In the ciliary ganglion of birds and, to a lesser extent, mammals, some preganglionic terminals form a giant calyx that wraps the whole cell body, one of the largest synapses in the body.

Intramural ganglia additionally receive sensory input from the organ itself and from sympathetic axons passing through, so they run genuine local reflexes.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Short and unmyelinated, conducting at about 0.5 to 1 metre per second, which hardly matters over such distances.

Because the axon is short, the whole delay from ganglion to effect is dominated by the effector, not by conduction.

The clearest human illustration is the pupil: the light reflex takes 200 to 250 milliseconds from flash to first movement, almost all of it spent in the retina and in the muscle, not in the nerve.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine at muscarinic receptors, with the subtype determining the effect: M3 at glands and smooth muscle causing secretion and contraction, and M2 at the heart slowing the pacemaker by opening potassium channels.

Nitric oxide is the great parasympathetic co transmitter, made on demand by neuronal nitric oxide synthase and used to relax rather than contract; it is the transmitter of the cavernous nerves and therefore the direct target of the drugs used for erectile dysfunction, which work by prolonging its second messenger.

Vasoactive intestinal peptide accompanies acetylcholine at glands and blood vessels, and adenosine triphosphate is co released in the bladder.

MYELINATION:

None.

Given axon lengths measured in millimetres, myelin would buy nothing, and its absence costs nothing.

This is one of the few places in the nervous system where the lack of myelin is simply the correct engineering choice rather than a compromise.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low by the standards of this book.

Short axons, small terminal fields, low firing rates and a target that is usually a slow smooth muscle or a gland make this an inexpensive neuron to run.

The exception is the nitrergic subtype, because making nitric oxide consumes the amino acid arginine and the cofactor tetrahydrobiopterin continuously, and when that cofactor runs short in diabetes the same enzyme starts making damaging oxygen radicals instead, which is one reason erectile failure is an early sign of diabetic vascular disease.

PLASTICITY:

The most striking example in clinical medicine is the tonic pupil of Adie syndrome.

Damage the ciliary ganglion and the surviving fibres regrow, but because the cells destined for the ciliary muscle vastly outnumber those destined for the iris, the iris is reinnervated mostly by focusing fibres.

The result is a pupil that no longer reacts to light but constricts strongly when the person looks at something close, a mismatch called light near dissociation that is visible with nothing but a torch.

Intramural cardiac neurons remodel after a heart attack and in atrial fibrillation, and pelvic ganglion neurons undergo programmed cell death after cavernous nerve injury during prostate surgery.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Satellite glia envelop each cell body as elsewhere, but the developmental relationship here is remarkable and specific: the neurons of the ciliary, pterygopalatine, submandibular and otic ganglia are not born in the neural crest and then migrated as a group, but are generated from Schwann cell precursors that crawl along the preganglionic axons and convert into neurons on arrival.

In these ganglia, the glia are literally the parent cells.

In the heart and airways, intramural ganglia are additionally supported by a resident macrophage population that is unusually dense and that modulates conduction directly.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Each ganglion gives its organ a specific flavour, and losing it produces a specific and immediately recognisable deficit.

Lose the ciliary ganglion and the eye has a wide fixed pupil that cannot focus on a book.

Lose the pterygopalatine ganglion and the eye is dry and the nose no longer runs.

Lose the submandibular and otic ganglia and the mouth is permanently dry, so teeth decay and swallowing becomes difficult.

Lose the intramural cardiac neurons and the heart loses its brake and its local reflex intelligence.

Lose the pelvic ones and the bladder cannot empty and erection is impossible.

Collectively, without these cells a body could be aroused but never settled, and could act but never restore itself.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Nitric oxide release makes these neurons unusual: the messenger is not stored in vesicles at all but manufactured at the moment of firing and allowed to diffuse through several cell diameters, so a single neuron relaxes a whole patch of tissue at once.

Intramural ganglia are true local processors, containing sensory neurons, interneurons and motor neurons, and they can run a complete reflex with the brain disconnected, which is why a transplanted heart still adjusts its own rhythm.

Ganglion cells release factors that maintain their target glands and blood vessels, and denervated salivary glands shrink.

Parasympathetic terminals in airway and gut walls talk directly to immune cells, and acetylcholine acting on the alpha7 nicotinic receptor of macrophages suppresses the release of inflammatory signals, which is the cellular basis of the inflammatory reflex.

And because they are born from glia, these neurons blur the boundary between the two great classes of nervous system cell.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Nicotinic drive from 1 to 3 preganglionic axons, usually with a safety factor above 1 so that every input spike produces an output spike.

◦ For intramural ganglia only, direct sensory input from stretch and chemical receptors in the organ wall.

◦ Passing sympathetic axons releasing noradrenaline onto inhibitory alpha2 receptors on the terminal.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Acetylcholine onto M3 receptors of glands, smooth muscle, iris sphincter and ciliary muscle.

◦ Acetylcholine onto M2 receptors of the sinoatrial and atrioventricular nodes, opening potassium channels and slowing the heart.

◦ Nitric oxide onto smooth muscle for relaxation, especially in erectile tissue, oesophagus and cerebral vessels.

◦ Vasoactive intestinal peptide for glandular blood flow.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage.

◦ M current activation.

◦ Nitric oxide synthase substrate and cofactor availability.

◦ Local reflex state for intramural networks.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Ciliary ganglion neuron count about 2,500 per side.

◦ Convergence 1 to 3 preganglionic inputs per cell.

◦ Axon length 0.2 millimetres to 30 millimetres.

◦ Conduction velocity 0.5 to 1 metre per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Muscarinic effect onset 100 to 300 milliseconds.

◦ Pupil constriction time constant about 0.3 seconds, redilation about 1.5 seconds.

◦ Vagal slowing of the heart complete within 1 second.

◦ Nitric oxide action 1 to 10 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Adie tonic pupil after ciliary ganglion damage, with light near dissociation caused by misrouted regrowth.

◦ Erectile dysfunction after cavernous nerve injury during pelvic surgery, caused by loss of nitrergic neurons in the pelvic ganglia.

◦ Achalasia, in which nitrergic neurons of the oesophageal wall are destroyed and the lower oesophagus will not open.

◦ Chagas disease, in which a parasite destroys intramural ganglia, producing a hugely dilated oesophagus and colon.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to relay a light signal through a ciliary ganglion neuron to the iris and to move the pupil, with a fast constriction and a slow redilation.
Create a number variable called pupil diameter with initial value seven.
Create a number variable called target diameter with initial value seven.
Create a number variable called light level with initial value five hundred.
Create a number variable called constriction time constant with initial value zero point three five.
Create a number variable called dilation time constant with initial value one point six.
Create a number variable called chosen time constant with initial value zero point three five.
Create a number variable called step size with initial value zero point zero two.
Create a number variable called change with initial value zero.
Repeat the following steps exactly two hundred times.
    Check if light level is greater than one hundred.
    If the result of that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set target diameter to two point seven.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set target diameter to six point seven.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if target diameter is less than pupil diameter.
    If the result of that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set chosen time constant to constriction time constant.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set chosen time constant to dilation time constant.
    End the conditional block.
    Set change to the difference of target diameter and pupil diameter.
    Set change to the quotient of change divided by chosen time constant.
    Set change to the product of change and step size.
    Set pupil diameter to the sum of pupil diameter and change.
    Display pupil diameter to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the output is a short trace that a reader can plot or simply compare against a video of their own eye.

# A ciliary ganglion neuron driving the pupil.
# Light raises the preganglionic rate; the ganglion relays it with a safety
# factor near one, so the interesting dynamics are the reflex latency and the
# asymmetry between fast constriction and slow redilation.
import math

DT = 0.002              # seconds per step
LATENCY_S = 0.22        # reflex latency before the pupil moves at all
TAU_CONSTRICT = 0.35    # seconds
TAU_DILATE = 1.60       # seconds
D_MAX, D_MIN = 7.0, 2.0 # millimetres

def target_diameter(lux):
    # Steady state pupil size falls with the logarithm of light intensity.
    return max(D_MIN, D_MAX - 1.6 * math.log10(max(lux, 0.0) + 1.0))

def simulate(seconds, light_on_at, lux_dark, lux_bright):
    d, trace = D_MAX, []
    for i in range(int(seconds / DT)):
        t = i * DT
        seen = t >= light_on_at + LATENCY_S
        target = target_diameter(lux_bright if seen else lux_dark)
        tau = TAU_CONSTRICT if target < d else TAU_DILATE
        d += DT * (target - d) / tau
        if i % 100 == 0:
            trace.append((round(t, 2), round(d, 2)))
    return trace

for t, d in simulate(4.0, 1.0, 0.5, 500.0):
    print("t = %4.2f s   pupil = %4.2f mm" % (t, d))

Nothing happens for the first 1.22 seconds, then the pupil snaps down over about a second, which is exactly what a clinician sees with a pen torch and exactly what a simulated eye must reproduce to look alive.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A neuron sitting on or inside the organ it controls, with an axon shorter than the ganglion is wide in some cases, receiving one faithful cholinergic input and releasing acetylcholine onto a muscarinic receptor or nitric oxide onto smooth muscle.

Stain for choline acetyltransferase and neuronal nitric oxide synthase in the wall of a bladder, an airway or a heart, and the small clusters of cells that light up are these and nothing else.

CHAPTER 261. ADRENAL CHROMAFFIN CELLS (Fight-Or-Flight Hormone Cell) (NON-COG)

When a car swerves into your lane, the punch you feel in your chest a second later was thrown by a cell that gave up its axon so it could shout into your blood instead.

Chromaffin is a staining barcode, meaning only that the granules brown when treated with chromium salts, which describes a laboratory reagent rather than a cell's purpose.

Fight-Or-Flight names the state the cell serves.

Hormone corrects the central misconception, because this is a nerve cell that lost its axon and now speaks into the bloodstream instead.

Cell rather than Neuron is the honest head noun, and it is driven by ordinary cholinergic preganglionic fibres of the splanchnic nerve arriving from spinal segments four to twelve of the chest, coming in two stocks, one storing adrenaline and one storing noradrenaline.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A chromaffin cell is a sympathetic nerve cell that became a gland, and it pours catecholamines, signalling molecules built from the amino acid tyrosine, straight into the blood so one cell reaches every organ at once.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is a plump polygon with no dendrites and no axon, crammed with dense-core vesicles, membrane bags about 120 nanometres across holding catecholamines glued to scaffold proteins called chromogranins, with one face on a sieve-like fenestrated capillary and the other receiving a splanchnic nerve ending.

SIZE:

The cell body is roughly 15 to 20 micrometres across, a fifth the width of a hair, and holds on the order of 10,000 to 30,000 vesicles, each carrying one to three million catecholamine molecules.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Adrenaline cells, or E cells, carry phenylethanolamine N-methyltransferase (PNMT), the final enzyme turning noradrenaline into adrenaline.

• Noradrenaline cells, or N cells, lack that enzyme and have darker, more eccentric granules.

• Mature human granule content is estimated at about 80 percent adrenaline, 16 percent noradrenaline and 4 percent dopamine.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The home is the adrenal medulla of both glands, wrapped inside the adrenal cortex, and that wrapping is a true interface because cortisol drains inward at concentrations reached nowhere else and switches on the adrenaline-making enzyme; further chromaffin cells form paraganglia, chiefly the organ of Zuckerkandl in front of the aorta, plus clusters near the carotid arteries, along the vagus nerve, and in the bladder and prostate walls.

NUMBER:

No modern stereological count exists in adult humans, but the medulla weighs about 500 milligrams and holds roughly 1 milligram of catecholamine per gram, giving a defensible bound of 20 million to 200 million cells, confidence low; they fall outside the 20 million autonomic ganglion neuron budget because they are axonless endocrine cells, contributing zero of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the body.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage is a leaky minus 55 to minus 60 millivolts with spontaneous firing at 1 to 2 hertz, driven by L-type calcium channels and braked by large-conductance calcium-activated potassium channels called BK channels, rising to 10 to 20 hertz when acetylcholine opens nicotinic receptors, ion pores that open when acetylcholine binds.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input is one class only, cholinergic preganglionic terminals from spinal segments T5 to T11 in the greater splanchnic nerve, built never to fail; output is not synaptic at all but enters the perimedullary capillary bed and reaches the heart in about 10 seconds, while gap junctions, protein tunnels between neighbours, let an active cluster recruit silent cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Not Applicable, and the reason is central to this cell: cortical glucocorticoid signalling diverted its developmental programme, so it never grows an axon and has no axon hillock, growth cone or conduction velocity to quote.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It secretes adrenaline, noradrenaline and a little dopamine, co-released with chromogranin A, enkephalins, which are the body's own opioid painkillers, neuropeptide Y, a long-acting vessel constrictor, and adenosine triphosphate (ATP); the incoming trigger is acetylcholine.

MYELINATION:

The cell has no axon and so no myelin, myelin being the fatty insulation on fast fibres, though its splanchnic supply is thinly myelinated B fibres conducting at 3 to 15 metres per second.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Tyrosine hydroxylase, the rate-limiting enzyme, needs oxygen, iron and the cofactor tetrahydrobiopterin, and each granule is loaded to about 0.5 molar by an ATP-driven proton pump, one of the steepest gradients any human cell maintains.

PLASTICITY:

Chronic stress, cold and repeated low blood sugar raise tyrosine hydroxylase and PNMT over days, and in the fetus these cells sense low oxygen directly and secrete with no nerve input, a birth safeguard switched off in the adult.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia here are sustentacular cells, which differ from astrocytes and oligodendrocytes in three concrete ways: they secrete glial cell line-derived neurotrophic factor rather than myelin proteins, they act as an adult stem-cell reservoir that can make new chromaffin cells, and they form a partial barrier between cell and blood that no other glia attempt.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the adrenal gland becomes a steroid factory with a hollow centre: nerves would still widen the pupil and speed the heart, but the drenching minutes-long chemical wave would go, glucose would rise sluggishly, and low blood sugar would stop producing the sweating and trembling that warn a person with diabetes to eat.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Secretion happens across the whole surface, so the computation is a graded translation of calcium into hormone across three vesicle pools, readily releasable, slowly releasable and sustained reserve.

Gap junctions make the medulla a partly coupled sheet, volume transmission is the entire output mode, adrenaline feeds back on the cell's own beta-adrenoceptors, enkephalin release performs local immune signalling, and the cell answers hypoxia, acidosis and low glucose with no neural instruction.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: splanchnic acetylcholine, 0.2 to 20 hertz; glucose, 2 to 8 millimolar; oxygen, 20 to 100 millimetres of mercury; cortisol, micromolar; circulating adrenaline as feedback.

• OUTPUTS: adrenaline and noradrenaline into the adrenal vein, 0.1 to 0.5 nanomolar in plasma at rest and 5 to 10 nanomolar in severe stress, with neuropeptide Y, enkephalins, chromogranin A and ATP, to heart, liver, muscle arterioles, fat, bronchi and islets.

• STATE: membrane voltage; intracellular calcium; the three pool sizes; tyrosine hydroxylase and PNMT activity.

• PARAMETERS: resting potential minus 55 to minus 60 millivolts; spontaneous rate 1 to 2 hertz; granule diameter 120 nanometres; 10,000 to 30,000 granules; granule content 0.5 molar; adrenaline fraction 0.8.

• TIME CONSTANTS: nicotinic current 5 to 20 milliseconds; spike 2 to 5 milliseconds; fast pool fusion 10 to 50 milliseconds; refilling 5 to 20 seconds; plasma adrenaline half-life about 2 minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: pheochromocytoma, a catecholamine-secreting tumour whose combined incidence with paraganglioma is roughly 3 per million per year, causing episodic hypertension, headache, palpitations and sweating, about 40 percent of cases germline and half of those in succinate dehydrogenase genes; loss of function causes hypoglycaemia unawareness.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert splanchnic nerve firing into graded adrenaline release from one chromaffin cell.
Create a number variable called calcium with initial value 0.1.
Create a number variable called ready_pool with initial value 20.
Create a number variable called released with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
While step is less than 200, repeat the following steps.
    Set drive to 1.
    If both step is greater than 50 and step is less than 120 are true, then perform the following actions.
        Set drive to 15.
    End the conditional block.
    Set calcium to the sum of calcium and the product of drive and 0.02.
    Set calcium to the difference of calcium and the product of calcium and 0.15.
    If calcium is greater than 0.5, then perform the following actions.
        Set released to the sum of released and the product of ready_pool and 0.2.
        Set ready_pool to the product of ready_pool and 0.8.
    End the conditional block.
    Set ready_pool to the sum of ready_pool and the product of the difference of 20 and ready_pool and 0.05.
    Set step to the sum of step and 1.
End the loop.
Display released to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the model is a short numerical loop and Python keeps it readable to a non-programmer.

# Chromaffin cell: nicotinic drive -> calcium -> pooled granule release.
DT, CA_DECAY, CA_GAIN = 0.01, 15.0, 0.03   # step size, clearance, drive gain
CA_THRESHOLD, FUSE, REFILL = 0.5, 0.2, 5.0 # fusion threshold, fraction, refill
READY_MAX = 20.0                           # ready pool capacity, granules

def simulate(steps=200, burst=(50, 120)):
    calcium, ready, released, log = 0.1, READY_MAX, 0.0, []
    for step in range(steps):
        drive = 15.0 if burst[0] <= step < burst[1] else 1.0    # hertz
        calcium += (CA_GAIN * drive - CA_DECAY * calcium) * DT
        if calcium > CA_THRESHOLD:                              # exocytosis
            released += FUSE * ready
            ready -= FUSE * ready
        ready += REFILL * (READY_MAX - ready) * DT              # refill
        log.append((step * DT, calcium, released))
    return released, log

if __name__ == "__main__":
    total, log = simulate()
    for t, ca, rel in log[::40]:
        print("t=%4.2fs calcium=%.3f release=%.2f" % (t, ca, rel))
    print("total granules released:", round(total, 2))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only cell in the human nervous system that is a neuron by birth, a gland by trade, has no axon at all, and turns brown when you drop chromium salts on it.

CHAPTER 262. SMALL INTENSELY FLUORESCENT (SIF) CELLS (Signal-Dimming Local Cell) (NON-COG)

Shine ultraviolet light on a treated sympathetic ganglion and most of it glows a dull green, but a handful of tiny cells blaze like sparks, and those sparks are working the volume control.

The old label is a microscope observation turned into an acronym, recording only that the cell glows brightly under catecholamine fluorescence.

Signal-Dimming names the work, because the cell sits between the incoming preganglionic terminal and the outgoing neuron's dendrites and damps the handover rather than adding to it.

Local names its reach, since it has no long axon and acts by paracrine release over a few micrometres.

Cell rather than Neuron is honest about its halfway position between neuron and gland, sharing a sympathoadrenal precursor with both the ganglion neurons and the adrenal medulla.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A small intensely fluorescent cell, always shortened to SIF cell, is a miniature dopamine-releasing cell inside a sympathetic ganglion, which is a relay station where a fibre from the spinal cord hands its signal to a second fibre running on to an organ; it is neither fibre but a third party that turns the relay's gain up or down.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is a small round bead dwarfed by the principal neurons, stuffed with dense-core vesicles of the kind chromaffin cells use, which is no accident because the two share an embryonic ancestor, and it clusters near blood vessels and under the ganglion capsule.

SIZE:

The cell body is about 10 to 15 micrometres across in rat sympathetic ganglia, roughly a third the diameter of the principal neurons beside it, and its process, when present, runs up to about 200 micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Type I cells have a long branching process, occur singly, carry granular vesicles of 80 to 100 nanometres, and behave like interneurons in the local circuit.

• Type II cells cluster near vessels or under the capsule, have short or absent processes, carry vesicles of 150 to 300 nanometres resembling adrenal granules, and behave like miniature endocrine cells.

• In the rat superior cervical ganglion, the top relay in the neck, type I predominates and carries mainly dopamine.

WHERE IT LIVES:

SIF cells occur throughout the paravertebral sympathetic chain flanking the spine, in the prevertebral coeliac, superior mesenteric and inferior mesenteric ganglia in front of the aorta, and in pelvic and some parasympathetic ganglia, favouring one explicit interface, the boundary between the ganglion's neuropil, meaning its tangle of fibres and synapses, and its blood supply.

NUMBER:

Rat superior cervical ganglion counts range from 26 to 274 cells against roughly 25,000 to 30,000 principal neurons, so 0.1 to 1 percent of a ganglion; no reliable human count exists, and applying that fraction to the several million neurons of the human sympathetic chain and prevertebral ganglia gives 10,000 to 200,000 per person, confidence low, at most 1 percent of the 20 million autonomic ganglion neurons and about two millionths of one percent of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

They can fire action potentials, but their small size gives a high input resistance, meaning a tiny current makes a large voltage change, and much of their release is graded and calcium-driven rather than spike-locked, while muscarinic agonists, drugs mimicking the slow action of acetylcholine, sharply raise their dopamine turnover.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are the same cholinergic preganglionic fibres that drive the principal neurons, plus sensory collaterals from the periphery, and outputs reach principal neuron dendrites and somata, producing a slow inhibitory postsynaptic potential lasting hundreds of milliseconds to seconds that is real but contested in size, so simulate it as a gain term rather than a decisive brake.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Type I cells have one unmyelinated process up to 200 micrometres long that never leaves the ganglion, so it is axon-like rather than a true axon; for type II cells this is Not Applicable, and the reason is that they often have no process and release straight from the cell body.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Dopamine is the signature transmitter, making SIF cells one of very few dopamine sources outside the brain, with some cells holding serotonin, also called 5-hydroxytryptamine, taken up from blood, a subset holding noradrenaline, and co-stored neuropeptide Y, enkephalins, somatostatin and adenosine triphosphate.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable in the sense of self-insulation, and the reason is that SIF cells and their processes are wholly unmyelinated and no oligodendrocyte or myelinating Schwann cell ever wraps them, though the preganglionic fibres driving them are thinly myelinated B fibres.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest in absolute terms because the cell is tiny but high per unit volume, since the cytoplasm is given over to tyrosine hydroxylase, aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase and the vesicular monoamine transporter type 2, which loads dopamine against a steep gradient.

PLASTICITY:

Glucocorticoid hormones increase SIF cell number and push them towards the chromaffin end of the spectrum while nerve growth factor pushes the same precursors towards a neuronal phenotype with longer processes, making this a rare mature cell whose identity stays negotiable for life.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Satellite glial cells, the flattened glia that wrap each ganglion neuron like clingfilm, wrap SIF cells only incompletely, and that is the special feature: the gaps face capillaries and interstitial spaces, so these glia are arranged to permit dopamine escape rather than contain it, the opposite of tight astrocytic uptake at a brain synapse.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete SIF cells and the ganglion becomes a faithful, dumb repeater passing every spike unchanged, losing local self-restraint and local blood-flow control, so its response to a long stressful stimulus would become more linear and less self-limiting.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Volume transmission dominates: dopamine released from the cell body diffuses tens of micrometres and influences many neurons without a single synapse, and release is graded with calcium rather than gated by spikes.

Some cells are gap-junction coupled into clusters that release together, they control blood flow at ganglionic capillaries, they signal to immune cells through enkephalins, and their transmitter phenotype stays under transcriptional control by circulating glucocorticoids.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: preganglionic acetylcholine at nicotinic and muscarinic receptors, 0.1 to 20 hertz; collateral sensory substance P; circulating glucocorticoid; local oxygen tension.

• OUTPUTS: dopamine into the ganglionic interstitium, high nanomolar to low micromolar near the release site, plus adenosine triphosphate, neuropeptide Y, enkephalin and somatostatin, onto principal sympathetic neurons and intraganglionic capillaries.

• STATE: intracellular calcium; vesicular dopamine store; tyrosine hydroxylase activity; extracellular dopamine near the cell; membrane voltage.

• PARAMETERS: soma 10 to 15 micrometres; type I vesicles 80 to 100 nanometres; type II vesicles 150 to 300 nanometres; process up to 200 micrometres; abundance 0.1 to 1 percent of the ganglion.

• TIME CONSTANTS: calcium transient 50 to 500 milliseconds; dopamine clearance 0.5 to 5 seconds; slow inhibitory postsynaptic potential 0.3 to 3 seconds; glucocorticoid phenotype shift days.

• FAILURE MODE: lost damping is a proposed contributor to sympathetic overactivity in hypertension and heart failure, these cells are a candidate origin for small neuroendocrine tumours in paraganglia, and their dopamine confounds pheochromocytoma testing, which measures plasma methoxytyramine, the breakdown product of dopamine.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute how a SIF cell damps a sympathetic ganglion during sustained preganglionic firing.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 2.
Create a number variable called sif_calcium with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called dopamine with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called gain with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called output_rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
While step is less than 300, repeat the following steps.
    Set drive to 2.
    If both step is greater than 60 and step is less than 240 are true, then perform the following actions.
        Set drive to 18.
    End the conditional block.
    Set sif_calcium to the sum of sif_calcium and the product of drive and 0.004.
    Set sif_calcium to the difference of sif_calcium and the product of sif_calcium and 0.08.
    Set dopamine to the sum of dopamine and the product of sif_calcium and 0.5.
    Set dopamine to the difference of dopamine and the product of dopamine and 0.05.
    Set gain to the quotient of 1 divided by the sum of 1 and dopamine.
    Set output_rate to the product of drive and gain.
    Set step to the sum of step and 1.
End the loop.
Display output_rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen again because the interesting content is a two-line feedback law and Python keeps that law visible rather than buried in boilerplate.

# SIF cell as a slow negative-gain controller inside a sympathetic ganglion.
DT, CA_GAIN, CA_DECAY = 0.01, 0.4, 8.0   # step, calcium rise, calcium clearance
DA_RELEASE, DA_CLEAR, KD = 50.0, 5.0, 1.0  # release, clearance, half-damping

def run(steps=300, burst=(60, 240)):
    ca, da, log = 0.0, 0.0, []
    for step in range(steps):
        drive = 18.0 if burst[0] <= step < burst[1] else 2.0   # hertz
        ca += (CA_GAIN * drive - CA_DECAY * ca) * DT
        da += (DA_RELEASE * ca - DA_CLEAR * da) * DT
        gain = 1.0 / (1.0 + da / KD)      # dopamine divides ganglionic gain
        log.append((step * DT, drive, da, drive * gain))
    return log

if __name__ == "__main__":
    for t, drive, da, out in run()[::30]:
        print("t=%4.2fs in=%4.1f Hz dopamine=%5.3f out=%5.2f Hz"
              % (t, drive, da, out))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the smallest cell in the ganglion, the brightest under a fluorescence microscope, the only dopamine cell in the sympathetic relay, and the only neuron whose identity as neuron or gland can still be changed in adulthood by a steroid hormone.

CHAPTER 263. INTRINSIC CARDIAC GANGLION NEURONS (Heartbeat Local-Reflex Neuron) (NON-COG)

A transplanted heart, cut free of every nerve from the brain, still steadies itself beat to beat, because the heart carries some forty thousand of its own neurons buried in the fat on its surface.

The existing name is an address, telling you only that the cell sits inside the heart.

Heartbeat names what it governs, rate, conduction and force of contraction.

Local-Reflex names the surprising fact, that these ganglia contain sensory neurons, interneurons and motor neurons together and can complete a whole reflex arc within the heart wall without consulting the brain.

Neuron marks it as a member of the network nicknamed the heart's little brain.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These neurons form the intrinsic cardiac nervous system, nicknamed the little brain of the heart, and because the collection includes sensory cells and local connector cells it can run whole reflex loops without the brain, sensing stretch in the atrial wall and adjusting rate, conduction and contraction strength.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Cell bodies are round to oval, clustered in epicardial fat pads, the soft yellow cushions on the heart's surface, and most are multipolar with unusually large tangled dendritic trees, the structural signature of a cell doing real integration rather than simple relay.

SIZE:

Cell bodies measure roughly 20 to 45 micrometres across, and one ganglion spans a few hundred micrometres to a few millimetres, holding from a handful to several hundred neurons.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Cholinergic efferent neurons that receive vagal input and slow the heart.

• Adrenergic and dual-phenotype neurons carrying markers of both branches, commoner than the old two-branch textbook picture allowed.

• Afferent, meaning sensory, neurons responding to stretch and chemical change in the heart wall.

• Local circuit neurons connecting one ganglion to another, the substrate for reflexes that never reach the brain.

• Nitrergic neurons releasing nitric oxide, a gas transmitter, strongly represented in the human heart.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The neurons sit in epicardial ganglionated plexuses, named fat-pad clusters comprising the superior right atrial, inferior right atrial, superior left atrial, posterolateral and posteromedial left atrial, and right and left coronary plexuses, plus ganglia of the ligament of Marshall; they concentrate at two named interfaces, the pulmonary vein ostia where vein meets left atrium, and the sinoatrial node region, with far sparser ventricular ganglia.

NUMBER:

Whole-mount counting of the human epicardial neural plexus gives roughly 43,000 neurons in adult hearts and about 94,000 in young hearts, meaning fetuses, neonates and children, so a working adult range is 35,000 to 100,000, confidence moderate because few hearts have been counted; that is 0.2 to 0.5 percent of the 20 million autonomic ganglion neurons and about 0.00005 percent of the whole nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting potential is around minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, and firing splits into phasic neurons, about 77 percent, which fire one to three spikes at stimulus onset and then fall silent however hard you push, and adapting neurons, about 23 percent, which fire a decaying train and show a shorter afterhyperpolarisation, the dip following each spike.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from vagal preganglionic fibres from the dorsal motor nucleus and nucleus ambiguus, from sympathetic postganglionic fibres of the stellate and middle cervical ganglia, from intrinsic sensory endings in the heart wall, and from other intrinsic cardiac neurons; outputs reach the sinoatrial node, atrioventricular node, atrial and ventricular muscle and coronary vessels, and transmission is deliberately not one for one.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Postganglionic axons are unmyelinated C fibres of 0.2 to 1.5 micrometres conducting at 0.5 to 2 metres per second over only a few millimetres to centimetres, ending in varicosities, beads along the fibre that release transmitter along its length rather than only at a tip.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine at M2 muscarinic receptors is the dominant efferent signal to pacemaker cells, noradrenaline at beta-1 receptors speeds and strengthens the heart, nitric oxide from neuronal nitric oxide synthase is a major human transmitter, and co-modulators include vasoactive intestinal peptide, neuropeptide Y, substance P, calcitonin gene-related peptide, galanin and adenosine triphosphate.

MYELINATION:

The neurons' own output axons are unmyelinated, the vagal preganglionic fibres reaching them are thinly myelinated B fibres, and some cardiac afferents running centrally are myelinated A-delta fibres, so one ganglion holds both insulated and bare wiring.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate and unceasing rather than spiky, since these cells work every second of life, and they are unusually dependent on their fat-pad capillaries, because cardiac ischaemia, meaning insufficient blood flow, alters their excitability within minutes.

PLASTICITY:

After a heart attack these neurons undergo sustained changes in excitability and transmitter phenotype, called neural remodelling, chronic spinal cord stimulation measurably alters their membrane properties and ganglionic transmission, and cutting the nerves from the brain changes their properties rather than silencing them.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Satellite glia here are unusually reactive: after myocardial infarction and in heart failure they upregulate glial fibrillary acidic protein and release inflammatory mediators including interleukin-6 that change neuronal excitability, behaviour closer to central astrocytes after injury than to the quiet satellite glia of a resting chain ganglion, and the surrounding fat cells add leptin and inflammatory signals found around no other ganglion.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the heart becomes a purely reflex organ driven from the brainstem with a long latency, losing beat-to-beat correction: it would no longer trim its own rate within one or two beats when filling pressure changed, the coupling of rate to contraction strength on standing would blur, and a transplanted heart could not regulate itself at all.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

They run complete reflex arcs outside the central nervous system, filter high-frequency preganglionic traffic instead of relaying it, and integrate convergent vagal, sympathetic and sensory input across large dendritic trees.

Nitric oxide diffuses to neighbours as a volume and retrograde signal, neuropeptides form a paracrine network mapped by single-cell sequencing, glial and fat-cell inflammatory signals directly change cardiac rhythm control, and gap-junction coupling lets a ganglion act partly as a unit.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: vagal preganglionic acetylcholine, 0 to 20 hertz; sympathetic noradrenaline, 0 to 10 hertz; intrinsic afferent drive scaled to atrial pressure in millimetres of mercury; local adenosine, potassium and hydrogen ion signalling ischaemia; interleukin-6 from glia.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine to sinoatrial M2 receptors, noradrenaline to beta-1 receptors, nitric oxide, vasoactive intestinal peptide and neuropeptide Y, onto sinoatrial node, atrioventricular node, atrial and ventricular myocardium and coronary arterioles, shifting heart rate by up to plus or minus 40 beats per minute.

• STATE: membrane voltage; M-current activation; intracellular calcium; adaptation current; vagal synaptic strength; local nitric oxide.

• PARAMETERS: resting potential minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts; threshold about minus 40 millivolts; input resistance 100 to 400 megaohms; phasic fraction 0.77; soma 20 to 45 micrometres; conduction 0.5 to 2 metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 15 to 40 milliseconds; spike 2 to 4 milliseconds; afterhyperpolarisation 50 to 300 milliseconds; M-current 100 to 200 milliseconds; nitric oxide about 1 second; post-infarct remodelling days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: overactivity of the pulmonary vein plexuses triggers atrial fibrillation, the commonest sustained rhythm disorder, and ablating those plexuses can abolish inducible atrial fibrillation; loss of these neurons in diabetic autonomic neuropathy gives a fixed unvarying heart rate and raises sudden death risk; post-infarct remodelling promotes ventricular arrhythmia.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether one phasic intrinsic cardiac neuron fires and by how much it slows the heart.
Create a number variable called membrane_voltage with initial value -58.
Create a number variable called adaptation_current with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called stretch_signal with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike_count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called heart_rate with initial value 75.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
While step is less than 400, repeat the following steps.
    Set stretch_signal to 0.
    If both step is greater than 100 and step is less than 300 are true, then perform the following actions.
        Set stretch_signal to 1.2.
    End the conditional block.
    Set membrane_voltage to the sum of membrane_voltage and the product of the difference of -58 and membrane_voltage and 0.05.
    Set membrane_voltage to the sum of membrane_voltage and stretch_signal.
    Set membrane_voltage to the difference of membrane_voltage and adaptation_current.
    If membrane_voltage is greater than -40, then perform the following actions.
        Set spike_count to the sum of spike_count and 1.
        Set membrane_voltage to -65.
        Set adaptation_current to the sum of adaptation_current and 1.5.
    End the conditional block.
    Set adaptation_current to the product of adaptation_current and 0.99.
    Set step to the sum of step and 1.
End the loop.
Set heart_rate to the difference of 75 and the product of spike_count and 1.8.
Display heart_rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because this neuron has to run inside a beat-by-beat whole-heart solver with a fixed real-time budget, and such solvers are written in C.

#include <stdio.h>

/* Phasic intrinsic cardiac neuron with an M-current-like adaptation brake. */
int main(void) {
    const double dt = 0.0005, tau_m = 0.025, tau_adapt = 0.150;
    const double v_rest = -58.0, v_th = -40.0, v_reset = -65.0;
    const double r_in = 250.0, d_adapt = 0.055;   /* megaohms, nanoamperes */
    double v = v_rest, i_adapt = 0.0;
    int spikes = 0;

    for (int step = 0; step < 4000; step++) {     /* 2 seconds */
        double t = step * dt;
        double stretch = (t > 0.25 && t < 1.25) ? 0.075 : 0.0;  /* nA */
        double i_in = 0.010 + stretch - i_adapt;                /* tonic + stretch */
        v += dt / tau_m * (-(v - v_rest) + r_in * i_in);
        i_adapt -= dt / tau_adapt * i_adapt;
        if (v >= v_th) { v = v_reset; i_adapt += d_adapt; spikes++;
                         printf("spike %d at t = %.4f s\n", spikes, t); }
    }
    double hr = 75.0 - 1.8 * spikes;              /* each spike shaves the rate */
    if (hr < 35.0) hr = 35.0;
    printf("spikes = %d, heart rate = %.1f beats per minute\n", spikes, hr);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is a neuron living in fat on the outside of a beating heart that fires two or three times and then refuses however hard you push, and it keeps a transplanted heart adjusting itself years after every nerve from the brain was cut.

CHAPTER 264. PELVIC GANGLION NEURONS (Pelvic-Organ Relay Neuron) (NON-COG)

Everywhere else in the body the two halves of the autonomic nervous system keep separate offices, but deep in the pelvis they share one room.

Pelvic ganglion is a map coordinate and says nothing about the job.

Pelvic-Organ names the territory served, the bladder and bowel functions these cells drive together with the erectile and genital blood-flow function they carry through acetylcholine and nitric oxide.

Relay is the honest word for their position and reflects a genuine recent upheaval, since molecular work reports four neuron classes here that fit neither the classic sympathetic nor the classic parasympathetic mould, with lumbar preganglionic fibres feeding roughly equal numbers of noradrenergic and cholinergic cells.

Neuron marks it as the final common path for pelvic organ control.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are the final output cells for the bladder, the lower bowel, the internal sphincters, the prostate, the uterus and the erectile tissue, and they are unique in that fight-or-flight sympathetic neurons and rest-and-digest parasympathetic neurons are intermingled in the same ganglion, fed by two different levels of the spinal cord.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell bodies are round to oval and, unusually, most have few dendrites or none, which is a strong functional clue: a cell without dendrites cannot pool many weak inputs, so it is built to be driven by one strong input.

SIZE:

Two size classes are consistently described, a common class of about 30 to 45 micrometres staining weakly to moderately for the acetylcholine-making enzyme, and a smaller class of about 15 to 25 micrometres staining very intensely.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Cholinergic neurons releasing acetylcholine to drive bladder emptying and bowel propulsion.

• Nitrergic neurons making nitric oxide, which relaxes smooth muscle and produces erection.

• Noradrenergic neurons closing the bladder neck and internal anal sphincter and relaxing the bladder during filling.

• Dual-phenotype neurons carrying markers of both autonomic branches, common here and awkward for textbook classification.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In humans the cells lie in the inferior hypogastric plexus, a flat sheet about three to five centimetres long on each side of the rectum, and in the vesical, prostatic, uterovaginal and rectal plexuses branching off it, plus scattered neurons in the organ walls; the plexus is itself the named interface where hypogastric nerves from spinal segments T10 to L2 meet pelvic splanchnic nerves from S2 to S4, lying beside the seminal vesicles or cervix, which is why pelvic cancer surgery so often damages it.

NUMBER:

No reliable human count exists and that gap should be stated rather than filled; the rat major pelvic ganglion holds roughly 8,000 to 12,000 neurons per side, and scaling for body size and human dispersion gives 20,000 to 100,000 in a person, confidence low, comfortably inside the tens of thousands allowed, which is 0.1 to 0.5 percent of the 20 million autonomic ganglion neurons and about one millionth of one percent of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting potential is minus 55 to minus 65 millivolts, cells divide into tonic and phasic firers with the phasic pattern set by the M-current, a slow potassium brake carried by KCNQ channels, and because most cells lack dendrites one preganglionic fibre usually fires them outright, so the ganglion is closer to a relay than a filter.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Sympathetic preganglionic input arrives through the hypogastric nerves and sacral input through the pelvic splanchnic nerves, both cholinergic and both acting on nicotinic receptors, with some bladder and bowel afferent input; outputs reach detrusor muscle, internal urethral and anal sphincters, cavernous tissue, vas deferens, prostate, uterus and rectum, and it should be flagged honestly that work published in 2016 and 2023 argues the sacral outflow is molecularly sympathetic, so the parasympathetic label is contested while the anatomy and pharmacology are not.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Postganglionic axons are unmyelinated C fibres of 0.2 to 1.5 micrometres conducting at 0.3 to 1.5 metres per second over a few centimetres, the longest being the cavernous nerves that run beside the prostate to the erectile tissue, ending in varicosities that release transmitter along their length.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine at M3 receptors contracts the detrusor, nitric oxide from neuronal nitric oxide synthase relaxes erectile tissue through cyclic guanosine monophosphate, the messenger that sildenafil protects, noradrenaline closes the bladder neck at alpha-1 receptors and relaxes the bladder at beta-3 receptors, and adenosine triphosphate at P2X receptors adds a fast contractile component, alongside vasoactive intestinal peptide, neuropeptide Y and galanin.

MYELINATION:

The output axons are unmyelinated throughout while the preganglionic fibres are thinly myelinated B fibres, and this bare output is one reason pelvic function recovers so slowly after injury, since unmyelinated fibres regenerate without the Schwann cell tube that guides a myelinated one.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest at rest and sharply episodic, with nitric oxide production the distinctive cost, requiring arginine, oxygen and the cofactor tetrahydrobiopterin, and when that cofactor runs short in diabetes the enzyme uncouples and makes damaging superoxide instead.

PLASTICITY:

These are among the most plastic neurons in the adult body: they carry androgen and oestrogen receptors, castration reduces nitrergic pelvic neurons and testosterone restores them, bladder outlet obstruction causes hypertrophy and a shift towards purinergic transmission, and pregnancy remodels the uterine-projecting population.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Satellite glia here turn destructive after injury, proliferating, upregulating glial fibrillary acidic protein and helping kill nitrergic neurons after cavernous nerve damage, quite unlike the protective satellite glia of a resting chain ganglion, while Schwann cells in the cavernous nerves decide whether erectile function returns by forming the bands of Büngner, aligned cellular tubes that regenerating axons crawl along.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the pelvic organs stay mechanically intact and neurologically silent: the bladder would fill and leak by overflow instead of emptying, the lower bowel would lose propulsion and coordinated sphincter release, and erection, lubrication and ejaculation would not occur, so what the pelvis loses is timing between organs.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Nitric oxide release is not synaptic at all but a gas diffusing through tissue, so one nitrergic neuron relaxes a volume rather than a point, and the muscarinic slow excitation closes the M-current and rewrites the firing pattern for seconds, a genuine state change rather than a summed input.

Purinergic co-release is dialled up in disease so the transmitter mixture is itself a variable, nuclear sex-steroid receptors make gene transcription part of the normal loop, nerve growth factor from the bladder wall lets the organ instruct its own innervation, and satellite glia and macrophages modulate excitability through immune signalling.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: sacral preganglionic acetylcholine, 0 to 20 hertz, at nicotinic and M1 receptors; lumbar preganglionic acetylcholine, 0 to 15 hertz; afferent input encoding bladder and bowel wall tension; circulating testosterone and oestradiol; nerve growth factor from the organ wall.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine to detrusor M3 receptors, adenosine triphosphate to P2X receptors, nitric oxide over a diffusion radius of roughly 100 to 200 micrometres, and noradrenaline to alpha-1 and beta-3 receptors, onto detrusor, urethral and anal sphincters, corpus cavernosum, vas deferens, prostate, uterus and rectum.

• STATE: membrane voltage; M-current activation; intracellular calcium; nitric oxide synthase activity; tetrahydrobiopterin availability; vesicular acetylcholine and adenosine triphosphate stores.

• PARAMETERS: soma 30 to 45 micrometres common class and 15 to 25 micrometres intense class; resting potential minus 55 to minus 65 millivolts; threshold about minus 40 millivolts; input resistance 150 to 500 megaohms; nitrergic fraction roughly 30 to 50 percent of the sacral population.

• TIME CONSTANTS: nicotinic excitatory postsynaptic potential 5 to 20 milliseconds; membrane 20 to 60 milliseconds; muscarinic slow excitation 2 to 30 seconds; nitric oxide 0.1 to 5 seconds; detrusor contraction 5 to 30 seconds; regeneration after injury months.

• FAILURE MODE: cavernous nerve injury during radical prostatectomy triggers RhoA and Rho-associated kinase signalling and caspase-3 dependent death of nitrergic neurons, causing erectile dysfunction; diabetic autonomic neuropathy degrades cholinergic and nitrergic function; spinal cord injury leaves these neurons alive but disconnected, giving detrusor-sphincter dyssynergia in which the bladder contracts against a closed sphincter.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn sacral preganglionic drive into bladder pressure and erectile relaxation from one pelvic ganglion neuron.
Create a number variable called sacral_drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called m_current with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called firing with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called nitric_oxide with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called bladder_pressure with initial value 8.
Create a number variable called relaxation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
While step is less than 300, repeat the following steps.
    Set sacral_drive to 0.
    If both step is greater than 80 and step is less than 200 are true, then perform the following actions.
        Set sacral_drive to 14.
    End the conditional block.
    If sacral_drive is greater than 5, then perform the following actions.
        Set m_current to the product of m_current and 0.9.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set m_current to the sum of m_current and the product of the difference of 1 and m_current and 0.05.
    End the conditional block.
    Set firing to the quotient of sacral_drive divided by the sum of 1 and m_current.
    Set nitric_oxide to the sum of the product of nitric_oxide and 0.9 and the product of firing and 0.4.
    Set bladder_pressure to the sum of 8 and the product of firing and 0.9.
    Set relaxation to the quotient of nitric_oxide divided by the sum of 2 and nitric_oxide.
    Set step to the sum of step and 1.
End the loop.
Display bladder_pressure to the console.
Display relaxation to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the pelvic neuron's key behaviour is a slow state change in one gain variable, and Python keeps that variable next to the physiology it represents.

# Pelvic ganglion neuron: muscarinic closure of the M-current turns a brief
# spinal command into a sustained void, with nitrergic relaxation alongside.
DT, M_CLOSE, M_RECOVER = 0.05, 2.0, 1.0     # step size, closing, recovery rates
NO_MAKE, NO_DECAY, K_NO = 8.0, 2.0, 2.0     # nitric oxide make, clear, half-max

def run(steps=300, void=(80, 200)):
    m_gate, no, out = 1.0, 0.0, []          # m_gate 1.0 = brake fully on
    for step in range(steps):
        voiding = void[0] <= step < void[1]
        sacral = 14.0 if voiding else 0.0                    # hertz
        if sacral > 5.0:
            m_gate -= M_CLOSE * m_gate * DT                  # muscarinic closure
        else:
            m_gate += M_RECOVER * (1.0 - m_gate) * DT
        firing = sacral / (1.0 + m_gate)                     # M-current divides
        no += (NO_MAKE * firing / 14.0 - NO_DECAY * no) * DT
        out.append((step * DT, firing, 8.0 + 0.9 * firing, no / (K_NO + no)))
    return out

if __name__ == "__main__":
    for t, f, p, r in run()[::25]:
        print("t=%5.2fs firing=%5.2f Hz bladder=%5.2f cmH2O relax=%.3f"
              % (t, f, p, r))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only ganglion neuron sharing its ganglion with the opposite autonomic branch, it usually has no dendrites at all, its phenotype answers to sex hormones for life, and a few tens of thousands of these cells carry the entire nerve supply for continence and sexual function.

CHAPTER 265. AIRWAY, BLADDER, AND PANCREATIC GANGLION NEURONS (Organ-Wall Filtering Neuron) (NON-COG)

The vagus nerve sends a torrent of orders down to your lungs and pancreas, and most of them are thrown away before they reach the muscle, by small knots of neurons whose whole job is deciding which orders are worth passing on.

The existing title is a committee minute listing three organs and a location, and it can only grow longer as more organs are added.

Organ-Wall names where these last-step neurons sit, embedded in the tissue they command rather than in a distant chain.

Filtering names the shared work the three have in common despite different organs, because these ganglia do not simply pass the incoming command on but integrate, gate and shape it, so that in the pancreas short bursts of preganglionic stimulation at twenty hertz raised the insulin secretion rate threefold in forty-six percent of preparations and left the rest unchanged.

Neuron marks it as a postganglionic cell embedded in its target organ.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are terminal ganglion neurons, meaning nerve cells living in the wall of the organ they control, and the three organs are grouped because their ganglia do the same job: they are deliberately bad relays, discarding most incoming commands so the organ can set how loudly it listens to the brain.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Airway neurons are notable for substantial dendritic trees, unusual in an autonomic ganglion and the site where preganglionic fibres land; bladder intramural neurons sit singly or in small groups inside the detrusor muscle and are more often adendritic; pancreatic neurons are multipolar with moderate arbors.

SIZE:

Cell bodies run about 20 to 40 micrometres across in all three organs, airway ganglia hold roughly 2 to 50 neurons each, bladder ganglia a handful to a few dozen, and human intrapancreatic ganglia are markedly larger at on average about 200 neurons.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Airway cholinergic neurons constricting airway smooth muscle through M3 receptors.

• Airway nitrergic and vasoactive intestinal peptide neurons, the only bronchodilator nerve supply humans have.

• Bladder cholinergic and purinergic neurons driving detrusor contraction, with nitrergic neurons relaxing the urethra.

• Pancreatic cholinergic neurons raising insulin secretion from islet beta cells and enzyme output from acinar cells.

• Pancreatic nitrergic and peptidergic neurons controlling pancreatic blood flow and duct secretion.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Airway ganglia lie in a superficial plexus outside the cartilage rings of the trachea and main bronchi and a deeper plexus between smooth muscle and mucosa, thinning out before the alveoli; bladder ganglia lie within detrusor bundles and suburothelial tissue of the dome, lateral walls and base; pancreatic ganglia lie in interlobular tissue near the islets, and two interfaces deserve naming, the airway muscle-to-mucosa boundary where mucosal sensory endings can reach the ganglion, and the pancreatic boundary between enzyme-making exocrine tissue and insulin-making islets.

NUMBER:

None of the three has a reliable human count, and the best bounds are 5,000 to 30,000 airway neurons, 5,000 to 50,000 bladder intramural neurons, which are known to be more numerous in humans than in rats, and 10,000 to 100,000 intrapancreatic neurons, all confidence low, giving 20,000 to 180,000 together with a best estimate near 60,000, which is 0.1 to 0.9 percent of the 20 million autonomic ganglion neurons and of the order of one millionth of one percent of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting potential is minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts with a high input resistance of 100 to 500 megaohms, and the measured signature is low transmission fidelity, the fraction of preganglionic spikes producing an output spike, which stays well below one in airway ganglia because a single nicotinic event gives only 5 to 20 millivolts against a threshold near minus 40 millivolts.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Preganglionic input is vagal for airway and pancreas and sacral through the pelvic nerves for bladder, all cholinergic at nicotinic receptors; mucosal sensory fibres release tachykinins, a peptide family including substance P and neurokinin A, directly onto the ganglion to raise its gain, sympathetic fibres damp it, and outputs reach airway smooth muscle, glands and vessels, detrusor and urothelium, and islet, acinar, duct and vascular targets.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Postganglionic axons are unmyelinated C fibres of 0.2 to 1 micrometre conducting at 0.3 to 1 metre per second over only hundreds of micrometres to a few millimetres, ending within 20 to 100 nanometres of airway and bladder smooth muscle but further from pancreatic targets, so pancreatic transmission is more diffuse.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine at M3 receptors is the primary output to airway and bladder muscle and to islet beta cells, nitric oxide and vasoactive intestinal peptide are the relaxant co-transmitters, adenosine triphosphate at P2X receptors grows prominent in overactive bladder, pituitary adenylate cyclase-activating polypeptide drives insulin release, and received peptides include substance P and neurokinin A at NK1, NK2 and NK3 receptors, with NK3 receptors shown specifically to regulate human bronchial ganglion neurons.

MYELINATION:

Output axons are unmyelinated, preganglionic vagal and pelvic fibres are thinly myelinated B fibres conducting at 3 to 15 metres per second, modulating sensory fibres are mostly unmyelinated C fibres, and no oligodendrocytes exist here because oligodendrocytes live only in the brain and spinal cord.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low and steady, since these cells fire at a few hertz for most of the day, except the pancreatic population during a meal, and nitric oxide synthesis is the distinctive cost, with shortage of the cofactor tetrahydrobiopterin in diabetes uncoupling the enzyme so it makes damaging superoxide instead.

PLASTICITY:

This is the clinically decisive aspect: allergen exposure acutely raises airway ganglion excitability and reduces filtering through tachykinins, so more vagal commands get through for the same brain input, chronic inflammation increases the number of substance P expressing neurons, purinergic transmission expands in bladder outlet obstruction, and intrapancreatic neurons hypertrophy and are reprogrammed in chronic pancreatitis and pancreatic cancer.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Satellite glia here are the local immune interface, expressing toll-like receptors and responding to inhaled allergen or virus by releasing cytokines that raise neuronal excitability, a sensing role satellite glia in a sympathetic chain ganglion do not perform, and resident mast cells and macrophages sit inside these ganglia, making them mixed neuro-immune structures.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete them and the three organs stay alive but deaf to the brain: the airways would lose resting bronchial tone and the reflex narrowing to cold air and smoke, mucus output would fall, the bladder would lose its intramural boosting stage and void weakly, and the pancreas would lose the cephalic phase of insulin release, the anticipatory squirt that appears when you smell food before eating.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The central operation is synaptic filtering with variable gain, which is arithmetic rather than relay, and dendritic integration in airway neurons decides whether summed subthreshold inputs cross threshold, so dendritic geometry is functionally decisive here in a way it is not in the pelvic ganglion.

Somatic peptide receptors let sensory nerves reset the gain for minutes, nitric oxide acts as both a diffusible volume signal and a retrograde messenger to the preganglionic terminal, these neurons control bronchial and pancreatic blood flow directly, they exchange signals with mast cells and macrophages, pancreatic ones sense glucose and fatty acids directly, and gap junctions let a ganglion act partly as a unit.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:
