• INPUTS: preganglionic acetylcholine at nicotinic receptors, 0 to 20 hertz, giving a 5 to 20 millivolt excitatory postsynaptic potential; substance P and neurokinin A from sensory endings, nanomolar, at NK1, NK2 and NK3 receptors; noradrenaline and adrenaline as damping; glucose 3 to 15 millimolar for the pancreatic population; interleukin-1 beta and tumour necrosis factor alpha from resident immune cells.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine to airway, detrusor and beta-cell M3 receptors, nitric oxide and vasoactive intestinal peptide as relaxant output, adenosine triphosphate to bladder P2X receptors, and pituitary adenylate cyclase-activating polypeptide, onto airway smooth muscle, submucosal glands, bronchial arterioles, detrusor, urethral muscle, islet beta and alpha cells, acinar cells, ducts and pancreatic arterioles.

• STATE: membrane voltage; summed synaptic conductance; gain multiplier set by tachykinin occupancy; intracellular calcium; nitric oxide synthase activity; refractory timer.

• PARAMETERS: resting potential minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts; threshold about minus 40 millivolts; input resistance 100 to 500 megaohms; single excitatory postsynaptic potential 5 to 20 millivolts; baseline fidelity 0.1 to 0.5 rising towards 0.8 when inflamed; soma 20 to 40 micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS: excitatory postsynaptic potential decay 10 to 40 milliseconds; membrane 20 to 60 milliseconds; tachykinin gain change 30 seconds to 10 minutes; nitric oxide 0.1 to 5 seconds; airway smooth muscle contraction 10 to 60 seconds; inflammatory phenotype change hours to days.

• FAILURE MODE: in asthma the ganglionic filter opens too far under tachykinin drive so discarded vagal traffic reaches the muscle and narrows the airway, which is why inhaled anticholinergic drugs such as tiotropium work, and the same pathway supplies most of the reversible obstruction in chronic obstructive pulmonary disease; rising ganglionic and purinergic gain produces urgency in overactive bladder; loss of pancreatic input removes the cephalic phase of insulin secretion, and diabetic autonomic neuropathy degrades all three populations at once.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to filter preganglionic vagal traffic through an airway ganglion whose gain is raised by inflammation.
Create a number variable called membrane_voltage with initial value -55.
Create a number variable called tachykinin with initial value 0.8.
Create a number variable called gain with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called input_spikes with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called output_spikes with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called fidelity with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
Set gain to the sum of 1 and the product of tachykinin and 1.6.
While step is less than 500, repeat the following steps.
    Set membrane_voltage to the sum of membrane_voltage and the product of the difference of -55 and membrane_voltage and 0.25.
    Set membrane_voltage to the sum of membrane_voltage and the product of tachykinin and 0.6.
    If the remainder of step divided by 8 is equal to 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set input_spikes to the sum of input_spikes and 1.
        Set membrane_voltage to the sum of membrane_voltage and the product of 6 and gain.
    End the conditional block.
    If membrane_voltage is greater than -40, then perform the following actions.
        Set output_spikes to the sum of output_spikes and 1.
        Set membrane_voltage to -60.
    End the conditional block.
    Set step to the sum of step and 1.
End the loop.
Set fidelity to the quotient of output_spikes divided by input_spikes.
Display fidelity to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

JavaScript is chosen because this model is most useful as an interactive slider a student drags to watch the ganglionic filter open, and JavaScript runs in a browser with no installation.

// Airway parasympathetic ganglion neuron as a gain-adjustable synaptic filter.
function runGanglion(inflamed, driveHz = 15, seconds = 2) {
  const DT = 0.001, V_REST = -55, V_TH = -40, V_RESET = -60;
  const TAU = 0.030, EPSP = 6.0;          // seconds; millivolts per input
  const tachykinin = inflamed ? 0.8 : 0.0; // sensory peptide occupancy, 0 to 1
  const gain = 1 + 1.6 * tachykinin;       // peptides boost synaptic efficacy
  const tonic = 4.0 * tachykinin;          // peptides also depolarise the soma
  const steps = Math.round(seconds / DT);
  const interval = Math.max(1, Math.round(1 / (driveHz * DT)));
  let v = V_REST, inputs = 0, outputs = 0;
  for (let step = 0; step < steps; step++) {
    v += (DT / TAU) * (-(v - V_REST) + tonic);          // leak toward rest
    if (step % interval === 0) { inputs++; v += EPSP * gain; }
    if (v >= V_TH) { outputs++; v = V_RESET; }          // postganglionic spike
  }
  return { inputs, outputs, fidelity: outputs / inputs, gain };
}
for (const inflamed of [false, true]) {
  const r = runGanglion(inflamed);
  console.log("inflamed=" + inflamed + " in=" + r.inputs + " out=" + r.outputs +
              " fidelity=" + r.fidelity.toFixed(2) + " gain=" + r.gain.toFixed(2));
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only class of neuron whose main job is throwing away most of the messages it receives, it lives inside the wall of the organ it commands, and when inflammation switches its filter off the result is an asthma attack, an urgent bladder, or a meal that never triggers its anticipatory insulin.


PART SEVENTEEN: ENTERIC NERVOUS SYSTEM - THE GUT'S OWN BRAIN

CHAPTER 266. INTRINSIC PRIMARY AFFERENT NEURONS (IPANS), ALSO CALLED ENTERIC SENSORY NEURONS (Gut-Content Sensing Neuron) (NON-COG)

Buried in your intestinal wall is a plump, many-armed cell that tastes your last meal from the inside and decides what to do, without consulting your brain.

The old label buries the cell under an acronym and borrows the vocabulary of the spinal cord for a cell that owes the brain nothing.

Gut-Content names what this cell actually samples, the chemistry and the physical state of whatever is inside the bowel at that moment, since these cells fire to acids, nutrients and other chemicals applied to the inner lining and to tension in the muscle wall.

Sensing names the faculty and marks the one thing the older label hid, that this is the gut's own first detector rather than a nerve borrowed from the brain or spinal cord.

Neuron marks it as the most numerous single class in some regions, about fifty-four percent of myenteric nerve cells counted in guinea pig duodenum, whose sensory identity was settled when action potentials were still recorded from it after all synaptic transmission was blocked.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An intrinsic primary afferent neuron, shortened to IPAN, is a sensory nerve cell living entirely inside the gut wall, and it is polymodal: one cell answers to distortion of the lining, muscle tension and food chemistry.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It has Dogiel type II shape, named for the histologist who drew it in the 1890s: a large smooth round body with several long tapering arms and no leaf-like dendrites.

SIZE:

Human type II cell bodies span about 700 to 1164 square micrometres in cross-section, some 30 to 40 micrometres across, and each arm runs 1 to 4 millimetres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Myenteric IPANs sit between the muscle coats, submucosal IPANs nearer the lining, and a 2023 human colon survey made two of its twenty classes multiaxonal sensory neurons.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies occupy the myenteric plexus, also called Auerbach's plexus, between longitudinal and circular muscle, and the submucosal or Meissner's plexus, from oesophagus to rectum.

Two interfaces matter: the mucosal interface, where endings meet the epithelium and its serotonin-releasing enterochromaffin cells, and the deep muscular plexus between circular muscle and submucosa.

NUMBER:

Of the 400 million neurons usually credited to the human enteric nervous system, IPANs are taken here as about 40 million, some 10 percent, or 0.04 percent of the whole nervous system.

Confidence is low: guinea-pig gives 20 to 30 percent, human colon as little as 4.6 percent, and a 2022 count put the whole enteric system at 168 million.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These are the classic AH neurons, where AH means afterhyperpolarising: a broad spike, then a trough lasting 1 to 20 seconds made by calcium-activated potassium channels that slow synaptic potentials close.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

They excite each other into self-sustaining assemblies, take serotonin from enterochromaffin cells at 5-HT3 and 5-HT4 receptors, and output to interneurons, motor neurons and secretion-driving neurons.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Several unmyelinated axons per cell, 0.5 to 1.5 micrometres thick, conducting at 0.2 to 0.5 metres per second, running around the gut rather than along it.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine gives fast excitation at nicotinic receptors, pores that open the instant transmitter binds, while substance P gives slow excitation and calcitonin gene-related peptide widens vessels.

MYELINATION:

None, because the enteric nervous system holds no myelinating glia, so a gut reflex takes hundreds of milliseconds rather than tens.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest per spike but oddly shaped, since the long afterhyperpolarisation forces the cell to pump out a heavy calcium load after every impulse.

PLASTICITY:

After colitis they stay hyperexcitable for weeks after the tissue heals, because protein kinase A shrinks the potassium current behind the trough.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Enteric glia answer IPAN firing with calcium waves and release adenosine triphosphate, S100B and D-serine back onto the neuron.

Uniquely, enteric ganglia have no blood-nerve barrier, so glia rather than a tight vessel wall are the only buffer between these cells and everything circulating in the gut wall.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete IPANs and the intestine can still be stretched, but it can no longer feel a pellet or taste a meal, so the gut loses all sense of its contents.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Mutual slow potentials hold a group of IPANs excited for a minute, a short-term memory built from ion channels, and the long trough acts as automatic gain control.

They release peptides from the cell body, couple through gap junctions, dilate arterioles with calcitonin gene-related peptide, and instruct the glia that set how leaky the lining is.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: mucosal distortion 0 to 1, muscle tension in millinewtons, luminal serotonin 0 to 1, synaptic input from other IPANs.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine and substance P onto interneurons and motor neurons, calcitonin gene-related peptide onto arterioles.

• STATE: membrane voltage in millivolts, calcium in micromolar, slow drive 0 to 1, refractory countdown.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 60 millivolts, threshold minus 35 millivolts, brake 25 millivolts per micromolar calcium.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 milliseconds, calcium clearance 3 seconds, afterhyperpolarisation 1 to 20 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: sensitisation gives the pain of irritable bowel syndrome, and losing these cells stops the gut starting propulsion.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether an intrinsic primary afferent neuron fires this millisecond.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called calcium with initial value 0.05.
Create a boolean variable called fired with initial value false.
Calculate the difference of 1 and slow drive.
Calculate the product of the result and calcium.
Calculate the product of 25 and the result.
Calculate the difference of sensory push and the result.
Calculate the difference of the result and voltage.
Calculate the quotient of the result divided by 10.
Calculate the sum of voltage and the result.
Set voltage to the result.
Calculate the product of calcium and 0.99967.
Set calcium to the result.
Check if voltage is greater than 25.
If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set fired to true.
    Set voltage to -10.
    Calculate the sum of calcium and 0.35.
    Set calcium to the result.
End the conditional block.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python chosen because its plain syntax exposes the calcium bookkeeping.
DT = 0.001                                   # one millisecond per step
v, ca, slow, refrac = -60.0, 0.05, 0.0, 0.0  # mV, micromolar, 0 to 1, seconds
spikes = 0
for i in range(20000):                       # 20 seconds of gut time
    stretch = 1.0 if 5000 <= i < 15000 else 0.0
    push = 30.0 * stretch + 8.0 * 0.2        # distortion plus serotonin
    brake = 25.0 * ca * (1.0 - 0.8 * slow)   # calcium activated K current
    v += (-(v + 60.0) + push - brake) * DT / 0.010
    ca -= ca * DT / 3.0                      # 3 s calcium clearance
    slow -= slow * DT / 20.0                 # slow potential fades
    refrac = max(0.0, refrac - DT)
    if v > -35.0 and refrac <= 0.0:
        v, refrac = -70.0, 0.010             # spike, then the long trough
        ca += 0.35
        spikes += 1
        if spikes == 3:
            slow = 1.0                       # a partner IPAN excites it
print("spikes in 20 s:", spikes)

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only gut neuron with a big smooth round body and many long arms, whose spike is followed by seconds of silence.

CHAPTER 267. INTERNEURONS (Gut-Circuit Bridging Neuron) (NON-COG)

Between the cell that feels and the cell that squeezes lies a middle layer whose whole job is to carry news along the gut and decide how far it travels.

Interneuron alone says only that the cell sits between two others, which is true of a great many cells and distinguishes none of them.

Gut-Circuit names the machine this cell belongs to, a complete reflex loop built entirely inside the bowel wall that can run a wave of digestion with the brain disconnected.

Bridging names the job, because this cell touches neither the lining nor the muscle but sits between the sensing cell and the motor cell and carries the message across the gap, connecting also to other cells of its own kind so the message can travel along the gut.

Neuron marks the scale of the job, since of about twenty chemically distinct classes catalogued in the myenteric layer, nine are bridging cells, three carrying signals one way and six the other.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An interneuron talks only to other nerve cells, and in the gut they form chains running lengthways, which is why the intestine contracts behind a lump of food and relaxes ahead of it.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Almost all are Dogiel type I cells, meaning one thin output fibre and short flat leaf-shaped dendrites on a small angular body.

SIZE:

Human myenteric type I cell bodies span about 259 to 487 square micrometres, some 18 to 25 micrometres across, with axons from 2 millimetres to several centimetres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The split is directional: guinea-pig small intestine has one ascending and three descending classes, while the 2023 human colon survey found three ascending and six descending classes.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Overwhelmingly in the myenteric plexus between the two muscle coats, from oesophagus to internal anal sphincter.

Their axons travel inside the internodal strands, the cables joining one ganglion to the next, so the interface that matters is ganglion to strand to ganglion.

NUMBER:

Interneurons are 12 to 18 percent of enteric neurons, so about 55 million of the 400 million total, roughly 14 percent, or 0.055 percent of the whole nervous system.

That figure is the sum of the two entries that follow, not an extra population.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

They are S neurons, where S means synaptic: brief excitatory potentials, a trough under 100 milliseconds, and fast excitation through nicotinic, 5-HT3 and P2X pores.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from sensory neurons, other interneurons and sympathetic and vagal fibres, and in human colon terminal baskets wrap 45 percent of myenteric cell bodies.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Single, unmyelinated, 0.3 to 1 micrometre thick, conducting at 0.2 to 0.5 metres per second, dropping swellings called varicosities at every ganglion.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine is the workhorse, with tachykinins added by ascending cells, nitric oxide, somatostatin or serotonin by descending cells, and adenosine triphosphate acting at P2X and P2Y1 receptors.

MYELINATION:

None, as in every intrinsic enteric neuron, so the system uses chains of short slow hops instead of one long fast fibre.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Higher per cell than sensory neurons during activity because these cells fire sustained trains, though at rest they are cheap.

PLASTICITY:

Muscularis macrophages in the muscle coat release bone morphogenetic protein 2 onto these neurons, and the neurons return colony stimulating factor 1, tuned by gut bacteria.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Elongated type II enteric glia inside the internodal strands wrap bundles of interneuron axons without ever myelinating them.

Glial S100B release is required to sustain the rhythmic colonic motor complex and acts mainly on cholinergic neurons, so silencing these glia leaves the rhythm in pieces.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove interneurons and a pellet provokes a ring of contraction exactly where it sits, with nothing squeezing behind and nothing opening ahead.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Slow potentials mediated by P2Y1 receptors hold a chain excited for hundreds of milliseconds, which is how relaxation outruns the contraction behind it.

They take presynaptic inhibition from sympathetic endings, so the brain can silence a chain without touching muscle, and their varicosities spill transmitter into open space.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: nicotinic, 5-HT3 and P2X fast excitation in millivolts, slow P2Y1 excitation, inhibitory noradrenaline from sympathetic fibres.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine plus a subclass-specific peptide or gas onto the next interneuron and onto motor neurons.

• STATE: membrane voltage, fast synaptic drive, slow synaptic drive, refractory countdown.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 60 millivolts, threshold minus 40 millivolts, chain reach 4 to 7 centimetres.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 6 milliseconds, fast potential 15 to 30 milliseconds, slow P2Y1 potential 0.3 to 2 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: losing these relays fragments propagation into slow transit constipation, and Chagas disease destroys them, producing megacolon.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to make one enteric interneuron relay an impulse onward and refuse to fire again while recovering.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called synaptic drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called recovery timer with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called relayed with initial value false.
If an upstream impulse arrived is true, then perform the following actions.
    Calculate the sum of synaptic drive and 70.
    Set synaptic drive to the result.
End the conditional block.
Calculate the product of synaptic drive and 0.875.
Set synaptic drive to the result.
Calculate the difference of synaptic drive and voltage.
Calculate the quotient of the result divided by 6.
Calculate the sum of voltage and the result.
Set voltage to the result.
Calculate the difference of recovery timer and 1.
Set recovery timer to the result.
Check if recovery timer is less than or equal to 0.
If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
    Check if voltage is greater than 20.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set relayed to true.
        Set voltage to -15.
        Set recovery timer to 40.
    End the conditional block.
End the conditional block.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C chosen because a relay chain is a tight loop over a fixed array. */
#include <stdio.h>
#define N 40                /* forty interneurons in one chain */
#define DT 0.001            /* seconds per step */
int main(void) {
    double v[N], s[N], refrac[N];
    int fired[N], prev[N], i, t;
    for (i = 0; i < N; i++) { v[i] = -60.0; s[i] = 0.0; refrac[i] = 0.0; fired[i] = 0; }
    for (t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
        for (i = 0; i < N; i++) prev[i] = fired[i];
        for (i = 0; i < N; i++) {
            if (i == 0 && t == 100) s[i] += 70.0;          /* sensory kick */
            if (i > 0 && prev[i - 1]) s[i] += 70.0;        /* nicotinic input */
            v[i] += (-(v[i] + 60.0) + s[i]) * DT / 0.006;  /* 6 ms membrane */
            s[i] -= s[i] * DT / 0.008;                     /* 8 ms decay */
            refrac[i] -= DT;
            fired[i] = 0;
            if (v[i] > -40.0 && refrac[i] <= 0.0) {
                fired[i] = 1; v[i] = -75.0; refrac[i] = 0.040;
                printf("t = %.3f s, cell %d relayed\n", t * DT, i);
            }
        }
    }
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the gut neuron that touches no muscle, no lining and no vessel, whose axon runs lengthways for centimetres passing the message on.

CHAPTER 268. ASCENDING INTERNEURONS (Behind-The-Food Relay Neuron) (NON-COG)

When a lump of food arrives, somebody must shout upstream "squeeze in behind it", and this is the cell that shouts.

Ascending is a word for anatomists meaning towards the mouth, and it requires a reader to hold a diagram in their head before it means anything.

Behind-The-Food fixes the cell in the only frame a reader can picture, the lump of food itself, and says which side of it this cell serves, because towards the mouth is behind the food as it travels, which is exactly where the gut must squeeze to push.

Relay names the work, since the cell takes the message from the sensing cell and hands it on to the excitatory muscle motor neurons that produce the contraction.

Neuron marks it as a cell of modest and measurable reach, running up to about ten millimetres towards the mouth and using acetylcholine with tachykinins as its chemical signal.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An ascending interneuron is a relay whose axon runs towards the mouth, carrying news of a bolus, a lump of food, backwards so the muscle behind it contracts.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Dogiel type I shape in the stubby pattern: a small body with a few thick blunt leaf-like dendrites, and one axon that turns towards the mouth at once.

SIZE:

Cell bodies span about 259 to 487 square micrometres, some 18 to 25 micrometres across, with axons of several millimetres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Guinea-pig small intestine has a single ascending class, while human colon has three, separated by which peptides accompany acetylcholine.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Myenteric plexus only, in the ganglia between longitudinal and circular muscle, from oesophagus to rectum.

Their axons occupy the oral half of the internodal strands, and the interface that matters is the ganglion-to-ganglion junction in the oral direction.

NUMBER:

About 18 million, roughly one third of the 55 million enteric interneurons, so 4.5 percent of enteric neurons and 0.018 percent of the whole nervous system.

Confidence is low, since the share is inferred from three ascending against six descending classes; this count is part of the interneuron total, not extra.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

S type, with fast nicotinic potentials of 10 to 25 millivolts and firing at 10 to 30 impulses per second, and tachykinin at NK3 receptors adds a slow depolarisation that lowers the threshold.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive as dense cholinergic baskets from sensory neurons and other ascending cells, with outputs to the next ascending cell and to excitatory muscle motor neurons.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Single, unmyelinated, 0.3 to 0.8 micrometres thick, conducting at 0.2 to 0.5 metres per second, strictly polarised towards the mouth.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine for fast transmission, substance P for slow excitation, and enkephalins, opioid peptides that damp neighbours; calretinin is the standard marker, not a transmitter.

MYELINATION:

None, so ascending signalling takes tens of milliseconds per centimetre and the contraction behind a bolus visibly lags the bolus.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Bursty rather than steady, nearly silent between meals then firing trains for the length of a peristaltic wave.

PLASTICITY:

Morphine and codeine silence this cholinergic pathway through mu opioid receptors, the main cause of opioid-induced constipation, and inflammation raises tachykinin expression here.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Star-shaped glia inside the ganglion sit between the incoming cholinergic baskets and this cell body, clearing potassium and transmitter debris.

Glial S100B sustains the colonic motor complex and acts preferentially on cholinergic neurons, which is this population.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete ascending interneurons and the gut only relaxes: everything opens ahead of a bolus, nothing closes behind it, and the contents slop back and forth.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The tachykinin slow potential is a genuine short-term memory that biases the gut towards repeating the movement it just made.

Enkephalin from these terminals feeds back onto the terminals themselves, and presynaptic alpha-2 receptors let sympathetic nerves veto release without changing firing.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: nicotinic fast excitation 10 to 25 millivolts per event, NK3 tachykinin slow excitation 0 to 1, alpha-2 adrenergic gain multiplier 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine and substance P onto the next ascending interneuron and onto excitatory muscle motor neurons.

• STATE: membrane voltage, fast drive, tachykinin drive, firing threshold, refractory countdown.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 58 millivolts, threshold minus 42 millivolts falling by up to 6, ganglion spacing 2 millimetres, relay delay 8 to 15 milliseconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 7 milliseconds, fast potential 20 milliseconds, refractory 30 milliseconds, tachykinin potential 5 to 20 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: silenced by opioids, giving constipation, and destroyed in Chagas disease and megacolon, where contraction behind the bolus fails.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to relay a contraction command one segment towards the mouth, more easily when tachykinin has recently been released.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called fast drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called tachykinin with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing threshold with initial value 16.
Create a boolean variable called passed on with initial value false.
If an impulse arrived from below is true, then perform the following actions.
    Calculate the sum of fast drive and 40.
    Set fast drive to the result.
    Calculate the sum of tachykinin and 0.15.
    Set tachykinin to the result.
End the conditional block.
Calculate the product of fast drive and 0.95.
Set fast drive to the result.
Calculate the product of tachykinin and 0.999875.
Set tachykinin to the result.
Calculate the product of 6 and tachykinin.
Calculate the difference of 16 and the result.
Set firing threshold to the result.
Calculate the difference of fast drive and voltage.
Calculate the quotient of the result divided by 7.
Calculate the sum of voltage and the result.
Set voltage to the result.
Check if voltage is greater than firing threshold.
If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set passed on to true.
    Set voltage to -12.
End the conditional block.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python chosen because the oral relay is one short readable loop.
DT, SEG, BOLUS = 0.001, 30, 10        # 30 ganglia 2 mm apart, bolus at 10
v = [-58.0] * SEG
epsp = [0.0] * SEG
slow = [0.0] * SEG
refrac = [0.0] * SEG
carry = [False] * SEG
for step in range(1500):
    fired = [False] * SEG
    for i in range(SEG):
        driven = i == BOLUS and 100 <= step < 300 and step % 50 == 0
        if driven or carry[i]:
            epsp[i] += 40.0               # fast nicotinic input
            slow[i] += 0.15               # tachykinin builds excitability
        epsp[i] -= epsp[i] * DT / 0.020
        slow[i] -= slow[i] * DT / 8.0
        v[i] += (-(v[i] + 58.0) + epsp[i]) * DT / 0.007
        refrac[i] = max(0.0, refrac[i] - DT)
        if v[i] > -42.0 - 6.0 * min(1.0, slow[i]) and refrac[i] <= 0.0:
            v[i], refrac[i], fired[i] = -70.0, 0.030, True
    carry = [False] * SEG
    for i in range(1, SEG):
        if fired[i]:
            carry[i - 1] = True           # hand one step towards the mouth
    if any(fired):
        reach = (BOLUS - min(i for i in range(SEG) if fired[i])) * 2
        print("t = %.3f s, reached %d mm oral" % (step * DT, reach))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the cholinergic, calretinin-positive cell wrapped in dense cholinergic baskets and pointed firmly at the mouth.

CHAPTER 269. DESCENDING INTERNEURONS (Ahead-Of-The-Food Relay Neuron) (NON-COG)

Ahead of every swallowed mouthful a wave of relaxation runs down the gut like a doorman opening doors.

Descending is the matching anatomist's word, meaning towards the anus, and it is equally opaque on its own.

Ahead-Of-The-Food places the cell on the far side of the food from its partner, using the same picture so the pair can be read as one system, because ahead of the food the gut must relax and open rather than squeeze.

Relay again names the handing-on, since this cell carries the sensing cell's message forward to the inhibitory muscle motor neurons whose nitric oxide slackens the ring of muscle.

Neuron marks it as the more elaborate arm of the circuit, with six chemically distinct classes described against three running the other way, and the inhibitory motor cells it commands reaching as far as twenty-five millimetres down the gut.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A descending interneuron is a relay whose axon runs towards the anus, so the muscle in front of a bolus relaxes before the lump arrives.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Dogiel type I shape in the spiny pattern, with thin prickly dendrites and a single axon that turns anally at once.

SIZE:

Cell bodies span about 259 to 487 square micrometres, some 18 to 25 micrometres across, with axons of 5 to 20 millimetres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Guinea pig has three descending classes, all cholinergic, adding nitric oxide synthase, somatostatin, or serotonin.

Human colon has six, and in one study 29 percent carried both choline acetyltransferase and nitric oxide synthase.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Myenteric plexus throughout the gut, densest in small intestine and colon, with axons filling the anal half of the internodal strands.

The critical interface is myenteric ganglion to deep muscular plexus, where inhibitory motor neurons cluster.

NUMBER:

About 37 million, two thirds of the enteric interneurons, so 9 percent of enteric neurons and 0.037 percent of the whole nervous system.

Confidence is low, since the share is inferred from six descending against three ascending classes; this count is part of the interneuron total, not extra.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

S type, with fast nicotinic and P2X potentials, but the signature is a slow potential at P2Y1 receptors, which answer adenosine triphosphate indirectly.

That potential lasts hundreds of milliseconds and is how these interneurons transmit during descending inhibition, so a chain once started keeps running.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs from sensory neurons, other descending interneurons and vagal fibres, with outputs onto the next descending cell and onto inhibitory muscle motor neurons.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Single, unmyelinated, 0.3 to 1 micrometre thick, conducting at 0.2 to 0.5 metres per second, polarised anally, the somatostatin class running furthest.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine is the fast transmitter in every class, adenosine triphosphate carries slow excitation at P2Y1 receptors, and class partners are nitric oxide, somatostatin, or serotonin.

MYELINATION:

None, and the slowness is offset by the long slow potential, which keeps a chain firing.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Sustained rather than bursty, because the slow potential holds the cell depolarised for seconds, and nitrergic members burn extra fuel.

PLASTICITY:

Ageing, diabetes and inflammation preferentially damage nitrergic descending circuitry, while gut bacteria remodel it through serotonin pathways.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Elongated glia in the internodal strands escort these long axons and, unusually, carry receptors for adenosine triphosphate, the transmitter these neurons use.

Glial calcium waves therefore travel alongside the descending neural wave.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove descending interneurons and every ring ahead of a bolus stays shut, so contraction behind a closed door achieves nothing.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The P2Y1 slow potential is a chemical timer turning a millisecond spike into a second of readiness, making the chain a delay line.

Their nitric oxide is a gas that crosses membranes freely and acts on anything within about 100 micrometres, the clearest volume transmission in the body.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: nicotinic and P2X fast excitation, P2Y1 slow excitation 0 to 1, presynaptic alpha-2 adrenergic inhibition.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine plus nitric oxide, somatostatin or serotonin onto the next descending cell and onto inhibitory motor neurons.

• STATE: membrane voltage, slow purinergic potential, refractory countdown, fixed class label.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 60 millivolts, threshold minus 40 millivolts, slow potential up to 30 millivolts, chain reach 4 to 7 centimetres.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 8 milliseconds, P2Y1 potential 0.3 to 2 seconds, refractory 50 milliseconds, wave travel 1 to 4 millimetres per second.

• FAILURE MODE: failed descending inhibition is the core lesion of achalasia and Hirschsprung disease, and its decay in diabetes gives gastroparesis.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to carry a wave of relaxation one segment towards the anus using a slow purinergic potential.
Create a number variable called slow potential with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called recovery timer with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called relaxation passed on with initial value false.
If an impulse arrived from above is true, then perform the following actions.
    Calculate the sum of slow potential and 0.9.
    Set slow potential to the result.
End the conditional block.
Calculate the product of slow potential and 0.9975.
Set slow potential to the result.
Calculate the product of 30 and slow potential.
Calculate the difference of the result and voltage.
Calculate the quotient of the result divided by 8.
Calculate the sum of voltage and the result.
Set voltage to the result.
Calculate the difference of recovery timer and 1.
Set recovery timer to the result.
Check if recovery timer is less than or equal to 0.
If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
    Check if voltage is greater than 20.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set relaxation passed on to true.
        Set voltage to -12.
        Set recovery timer to 50.
    End the conditional block.
End the conditional block.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

// JavaScript chosen so the wave can be watched in any browser console.
const N = 60, DT = 0.001;              // 60 ganglia 2 mm apart, 120 mm of gut
const slow = new Array(N).fill(0);     // P2Y1 slow potential, 0 to 1
const v = new Array(N).fill(-60);      // membrane voltage in millivolts
const refrac = new Array(N).fill(0);
let fired = new Array(N).fill(false);
let furthest = -1;
for (let t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
  const prev = fired.slice();
  fired = new Array(N).fill(false);
  for (let i = 0; i < N; i++) {
    if (i === 0 && t >= 200 && t < 400) slow[i] += 0.9 * DT / 0.05;  // bolus
    if (i > 0 && prev[i - 1]) slow[i] += 0.9;                        // relay
    slow[i] -= slow[i] * DT / 0.400;                                 // 400 ms
    v[i] += (-(v[i] + 60) + 30 * slow[i]) * DT / 0.008;              // 8 ms
    refrac[i] -= DT;
    if (v[i] > -40 && refrac[i] <= 0) {
      fired[i] = true; v[i] = -72; refrac[i] = 0.050;
    }
  }
  const front = fired.lastIndexOf(true);
  if (front > furthest) {
    furthest = front;
    console.log((t * DT).toFixed(3) + " s: reached " + front * 2 + " mm anal");
  }
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the gut neuron pointed at the anus, sparsely basketed, driven by a slow purinergic potential and carrying nitric oxide, somatostatin or serotonin.

CHAPTER 270. EXCITATORY MUSCLE MOTOR NEURONS (NON-COG)

This is the cell that makes the squeeze, the last nerve in the chain and the only one your gut muscle obeys.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An excitatory muscle motor neuron is the final path from the gut's brain to its muscle, releasing acetylcholine and a peptide that let calcium into muscle.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Dogiel type I shape in the stubby pattern, with one axon that leaves the nerve net and runs through muscle as a bare fibre studded with swellings called varicosities.

SIZE:

Cell bodies span about 259 to 487 square micrometres, and in human colon 95 percent of traced muscle motor neurons lay within 6 millimetres of their target.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

They divide by target, circular or longitudinal muscle, and human colon holds four excitatory classes separated by which peptides accompany acetylcholine.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies sit in the myenteric plexus from oesophagus to internal anal sphincter, with a few in the submucosal plexus supplying the thin muscle sheet under the lining.

Terminals lie in the tertiary plexus on longitudinal muscle and the deep muscular plexus inside circular muscle, meeting the SIP syncytium of muscle, interstitial cells of Cajal and platelet-derived growth factor receptor alpha cells.

NUMBER:

About 70 million, some 17.5 percent of the 400 million enteric neurons and 0.07 percent of the whole nervous system, the largest class in this section.

Confidence is moderate: guinea pig gives 24 percent of myenteric neurons for longitudinal muscle plus 12 percent for circular.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

S type, with fast nicotinic potentials, a trough of tens of milliseconds, and trains of 5 to 30 impulses per second between silent intervals.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from sensory neurons and ascending interneurons as dense cholinergic baskets, and outputs reach muscarinic receptors, tachykinin receptors and intramuscular interstitial cells of Cajal.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Single, unmyelinated, 0.3 to 1 micrometre thick, short, running locally or slightly orally, ending in hundreds of varicosities over a few square millimetres.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine is the main transmitter, cleared within tens of milliseconds by acetylcholinesterase, while substance P and neurokinin A give a slower contraction that survives atropine.

MYELINATION:

None, and none is needed, because the muscle sheet spreads excitation itself, so the gut uses many slow fibres rather than fast ones.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The highest sustained demand here, since these cells refill hundreds of vesicles at hundreds of varicosities at every wave, and fail early in ischaemia.

PLASTICITY:

Muscle above an obstruction hypertrophies so the same release gives more force, and inflammation raises tachykinin, adding atropine-resistant cramps.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Spindle-shaped glia inside the muscle coats wrap intramuscular axons, buffer the potassium heavy firing releases and recycle choline beside the varicosities.

Muscularis macrophages act as local microglia, supporting these terminals and taking colony stimulating factor 1 from the neurons in return.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove excitatory motor neurons and the gut becomes a slack bag: the muscle still twitches, but nothing turns a decision into a real contraction.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

They write into a shared electrical medium rather than commanding muscle, since their acetylcholine acts first on interstitial cells of Cajal, which pass it on through gap junctions.

Transmitter escapes varicosities into a gap 50 to 100 nanometres wide, recruiting a field rather than one target, and they release colony stimulating factor 1 to local macrophages.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: nicotinic fast excitation 10 to 25 millivolts, tachykinin slow excitation, alpha-2 adrenergic gain multiplier.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine onto M2 and M3 receptors and substance P onto NK1 and NK2 receptors of muscle and interstitial cells of Cajal.

• STATE: membrane voltage, acetylcholine level, tachykinin level, muscle voltage, normalised force.

• PARAMETERS: muscle rest minus 55 millivolts, mechanical threshold minus 45 millivolts, acetylcholine gain 9 and tachykinin gain 7 millivolts per unit.

• TIME CONSTANTS: acetylcholine clearance 60 milliseconds, tachykinin 2.5 seconds, muscle membrane 30 milliseconds, force 150 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: losing cholinergic drive gives the flaccid bowel of pseudo-obstruction, while unopposed drive gives spasm and a tight sphincter.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn a burst of impulses in an excitatory muscle motor neuron into a change in muscle voltage.
Create a number variable called acetylcholine with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called tachykinin with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called muscle voltage with initial value -55.
Create a number variable called target voltage with initial value -55.
If the motor neuron fired this millisecond is true, then perform the following actions.
    Calculate the sum of acetylcholine and 1.
    Set acetylcholine to the result.
    Calculate the sum of tachykinin and 0.35.
    Set tachykinin to the result.
End the conditional block.
Calculate the product of acetylcholine and 0.9835.
Set acetylcholine to the result.
Calculate the product of tachykinin and 0.9996.
Set tachykinin to the result.
Calculate the product of 9 and acetylcholine.
Set target voltage to the result.
Calculate the product of 7 and tachykinin.
Calculate the sum of target voltage and the result.
Calculate the difference of the result and 55.
Set target voltage to the result.
Calculate the difference of target voltage and muscle voltage.
Calculate the quotient of the result divided by 30.
Calculate the sum of muscle voltage and the result.
Set muscle voltage to the result.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python chosen so the fast and slow arms read side by side.
DT = 0.001                                       # seconds per step
ach = tk = 0.0                                   # transmitter at the muscle
vm, force = -55.0, 0.0                           # millivolts, 0 to 1
for step in range(4000):
    t = step * DT
    if 0.4 <= t < 0.8 and step % 100 == 0:       # 10 Hz burst for 400 ms
        ach += 1.0                               # varicosities release
        tk += 0.35                               # peptide needs bursts
    ach -= ach * DT / 0.060                      # cholinesterase clears it
    tk -= tk * DT / 2.500                        # peptide lingers
    target = -55.0 + 9.0 * ach + 7.0 * tk        # muscarinic plus tachykinin
    vm += (target - vm) * DT / 0.030             # muscle membrane, 30 ms
    drive = min(1.0, max(0.0, vm + 45.0) / 25.0) # threshold minus 45 mV
    force += (drive - force) * DT / 0.150        # force lags voltage
    if step % 400 == 0:
        print("t = %.1f s, Vm = %.1f mV, force = %.3f" % (t, vm, force))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the cholinergic, heavily basketed cell whose short axon ends as a string of beads inside muscle, and whose failure makes the bowel go limp.

CHAPTER 271. INHIBITORY MUSCLE MOTOR NEURONS (NON-COG)

Every swallow depends on a nerve cell whose entire working life is spent telling muscle not to squeeze.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Gut muscle carries a constant rhythm and tone, so relaxation must be commanded, and this cell commands it.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Dogiel type I shape in the spiny pattern, with an axon that turns anally before entering muscle and ends in varicosities in the deep muscular plexus.

SIZE:

Cell bodies span about 259 to 487 square micrometres, some 18 to 25 micrometres across, with anal axons of 2 to 15 millimetres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Human colon holds four inhibitory classes, divided by target muscle and by their mixture of nitric oxide, vasoactive intestinal peptide and adenosine triphosphate.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Myenteric plexus from oesophagus to internal anal sphincter, with terminals in the deep muscular plexus, and specialised members at the lower oesophageal sphincter, the pylorus and the internal anal sphincter.

The interface is again the SIP syncytium, but split: the purinergic arm acts on platelet-derived growth factor receptor alpha cells, the nitrergic arm on interstitial cells.

NUMBER:

About 45 million, some 11 percent of the 400 million enteric neurons and 0.045 percent of the whole nervous system, taking this part's total to about 210 million.

Confidence is moderate: guinea pig gives 16 percent of myenteric neurons for circular muscle, and nitric oxide synthase marks 20 to 40 percent in human colon.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Many fire tonically at a few impulses per second, holding muscle relaxed by default, so contraction needs them switched off.

Their firing gives a two-phase inhibitory junction potential: a fast purinergic dip near 150 milliseconds, then a slow nitrergic trough.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs from descending interneurons, sensory neurons and vagal fibres that trigger receptive relaxation, with outputs onto muscle, interstitial cells of Cajal and platelet-derived growth factor receptor alpha cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Single, unmyelinated, 0.3 to 1 micrometre thick, longer than the excitatory equivalent, and because nitric oxide is a gas its field exceeds its arbor.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Nitric oxide, made on demand by neuronal nitric oxide synthase, gives the slow relaxation, adenosine triphosphate at P2Y1 receptors the fast dip, and vasoactive intestinal peptide a long tail.

MYELINATION:

None, and speed matters less because two of the three transmitters work through cascades taking hundreds of milliseconds.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Distinctive, because making nitric oxide burns arginine, oxygen and nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate continuously, and losing tetrahydrobiopterin yields superoxide.

PLASTICITY:

Diabetes lowers nitric oxide synthase before the neuron dies, partly reversibly with insulin, and nitrergic neurons from human stem cells improved motility in mice.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Spindle glia inside the muscle matter more here than anywhere, because nitric oxide is destroyed within seconds by oxygen radicals.

Muscularis macrophages nearby make the reactive oxygen that kills these terminals, so the glia-macrophage balance decides whether the gut opens.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove inhibitory motor neurons and the region clamps shut: the gullet will not pass food, the anus will not pass stool.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Nitric oxide is not stored in vesicles but made on demand when calcium enters the terminal, then diffuses to any cell within 100 micrometres.

The purinergic arm writes to a relay layer rather than to muscle, and their tonic firing makes much of gut control disinhibition.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: nicotinic excitation from descending interneurons, vagal drive, oesophageal stretch, sympathetic modulation.

• OUTPUTS: nitric oxide onto muscle and interstitial cells of Cajal, adenosine triphosphate onto P2Y1 receptors, peptide onto both.

• STATE: membrane voltage, tonic rate, purinergic, nitrergic and peptide levels, muscle voltage.

• PARAMETERS: muscle rest minus 55 millivolts, junction potential 10 to 25 millivolts, purinergic weight 2.5, nitrergic weight 1.8.

• TIME CONSTANTS: purinergic 120 milliseconds, nitrergic 900 milliseconds, peptide 6 seconds, muscle membrane 50 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: selective loss causes achalasia, infantile hypertrophic pyloric stenosis, gastroparesis, Hirschsprung disease and chronic pseudo-obstruction.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn a burst of impulses in an inhibitory motor neuron into a two-stage relaxation.
Create a number variable called purine with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called nitric oxide with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called muscle voltage with initial value -55.
Create a number variable called target voltage with initial value -55.
If the motor neuron fired this millisecond is true, then perform the following actions.
    Calculate the sum of purine and 1.
    Set purine to the result.
    Calculate the sum of nitric oxide and 1.
    Set nitric oxide to the result.
End the conditional block.
Calculate the product of purine and 0.99169.
Set purine to the result.
Calculate the product of nitric oxide and 0.99889.
Set nitric oxide to the result.
Set target voltage to -55.
Calculate the product of 2.5 and purine.
Calculate the difference of target voltage and the result.
Set target voltage to the result.
Calculate the product of 1.8 and nitric oxide.
Calculate the difference of target voltage and the result.
Set target voltage to the result.
Calculate the difference of target voltage and muscle voltage.
Calculate the quotient of the result divided by 50.
Calculate the sum of muscle voltage and the result.
Set muscle voltage to the result.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C chosen because the three arms run on different clocks. */
#include <stdio.h>
#define DT 0.001            /* seconds per step */
int main(void) {
    double purine = 0.0;    /* ATP at P2Y1 on PDGFRa cells */
    double no = 0.0;        /* nitric oxide on muscle and ICC */
    double vip = 0.0;       /* vasoactive intestinal peptide */
    double vm = -55.0;      /* muscle voltage, millivolts */
    double target, t;
    int step, spike;
    for (step = 0; step < 4000; step++) {
        t = step * DT;
        spike = (t > 0.5 && t < 0.7 && (step % 50) == 0);  /* 20 Hz burst */
        if (spike) { purine += 1.0; no += 1.0; vip += 0.25; }
        purine -= purine * DT / 0.120;   /* fast dip */
        no     -= no     * DT / 0.900;   /* slow trough */
        vip    -= vip    * DT / 6.000;   /* peptide tail */
        target = -55.0 - 2.5 * purine - 1.8 * no - 1.2 * vip;
        vm += (target - vm) * DT / 0.050;
        if ((step % 200) == 0) printf("%.2f s, Vm = %.1f mV\n", t, vm);
    }
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron whose transmitter is a gas made on the spot, named in four diseases of sphincters that will not open.

CHAPTER 272. SECRETOMOTOR NEURONS (NON-COG)

Roughly nine litres of watery fluid pour into your small intestine every day, and a thin sheet of nerve cells hidden under the lining decides how much of it is pushed out in the first place.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A secretomotor neuron is the final command cell for fluid secretion by the gut lining.

It sits in the submucosal plexus, which is a lace of tiny nerve cell clusters buried in the soft layer just beneath the lining, and it sends a single axon a fraction of a millimetre to the crypts, the narrow pits where new lining cells are born.

When it fires, crypt cells pump chloride, a negatively charged salt particle, out into the gut cavity, and sodium and water follow it by simple physics.

This entry covers the members of the family whose axons end only on the lining and never on a blood vessel.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is small, smooth and oval, with one axon and a modest crown of short dendrites, the receiving branches, whose tips are flattened like little leaves.

That is the shape the Russian anatomist Alexander Dogiel called type I in 1899.

The axon leaves the ganglion, threads up through the thin muscle sheet of the mucosa, and breaks into a spray of varicosities, which are bead-like swellings strung along an axon that release chemical messenger without forming a classical junction.

SIZE:

The cell body is about 15 to 25 micrometres across in guinea pig and roughly 20 to 35 micrometres in human tissue, so about one third the width of a human hair.

Most axons travel under 3 millimetres, mainly around the circumference of the gut and into the mucosa directly overhead.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

There are two chemical families.

Cholinergic secretomotor neurons carry the enzyme choline acetyltransferase (ChAT), which builds acetylcholine, and are usually loaded with neuropeptide Y (NPY), somatostatin, galanin and dynorphin.

Non-cholinergic secretomotor neurons carry vasoactive intestinal peptide (VIP) instead, and in mouse the ones that supply only the lining are distinguished from the ones that also supply vessels by carrying tyrosine hydroxylase (TH), an enzyme in the noradrenaline pathway.

In guinea pig small intestine the cholinergic NPY class is about 33 per cent of submucosal neurons and the VIP class about 43 per cent.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Submucosal ganglia along the whole small intestine and colon, and in humans these form two distinct sheets, an inner submucosal plexus close to the lining and an outer submucosal plexus close to the circular muscle.

The critical interface is the muscularis mucosae, the hair-thin muscle sheet the axons must cross to reach the crypt epithelium, and the axons then run inside the lamina propria, the soft tissue core of each villus.

Submucosal ganglia are sparse in the human oesophagus and stomach, so this cell is overwhelmingly an intestinal resident.

NUMBER:

Best estimate about 35 million in an adult human, roughly 9 per cent of the 400 million neurons of the enteric nervous system (ENS), the gut's own nervous system.

Confidence is low to moderate, because the figure is scaled from guinea pig proportions and from human counts showing 68 to 73 per cent of submucosal neurons are cholinergic; nobody has counted them directly in a whole human gut.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

This is an S neuron, meaning synaptic, and in a small human study Dogiel type I cells rested at minus 58.0 plus or minus 8.5 millivolts with an input resistance of 59.2 plus or minus 22.5 megaohms.

Fast excitatory postsynaptic potentials (EPSPs), which are brief voltage nudges caused by an incoming signal, arrive through nicotinic acetylcholine receptors and are often large enough to fire the cell on their own.

There is no slow afterhyperpolarisation, so the cell can follow its input faithfully instead of pausing after each spike.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from intrinsic primary afferent neurons (IPANs), the gut's own sensory cells, from cholinergic interneurons descending in the submucosal plexus, and from neighbouring cholinergic submucosal neurons that converge and diverge to amplify the signal.

Sympathetic axons from outside the gut clamp the cell down through alpha-2 adrenoceptors, and this is the main brake.

The output is not a true synapse at all: varicosities dump transmitter into the space beside the crypt cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Fine, bare and slow, roughly 0.1 to 1 micrometre thick, conducting at a walking pace of about 0.1 to 0.5 metres per second.

A single axon can supply several crypts, so one cell governs a small patch of lining rather than a single cell.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine acts on muscarinic M3 receptors on crypt cells, raising internal calcium and opening chloride channels.

VIP acts on VPAC1 receptors, switching on the enzyme adenylyl cyclase, raising cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cyclic AMP), and opening the cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator (CFTR), the chloride channel that is broken in cystic fibrosis.

Neuropeptide Y and somatostatin work the other way and restrain secretion, so the same cell carries both accelerator and brake.

MYELINATION:

None.

No axon anywhere in the enteric nervous system is myelinated, because distances are short and enteric glia, not Schwann cells, wrap the bundles, so conduction is deliberately slow and cheap.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The neuron itself is modest, but the tissue it drives is not, since pumping salt against a gradient is one of the body's larger energy bills.

Enteric neurons burn glucose and glutamine and sit in one of the best perfused tissues in the body, which is why the submucosa is packed with capillaries.

PLASTICITY:

Cholera toxin makes guinea pig submucosal secretomotor neurons sustainedly hyperexcitable, an effect that outlasts the toxin and depends on serotonin type 3, nicotinic and neurokinin 1 receptors.

Inflammation shifts the peptide mix, raising VIP expression, and long opioid exposure suppresses then re-sensitises these cells, which is why opioid constipation rebounds.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Two glial populations matter here and both do something unusual.

Intraganglionic submucosal glia carry VPAC receptors and answer neuronal adenosine triphosphate (ATP) with calcium waves that pass through connexin-43 hemichannels, so the glia eavesdrop on the very transmitter the neuron uses.

Mucosal glia leave the ganglion entirely and sit inside the lamina propria between the nerve terminal and the epithelium, a position no glial cell holds anywhere else in the body, where they release ATP and prostaglandins that tune secretion and help hold the barrier shut.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this cell and the lining becomes a passive sponge that only absorbs.

The thin watery film that floats mucus off the surface thins, bacteria press directly against the epithelium, and the flush reflex that washes an irritant away is simply gone.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This neuron does not synapse onto its target; it uses volume transmission, releasing messenger into a shared space so one burst changes the state of a whole crypt for minutes.

Slow EPSPs lasting tens of seconds close potassium channels and shift the cell into a different operating mode rather than merely adding a spike.

After cholera toxin, protein kinase A driven changes leave the cell excitable for days, which is memory in a motor neuron.

It also talks to mucosal mast cells, immune cells that release histamine, in both directions, and its VIP acts on immune cells as well as on epithelium.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: nicotinic fast EPSPs from IPANs and interneurons at 0 to 40 hertz; slow EPSPs from serotonin and tachykinins; alpha-2 adrenergic inhibition from sympathetic axons at 0 to 10 hertz; mast cell histamine and prostaglandin as a modulatory scalar from 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine and VIP released as volume transmission onto crypt enterocytes, expressed as a chloride flux in microamperes per square centimetre of epithelium.

• STATE: membrane potential in millivolts; slow EPSP conductance; presynaptic vesicle pool; target cell cyclic AMP; target cell calcium; a long-term excitability scalar.

• PARAMETERS: resting potential minus 58 millivolts; input resistance 59 megaohms; threshold about minus 38 millivolts; fast EPSP amplitude 3 to 25 millivolts; refractory period about 4 milliseconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; fast EPSP 20 milliseconds; slow EPSP 10 to 60 seconds; target cyclic AMP about 8 seconds; target calcium about 1 second; toxin induced hyperexcitability 1 to 7 days.

• FAILURE MODE: runaway firing gives secretory diarrhoea as in cholera and travellers' diarrhoea; silence gives dry, sticky, slow-moving contents and constipation, as in opioid use and in enteric neuropathy.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn a burst of secretomotor spikes into fluid secreted by one intestinal crypt.
Create a number variable called cyclic_amp with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called calcium with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called chloride_flux with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called total_fluid with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step_seconds with initial value 0.001.
Create a list variable called spike_train with initial value empty list.
Call the function named load_spike_flags_for_twenty_seconds.
For each spike_flag in spike_train, perform the following operations.
    If spike_flag is equal to 1, then perform the following actions.
        Set cyclic_amp to the sum of cyclic_amp and 0.6.
        Set calcium to the sum of calcium and 0.25.
    End the conditional block.
    Set cyclic_amp to the difference of cyclic_amp and the product of cyclic_amp and 0.000125.
    Set calcium to the difference of calcium and the product of calcium and 0.0011.
    Set chloride_flux to the quotient of cyclic_amp divided by the sum of cyclic_amp and 1.
    Set chloride_flux to the sum of chloride_flux and the quotient of calcium divided by the sum of calcium and 1.
    If chloride_flux is greater than 1, then perform the following actions.
        Set chloride_flux to 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Set total_fluid to the sum of total_fluid and the product of chloride_flux and step_seconds.
End the loop.
Display total_fluid to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the model is a handful of coupled leaky variables and Python keeps that arithmetic visible rather than hiding it in boilerplate.

# Secretomotor drive to one intestinal crypt.
DT = 0.001  # seconds per step

def crypt_secretion(spike_times, duration=20.0):
    camp = 0.0       # VIP -> adenylyl cyclase -> cyclic AMP, arbitrary units
    calcium = 0.0    # acetylcholine -> M3 -> calcium rise, arbitrary units
    total = 0.0      # cumulative chloride flux
    peak = 0.0
    steps = int(duration / DT)
    spikes = {round(t / DT) for t in spike_times}
    for i in range(steps):
        if i in spikes:
            camp += 0.60      # VIP released by this spike
            calcium += 0.25   # acetylcholine released by this spike
        camp -= camp * DT / 8.0        # cyclic AMP decays, tau 8 s
        calcium -= calcium * DT / 0.9  # calcium decays, tau 0.9 s
        cftr = camp / (camp + 1.0)        # cyclic AMP opens CFTR, saturating
        cacc = calcium / (calcium + 1.0)  # calcium opens a second Cl channel
        # the two channel families overlap, so they are combined, not added
        flux = 12.0 * (cftr + cacc - cftr * cacc)
        total += flux * DT
        if flux > peak:
            peak = flux
    return total, peak

burst = [1.0 + 0.05 * k for k in range(40)]  # 2 s of firing at 20 Hz
amount, top = crypt_secretion(burst)
print("peak flux %.2f, total secreted %.1f" % (top, amount))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A small uniaxonal cell body in a submucosal ganglion whose only axon ends among crypt cells and touches no blood vessel and no muscle, carrying either neuropeptide Y with choline acetyltransferase or vasoactive intestinal peptide with tyrosine hydroxylase, firing repetitively with no long pause afterwards.

CHAPTER 273. SECRETOMOTOR AND VASODILATOR NEURONS (NON-COG)

This is the cell that solved a plumbing problem: you cannot pour litres of water into the gut without first opening the tap that feeds it.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This neuron has one cell body and two jobs, because its axon forks.

One branch runs to the crypts of the gut lining and orders fluid secretion; the other branch runs to the small arteries in the submucosa and orders them to widen.

It therefore couples demand to supply, since the water secreted into the gut comes out of the bloodstream and must be replaced within seconds.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A smooth oval cell body in a submucosal ganglion with short lamellar dendrites, and one axon that divides within a millimetre or two of leaving.

The vascular branch coils around an arteriole, a small artery roughly a tenth of a millimetre wide, forming a basket of varicosities in the outer coat of the vessel.

The mucosal branch climbs to the crypt and fans out there.

SIZE:

Cell body roughly 20 to 30 micrometres across, similar to its mucosa-only cousin.

The two branches together may cover 4 to 6 millimetres of tissue, making this one of the wider-reaching submucosal cells.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two chemical classes are recognised in guinea pig and mouse.

The non-cholinergic class carries vasoactive intestinal peptide with neuropeptide Y and calretinin, a calcium-binding protein used as a marker.

The cholinergic class carries choline acetyltransferase with calretinin, and often calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) and somatostatin.

Importantly, staining shows vasoactive intestinal peptide and the vesicular acetylcholine transporter are not found in the same axons on intestinal arteries, so the two classes run in parallel rather than mixing.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Submucosal ganglia of the small intestine and colon, in both the inner and the outer submucosal plexus in humans.

Its targets straddle two very different compartments: the epithelium of the crypts inside the mucosa, and the arterioles of the submucosal vascular plexus, so this cell physically bridges the neural, epithelial and vascular tissues of the gut wall.

NUMBER:

Best estimate about 45 million in an adult human, roughly 11 per cent of the 400 million neurons of the enteric nervous system.

Confidence is low, because the dual projection can only be proven by tracing both branches of one axon, which has been done in animals and not at scale in humans.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

An S neuron with fast nicotinic excitatory postsynaptic potentials and no slow afterhyperpolarisation, resting near minus 55 to minus 60 millivolts.

Many of these cells fire tonically at 0.5 to 2 hertz even at rest, which is what keeps a baseline of blood flow and secretion running between meals.

Slow excitatory postsynaptic potentials lift that baseline for tens of seconds after a meal arrives.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs from intrinsic primary afferent neurons sampling the lumen, from submucosal and myenteric cholinergic interneurons, and from vagal pathways relayed through enteric circuits.

Sympathetic noradrenergic axons inhibit the cell body directly, which shuts down secretion and dilation together during exercise or blood loss.

Outputs are non-synaptic release onto crypt epithelium and onto vascular smooth muscle and endothelium.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Bare, fine, roughly 0.2 to 1 micrometre thick, conducting at about 0.2 to 0.5 metres per second, with hundreds of varicosities distributed unequally between the two branches.

Because release is by diffusion, the vascular branch does not need to touch the muscle cells it controls.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Vasoactive intestinal peptide acts on VPAC1 receptors on crypt cells to open CFTR chloride channels, and on VPAC receptors on vascular smooth muscle to relax it directly.

Acetylcholine acts on muscarinic M3 receptors on crypt cells, and on M3 receptors on the endothelium, the single cell layer lining the vessel, which then makes nitric oxide, a gas that diffuses into the muscle and relaxes it.

Neuropeptide Y and somatostatin act as restraining co-transmitters.

MYELINATION:

None, for the same reason as every other enteric neuron: distances are short, glia rather than Schwann cells wrap the bundles, and slow conduction is adequate for a tissue whose responses take seconds.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest for the neuron, large for the consequences, since after a meal blood flow in the superior mesenteric artery can roughly double and the mucosa takes most of that increase.

Continuous low-rate firing means these cells never fully rest, so their mitochondria are correspondingly busy.

PLASTICITY:

Chronic inflammation raises vasoactive intestinal peptide content and can uncouple the two branches, giving secretion without matching flow.

In experimental Chagas disease, caused by the parasite Trypanosoma cruzi, neurons containing vasoactive intestinal peptide and calretinin survive disproportionately while others die, so this population is unusually robust.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Glia here work at two addresses.

Mucosal glia near the crypt do what they do for any secretomotor terminal, but the glia along the vascular branch behave more like brain astrocytes at a blood vessel, sitting between the axon and the vessel wall and shaping the local chemical environment.

Enteric glia are also required for the barrier and for normal transit, so removing them degrades both halves of this cell's job at once.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the gut can still secrete and can still dilate, but not together.

The result is a mucosa that dries out at the villus tips during a large meal, because fluid leaves faster than blood replaces it, and the tips are the part of the lining that dies first when supply fails.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The forked axon means a single spike train is broadcast to two tissues at once, so the cell performs a fixed allocation rather than a decision.

Release is volume transmission at both ends, and the vascular branch also recruits the endothelium as a second messenger relay, making the vessel itself part of the computation.

Nitric oxide generated by the endothelium diffuses backwards and can modulate the nerve terminal, a retrograde message.

The cell also participates in functional hyperaemia, the local matching of blood flow to work, which is the gut version of the mechanism that lights up a brain scan.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: nicotinic fast EPSPs at 0 to 30 hertz from IPANs and interneurons; slow EPSPs from tachykinins and serotonin; sympathetic alpha-2 inhibition at 0 to 15 hertz; luminal nutrient signal relayed as a scalar from 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: mucosal chloride secretion in microlitres per square centimetre per minute; arteriolar dilation expressed as a change in radius in micrometres or as a flow multiplier from 1.0 to about 3.0.

• STATE: membrane potential; transmitter pool; crypt cyclic AMP; arteriolar smooth muscle tone; an internal deficit accumulator recording water demanded but not supplied.

• PARAMETERS: resting potential minus 57 millivolts; tonic rate 0.5 to 2 hertz; maximum flow multiplier about 3; branch split ratio roughly 50:50 by varicosity count.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; transmitter clearance about 5 seconds; epithelial response about 1 second; arteriolar smooth muscle relaxation 3 to 6 seconds; postprandial hyperaemia 30 to 90 minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of the vascular branch produces villus tip ischaemia during digestion; loss of the mucosal branch produces dry hard stool with normal blood flow; sympathetic overdrive during shock silences both and is a step towards non-occlusive mesenteric ischaemia.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to send one spike train down both branches of a secretomotor and vasodilator neuron and to record any shortfall between water secreted and water supplied.
Create a number variable called transmitter with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called secretion with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called flow with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called target_flow with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called supply with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called deficit with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing_rate with initial value 6.
Create a number variable called step_seconds with initial value 0.01.
Repeat the following steps exactly 3000 times.
    Set transmitter to the sum of transmitter and the product of firing_rate and 0.0005.
    Set transmitter to the difference of transmitter and the product of transmitter and 0.002.
    Set secretion to the product of 6 and the quotient of transmitter divided by the sum of transmitter and 0.4.
    Set target_flow to the sum of 1 and the product of 2.2 and the quotient of transmitter divided by the sum of transmitter and 0.6.
    Set flow to the sum of flow and the product of the difference of target_flow and flow and 0.0025.
    Set supply to the product of 5.5 and the difference of flow and 1.
    If secretion is greater than supply, then perform the following actions.
        Set deficit to the sum of deficit and the product of the difference of secretion and supply and step_seconds.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display secretion to the console.
Display flow to the console.
Display deficit to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the interesting part is bookkeeping across two targets with different speeds, and Python makes the two update rules sit side by side and be compared at a glance.

# One neuron, two branches: crypt secretion and arteriolar dilation.
DT = 0.01  # seconds

def dual_branch(rate_hz, seconds=30.0):
    transmitter = 0.0   # released VIP or acetylcholine, arbitrary units
    flow = 1.0          # 1.0 means resting blood flow
    secretion = 0.0     # water demanded by the crypts
    deficit = 0.0       # water demanded but not yet supplied
    per_step = rate_hz * DT
    for _ in range(int(seconds / DT)):
        transmitter += per_step * 0.05
        transmitter -= transmitter * DT / 5.0   # clearance, tau 5 s
        # the epithelium answers within about a second
        secretion = 6.0 * transmitter / (transmitter + 0.4)
        # the arteriole knows where it is going but gets there slowly
        target = 1.0 + 2.2 * transmitter / (transmitter + 0.6)
        flow += (target - flow) * DT / 4.0      # smooth muscle, tau 4 s
        supply = 5.5 * (flow - 1.0)
        if secretion > supply:
            deficit += (secretion - supply) * DT
    return secretion, flow, deficit

for hz in (0.5, 2.0, 10.0):
    s, f, d = dual_branch(hz)
    print("%5.1f Hz -> secretion %.2f, flow %.2fx, deficit %.2f" % (hz, s, f, d))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A submucosal cell body whose single axon can be traced to two completely different tissues, a crypt and an arteriole, in the same preparation, marked by calretinin, and firing slowly and continuously even when the gut is empty.

CHAPTER 274. VASOMOTOR NEURONS (NON-COG)

The gut has its own private circulatory control room, and it is staffed almost entirely by nerve cells that can only say open.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A vasomotor neuron is an enteric neuron whose output target is a blood vessel and nothing else.

It sets the calibre of the arterioles that feed the mucosa, and because flow through a tube rises with the fourth power of its radius, a modest widening produces a very large change in supply.

There is an honest asymmetry to state plainly: the enteric nervous system supplies dilators, while the constrictor commands arrive from outside the gut along sympathetic nerves, so this cell type is functionally a vasodilator population.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small to medium uniaxonal cell body of Dogiel type I shape with short lamellar dendrites, sitting in a submucosal ganglion, usually in the outer submucosal plexus near the vessels it serves.

The axon runs along the connective tissue sleeve of an arteriole and forms a loose helical basket of varicosities around it, rather than terminating at a point.

Some myenteric neurons also send axons to arteries entering the gut wall, so a minority of this population lives between the muscle layers.

SIZE:

Cell body roughly 15 to 30 micrometres across.

The axon typically follows a single arteriole and its branches for 2 to 5 millimetres, so one neuron governs the supply to a patch of mucosa a few millimetres wide.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Cholinergic vasomotor axons are identified by the vesicular acetylcholine transporter, the protein that loads acetylcholine into vesicles, and originate from submucosal cell bodies.

Non-cholinergic vasomotor axons carry vasoactive intestinal peptide instead, and the two markers are not found together in the same axons on intestinal arteries, confirming two separate populations.

A third minor group carries neuronal nitric oxide synthase (nNOS), the enzyme that makes nitric oxide directly in the nerve terminal.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Submucosal ganglia of the small intestine and colon, concentrated in the outer submucosal plexus; a smaller contingent in myenteric ganglia.

The functional address is the interface between the nervous system and the vascular system inside the gut wall, specifically the adventitia, the outer connective coat, of submucosal arterioles, and the arteries that penetrate the muscle from the serosal surface.

NUMBER:

Best estimate about 8 million in an adult human, roughly 2 per cent of the 400 million neurons of the enteric nervous system.

Confidence is low; this is the least well counted of the submucosal classes because pure vasomotor cells are hard to separate from cells that also supply the mucosa, and the true figure could plausibly be half or double this.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

An S neuron with fast nicotinic excitatory postsynaptic potentials, resting around minus 55 millivolts, firing tonically at low rates to maintain a resting vascular tone.

These cells show prominent slow excitatory postsynaptic potentials, which is how a meal produces a sustained rise in flow rather than a twitch.

They are also strongly hyperpolarised by noradrenaline, the sympathetic transmitter, so the sympathetic system both constricts the vessel directly and switches off the neuron that would dilate it.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from intrinsic primary afferent neurons responding to nutrients and acid in the lumen, from cholinergic interneurons, and from extrinsic sensory fibres releasing calcitonin gene-related peptide.

Sympathetic postganglionic axons from the coeliac and superior mesenteric ganglia inhibit the cell body.

The output is onto vascular smooth muscle and endothelium, with no morphologically specialised junction.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:
