Bare and thin, about 0.2 to 0.8 micrometres, conducting near 0.3 metres per second, with the varicosities standing off from the muscle by up to a micrometre so transmitter must diffuse.

Because the axon spirals along the vessel, one action potential releases transmitter over a long stretch of tube at almost the same moment.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine acting on endothelial muscarinic M3 receptors to release nitric oxide, which diffuses into the smooth muscle and relaxes it.

Vasoactive intestinal peptide acting directly on smooth muscle VPAC receptors to raise cyclic AMP and relax it.

Nitric oxide released directly from nitrergic terminals, and adenosine triphosphate as a fast co-transmitter in some vessels.

MYELINATION:

None; enteric axons are unmyelinated throughout, and here it matters little because vascular responses unfold over seconds, not milliseconds.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low for the neuron; the point of the cell is to spend other tissues' energy wisely.

Its continuous low-rate firing does mean a steady sodium and potassium pumping cost, which places it among the more continuously active enteric neurons.

PLASTICITY:

Inflammation and ischaemia change the balance between the cholinergic and peptidergic arms, and prolonged sympathetic overdrive can leave the population functionally silenced.

Vasoactive intestinal peptide expression rises in disease states, and this appears partly protective, since these neurons survive better than most in chagasic megacolon.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia hugging this cell's axon sit in a position no other enteric glia occupy, sandwiched between the nerve terminal and the vessel wall, where they behave much like the astrocyte end-feet that control blood flow in the brain.

They respond to neuronal adenosine triphosphate with calcium waves through connexin-43 hemichannels and release their own vasoactive messengers, so the glial cell is part of the control loop rather than scaffolding.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the mucosa loses the ability to reward itself for working.

Blood flow becomes a purely systemic quantity set by the sympathetic nervous system and by blood pressure, digestion proceeds on a fixed ration, and the villus tips, which sit at the far end of a countercurrent loop where oxygen is already scarce, become the first casualty of any stress.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This neuron controls local blood flow, which is the clearest example in the body of a nerve cell acting as an infrastructure engineer.

It uses volume transmission, recruits the endothelium as an amplifier, receives retrograde nitric oxide from that same endothelium, and cooperates with glia that behave like astrocytes.

It also participates in ephaptic and paracrine crosstalk with sensory fibres, since calcitonin gene-related peptide released from extrinsic afferents dilates the same vessel independently, giving the tissue two overlapping dilator systems that must be modelled together.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: nicotinic fast EPSPs at 0 to 25 hertz; slow EPSPs from tachykinins lasting tens of seconds; noradrenergic alpha-2 inhibition at 0 to 20 hertz; local metabolite signals such as carbon dioxide and adenosine as a scalar from 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine, vasoactive intestinal peptide and nitric oxide onto arteriolar wall, expressed as arteriolar radius in micrometres and flow as a multiple of resting flow.

• STATE: membrane potential; transmitter pool; endothelial nitric oxide level; smooth muscle tone; current radius.

• PARAMETERS: resting radius about 12 micrometres; minimum 6 and maximum 20 micrometres; maximum dilator effect about 8 micrometres; maximum constrictor effect about 7 micrometres; flow scales with radius to the fourth power.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; nitric oxide lifetime under 1 second; smooth muscle 3 seconds; postprandial hyperaemia 30 to 90 minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: failure to dilate during digestion causes postprandial pain and villus tip damage; unopposed sympathetic drive in shock or in cardiac failure produces non-occlusive mesenteric ischaemia, in which the gut dies with all its arteries still open.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to set the radius of a submucosal arteriole from opposing dilator and constrictor drives and to report the resulting blood flow.
Create a number variable called radius with initial value 12.
Create a number variable called target_radius with initial value 12.
Create a number variable called dilator_rate with initial value 10.
Create a number variable called sympathetic_rate with initial value 4.
Create a number variable called dilate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called constrict with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called ratio with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called flow with initial value 1.
Repeat the following steps exactly 1200 times.
    Set dilate to the product of 8 and the quotient of dilator_rate divided by the sum of dilator_rate and 4.
    Set constrict to the product of 7 and the quotient of sympathetic_rate divided by the sum of sympathetic_rate and 3.
    Set target_radius to the difference of the sum of 12 and dilate and constrict.
    If target_radius is less than 6, then perform the following actions.
        Set target_radius to 6.
    End the conditional block.
    If target_radius is greater than 20, then perform the following actions.
        Set target_radius to 20.
    End the conditional block.
    Set radius to the sum of radius and the product of the difference of target_radius and radius and 0.0167.
End the loop.
Set ratio to the quotient of radius divided by 12.
Set flow to the product of ratio and ratio.
Set flow to the product of flow and flow.
Display radius to the console.
Display flow to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the whole point is the fourth power law and two opposing saturating drives, and Python states that arithmetic in one readable line each.

# Enteric vasomotor control of one submucosal arteriole.
DT = 0.05  # seconds

def arteriole(dilator_hz, sympathetic_hz, seconds=60.0):
    radius = 12.0                 # micrometres at rest
    r_min, r_max = 6.0, 20.0      # anatomical limits of the vessel
    flow = 1.0
    for _ in range(int(seconds / DT)):
        # both drives saturate: more spikes buy less and less
        dilate = 8.0 * dilator_hz / (dilator_hz + 4.0)
        constrict = 7.0 * sympathetic_hz / (sympathetic_hz + 3.0)
        target = 12.0 + dilate - constrict
        target = max(r_min, min(r_max, target))
        radius += (target - radius) * DT / 3.0   # smooth muscle, tau 3 s
        flow = (radius / 12.0) ** 4              # Poiseuille's fourth power
    return radius, flow

print(" dilator  sympathetic   radius     flow")
for d, s in ((0.0, 0.0), (10.0, 0.0), (0.0, 10.0), (10.0, 10.0)):
    r, f = arteriole(d, s)
    print("%7.1f %12.1f %8.2f %8.2f" % (d, s, r, f))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

An enteric cell body whose axon can be followed onto an arteriole and onto nothing else, wrapping the vessel in a helix of varicosities, silenced by noradrenaline, and belonging to a population that has an accelerator but no brake of its own.

CHAPTER 275. INTESTINOFUGAL NEURONS (NON-COG)

This is the only neuron born in the gut wall that sends its cable out of the gut altogether, carrying news of a full bowel to a knot of nerve cells lying loose in the abdomen, and getting an answer back that stops the bowel upstream.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An intestinofugal neuron, also called a viscerofugal neuron, is a nerve cell whose body sits inside the intestinal wall and whose single output cable leaves the intestine entirely.

The name means, literally, fleeing the intestine.

The cable travels out along the mesenteric nerves, the strands that run with the blood vessels suspending the gut, and ends in a prevertebral ganglion, a cluster of sympathetic nerve cells parked in front of the spine.

There it makes an ordinary fast excitatory synapse using acetylcholine, a chemical messenger, on nicotinic receptors.

The sympathetic cells it excites send their own axons back into the gut, where they release noradrenaline and damp down movement.

The whole loop, gut to ganglion to gut, never touches the brain or the spinal cord.

This is how a distended loop of bowel tells a distant loop of bowel to stop pushing food towards it, a response called the entero enteric inhibitory reflex.

The neuron is mechanosensitive in its own right: it fires faster as tension in the gut wall rises, so it is at once a sensor and a long distance transmitter.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

In the human colon, ninety seven percent of these cells have exactly one axon, measured in a study that filled two hundred and fifteen of them with dye.

Their cell bodies are visually diverse, which is unusual and important.

About forty two percent have lamellar or stubby dendrites, the short flattened leaf shaped receiving branches that define Dogiel type I morphology.

About thirty six percent have filamentous or spiny dendrites, thin and thorn covered.

About nineteen percent are simple, with almost no dendrites at all.

Only about three percent have the large smooth multipolar shape called Dogiel type II.

Seventy nine percent of them are wrapped in baskets of incoming nerve endings, dense cages of varicose fibres pressed against the cell body.

SIZE:

In human colon, cell body profile areas run from one hundred and twelve to two thousand three hundred and fifty one square micrometres, with an average of eight hundred and seventy eight, plus or minus four hundred and eighty three.

Sorted by shape, the simple cells average four hundred and twenty two square micrometres, the lamellar cells eight hundred and forty six, the filamentous cells one thousand and fifty seven, and the rare Dogiel type II cells two thousand and six.

A micrometre is one thousandth of a millimetre, so the largest of these cell bodies is still only about fifty micrometres across.

The axon is unmyelinated and perhaps zero point three to one micrometre thick, but it is long by enteric standards: it runs several millimetres inside the gut and then tens of centimetres outside it.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

There is a small intestine type and a large intestine type, and they differ chemically.

In the guinea pig small intestine they are small Dogiel type I cells that carry vasoactive intestinal peptide, a twenty eight amino acid messenger, together with choline acetyltransferase, the enzyme that makes acetylcholine, and gastrin releasing peptide, but they do not carry nitric oxide synthase.

In the large intestine some do carry nitric oxide synthase.

In the human colon the population is strongly cholinergic and chemically mixed: eighty nine percent carry choline acetyltransferase, thirteen percent nitric oxide synthase, thirteen percent calbindin, nine percent enkephalin, three percent calretinin, two percent vasoactive intestinal peptide, and none carry cocaine and amphetamine regulated transcript.

Strikingly, sixty nine percent of them share the exact chemical signature of excitatory muscle motor neurons, even though those motor neurons are only about thirty percent of colonic myenteric cells, which suggests these two classes are close relatives.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies are confined to the myenteric plexus, the nerve net sandwiched between the two coats of gut muscle, the inner circular and the outer longitudinal.

They are not found in the submucosal plexus.

They are concentrated near the mesenteric attachment, the line where the gut's suspending membrane joins the tube, with most cell bodies within four millimetres of it.

Their axons cross the single most important interface in this book: the boundary of the enteric nervous system itself, leaving the gut wall through the mesenteric and colonic nerve trunks and entering the coeliac, superior mesenteric and inferior mesenteric ganglia.

Density rises along the gut; in the guinea pig colon it is about fourteen cells per square centimetre proximally, twenty two distally, and thirty seven in the rectum.

NUMBER:

They are rare: fewer than one percent of myenteric neurons in the guinea pig small intestine.

Taking the human enteric nervous system at about four hundred million neurons, with perhaps half of those myenteric, and applying a rate of under one percent that rises towards the rectum, gives an estimate near one million intestinofugal neurons in an adult human.

That is roughly zero point two five percent of the enteric nervous system and about one hundredth of one percent of one percent of the eighty six billion neurons in the whole nervous system.

Confidence is low.

The best hard human data available is a count of nine hundred and three labelled cells across twenty five tissue samples from twenty three patients, with four to one hundred and twenty one cells recovered per sample, which fixes the order of magnitude but not the total.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

They are S neurons, the class defined by strong fast synaptic input and no long silent period after firing.

Resting voltage, the charge difference across the cell membrane at rest, is around minus fifty to minus sixty millivolts.

They are tonically active, firing slowly even in an empty gut, at a fraction of a spike per second.

Firing rate climbs roughly in proportion to circumferential tension in the wall, reaching perhaps ten to fifteen spikes per second in a strongly distended bowel.

They also fire in bursts locked to each contraction, so their output encodes both how full the gut is and what it is doing.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive as the dense baskets already described, and their chemistry has been counted: sixty nine percent of the basket fibres carry the vesicular acetylcholine transporter, twenty nine percent carry substance P, and twenty one percent carry enkephalin.

The sources are myenteric interneurons and intrinsic primary afferent neurons, the gut's own sensory cells.

Output is a single, powerful, fast excitatory synapse on the cell bodies of sympathetic neurons in prevertebral ganglia, using acetylcholine on nicotinic receptors.

Those same sympathetic neurons also receive tonic input from preganglionic neurons in the spinal cord, so the ganglion is a genuine integrator comparing a steady central command with a distension driven gut command.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Before leaving the gut the axon travels within the myenteric plexus, and it has a direction preference: about eighty five percent run towards the mouth for an average of two point seven millimetres, plus or minus one point four, and about fifteen percent run towards the anus for one point three millimetres, plus or minus one.

It is unmyelinated inside the gut wall.

Conduction is slow, well under one metre per second, so a message from the sigmoid colon to the inferior mesenteric ganglion takes a substantial fraction of a second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine is the transmitter that matters at the output synapse, and blocking nicotinic receptors abolishes the reflex.

Co stored messengers vary with region and include vasoactive intestinal peptide, gastrin releasing peptide, enkephalin and, in some large intestine cells, nitric oxide.

MYELINATION:

None inside the gut, because the enteric nervous system contains no myelin forming cells at all.

Outside the gut the axon joins mesenteric nerve trunks that do contain myelinated fibres, but the intestinofugal axon itself remains unmyelinated and is carried instead by non myelinating Schwann cells.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Higher than a typical enteric neuron, for one reason: axon length.

A cell body of perhaps eight hundred square micrometres must maintain a cable tens of centimetres long, which means continuous transport of proteins, mitochondria and vesicles along microtubule tracks at rates of tens to hundreds of millimetres per day.

Long thin axons are also the first casualties of poor blood supply, which is why this class fails early in ischaemia and diabetes.

PLASTICITY:

Chronic distension, as in obstruction or megacolon, increases their firing and can leave the inhibitory loop locked on.

After spinal cord injury the descending control of the prevertebral ganglia is lost and the intestinofugal loop becomes the dominant remaining controller of that ganglion, a dramatic natural example of circuit reweighting.

In experimental colitis their numbers and chemistry shift, with more nitric oxide synthase appearing.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Inside the gut they are supported by enteric glia, which wrap the cell body and the intraganglionic axon but never make myelin.

Uniquely among the neurons in this section, part of this cell lives outside enteric glial territory: once the axon enters the mesenteric nerve it is handed over to Schwann cells, the peripheral nervous system's support cells, which arrange themselves in Remak bundles, meaning several bare axons tucked into grooves in one Schwann cell.

No other enteric neuron changes its glial partner halfway along its length, and any simulation that models glial support must therefore give this cell two support regimes.

Resident macrophages in the muscle coats complete the local environment and are required to keep enteric neurons alive.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this cell and the gut loses its long range self awareness.

Each segment would still handle its own contents perfectly well, because local reflexes are intact, but no segment would know that a distant segment was overloaded.

Food would continue to be propelled into a bowel that was already full.

The coordinated shutdown that protects an obstructed intestine, and the slowing of the stomach when the colon is loaded, would both disappear.

The prevertebral ganglia would be reduced to relay stations for spinal commands instead of true integrators, and a whole class of reflexes would vanish from the body.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This cell is mechanosensitive at the soma, meaning the cell body itself, not a specialised ending, converts mechanical stretch into electrical activity, probably through mechanically gated ion channels in the membrane.

It fires in step with the gut's own contractions, so it reports not only load but rhythm.

It performs signal transformation across an anatomical boundary, converting an intramural mechanical variable into a synaptic current in a ganglion outside the organ.

It sets up a negative feedback loop with a delay of hundreds of milliseconds, which any simulation must implement as a delay line, not an instantaneous coupling.

Because so many of them share the excitatory motor neuron chemical code, there is an open and interesting possibility that they are motor neurons that also happen to project outwards, blurring the sensory and motor categories entirely.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Circumferential wall tension in grams, zero to about ten, sensed directly at the cell body.

◦ Fast nicotinic excitatory postsynaptic potentials from myenteric interneurons, five to twenty millivolts, arriving in baskets.

◦ Slow excitatory postsynaptic potentials from substance P and enkephalin fibres, modulating gain over seconds.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Action potentials conducted out of the gut to prevertebral ganglion neurons in the coeliac, superior mesenteric and inferior mesenteric ganglia.

◦ Acetylcholine quanta producing fast excitatory postsynaptic potentials of about five to fifteen millivolts in those sympathetic neurons.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage in millivolts.

◦ Adaptation variable tracking recent firing, so that a sustained distension gives a decaying rather than a constant rate.

◦ Axonal delay buffer holding spikes in transit.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Baseline rate about zero point five spikes per second.

◦ Gain about one spike per second per gram of tension.

◦ Ceiling about twelve to fifteen spikes per second.

◦ Convergence of roughly five to fifteen intestinofugal neurons onto one sympathetic neuron.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane about twenty milliseconds.

◦ Adaptation five to thirty seconds.

◦ Axonal conduction delay two hundred to eight hundred milliseconds depending on distance.

◦ Reflex loop, from distension to inhibition of a distant segment, roughly one second.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Overactivity locks the loop on and stops the gut, which is a major component of post operative ileus, the paralysed bowel that follows abdominal surgery.

◦ Underactivity or loss, as in long standing diabetes, ageing or Chagas disease, allows one segment to keep loading another and contributes to distension and slow transit constipation.

◦ After spinal cord injury the loop runs unsupervised, producing poorly coordinated colonic behaviour.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert gut wall tension into a firing rate in an intestinofugal neuron and then decide whether the sympathetic neuron it drives reaches threshold.
Create a number variable called wall_tension with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called baseline_rate with initial value 0.5.
Create a number variable called gain with initial value 1.1.
Create a number variable called maximum_rate with initial value 12.
Create a number variable called firing_rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called ganglion_voltage with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called ganglion_threshold with initial value 12.
Create a number variable called epsp_size with initial value 8.
Create a number variable called converging_axons with initial value 6.
Create a number variable called sympathetic_spikes with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called tension_samples with initial value empty list.
For each sample in tension_samples, perform the following operations.
    Set wall_tension to sample.
    Calculate the product of gain and wall_tension.
    Calculate the sum of baseline_rate and the result.
    Set firing_rate to the result.
    Check if firing_rate is greater than maximum_rate.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing_rate to maximum_rate.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of firing_rate and converging_axons.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.001.
    Calculate the product of the result and epsp_size.
    Calculate the sum of ganglion_voltage and the result.
    Set ganglion_voltage to the result.
    Calculate the product of ganglion_voltage and 0.967.
    Set ganglion_voltage to the result.
    Check if ganglion_voltage is greater than or equal to ganglion_threshold.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of sympathetic_spikes and 1.
        Set sympathetic_spikes to the result.
        Set ganglion_voltage to 0.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display firing_rate to the console.
Display sympathetic_spikes to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because the interesting part of this cell is bookkeeping, converging spike trains and a
# threshold, and Python expresses that with no boilerplate and no external libraries.

BASELINE_HZ     = 0.5     # ongoing firing with the gut empty
GAIN_HZ_PER_G   = 1.1     # extra spikes per second per gram of wall tension
MAX_HZ          = 12.0    # ceiling observed in recordings
EPSP_MV         = 8.0     # fast nicotinic potential at the sympathetic neuron
GANGLION_TAU    = 0.030   # membrane time constant of that neuron, seconds
GANGLION_THRESH = 12.0    # millivolts above rest needed to make it fire

def encode(tension_grams):
    """Tension in the gut wall becomes a firing rate, with a floor and a ceiling."""
    return min(BASELINE_HZ + GAIN_HZ_PER_G * tension_grams, MAX_HZ)

def sympathetic_output(tension_grams, converging_axons, seconds=1.0, dt=0.001):
    rate = encode(tension_grams)
    interval = 1.0 / rate
    # stagger the converging axons so their volleys do not all land together
    next_spike = [interval * k / converging_axons for k in range(converging_axons)]
    v = 0.0
    out_spikes = 0
    for step in range(int(seconds / dt)):
        t = step * dt
        for k in range(converging_axons):
            if t >= next_spike[k]:
                v += EPSP_MV
                next_spike[k] += interval
        v -= v * (dt / GANGLION_TAU)      # the ganglion cell leaks between volleys
        if v >= GANGLION_THRESH:
            out_spikes += 1               # sympathetic inhibition is sent back to the gut
            v = 0.0
    return round(rate, 2), out_spikes

print("empty gut:     ", sympathetic_output(0.0, 6))
print("distended gut: ", sympathetic_output(6.0, 6))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Cut the mesenteric nerve, apply a retrograde tracer to the stump, and wait: the only cell bodies that light up inside the gut wall belong to this class.

No other enteric neuron sends an axon out of the organ.

Look for a myenteric cell body near the mesenteric border, wearing a dense basket of cholinergic endings, firing tonically in time with the gut's contractions and faster as the wall is stretched, and carrying choline acetyltransferase in nearly nine cases out of ten.

It is the gut's outgoing post, and the letter it sends always says the same thing: slow down.

CHAPTER 276. DOGIEL TYPE I (NON-COG)

In 1899 a Russian histologist dropped methylene blue into a strip of gut, looked down his microscope, and drew a flattened cell fringed with short leafy flaps and trailing a single long cable, and more than a century later that drawing is still the commonest neuron shape in your intestine.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Dogiel type I is a shape, not a job.

It is the first of the morphological classes named by Alexander Stanislavovich Dogiel, and it describes a nerve cell with one axon, the single output cable, and a ring of short, flattened, leaf shaped dendrites, the receiving branches, pressed close to the cell body.

Almost every working part of the gut's nerve net wears this shape: the excitatory motor neurons that contract muscle, the inhibitory motor neurons that relax it, the ascending and descending interneurons that pass messages along the tube, the secretomotor neurons that command fluid, and the intestinofugal neurons that report to ganglia outside the gut.

If the enteric nervous system, which is the gut's own brain, has a standard chassis, this is it.

Understanding Dogiel type I therefore means understanding the default enteric neuron: fast in, fast out, no long memory, one destination.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is flattened, angular or club shaped, not round, because it is squeezed into a ganglion that is itself squeezed between two moving sheets of muscle.

Around it sit roughly four to twenty short dendrites.

The classic dendrites are lamellar, meaning flat and leaf like, hugging the surfaces of neighbouring cells, which packs a large receiving area into almost no volume.

A single axon leaves one pole, often after a short unbranched stalk, and runs away along an internodal strand, the nerve cable that links one ganglion to the next.

Incoming fibres form baskets, cages of beaded endings, around the cell body and over the lamellar flaps, and it is on those flaps that most fast synapses land.

SIZE:

Long axis fifteen to thirty five micrometres, short axis ten to twenty, so the flattened profile covers roughly three hundred to eleven hundred square micrometres.

A micrometre is one thousandth of a millimetre.

Measured directly in human colon, the lamellar and stubby subtype averages eight hundred and forty six square micrometres and the filamentous subtype one thousand and fifty seven, with the simplest cells down at four hundred and twenty two.

Dendrites are short, typically ten to fifty micrometres, which is the single most useful number for a modeller: they are too short for meaningful electrical filtering, so inputs sum almost linearly at the cell body.

Axons range from under a millimetre for a local motor neuron to more than a hundred millimetres for a long descending interneuron.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The German anatomist Hans Werner Stach refined Dogiel's scheme and added types III to VII, most of which are variations on the type I plan.

Practically, four variants matter.

Lamellar cells have flat leafy dendrites and are the textbook form.

Filamentous or spiny cells have thin, thorn covered dendrites and are common in the human colon, where they account for about thirty six percent of viscerofugal cells.

Simple cells have almost no dendrites and the smallest cell bodies.

Long dendrite cells, sometimes called Stach type III, have several dendrites that leave the ganglion entirely and are found among descending interneurons.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Everywhere the enteric nervous system exists.

They fill the myenteric plexus, the net between the circular and longitudinal muscle coats, from the striated upper oesophagus to the internal anal sphincter.

They fill the submucosal plexus too, in both its human layers, the inner one long called the plexus of Meissner and the outer one associated with Schabadasch and Henle.

They occur in the gallbladder, the pancreatic ducts and the biliary tree.

Their axons cross every interface the gut has: into the circular muscle, into the longitudinal muscle, into the muscularis mucosae, across into the mucosa, onto arterioles, and in the case of intestinofugal cells out of the organ entirely.

NUMBER:

This is a shape category, so it must not be added to the budget as a separate population.

Every Dogiel type I neuron has already been counted once under the functional class it belongs to, whether motor neuron, interneuron or secretomotor neuron.

As a fraction, roughly sixty to seventy five percent of myenteric neurons and a large majority of submucosal neurons carry this morphology.

Against the working estimate of four hundred million enteric neurons in an adult human, that corresponds to on the order of two hundred and fifty to three hundred million cells, or roughly zero point three percent of the eighty six billion neurons in the whole human nervous system.

Confidence in the fraction is moderate and rests on filled cell studies in guinea pig, mouse, pig and human; confidence in the absolute number is low, because human enteric totals published since 2022 span one hundred million to six hundred million.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Every Dogiel type I neuron tested is an S neuron, where S stands for synaptic.

The defining feature is a large fast excitatory postsynaptic potential, a brief upward voltage jump caused by acetylcholine landing on nicotinic receptors, big enough on its own to trigger a spike.

Resting voltage sits near minus forty five to minus sixty millivolts, and threshold near minus thirty five.

The action potential is brief, one to three milliseconds, blocked completely by tetrodotoxin, the pufferfish poison that plugs sodium channels, and it has no calcium hump on its falling edge.

Afterwards there is only a brief afterhyperpolarisation of a few tens of milliseconds, so the cell can fire again almost immediately and can follow input trains up to thirty or forty spikes per second.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

They receive dense fast cholinergic input, plus slow excitatory postsynaptic potentials driven by tachykinins such as substance P and by serotonin, which lift excitability for tens of seconds.

They receive inhibition from noradrenaline released by sympathetic fibres and from opioid peptides released by their own neighbours, both acting mainly by reducing transmitter release at the terminals arriving on them.

Their outputs depend on their job: smooth muscle, secretory epithelium, arterioles, other enteric neurons, or sympathetic ganglion cells.

The important structural point is that one axon means one broadcast, so a Dogiel type I neuron cannot send different messages to different targets.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Unmyelinated, zero point two to one micrometre thick, conducting at roughly zero point three to one point five metres per second.

The axon is varicose along much of its length, so it releases transmitter as it passes rather than only at its tip, which lets a single descending interneuron seed an entire stretch of gut.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Heterogeneous, because the class is defined by shape.

Excitatory motor neurons use acetylcholine with substance P.

Inhibitory motor neurons use nitric oxide, a gas, together with vasoactive intestinal peptide and adenosine triphosphate or a close relative acting on purinergic receptors.

Descending interneurons use acetylcholine with nitric oxide, serotonin or somatostatin.

Secretomotor cells use acetylcholine with neuropeptide Y, or vasoactive intestinal peptide alone.

MYELINATION:

None, in any of them, anywhere in the gut.

The enteric nervous system has no myelin forming cells, so this most numerous of enteric shapes is also uniformly bare.

The consequence for a simulation is that conduction delays scale simply with distance divided by about one metre per second.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Ordinary for a small neuron, dominated by the sodium potassium pump that restores the ionic gradients after each spike.

Because these cells can fire in long high frequency bursts during a peristaltic wave, their energy use is bursty rather than steady, rising several fold for a second or two at a time.

Long axon members, particularly descending interneurons, additionally pay for continuous axonal transport over tens of millimetres.

PLASTICITY:

Slow excitatory postsynaptic potentials are the main plasticity mechanism, converting a brief input into a minute of raised excitability through protein kinase A and protein kinase C, enzymes that alter potassium channels.

After inflammation these cells become hyperexcitable and their input baskets remodel.

They can also change transmitter phenotype, with more nitric oxide synthase appearing after injury.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Enteric glia wrap the cell bodies and fill the spaces between them, and here they do something astrocytes never have to: they hold a ganglion together while it is stretched and squashed thousands of times a day by the muscle around it.

The glial sheet has no blood vessels inside the ganglion, so oxygen and glucose must diffuse in from outside, and enteric glia manage that supply.

Glia around these neurons respond to their adenosine triphosphate through P2Y1 receptors and answer with their own, spreading activity sideways.

Muscularis macrophages sit alongside and release bone morphogenetic protein 2, which these neurons need; removing macrophages damages the neurons, which is a relationship with no exact parallel in the brain.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this one shape and the gut is left with sensors and no actuators.

The large smooth Dogiel type II cells would still detect the meal, but nothing would contract, nothing would relax, nothing would secrete, and no message would travel more than one ganglion.

Peristalsis, the travelling squeeze that moves food, would be abolished outright, because both its pushing and its relaxing arms are built from Dogiel type I cells.

The region would keep its senses and lose all its behaviour.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The lamellar dendrite is itself a computational adaptation: flat, short and electrically compact, it lets dozens of synapses sum almost linearly at the soma in a cell that has no room for a dendritic tree.

Their varicose axons practise volume transmission, releasing messengers into tissue fluid over stretches of millimetres rather than at private junctions.

Nitric oxide releasing members send a gas that diffuses freely through membranes and cannot be aimed at all, so their output is a cloud, not an arrow.

Many are coupled by gap junctions to enteric glia, and some to each other, giving weak electrical sharing.

They are targets and sources of immune signalling, and they instruct the surrounding muscle's development, without which the gut's muscle layers do not mature properly.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Fast nicotinic excitatory postsynaptic potentials, five to thirty millivolts, arriving at zero to fifty hertz on lamellar dendrites.

◦ Slow excitatory postsynaptic potentials from tachykinins and serotonin, two to fifteen millivolts, lasting ten to sixty seconds.

◦ Presynaptic inhibition from noradrenaline and opioids, expressed as a multiplier between zero and one on input strength.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ A single spike train on one axon, broadcast to all of that axon's targets.

◦ Transmitter released both at terminals and from varicosities along the axon.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage in millivolts.

◦ Refractory timer in milliseconds.

◦ Slow potential pool in millivolts.

◦ Presynaptic gain between zero and one.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting voltage minus fifty five millivolts, threshold minus thirty five, reset minus sixty two.

◦ Number of dendritic input compartments, four to twenty.

◦ Conduction velocity zero point three to one point five metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane ten to twenty five milliseconds, short because the cell is small.

◦ Refractory period two to five milliseconds.

◦ Fast excitatory postsynaptic potential decay twenty to fifty milliseconds.

◦ Slow excitatory postsynaptic potential decay ten to sixty seconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Congenital absence, as in Hirschsprung disease where these cells never migrate into the distal bowel, leaves a segment that cannot relax and causes obstruction in newborns.

◦ Destruction by the parasite of Chagas disease produces megacolon and megaoesophagus.

◦ Loss of the nitric oxide releasing members produces achalasia and gastroparesis, in which sphincters and stomach will not let go.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to sum the inputs arriving on the lamellar dendrites of a Dogiel type I neuron and decide whether it fires on its single axon.
Create a number variable called membrane_voltage with initial value -55.
Create a number variable called resting_voltage with initial value -55.
Create a number variable called threshold with initial value -35.
Create a number variable called reset_voltage with initial value -62.
Create a number variable called refractory_timer with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called dendritic_sum with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called presynaptic_gain with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called spike_count with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called dendrite_inputs with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 2000 times.
    Set dendritic_sum to 0.
    For each dendrite_value in dendrite_inputs, perform the following operations.
        Calculate the product of dendrite_value and presynaptic_gain.
        Calculate the sum of dendritic_sum and the result.
        Set dendritic_sum to the result.
    End the loop.
    Check if refractory_timer is greater than 0.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the difference of refractory_timer and 1.
        Set refractory_timer to the result.
        Skip to the next iteration of the loop.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of resting_voltage and membrane_voltage.
    Calculate the sum of the result and dendritic_sum.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.04.
    Calculate the sum of membrane_voltage and the result.
    Set membrane_voltage to the result.
    Check if membrane_voltage is greater than or equal to threshold.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of spike_count and 1.
        Set spike_count to the result.
        Set membrane_voltage to reset_voltage.
        Set refractory_timer to 6.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display spike_count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C is chosen because a whole gut simulation must hold hundreds of millions of these cells at once, and a
   small packed struct stepped by one tight function is the cheapest honest way to store and advance them. */
#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    float v;           /* membrane potential, millivolts */
    float refractory;  /* seconds of refractory period remaining */
} DogielI;

#define V_REST     (-55.0f)
#define V_THRESH   (-35.0f)
#define V_RESET    (-62.0f)
#define TAU_M        0.012f   /* seconds, short because the cell body is tiny */
#define REFRACTORY   0.003f   /* seconds, and no slow afterhyperpolarisation follows */
#define DT           0.0005f  /* seconds */

/* Each lamellar dendrite is one input compartment, and they sum almost linearly. */
static int step_cell(DogielI *c, const float *dendrite_mv, int n_dendrites, float gain)
{
    float sum = 0.0f;
    for (int d = 0; d < n_dendrites; d++)
        sum += dendrite_mv[d] * gain;          /* gain is the presynaptic brake, 0 to 1 */
    if (c->refractory > 0.0f) {
        c->refractory -= DT;
        return 0;
    }
    c->v += ((V_REST - c->v) + sum) * (DT / TAU_M);
    if (c->v >= V_THRESH) {
        c->v = V_RESET;
        c->refractory = REFRACTORY;
        return 1;                              /* one spike leaves on the single axon */
    }
    return 0;
}

int main(void)
{
    float dendrites[6] = { 4.0f, 3.0f, 5.0f, 2.0f, 4.0f, 3.0f };   /* millivolts each */
    for (int trial = 0; trial < 2; trial++) {
        DogielI cell = { V_REST, 0.0f };
        float gain = (trial == 0) ? 1.0f : 0.4f;   /* second trial adds sympathetic inhibition */
        int spikes = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < 2000; i++)             /* one second of simulated time */
            spikes += step_cell(&cell, dendrites, 6, gain);
        printf("gain %.1f  spikes in 1 s: %d\n", gain, spikes);
    }
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Fill the cell with dye and count the processes: exactly one is long and smooth and leaves the ganglion, and all the rest are short flattened flaps that do not.

Put an electrode in it and stimulate the nerve strand running into the ganglion, and you get a large fast excitatory postsynaptic potential that is abolished by hexamethonium, a drug that blocks nicotinic receptors, followed by an action potential that vanishes in tetrodotoxin and is over in three milliseconds with no long silence afterwards.

That combination, one axon, leafy dendrites, big nicotinic input and no slow afterhyperpolarisation, identifies the working majority of the gut's nervous system.

CHAPTER 277. DOGIEL TYPE II (NON-COG)

Make this cell fire once and it falls silent for the next several seconds, a sulk so long, so deep and so reliable that physiologists named an entire class of neurons after it.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Dogiel type II is the second shape drawn by Alexander Stanislavovich Dogiel in 1899, and it is the odd one out in the gut.

Where the standard enteric neuron is small, flattened and has one output cable, this one is large, smooth and oval, and it sends out several long processes, each of which behaves like an axon.

It has, in effect, no dendrites at all.

Most of these cells are intrinsic primary afferent neurons, meaning the gut's own sensory cells: they sample what is happening in the lining and in the wall and start the reflexes that follow, without asking the brain.

Some of their processes end just beneath the epithelium, the gut's inner skin, where they listen for serotonin released by enterochromaffin cells, the chemical sentinels that taste the gut contents.

Others are distorted directly when the wall is stretched.

When enough of these cells agree that something has arrived, the gut squeezes behind it, relaxes in front of it, and secretes fluid onto it.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is large, round or oval, and smooth, with no leafy flaps.

From it emerge two to ten long processes, typically three to seven, each thick, unbranched near the soma, and each capable of carrying an action potential.

Some processes run around the gut circumference within the myenteric plexus, contacting other neurons on the way.

Others turn inward, cross the muscle and the muscularis mucosae, and end as fine terminals in the lamina propria beneath the epithelium.

Because more than one process is axon like, this cell can send a message in several directions at once, which no Dogiel type I neuron can do.

Their smooth surface is almost completely covered by glia, leaving few bare patches, and they receive far fewer fast synapses than their neighbours.

SIZE:

These are the largest neurons in the gut wall.

Cell bodies commonly measure twenty to fifty micrometres across, and in human colon the rare Dogiel type II cells that also project outside the gut have profile areas averaging two thousand and six square micrometres, against an average of eight hundred and seventy eight for all shapes together.

A micrometre is one thousandth of a millimetre, so fifty of these cell bodies in a row still make up only two and a half millimetres.

Their processes are long, often one to several millimetres inside the plexus, and about zero point five to one point five micrometres thick.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Three practical variants exist.

Myenteric Dogiel type II cells, in the net between the muscle layers, are the classic intrinsic primary afferent neurons and are the largest.

Submucosal Dogiel type II cells, in the layer under the lining, are somewhat smaller and make up roughly one in ten submucosal neurons in the guinea pig small intestine.

A small third group projects out of the gut to prevertebral ganglia, accounting for about three percent of human colonic viscerofugal cells, showing that the shape is not reserved exclusively for sensory work.

Species matters here: they are abundant and easy to find in guinea pig, harder to find in mouse, and still debated in humans, where cells with the shape and the matching chemistry have been identified in the small intestine but not counted with confidence.

WHERE IT LIVES:

They live in the myenteric plexus, between the circular and longitudinal muscle coats, and in both layers of the human submucosal plexus, the inner one long called the plexus of Meissner and the outer one associated with Schabadasch and Henle.

They are most numerous in the small intestine and colon, sparse in the stomach, and effectively absent from the oesophagus.

Their defining interface is the mucosal one: processes leave the plexus, pass through the muscularis mucosae, and terminate in the lamina propria a few micrometres beneath the epithelial cells, close enough for serotonin released into that space to reach them.

NUMBER:

Like Dogiel type I, this is a shape category and must not be added to the budget twice.

Every cell with this shape has already been counted as an intrinsic primary afferent neuron or, rarely, as an intestinofugal neuron.

As a fraction, they are commonly put at roughly a quarter of myenteric neurons in the guinea pig small intestine and about eleven percent of submucosal neurons there, with lower fractions reported in mouse and human.

Applied cautiously to the working estimate of four hundred million human enteric neurons, that suggests somewhere between thirty and sixty million cells carry this morphology, or roughly zero point zero four to zero point zero seven percent of the eighty six billion neurons in the whole nervous system.

Confidence is low, and the honest statement is that no reliable human count exists; the guinea pig fractions are firm, the human ones are not.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

This is the AH neuron, and the letters stand for afterhyperpolarisation, the long silent dip that follows its action potential.

The action potential itself is broad, two to four milliseconds, with a distinct hump on its falling edge produced by calcium flowing in, and that hump survives tetrodotoxin, the pufferfish poison that blocks sodium channels, so the spike is partly a calcium spike.

The calcium that enters then opens intermediate conductance calcium activated potassium channels, known as IK or KCa3.1, which are insensitive to apamin and do not care about voltage.

The result is a hyperpolarisation lasting seconds.

In pig myenteric neurons two populations were measured: one with a medium afterhyperpolarisation averaging seven hundred and seventy three milliseconds, plus or minus seven hundred and fifty three, and one with a slow afterhyperpolarisation averaging four thousand two hundred and five milliseconds, plus or minus one thousand four hundred and eighty three.

These channels have been confirmed in human enteric neurons.

Resting voltage is deep, often minus sixty to minus seventy millivolts, and the cells are usually silent until recruited.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

They receive very few fast excitatory inputs, which is unusual and diagnostic.

What they receive instead are slow excitatory postsynaptic potentials, lasting tens of seconds, which work by closing the very potassium channels that produce the afterhyperpolarisation, converting a reluctant cell into an eager one.

Crucially they synapse on each other, so a group of these neurons forms a self exciting assembly: once enough of them are recruited, the assembly holds itself active long after the original stimulus has gone.

Their outputs go to ascending and descending interneurons, to excitatory and inhibitory muscle motor neurons, to secretomotor neurons, and back onto other Dogiel type II cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Multiple axon like processes, unmyelinated, roughly zero point five to one point five micrometres thick, conducting at about zero point five to one metre per second.

Because several processes leave the same soma, an action potential generated anywhere propagates into all of them, including back into the cell body, so the cell has no single input end and no single output end.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine is the main fast transmitter, made by choline acetyltransferase.

Tachykinins, principally substance P, provide the slow excitatory signal onto their targets and onto each other.

Between eighty two and eighty four percent of guinea pig ileal Dogiel type II cells contain calbindin, a calcium binding protein, which is the standard marker used to find them.

Many also carry calcitonin gene related peptide, and some carry neuromedin U.

MYELINATION:

None.

No enteric neuron is myelinated, because the gut wall contains no myelin forming cells.

For this cell that matters less than usual, since its calcium loaded, slow spiking style is not built for speed in the first place.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Distinctly higher than its neighbours, for a specific reason: every action potential admits a slug of calcium that must then be pumped back out or buried in internal stores, and calcium handling is expensive.

The large cell body, the several long processes and the heavy load of calbindin all add to the bill.

This is a plausible part of why these cells are among the first to be lost in ageing, diabetes and ischaemia.

PLASTICITY:

The afterhyperpolarisation is not fixed; it is a dial.

Protein kinase A, an enzyme switched on by cyclic adenosine monophosphate, reduces the afterhyperpolarisation current and can turn a cell that fires once every ten seconds into one that fires steadily.

After inflammation of the intestine these cells become persistently hyperexcitable, with smaller afterhyperpolarisations, more input resistance and a lower threshold, and the change outlasts the inflammation by weeks.

This is one of the leading candidate mechanisms for the exaggerated reflexes and discomfort of post infectious irritable bowel syndrome.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Enteric glia wrap these smooth cell bodies more completely than any other enteric neuron, leaving very little membrane exposed, which is consistent with their scarcity of fast synapses and means the glial sheet is physically interposed between them and the outside world.

Those glia are not passive: they release adenosine triphosphate and hydrogen sulfide that alter the excitability of exactly these cells, and they take up and recycle the calcium and potassium that a broad calcium spike dumps into the narrow space around the soma.

Glia also accompany the mucosal processes right up to the epithelium, sitting between the nerve ending and the serotonin releasing cell it listens to.

Resident macrophages complete the environment and are required for enteric neuron survival.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take this cell away and the gut goes numb to itself.

Muscle and glands would still work if commanded, and the brain could still order movement through the vagus nerve, but the gut would no longer notice its own contents.

A meal arriving in a segment would produce no local reflex: no squeeze behind it, no relaxation ahead of it, no wash of fluid onto it.

Peristalsis triggered from inside the gut would be abolished, which is exactly what happens when the mucosa is stripped or these cells are blocked in an isolated piece of intestine.

The region would keep every one of its abilities and lose its awareness.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Almost everything interesting about this cell happens outside the standard spike model.

The afterhyperpolarisation is a genuine one cell memory: it stores how recently the cell fired, in a chemical variable, calcium, on a timescale of seconds, and it can be rewritten by neuromodulators.

Its action potential back propagates into every one of its processes, so firing is a whole cell event, not a directed output.

It engages in recurrent self excitation through slow potentials, giving the network a working memory measured in tens of seconds.

It performs sensory transduction without a specialised receptor organ, using its own bare process membrane.

It is a target of immune and microbial signalling, since bacterial products and mast cell mediators alter its excitability directly.

And it takes part in volume transmission, releasing tachykinins that spread through the ganglion rather than into private junctions.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Serotonin arriving by diffusion from enterochromaffin cells, roughly zero to one hundred nanomolar, acting on 5-HT3 receptors on mucosal processes.

◦ Mechanical distortion of the processes and soma, expressed as an equivalent depolarising current of zero to forty picoamperes.

◦ Slow excitatory postsynaptic potentials from tachykinins, which reduce the afterhyperpolarisation conductance by up to eighty percent for tens of seconds.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Action potentials propagating into all processes simultaneously.

◦ Acetylcholine and substance P released onto interneurons, motor neurons, secretomotor neurons and other Dogiel type II cells.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage in millivolts.

◦ Internal calcium concentration in arbitrary units, incremented by each spike.

◦ Afterhyperpolarisation conductance, a function of that calcium.

◦ Modulation factor between zero and one, set by protein kinase A activity.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting voltage minus sixty two millivolts, threshold minus thirty eight millivolts.

◦ Calcium added per spike, one unit.

◦ Potassium reversal potential minus ninety millivolts.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane about fifteen milliseconds.

◦ Action potential width two to four milliseconds.

◦ Medium afterhyperpolarisation about zero point eight seconds.

◦ Slow afterhyperpolarisation two to twenty seconds, averaging about four seconds in pig.

◦ Slow excitatory postsynaptic potential ten to sixty seconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Hyperexcitability after infection or inflammation, through loss of the afterhyperpolarisation, produces exaggerated secretory and motor reflexes and is a candidate mechanism of post infectious irritable bowel syndrome.

◦ Loss of these cells, in ageing, diabetes, ischaemia or Chagas disease, leaves a gut that cannot start its own reflexes and empties poorly.

◦ Blocking the intermediate conductance potassium channel removes the brake entirely and produces continuous firing.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate the calcium controlled slow afterhyperpolarisation that sets the firing rhythm of a Dogiel type II sensory neuron.
Create a number variable called membrane_voltage with initial value -62.
Create a number variable called resting_voltage with initial value -62.
Create a number variable called threshold with initial value -38.
Create a number variable called internal_calcium with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called potassium_reversal with initial value -90.
Create a number variable called sensory_drive with initial value 30.
Create a number variable called modulation with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called ahp_pull with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called open_fraction with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called spike_times with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 30000 times.
    Calculate the sum of internal_calcium and 1.
    Calculate the quotient of internal_calcium divided by the result.
    Set open_fraction to the result.
    Calculate the difference of membrane_voltage and potassium_reversal.
    Calculate the product of open_fraction and the result.
    Calculate the product of the result and modulation.
    Set ahp_pull to the result.
    Calculate the difference of resting_voltage and membrane_voltage.
    Calculate the sum of the result and sensory_drive.
    Calculate the difference of the result and ahp_pull.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.0667.
    Calculate the sum of membrane_voltage and the result.
    Set membrane_voltage to the result.
    Calculate the product of internal_calcium and 0.99967.
    Set internal_calcium to the result.
    Check if membrane_voltage is greater than or equal to threshold.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add clock to the end of spike_times.
        Calculate the sum of internal_calcium and 1.
        Set internal_calcium to the result.
        Set membrane_voltage to -67.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of clock and 0.001.
    Set clock to the result.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in spike_times.
Display the result to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because the whole story of this cell is two interacting variables, voltage and calcium,
# and Python lets those two lines of physics sit side by side where a reader can see them.

DT           = 0.001    # seconds
V_REST       = -62.0    # millivolts, these cells sit deep and quiet
V_THRESH     = -38.0    # millivolts
TAU_M        = 0.015    # membrane time constant, seconds
CA_PER_SPIKE = 1.0      # calcium admitted by the shoulder of the action potential
CA_HALF      = 1.0      # calcium giving half maximal channel opening
TAU_CA       = 3.0      # seconds, calcium clearance sets the length of the silence
G_AHP        = 1.0      # strength of the KCa3.1 conductance
E_K          = -90.0    # potassium reversal potential, millivolts

def ah_neuron(sensory_drive_mv, modulation=1.0, seconds=30.0):
    """modulation below 1.0 mimics protein kinase A shrinking the afterhyperpolarisation."""
    v, ca, spikes = V_REST, 0.0, []
    for step in range(int(seconds / DT)):
        open_fraction = ca / (ca + CA_HALF)          # KCa3.1 channels track calcium
        ahp_pull = G_AHP * modulation * open_fraction * (v - E_K)
        v += ((V_REST - v) + sensory_drive_mv - ahp_pull) * (DT / TAU_M)
        ca -= ca * (DT / TAU_CA)                     # calcium is pumped away slowly
        if v >= V_THRESH:
            spikes.append(round(step * DT, 3))
            ca += CA_PER_SPIKE                       # tetrodotoxin resistant calcium shoulder
            v = V_REST - 5.0
    return spikes

healthy = ah_neuron(30.0)
inflamed = ah_neuron(30.0, modulation=0.25)          # inflammation blunts the brake
print("healthy spikes in 30 s:", len(healthy), "first few at", healthy[:4])
print("inflamed spikes in 30 s:", len(inflamed), "first few at", inflamed[:4])

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Look for the biggest, smoothest, roundest cell body in the ganglion, with several long processes and not one leafy flap.

Stain it for calbindin and, in guinea pig ileum, more than four cells in five will light up.

Then put an electrode in it: the action potential is broad, it has a hump on the way down, the hump survives tetrodotoxin, and afterwards the cell sits hyperpolarised and unresponsive for seconds while everything around it carries on firing.

Nothing else in the human body combines that shape with that silence.

CHAPTER 278. DOGIEL TYPE III (NON-COG)

In 1899 a Russian anatomist dyed a scrap of gut blue and found a nerve cell shaped like a sea anemone, ten long arms and not one leaving home.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This relay cell of the enteric nervous system (ENS), the nerve net in the gut wall that runs digestion without the brain, was named by Alexander Stanislavovich Dogiel in 1899 by shape alone.

It has one axon, the output fibre, and up to ten dendrites, the input branches, all long, branched, tapering and ending inside its own ganglion, a bead of nerve cell bodies; it is an interneuron, speaking only to other neurons.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is smooth and oval with dendrites leaving radially like spokes, the formal test against the human type V neuron, whose dendrites and axon share one stem.

SIZE:

Human type III cell bodies are unmeasured, so they are bracketed by the measured human types, type II at 904 plus or minus 210 square micrometres and type V at 449 plus or minus 110, giving fifteen to thirty micrometres wide.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Pig type III cells make nitric oxide and human ones do not, and within human tissue some carry calbindin and some calretinin, two calcium-binding marker proteins.

WHERE IT LIVES:

They sit in the myenteric plexus, also called Auerbach's plexus, the sheet between the outer longitudinal and inner circular muscle coats, and only in human duodenum, jejunum and ileum, not stomach, colon or submucosal plexus.

The cell straddles an interface, dendrites inside the ganglion and axon crossing into the interganglionic strand.

NUMBER:

Nobody has counted this type, so within this section's budget of about 400 million enteric neurons a defensible range is two to eight million, half a percent to two percent of the enteric nervous system, at low confidence given a 2022 recount by Michel and colleagues nearer 168 million.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Enteric cells are either AH cells, which slump for seconds after a spike, or S cells, which fire when driven, and this presumed S cell should rest near minus 58 to minus 76 millivolts with fast synaptic blips of 3.5 to 16.5 millivolts.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The long dendrites carry fast nicotinic synapses from the gut's own sensory cells and from neighbouring interneurons, and the axon drives the motor neurons that move muscle.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

One axon and only one, bare, under two micrometres wide, conducting at 0.1 to 0.5 metres per second and ending in varicosities, beads of transmitter strung along the fibre.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Human type III cells make no nitric oxide and are mostly cholinergic, releasing acetylcholine and staining for choline acetyltransferase (ChAT), the enzyme that builds it.

MYELINATION:

None, a rule of the whole organ, because the gut wall holds no oligodendrocytes and no myelinating cells inside its ganglia, so every enteric signal is slow.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest, since the cell is small and runs none of the seconds-long potassium currents of the sensory class, and enteric ganglia hold no capillaries, so fuel diffuses in from surrounding vessels.

PLASTICITY:

Human myenteric numbers fall with age and inflamed survivors switch peptides rather than die, so the likeliest change here is silent movement of synapses along the long dendritic tree.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Enteric glia wrap ganglionic neurons far more tightly than a brain astrocyte does, carrying glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP), the protein S100B and the factor Sox10 where up to eighty percent of glia just outside carry none, and their S100B sets gut motor circuit excitability.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this cell and the ganglion becomes a switchboard of private conversations instead of a room where everyone hears everyone, because every other relay hears only synapses at its own doorstep.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The tapering dendrite is a filter, not a wire, so a synapse two hundred micrometres out delivers a fraction of the voltage it would at the cell body: this neuron computes with distance.

It also receives slow synaptic events lasting tens of seconds, spills transmitter into shared ganglionic fluid by volume transmission, and is tuned by enteric glial calcium waves and by nitric oxide from neighbours, a gas needing no synapse.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: fast nicotinic events of 3.5 to 16.5 millivolts tagged with dendritic distance 0 to 300 micrometres, plus slow events as a gain of 1.0 to 2.0.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine onto excitatory and inhibitory motor neurons and other interneurons, as spike times at 0 to 30 hertz.

• STATE: membrane voltage, per-synapse distance, slow excitability gain, refractory countdown.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 58 to minus 76 millivolts, threshold minus 40 millivolts, length constant 200 to 300 micrometres, dendrite count 3 to 10.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 20 milliseconds, fast event 7 to 52 milliseconds, slow event 10 to 300 seconds, axonal delay 2 to 300 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: losing cholinergic myenteric interneurons breaks the reflex chain, giving achalasia, chronic intestinal pseudo-obstruction and slow transit constipation.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether a Dogiel type three interneuron relays a signal, weakening each input by its distance out along the long dendrite.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value -65.
Create a number variable called index with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called weight with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called strengths with initial value empty list.
Create a list variable called distances with initial value empty list.
While index is less than the total number of items in strengths remains true, repeat the following steps.
    Set weight to the difference of 1 and the quotient of the item at position index in distances divided by 250.
    If weight is less than 0 is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set weight to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set voltage to the sum of voltage and the product of the item at position index in strengths and weight.
    Set index to the sum of index and 1.
End the loop.
Display voltage to the console.
Return voltage to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python was chosen because the model is a few lines of arithmetic a novice can read as English.

import math

REST_MV, THRESH_MV, LAMBDA_UM = -65.0, -40.0, 250.0

def relay(inputs):
    """inputs: (strength in mV, distance from the cell body in micrometres)."""
    v = REST_MV
    for strength_mv, distance_um in inputs:
        # Real dendrites decay exponentially; the ERC used a linear stand-in.
        v += strength_mv * math.exp(-distance_um / LAMBDA_UM)
    return v, v >= THRESH_MV

for name, pat in (("near", [(6.0, 20.0)] * 5), ("far", [(6.0, 300.0)] * 5)):
    print("{:>4}: {:6.2f} mV fired={}".format(name, *relay(pat)))

# near: -37.31 mV fired=True
#  far: -55.96 mV fired=False

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

One axon plus up to ten long, branched, tapering dendrites all stopping inside the home ganglion, which rules out type I with its short leafy plates, type II whose long processes are all axons that leave, and type V whose processes share one stem.

CHAPTER 279. FILAMENTOUS NEURONS (NON-COG)

Most gut neurons look like little trees; this one looks like a dandelion clock, a round body with fourteen hair-thin filaments sticking out in every direction.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This cell of the enteric nervous system (ENS), the nerve net in the gut wall, is named for its dendrites, the input branches, which are thin, straight and never flattened into the leaf-like plates other gut neurons carry.

Dye-filling guinea pig small intestine, Simon Brookes and colleagues found filamentous cells averaged fourteen fine processes up to fifty micrometres long and made up seven percent of the sample.

Many send their axon out of the gut entirely, and these intestinofugal neurons, from the Latin for fleeing the intestine, tell the sympathetic nervous system how loaded a loop of bowel is.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The filaments leave the small compact cell body as a starburst and never taper to a point, the fine distinction from the long-dendritic type III cell, and one dye-filled human colonic example had an axon that forked in two.

SIZE:

Each filament reaches about fifty micrometres, so the input field is a sphere roughly one hundred micrometres across, and the filaments are under one micrometre thick, at the resolving limit of a light microscope.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Filamentous intestinofugal neurons, whose axon leaves the gut for a prevertebral sympathetic ganglion.

• Filamentous interneurons, whose axon stays inside the plexus.

• The stout human colonic variant, with thicker branching dendrites and a forking axon.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The cell bodies sit in the myenteric plexus, also called Auerbach's plexus, between the two muscle coats of small intestine and colon, and some in the submucosal plexus, also called Meissner's plexus.

The defining interface: intestinofugal axons leave the gut wall through the mesenteric nerves to end in the coeliac, superior mesenteric and inferior mesenteric ganglia in front of the spine.

NUMBER:

If the guinea pig seven percent carries over, filamentous neurons would number about twenty-eight million within this section's budget of roughly 400 million enteric neurons, but human sampling is thin, so the range is four to twenty-eight million, best figure twelve million or three percent, at low to moderate confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These are S neurons, firing when driven with none of the seconds-long slump of the sensory AH class, resting between minus 58 and minus 76 millivolts, and the intestinofugal members fire tonically, meaning steadily, at a rate climbing with pressure inside the bowel.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The fourteen short filaments deliver roughly equal weight, making this a fair-minded counter rather than a distance-weighted computer, and intestinofugal output excites sympathetic neurons that send noradrenaline back to slow the gut.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

One unmyelinated axon, often forking soon after it leaves, running at 0.1 to 0.5 metres per second, and in intestinofugal members crossing several centimetres of mesenteric nerve, so an ileal signal can take a full second to arrive.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine is primary and these cells are reliably immunoreactive for choline acetyltransferase (ChAT), the enzyme that builds it, while many also store peptides such as vasoactive intestinal peptide (VIP) and enkephalin, and none makes nitric oxide.

MYELINATION:

None, which matters because part of the axon leaves the gut, where a mesenteric nerve carries it in a Remak bundle, a trough in a non-myelinating Schwann cell holding many bare axons, so the fibre is escorted but never insulated.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low for the small cell body, which lives on diffusion because enteric ganglia hold no capillaries, but high for the intestinofugal axon, which keeps centimetres of bare membrane charged during tonic firing.

PLASTICITY:

Chronic distension and partial obstruction change the activity and chemistry of these neurons, fitting for a cell whose job is to report load, so the peptide cargo should be modelled as adjustable.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Enteric glia sheathe the cell body and all fourteen filaments, carrying glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP), the protein S100B and the factor Sox10, but up to eighty percent of glia outside ganglia lack those markers and the mesenteric custodian is a Schwann cell, so no other enteric neuron passes between three glial regimes.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this class and the gut loses its outbound voice, so the bowel becomes a set of independent segments acting on local news instead of an organ that coordinates over half a metre without troubling the brain.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This cell is an integrator in the engineering sense, summing distension over seconds and holding a rate that encodes accumulated load rather than single events.

Its peptide release from varicosities is volume transmission, lifting prevertebral excitability for tens of seconds inside a ganglion that also receives spinal input, making that ganglion a site of peripheral integration.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: fast nicotinic events, 3.5 to 16.5 millivolts, on up to 14 filaments of equal weight; distension as pressure, 0 to 40 millimetres of mercury.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine plus peptides onto the coeliac, superior mesenteric and inferior mesenteric ganglia, at 0.5 to 20 hertz.

• STATE: count of active filaments, running pressure estimate, voltage, tonic output rate.

• PARAMETERS: filament count 14 over a range of 8 to 20, filament length up to 50 micrometres, rest minus 58 to minus 76 millivolts, extramural axon 2 to 15 centimetres.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 20 milliseconds, fast event 7 to 52 milliseconds, pressure integration 1 to 10 seconds, extramural delay 40 to 1500 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: losing this signal abolishes the reflex by which a distended loop inhibits its neighbours, and in Hirschsprung's disease no such cell exists in the affected bowel.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert gut wall stretch, sampled by fourteen thin filaments, into the tonic rate an intestinofugal neuron sends out of the bowel to a sympathetic ganglion.
Create a number variable called active with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called pressure with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called rate with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called reports with initial value empty list.
For each report in reports, perform the following operations.
    If report is greater than 0 is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set active to the sum of active and 1.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
If active is greater than 14 is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set active to 14.
End the conditional block.
Set rate to the quotient of active divided by 14.
Set rate to the product of rate and the quotient of pressure divided by the sum of pressure and 12.
Set rate to the product of rate and 19.5.
Set rate to the sum of rate and 0.5.
Display rate to the console.
Return rate to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python was chosen because the model is pure arithmetic with no libraries, so the reader can run it unchanged.

FILAMENTS = 14         # measured average number of fine processes
REST_HZ = 0.5          # model parameter: background chatter
MAX_HZ = 20.0          # model parameter: ceiling rate
HALF_MMHG = 12.0       # model parameter: pressure giving half drive

def rate(pressure_mmhg, active_filaments):
    """Tonic rate sent out of the gut to a prevertebral ganglion."""
    active = max(0, min(active_filaments, FILAMENTS))  # cannot exceed 14
    # Saturating: doubling a small load matters, doubling a big one does not.
    saturating = pressure_mmhg / (pressure_mmhg + HALF_MMHG)
    return REST_HZ + (MAX_HZ - REST_HZ) * (active / FILAMENTS) * saturating

for mmhg, active in ((0, 0), (6, 4), (12, 9), (30, 14)):
    print("{:>2} mmHg {:>2}/14 -> {:5.2f} Hz".format(mmhg, active, rate(mmhg, active)))

#  0 mmHg  0/14 ->  0.50 Hz
#  6 mmHg  4/14 ->  2.36 Hz
# 12 mmHg  9/14 ->  6.77 Hz
# 30 mmHg 14/14 -> 14.43 Hz

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

About fourteen hair-thin filaments radiating like a dandelion clock, none flattened and none tapering, which rules out types I and III, while the single axon rules out type II, and its intestinofugal members carry the gut's own signal out to the sympathetic ganglia in front of the spine.

CHAPTER 280. DENDRITIC TYPE II NEURONS (Many-Axon Tufted Gut Neuron) (NON-COG)

This is the cell that lets your intestine feel itself, and this version of it grew a beard.

Type II is a numbered slot in a hundred-year-old catalogue and teaches nothing, so the number goes and the two verified structural facts become the name.

Many-Axon names what defines the type II family, several processes that all look like axons and all conduct action potentials, rather than one axon and many receiving branches.

Tufted names the feature that separates this variant from the plain adendritic form, namely the extra true dendrites it carries alongside those axons.

Gut Neuron fixes it to the intestinal wall, and the name stops there on purpose, because although a sensory role has been proposed by analogy with guinea pig work, these cells are only occasionally found in the human small intestinal myenteric plexus and their human function remains unknown.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The Dogiel type II neuron is the sensing cell of the enteric nervous system (ENS), the nerve net in the gut wall that runs digestion without the brain, and it is an intrinsic primary afferent neuron (IPAN), a sensory cell sitting inside the organ it senses.

Its long processes are all axons, meaning output fibres, so the classic form has no true dendrites, but the dendritic variant, found by Hans-Joachim Stach in pig and later in human small intestine, adds short dendrites on the cell body and projects centimetres anally.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The largest cell body in the plexus, smooth and oval, with several long axons leaving at widely spaced points, a beard of short dendrites, and one axon crossing the circular muscle to end beneath the gut lining.

SIZE:
