Human Dogiel type II cell bodies average 904 plus or minus 210 square micrometres in cross-section, twice the 449 plus or minus 110 of the human type V neuron, which as a circle gives a diameter of about 34 micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• The beard: non-dendritic type II cells, the common form, versus dendritic type II cells with short somatic dendrites.

• Projection: circumferential in the majority, versus the long anal projection of the dendritic variant.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The cell body sits in the myenteric plexus, also called Auerbach's plexus, between the two muscle coats, reported for this variant in human duodenum, jejunum and ileum, while type II cells generally also occupy the submucosal plexus, also called Meissner's plexus.

The critical interface: one process crosses the circular muscle and submucosa to end in the lamina propria, the tissue under the cell layer lining the bowel, so the neuron spans the whole gut wall.

NUMBER:

Type II cells are about ten percent of human small intestinal myenteric neurons and under one percent in the stomach, and this variant is only occasionally identified, giving perhaps one to four million cells within this section's budget of about 400 million, a quarter of one percent to one percent of the enteric nervous system, at low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Type II cells are the classic AH neurons, whose broad 2.9 millisecond action potential is humped on its falling phase by calcium that opens calcium-activated potassium channels, producing a slow slump below the minus 58 to minus 76 millivolt resting level that averages seven seconds, ranges from two to ten, and forbids firing throughout.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

They synapse onto one another in self-reinforcing assemblies, their mucosal endings are excited by serotonin from enterochromaffin cells in the lining, and a slow synaptic event shuts the slump down and turns a cell firing once every seven seconds into one firing steadily.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Several axons, typically three to ten, all unmyelinated, which is the clearest structural break from every other enteric class, one probing the mucosa, others circumferential, and in this variant at least one running centimetres anally at 0.1 to 0.5 metres per second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine is the main transmitter, carried with choline acetyltransferase (ChAT) in nearly all type II cells, alongside tachykinins such as substance P, and over eighty percent of guinea pig type II cells contain the marker protein calbindin, while this variant makes no nitric oxide.

MYELINATION:

None on any process, because the gut wall has no oligodendrocytes and no myelinating cells inside its ganglia, a real cost for a sensory cell and one reason urgent pain is delegated to myelinated spinal fibres outside the gut.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The highest of the three types here, since the largest cell body in the plexus feeds several long bare axons, every spike admits calcium that must be pumped out, and the long slump pumps potassium back with no capillary inside the ganglion to help.

PLASTICITY:

After gut infection these sensory cells become hyperexcitable as the slump shrinks or vanishes, a change that outlasts the inflammation and is a leading account of post-infectious irritable bowel syndrome, driven partly by mediators from resident mast cells.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Enteric glia wrap this cell more completely than any other in the plexus, because a large body with a seconds-long potassium efflux floods a tiny space with no capillary to drain it, and those glia answer its firing with calcium waves and release adenosine triphosphate (ATP), which acts back on neurons as a transmitter.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the intestine goes numb to itself: muscle would still contract, driven by pacemaker cells and motor neurons, but nothing would know that food had arrived, so peristalsis becomes a blind fixed rhythm instead of a response.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The slow slump is an analogue memory whose depth encodes how recently and how often the cell fired, while a slow synaptic event is a longer memory in which a peptide activates protein kinase C and closes those same potassium channels for minutes.

These cells are coupled into assemblies that excite one another, so the functional unit is a group, and they use volume transmission, receive nitric oxide from neighbours, drive glial calcium waves and are sensitised by mast cell mediators.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: serotonin at mucosal endings, 0.1 to 10 micromolar; stretch as pressure, 0 to 40 millimetres of mercury; slow tachykinin events as a 0.1 to 1.0 multiplier on slump conductance.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine and tachykinins onto interneurons, motor neurons and other type II cells, at 0.1 to 20 hertz.

• STATE: voltage, slump conductance, intracellular calcium, slow-input gain, delay buffer for the anal axon.

• PARAMETERS: cell body 904 plus or minus 210 square micrometres, axon count 3 to 10, rest minus 58 to minus 76, threshold minus 40, reset minus 75 millivolts.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 20 milliseconds, spike half-width 2.9 milliseconds, slump about 7 seconds over a 2 to 10 range, slow event 10 to 300 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: gut infection leaves the cell hyperexcitable; losing it removes the peristaltic trigger, contributing to chronic intestinal pseudo-obstruction; in Parkinson's disease it accumulates misfolded alpha-synuclein years before tremor.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to run one millisecond of a dendritic Dogiel type two sensory neuron, in which a slow afterhyperpolarisation silences the cell for seconds unless a slow excitatory input releases that brake.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value -65.
Create a number variable called brake with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called gain with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called change with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called slow_input with initial value false.
Create a boolean variable called spike with initial value false.
If slow_input is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set gain to 0.15.
End the conditional block.
Set change to the difference of 30 and the product of brake and gain.
Set change to the sum of change and the difference of -65 and voltage.
Set voltage to the sum of voltage and the quotient of change divided by 20.
Set brake to the difference of brake and the quotient of brake divided by 7000.
If voltage is greater than or equal to -40 is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set spike to true.
    Set voltage to -75.
    Set brake to the sum of brake and 12.
End the conditional block.
Return spike to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python was chosen because the seven-second slump only shows itself over twenty thousand time steps, which Python runs with no libraries.

DT_MS, TAU_M_MS, TAU_AHP_MS = 1.0, 20.0, 7000.0   # AHP measured at about 7 s
V_REST, V_THRESH, V_RESET = -65.0, -40.0, -75.0
AHP_STEP_MV = 12.0      # hyperpolarising drive added by one spike
SLOW_GAIN = 0.15        # a slow synaptic event shuts the AHP down

def run(drive_mv, slow_on, duration_ms=20000):
    """Count spikes of one AH-type gut sensory neuron."""
    v, ahp, spikes = V_REST, 0.0, 0
    for _ in range(int(duration_ms / DT_MS)):
        gate = SLOW_GAIN if slow_on else 1.0
        effective = drive_mv - ahp * gate    # the brake, possibly released
        v += DT_MS * ((V_REST - v) + effective) / TAU_M_MS
        ahp -= DT_MS * ahp / TAU_AHP_MS      # brake bleeds away over seconds
        if v >= V_THRESH:
            v, spikes, ahp = V_RESET, spikes + 1, ahp + AHP_STEP_MV
    return spikes

for label, slow_on in (("brake on ", False), ("brake off", True)):
    print("{}: {:>3} spikes in 20 s".format(label, run(30.0, slow_on)))

# brake on :   3 spikes in 20 s
# brake off:  12 spikes in 20 s
# Same drive, four times the output: that is the slow synaptic switch.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A large smooth cell body of about 900 square micrometres whose several long processes are all axons, plus a fringe of short dendrites the ordinary type II cell lacks, and a broad 2.9 millisecond spike humped on its falling edge followed by a seven second slump during which nothing will make it fire.


PART EIGHTEEN: OTHER PERIPHERAL AND SPECIAL NEURONS

CHAPTER 281. CAROTID BODY AND AORTIC BODY GLOMUS CELL-ASSOCIATED AFFERENTS (Oxygen-Shortage Breath-Driving Neuron) (NON-COG)

Hold your breath and something in your neck starts shouting long before you feel the urge to breathe.

The existing label is an address plus a committee minute, naming two arterial locations and a partner cell without saying what the wire does.

Oxygen-Shortage names the stimulus it owns, since when arterial oxygen tension falls the partner glomus cell shuts its oxygen-sensitive potassium channels, depolarises, and floods this nerve ending with adenosine triphosphate (ATP) acting on P2X receptors.

Breath-Driving names the consequence, because the signal travels the glossopharyngeal nerve from the carotid body and the vagus nerve from the aortic body straight into the brainstem breathing centres and raises ventilation within seconds.

Neuron marks it as an afferent whose urgency is built into the anatomy, since the carotid body takes roughly one thousand to two thousand millilitres of blood per hundred grams of tissue per minute, the highest perfusion of any organ, so it samples arterial blood almost unchanged.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This fibre carries one message to the brainstem: the blood is running out of oxygen.

The sensing is done by neighbouring glomus cells, so this neuron reports rather than deliberates, which makes it non-cognitive.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is pseudounipolar, meaning one stalk leaves the round cell body and splits into two long branches like a letter T.

The far branch sheds its insulation and wraps four to twenty glomus cells in cup-shaped endings.

SIZE:

The cell body is 20 to 45 micrometres across, a micrometre being one thousandth of a millimetre.

Its fibres run 1.3 to 7.5 micrometres thick when insulated and 0.17 to 1.7 when bare.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Large units, one insulated A fibre serving up to about thirty glomus cells, strongly sensitive to falling oxygen.

• Small units, one bare C fibre serving a handful of cells and responding less sensitively.

Aortic body afferents are the same design carried in the vagus nerve.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Carotid body afferent cell bodies sit in the petrosal ganglion of the ninth cranial nerve at the jugular foramen, reaching the carotid fork by the carotid sinus nerve.

Aortic afferents sit in the nodose ganglion of the vagus and end on the aortic arch, pulmonary trunk and subclavian arteries.

All end centrally in the commissural and caudal medial nucleus of the solitary tract.

The defining interface is this: the far ending lies outside the blood-brain barrier in raw arterial blood, the near ending deep inside the protected brain.

NUMBER:

No reliable human count exists.

In the rat, Laidler and Kay found 15,920 to 30,770 glomus cells across both carotid bodies, over 95 per cent of the organ's fibres are afferent, and one fibre serves up to thirty glomus cells.

A defensible bound is 2,000 to 20,000 such neurons per person, low confidence in the value and high in the order.

That is about one neuron in ten million, or 0.00001 per cent of the nervous system, inside this section's budget.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Each fibre ticks at a few impulses per second in room air.

Firing then climbs along a hyperbola as oxygen falls, turning steeply below about 60 millimetres of mercury, and it barely adapts because this is an alarm.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Its far ending is postsynaptic, receiving adenosine triphosphate (ATP) at P2X2 and P2X3 receptors and acetylcholine at nicotinic receptors.

Centrally it releases glutamate onto solitary tract neurons driving the pre-Botzinger complex, the rostral ventrolateral medulla and the paraventricular nucleus.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Insulated fibres conduct at 3 to 30 metres per second and bare ones below 2.

The axon branches heavily inside the organ and ships mitochondria outward to keep its distant ending alive.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases glutamate centrally.

Many also release substance P and calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) from the peripheral ending back onto glomus cells and vessels, making the contact two-way.

MYELINATION:

Insulated fibres carry myelin, a fatty spiral laid down by Schwann cells that lets the signal leap between gaps.

It stops short of the ending, because a bare membrane is needed to receive transmitter.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The carotid body has the highest blood flow per gram of any tissue in the body, around 2,000 millilitres per 100 grams per minute.

This ending is therefore never short of fuel even when the body is.

PLASTICITY:

Sustained low oxygen grows the organ several fold through the oxygen-sensing factor HIF-2 alpha, encoded by the gene EPAS1.

The repeated brief drops of sleep apnoea sensitise the pathway, leaving the sympathetic system hot all day.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Type II sustentacular cells here are unlike glia elsewhere.

They carry glial fibrillary acidic protein like astrocytes but also pannexin-1 channels releasing their own amplifying ATP, and they double as stem cells building new glomus cells in chronic hypoxia.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and breathing continues on carbon dioxide alone.

But altitude provokes no extra breathing, breath-hold loses its urgency, and the gasp ending an apnoea is delayed, so the region keeps its metronome and loses its siren.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The ending is presynaptic and postsynaptic to the same glomus cell.

Its peptides widen local vessels, so the sensor helps set the conditions it measures, nitric oxide damps it by volume transmission, and denervation makes the glomus cells shrink away.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: arterial oxygen pressure in millimetres of mercury, 20 to 110; carbon dioxide pressure, 20 to 80; pH, 7.0 to 7.6; cleft ATP and acetylcholine from named source glomus cells.

• OUTPUTS: an impulse train to named target neurons of the solitary tract nucleus, plus substance P and CGRP onto glomus cells and arterioles.

• STATE: ending membrane potential, receptor occupancy, refractory countdown, and a slow term tracking recent hypoxia.

• PARAMETERS: half-maximal oxygen pressure near 40 millimetres of mercury; Hill exponent near 2; tonic rate 1 to 4 impulses per second; ceiling 60 to 100.

• TIME CONSTANTS: synaptic delay a few milliseconds; whole reflex 1 to 3 seconds; sensitisation over hours; organ growth over weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: overactivity drives sympathetic overdrive in sleep apnoea, resistant hypertension and heart failure, and removing one carotid body cut systolic pressure by about 26 millimetres of mercury in eight of fifteen resistant hypertensives, while paragangliomas here grow about 0.83 millimetres a year.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn arterial oxygen and carbon dioxide pressures into a firing rate in a carotid body afferent fibre.
Create a number variable called oxygenPressure with initial value 100.
Create a number variable called carbonDioxidePressure with initial value 40.
Create a number variable called oxygenSquared with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called potassiumOpen with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called releaseDrive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firingRate with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called firingHistory with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 20 times.
    Set oxygenSquared to the product of oxygenPressure and oxygenPressure.
    Set potassiumOpen to the quotient of oxygenSquared divided by the sum of oxygenSquared and 1600.
    Set releaseDrive to the difference of 1 and potassiumOpen.
    Set releaseDrive to the product of releaseDrive and releaseDrive.
    Set releaseDrive to the product of releaseDrive and releaseDrive.
    Set releaseDrive to the sum of releaseDrive and the product of 0.01 and the difference of carbonDioxidePressure and 40.
    Set firingRate to the sum of 1.5 and the product of releaseDrive and 90.
    Add firingRate to the end of firingHistory.
    Display firingRate to the console.
    Set oxygenPressure to the difference of oxygenPressure and 4.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the model is a short chain of continuous equations that the standard library alone will run anywhere.

import math

# Oxygen closes background potassium channels in the glomus cell, the cell
# depolarises, calcium enters, transmitter is released, the afferent fires.

HALF = 40.0        # mmHg giving half closure
CA_HALF = 0.5      # calcium for half maximal release

def afferent_rate(pao2, paco2=40.0, ph=7.40):
    open_k = pao2 ** 2 / (pao2 ** 2 + HALF ** 2)
    acid = (7.40 - ph) * 40.0 + (paco2 - 40.0) * 0.35
    vm = -55.0 + 35.0 * (1.0 - open_k) + acid       # glomus cell millivolts
    calcium = 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp(-(vm + 30.0) / 4.0))
    drive = calcium / (calcium + CA_HALF)
    return 1.5 + 88.5 * drive                       # impulses per second

if __name__ == "__main__":
    for po2 in (100, 80, 60, 45, 35, 25):
        print(po2, "mmHg ->", round(afferent_rate(po2), 1), "impulses per second")
    print("hypoxia plus hypercapnia:", round(afferent_rate(45, 55, 7.30), 1))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

This is the only sensory neuron whose receptive field is a drop of arterial blood, and the only common one that does not own its transducer.

Find a neck fibre silent about touch and screaming about oxygen, and you have found it.

CHAPTER 282. MERKEL CELLS (NON-COG)

Run a fingertip across a page of Braille and the dots are read, one by one, by cells that are not nerve cells at all.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A Merkel cell is a small pale oval cell in the deepest layer of the epidermis, and it must be flagged as not a neuron but a skin cell, drawing nothing from this section's neuron budget.

It senses touch and releases messengers onto a nerve ending at its base, and that partnership is the slowly adapting type I mechanoreceptor, written SA1, which resolves edges, corners and curvature.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell is oval and clear with a lobed nucleus, riveted to its neighbours by desmosomes and spiked on top with microvilli, so any skin deformation tugs its membrane.

Below, it is flattened against a disc-shaped nerve ending, backed by dense-core granules 80 to 120 nanometres across.

SIZE:

Merkel cells are 10 to 15 micrometres across, slightly larger than the skin cells around them.

The partner fibre is a thick insulated A-beta axon 6 to 12 micrometres in diameter.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Glabrous skin Merkel cells, clustered at the tips of the epidermal ridges that make fingerprints.

• Hairy skin Merkel cells, gathered into raised touch domes, one of which was found to hold 137 cells.

• Follicle-associated Merkel cells in collars round hair shafts, and mucosal cells of mouth and gum.

A second split separates innervated cells from non-innervated ones, which appear to signal only to local skin and immune cells.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Merkel cells live in the stratum basale, the deepest epidermal layer, densest in fingertips, palms, soles, lips and tongue tip, in touch domes throughout hairy skin, around hair follicles and in oral mucosa.

The critical interface is the dermal-epidermal junction, the basement membrane below which the nerve runs and through which it must pass, shedding myelin and Schwann cell, so this synapse sits in tissue with no blood supply.

NUMBER:

Merkel cells are not neurons and draw nothing from the neuron budget, which must not be misread as an omission.

Published fingertip densities differ fivefold, at about 2,000 per square centimetre in one histological series and about 10,000 in a widely cited review.

Their nerve partners are far fewer, at roughly 70 to 100 SA1 fibres per square centimetre at the fingertip, and one hand carries about 17,000 tactile afferent units of which SA1 units are commonly given as 13 to 25 per cent.

No whole-body count is published, and scaling over sparser skin gives a low-confidence bound of tens of millions.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Merkel cells are electrically excitable, which is startling for a skin cell.

Their trigger is PIEZO2, a ring-shaped protein whose pore opens on stretch, found in about 80 per cent of identified human Merkel cells and inactivating in about ten milliseconds, so the SA1 discharge bursts near 100 impulses per second during the press and settles to 10 to 40.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Each cell synapses onto one flattened A-beta ending whose fibre collects from a cluster a few millimetres wide.

That fibre runs unbroken to the cuneate or gracile nucleus, then crosses to the ventral posterolateral thalamus and on to area 3b of somatosensory cortex.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Not applicable to the Merkel cell, which has no axon and cannot conduct an impulse any distance, an absence that is definitional rather than an oversight.

The axon belongs to its partner neuron in a dorsal root or trigeminal ganglion, conducting at 35 to 75 metres per second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Serotonin, also called 5-hydroxytryptamine or 5-HT, is the transmitter released onto the nerve ending.

Work published in 2016 showed touch triggers its release onto fast 5-HT3A and slower metabotropic receptors, and that blockade degrades both electrical and behavioural responses to touch.

MYELINATION:

The Merkel cell has none, since it is not a neuron.

Its partner axon is myelinated throughout the dermis, which is why touch beats pain to the spinal cord, and both myelin and Schwann cell stop at the basement membrane.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The epidermis contains no blood vessels, so the cell lives on oxygen and glucose diffusing up from dermal capillaries.

That caps its metabolic rate and explains why it sits in the layer nearest the supply.

PLASTICITY:

The population is rebuilt from local progenitors over weeks to months.

The nerve must therefore keep forming synapses with new partners while the fibre itself persists, a structural plasticity with no central equivalent.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

There are no astrocytes, oligodendrocytes or microglia here at all, which is a real difference rather than missing data.

The support cells are keratinocytes, ordinary skin cells that anchor the Merkel cell, transmit force to it, and release ATP that can excite the afferent independently.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete Merkel cells and the fingertip still feels motion, vibration and stretch.

What it loses is form: the steady high-resolution image of a shape pressed into skin, and with it Braille, the milled edge of a coin, and curvature judged in the dark.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The cell is receptosecretory, sensing and secreting at once.

The complex splits its computation across two cells and two genomes, PIEZO2 in the Merkel cell timing the onset while PIEZO2 in the nerve ending sustains the response, with desmosomes coupling neighbours mechanically.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: indentation depth in millimetres, 0 to about 2; indentation velocity in millimetres per second; local curvature and strain; temperature, since cooling raises firing.

• OUTPUTS: serotonin into the cleft onto the named target A-beta slowly adapting type I afferent, plus CGRP and other peptides onto vessels and immune cells.

• STATE: PIEZO2 inactivation level, intracellular calcium, releasable serotonin pool fraction, membrane potential.

• PARAMETERS: threshold near 10 micrometres of indentation; dynamic gain near 20 impulses per second per millimetre per second; static gain near 25 per millimetre; receptive field 2 to 4 millimetres.

• TIME CONSTANTS: PIEZO2 inactivation about 10 milliseconds; synaptic delay 1 to 3 milliseconds; slow adaptation over 1 to 10 seconds; cell replacement over weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of PIEZO2 function in humans causes profound loss of touch and limb position sense while pain survives, and malignant change gives Merkel cell carcinoma at about 0.68 to 0.7 cases per 100,000 people yearly in the United States, from Merkel cell polyomavirus or ultraviolet damage.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert an indentation of the skin into the slowly adapting impulse train of a Merkel cell and its afferent fibre.
Create a number variable called stepCount with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called indentation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called previousIndentation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called indentationRate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called fastComponent with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called staticDrive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called serotoninPool with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called firingRate with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called impulseRecord with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 300 times.
    Set stepCount to the sum of stepCount and 1.
    If stepCount is less than or equal to 100, then perform the following actions.
        Set indentation to the sum of indentation and 0.005.
    End the conditional block.
    Set indentationRate to the quotient of the difference of indentation and previousIndentation divided by 0.001.
    Set previousIndentation to indentation.
    Set fastComponent to the sum of fastComponent and the product of 0.1 and the difference of indentationRate and fastComponent.
    Set staticDrive to the difference of indentation and 0.01.
    If staticDrive is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set staticDrive to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set firingRate to the sum of the product of 20 and fastComponent and the product of 25 and the product of staticDrive and serotoninPool.
    Set serotoninPool to the difference of serotoninPool and the product of 0.00002 and firingRate.
    Set serotoninPool to the sum of serotoninPool and the product of 0.0007 and the difference of 1 and serotoninPool.
    Add firingRate to the end of impulseRecord.
End the loop.
Display firingRate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because this model is the natural firmware for a prosthetic fingertip, running in a small microcontroller with fixed timing and no libraries.

#include <stdio.h>

/* Merkel cell to afferent model, two stages. Build with cc -std=c99 */
int main(void) {
    const double dt = 0.001, tau_fast = 0.010, tau_pool = 1.500;
    double indent = 0.0, prev = 0.0, fast = 0.0, pool = 1.0;
    int step;
    for (step = 0; step < 3000; step++) {
        double t = step * dt, rate, drive, firing;
        indent = (t < 0.100) ? (0.5 * t / 0.100) : 0.5;  /* ramp then hold 0.5 mm */
        rate = (indent - prev) / dt;
        prev = indent;
        if (rate < 0.0) rate = 0.0;
        fast += dt * (rate - fast) / tau_fast;           /* PIEZO2, 10 ms decay */
        drive = indent - 0.010;                          /* 10 micrometre threshold */
        if (drive < 0.0) drive = 0.0;
        firing = 20.0 * fast + 25.0 * drive * pool;      /* impulses per second */
        pool += dt * (1.0 - pool) / tau_pool;            /* serotonin refill */
        pool -= dt * firing * 0.02;                      /* serotonin use */
        if (pool < 0.05) pool = 0.05;
        if (step % 300 == 0)
            printf("t=%5.3f s  indent=%5.3f mm  firing=%6.1f Hz\n", t, indent, firing);
    }
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is a skin cell behaving like a nerve cell, holding a synapse above the basement membrane where no neuron can go.

Look for cytokeratin 20 as a dot beside its nucleus and a flattened nerve disc pressed against its base.

CHAPTER 283. PINEALOCYTES (Night-Length Reporting Cell) (NON-COG)

Deep in the middle of your skull a pinecone-shaped gland spends every night turning darkness into a hormone.

The old name is pure address, telling you only that the cell sits in the pine-cone-shaped gland.

Night-Length names the quantity it actually measures, because the cell is silent in light and secretes melatonin only in darkness, so the length of its nightly secretory episode tracks the length of the night and thereby the season.

Reporting names the job, since the cell does not decide anything but publishes the measurement into the bloodstream for the whole body to read.

Cell rather than Neuron is the honest head noun, and it is the gland's dominant voice, making up about ninety-five percent of the gland's cells, its output switched on by noradrenaline from the superior cervical ganglion.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A pinealocyte makes melatonin, the chemical announcement of biological night, and must be flagged as not a neuron, drawing nothing from this section's neuron budget.

It descends from a light-sensitive nerve cell that in fish and birds still sees light through the skull, and its work now is arithmetic on time: it turns the length of a nerve signal switched on only in darkness into a nightly pulse of melatonin.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is round or pear-shaped with a large pale nucleus and sends club-shaped processes ending in swellings against capillary walls.

Its signature is the synaptic ribbon, a dense rod built around the protein RIBEYE, the organelle of retinal photoreceptors, here in a cell with no postsynaptic partner.

SIZE:

Pinealocytes are 7 to 11 micrometres across, the dark type reaching about 11.2.

The gland's median volume by magnetic resonance imaging is 88.5 cubic millimetres, range 12.3 to 411, with means near 99 to 104.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Type 1, the light pinealocytes, pale and mitochondria-rich, more numerous than type 2 in both children and adults.

• Type 2, the dark pinealocytes, with a deeply infolded nuclear envelope, abundant rough endoplasmic reticulum and numerous cilia.

Single-cell sequencing has since confirmed two molecularly distinct populations with different daily gene expression.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Pinealocytes exist only in the pineal gland, or epiphysis cerebri, hanging from the roof of the third ventricle on the pineal stalk, in the quadrigeminal cistern above the superior colliculi and behind the habenular and posterior commissures.

Two interfaces define it: the gland lies outside the blood-brain barrier with windowed, fenestrated capillaries, making it a circumventricular organ open to the general circulation, and the pineal recess opens into the third ventricle, so its processes also face cerebrospinal fluid.

NUMBER:

No published stereological count of human pinealocytes exists, and that gap must be stated rather than papered over.

About 95 per cent of human pineal cells are pinealocytes, the rest fibrous astrocytes.

In sheep, counting gave 3.24 million per gland before puberty rising to 7.38 million after, so a defensible human bound is a few million to perhaps twenty million, with moderate confidence and none of the neuron budget.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Mammalian pinealocytes are not spiking cells, which is the honest headline.

Their real signal is cyclic adenosine monophosphate, or cyclic AMP, an internal messenger whose rise switches on the melatonin factory, so output is graded and measured in picograms per millilitre rather than impulses per second.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The cell is postsynaptic to sympathetic endings and presynaptic to nobody.

The chain runs from light-sensitive retinal ganglion cells along the retinohypothalamic tract to the suprachiasmatic nucleus, then the paraventricular nucleus, the intermediolateral cell column of the upper thoracic cord, and the superior cervical ganglion, whose fibres climb back as the nervi conarii.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Not applicable, because a pinealocyte has no axon: its processes are short secretory extensions on capillaries that conduct no impulse, an absence that is definitional rather than unknown.

The axons inside the gland belong to unmyelinated sympathetic neurons.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its product is melatonin, made from tryptophan through serotonin to N-acetylserotonin by arylalkylamine N-acetyltransferase (AANAT), the rate-limiting step and the clock's actual lever, then to melatonin by acetylserotonin O-methyltransferase (ASMT).

The transmitter it receives is noradrenaline at beta-1 adrenoceptors, which raises cyclic AMP, activates protein kinase A and switches on the AANAT gene.

MYELINATION:

Not applicable, since there is no axon to insulate.

The sympathetic fibres reaching it are unmyelinated, which is why the nightly signal arrives as a slow chemical tide rather than a fast volley.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

For its size the pineal is one of the most richly perfused structures in the head.

Synthesis consumes the essential amino acid tryptophan and requires S-adenosylmethionine as methyl donor, with energy use rising steeply at night.

PLASTICITY:

The plasticity is transcriptional and dramatic: AANAT protein rises many-fold at night and is destroyed by the proteasome within minutes of light, so one bright light at two in the morning collapses production.

Season is encoded by the duration of the pulse rather than its height.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Pineal interstitial cells are fibrous astrocytes that, unlike astrocytes elsewhere, maintain no blood-brain barrier, because the capillaries are deliberately leaky.

Recent transcriptomic work implicates the local microglia in actively building the calcified grains of brain sand, which microglia do nowhere else.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take pinealocytes out and sleep still occurs, driven by the hypothalamus.

But its timing drifts, seasonal physiology flattens, and the nightly dip in body temperature loses its anchor, so the region keeps its geography and loses its calendar.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The computation here is gene transcription plus regulated protein destruction, over minutes to hours.

The output is volume transmission in its purest form, since melatonin is fat-soluble, is never stored in vesicles, and diffuses straight through the membrane into blood and cerebrospinal fluid, feeding back onto the cell's own receptors.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: noradrenaline from named source sympathetic terminals of the superior cervical ganglion, gated by the suprachiasmatic nucleus; retinal light in lux, 0 to 100,000; circulating tryptophan.

• OUTPUTS: melatonin into capillary blood and cerebrospinal fluid, with nocturnal plasma peaks near 60 to 80 picograms per millilitre in young and middle-aged adults, and three-in-the-morning reference limits of 18.3 to 134 picograms per millilitre in men and 19.0 to 197 in women.

• STATE: cyclic AMP concentration, AANAT protein level, serotonin pool size, intracellular calcium, accumulated melatonin.

• PARAMETERS: beta-1 adrenoceptor density; half-suppressing light of the order of tens of lux; maximum synthesis rate; proteasomal degradation constant for AANAT.

• TIME CONSTANTS: cyclic AMP rise and fall in 5 to 15 minutes; AANAT destruction in minutes after light; melatonin half-life 30 to 50 minutes; the nightly pulse 8 to 10 hours.

• FAILURE MODE: gland loss or sympathetic denervation as in Horner syndrome abolishes the rhythm, while pineocytoma, pineoblastoma and germ cell tumours compress the midbrain tectum causing Parinaud syndrome, a paralysis of upward gaze, and can cause hydrocephalus.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert the length of the night into a nightly pulse of melatonin from a pinealocyte.
Create a number variable called clockHour with initial value 18.
Create a number variable called sympatheticDrive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called cyclicAmp with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called acetylTransferase with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called synthesis with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called breakdown with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called melatonin with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called melatoninTrace with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 96 times.
    Set sympatheticDrive to 0.
    If either clockHour is greater than or equal to 21 or clockHour is less than 7 is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set sympatheticDrive to 0.9.
    End the conditional block.
    Set cyclicAmp to the sum of cyclicAmp and the product of 3 and sympatheticDrive.
    Set cyclicAmp to the difference of cyclicAmp and the product of 1.67 and cyclicAmp.
    Set synthesis to the quotient of the product of 5 and cyclicAmp divided by the sum of cyclicAmp and 1.
    Set breakdown to the product of acetylTransferase and the sum of 0.1 and the difference of 1.5 and the product of 1.5 and sympatheticDrive.
    Set acetylTransferase to the sum of acetylTransferase and the difference of synthesis and breakdown.
    Set melatonin to the sum of melatonin and the difference of the product of 0.75 and acetylTransferase and the product of 0.42 and melatonin.
    Add melatonin to the end of melatoninTrace.
    Display melatonin to the console.
    Set clockHour to the remainder of the sum of clockHour and 0.25 divided by 24.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because this is a slow chemical model where readability matters more than speed, and clock arithmetic over hours reads cleanly in floating point.

# A night window opens the sympathetic gate, light closes it within minutes,
# cyclic AMP drives AANAT, and AANAT makes melatonin.

DT, CAMP_TAU, MEL_TAU, SCALE = 0.25, 0.15, 0.60, 5.0   # hours, and a pg/mL scale

def run(pulse=None):
    hour, camp, aanat, mel, trace = 18.0, 0.0, 0.0, 0.0, {}
    for _ in range(int(24 / DT)):
        night = 1.0 if (hour >= 21.0 or hour < 7.0) else 0.0
        lux = 300.0 if (pulse and pulse[0] <= hour < pulse[1]) else (1.0 if night else 500.0)
        drive = night * 900.0 / (900.0 + lux * lux)     # half suppression near 30 lux
        camp += DT * (12.0 * drive - camp / CAMP_TAU)
        aanat += DT * (5.0 * camp / (camp + 1.0) - aanat * (0.4 + 6.0 * (1.0 - drive)))
        mel += DT * (3.0 * aanat - mel / MEL_TAU)
        trace[round(hour, 2)] = round(mel * SCALE, 1)
        hour = (hour + DT) % 24.0
    return trace

if __name__ == "__main__":
    normal, pulsed = run(), run(pulse=(2.0, 3.0))
    for h in (22.0, 0.0, 2.0, 2.5, 3.0, 4.0, 6.0):
        print(h, "h  normal", normal[h], "pg/mL   light pulse", pulsed[h], "pg/mL")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only cell in the body keeping the synaptic ribbon of a photoreceptor while having nothing to see and nobody to talk to.

It holds more serotonin than any other tissue on earth, and an enzyme that appears at dusk and is shredded by light within minutes.

CHAPTER 284. PARANEURONS (Sense-And-Secrete Cell) (NON-COG)

There is a whole secret nervous system in your body made of cells that were never invited to call themselves neurons.

Paraneuron is a committee term built from a Greek prefix meaning beside, and it teaches a student nothing about what the cells do.

Sense names the first half of the definition, that these cells detect an adequate stimulus such as light, stretch, a chemical, or a taste.

Secrete names the second half, that they answer that stimulus by releasing the contents of neurotransmitter-like or neurosecretion-like granules onto a nearby nerve ending or into the blood.

Cell is the honest head noun, since this is a category rather than a single cell type, and the original three criteria, such granules, transmitter-like contents, and stimulus-triggered release, are exactly what the new name records with no invented function added.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Paraneuron names a family, coined by the Japanese histologist Tsuneo Fujita in 1977 for scattered cells of the gut, lung, tongue, skin and adrenal gland that do what a neuron does but lack an axon.

His definition has three tests: the cell holds granules like synaptic vesicles, it makes substances identical or close to neurotransmitters, and it recognises a stimulus and answers by releasing them.

None is a neuron and none draws on this section's neuron budget, yet they are the body's interoceptive front end, and Fujita concluded there is no distinct boundary between neurons and paraneurons.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The shared design is a polarised bottle-shaped cell whose outward end faces gut lumen, airway, mouth or blood and carries the sensor.

The inward end holds dense-core granules 80 to 300 nanometres across and often a true presynaptic active zone.

SIZE:

Cell bodies run about 5 to 30 micrometres: Merkel cells 10 to 15, pinealocytes 7 to 11, glomus cells roughly 10 to 15, enteroendocrine cells 10 to 20 tall, taste receptor cells 40 to 70 long and slender.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Open type, reaching the lumen with a tuft of microvilli and tasting its contents, as most enteroendocrine cells and all taste cells do.

• Closed type, with no luminal contact, answering stretch, blood chemistry or nerve input, as glomus and chromaffin cells do.

• Wired, forming a true synapse onto a sensory nerve ending and delivering in milliseconds, as Merkel, glomus, taste and neuropod cells do.

• Volume-transmitting, releasing into blood over seconds to hours, as hormone-secreting gut cells and pinealocytes do.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Paraneurons occupy every interface between the body and what is not the body.

• Gastrointestinal epithelium from stomach to rectum, as enterochromaffin, L, K, I, D and G cells, including the neuropod subset.

• Airway epithelium, as pulmonary neuroendocrine cells and their clusters, the neuroepithelial bodies at airway branch points.

• Taste buds of the fungiform, foliate and circumvallate papillae, plus palate, epiglottis and upper oesophagus.

• Carotid bodies at the carotid bifurcation, aortic bodies on the aortic arch, adrenal medulla and paraganglia including the organ of Zuckerkandl.

• Sympathetic ganglia as small intensely fluorescent cells, basal epidermis as Merkel cells, pineal gland as pinealocytes, urethra and nose as brush cells.

NUMBER:

Enteroendocrine cells are about 1 per cent of the intestinal epithelium, making the gut lining the largest endocrine organ in the body, with roughly 100 million shed and replaced daily.

Taste receptor cells number roughly 100,000 to 800,000 across 2,000 to 8,000 taste buds of 50 to 100 cells each, pulmonary neuroendocrine cells are about 0.5 per cent of airway epithelium, and rat carotid bodies hold about 8,700 to 11,500 glomus cells each.

The class reaches hundreds of millions of cells, yet contributes exactly zero to the neuron budget.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Many are genuinely excitable, with enteroendocrine and taste cells firing sodium action potentials, glomus and Merkel cells firing calcium spikes, and chromaffin cells firing like sympathetic neurons.

Pinealocytes never spike, and the shared currency across the class is intracellular calcium.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Wired paraneurons synapse onto primary sensory neurons and are effectively the true first cell of those pathways.

Neuropod cells synapse onto vagal neurons of the nodose ganglion and transduce a sugar stimulus in milliseconds using glutamate, while taste type II cells release ATP through CALHM1 channels onto geniculate afferents.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Not applicable in the strict sense, because by definition a paraneuron has no axon, and that single absence kept the class out of neuroscience for a century.

The nearest approach is the neuropod, a basal process a few tens of micrometres long packed with presynaptic proteins.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The list reads like a textbook, which was Fujita's point.

Serotonin from enterochromaffin, Merkel and taste type III cells; glutamate from neuropod cells; adenosine triphosphate (ATP) from taste type II and glomus cells; adrenaline from chromaffin cells; melatonin from pinealocytes; plus cholecystokinin, glucagon-like peptide 1, somatostatin and ghrelin.

MYELINATION:

Not applicable, because no paraneuron has an axon to myelinate.

The nerve fibres they speak to are frequently myelinated up to the point of contact and bare thereafter, a consistent rule across the class.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

These are secretory factories whose costs lie in synthesis and packaging rather than ion pumping, and enteroendocrine cells live only days to weeks.

Glomus cells by contrast sit in the most richly perfused tissue in the body, around 2,000 millilitres per 100 grams per minute.

PLASTICITY:

Here they differ most sharply from neurons, because they are replaceable.

Gut cells regenerate from intestinal stem cells, taste cells every one to three weeks, Merkel cells over months, and glomus cells from resident stem cells under a HIF-2 alpha programme that can enlarge the organ several fold.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Several partners are unlike glia anywhere else: carotid body type II sustentacular cells look glial yet double as stem cells and release their own ATP through pannexin-1 channels.

Taste bud type I cells are the tongue's glia, mopping up transmitter and buffering potassium exactly as an astrocyte does.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the paraneurons and every surface goes numb to chemistry.

Food is swallowed without taste and without the hormones that release insulin and end a meal, the airway does not know it has been irritated, and the blood is never checked for oxygen.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

These cells compute with secretion, and can do it two ways at once, a neuropod cell firing a glutamate synapse in milliseconds while releasing a peptide hormone that acts minutes later.

Because they are born and die throughout life, a simulation must model birth and death of sensors, not only their state.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: a subtype-specific stimulus with named units, being luminal glucose in millimoles per litre for gut cells, arterial oxygen pressure in millimetres of mercury for glomus cells, indentation in millimetres for Merkel cells, and noradrenaline for pinealocytes.

• OUTPUTS: a named transmitter or hormone sent either across a synapse to a named afferent neuron or into the bloodstream, the two routes modelled separately because their latencies differ by four orders of magnitude.

• STATE: intracellular calcium, membrane potential where excitable, readily releasable vesicle pool, reserve granule pool, and cell age since differentiation.

• PARAMETERS: stimulus threshold and half-maximal concentration; maximum release rate; vesicle pool sizes; cell lifetime, days for gut cells, weeks for taste cells, months for Merkel cells, years for glomus cells.

• TIME CONSTANTS: synaptic release in 1 to 10 milliseconds for wired subtypes; hormone action in seconds to minutes; transcription in tens of minutes; turnover in days to months.

• FAILURE MODE: uncontrolled proliferation gives neuroendocrine tumours including carcinoid tumours, phaeochromocytoma, carotid body paraganglioma and Merkel cell carcinoma, while losing enteroendocrine signalling wrecks appetite, glucose control and gut motility.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to test whether a candidate cell is a paraneuron and to classify how fast its message reaches the nervous system.
Create a boolean variable called hasSecretoryGranules with initial value true.
Create a boolean variable called makesNeuralMessenger with initial value true.
Create a boolean variable called releasesOnStimulus with initial value true.
Create a boolean variable called makesSynapseOnAfferent with initial value true.
Create a boolean variable called passesFujitaTest with initial value false.
Create a number variable called totalDelay with initial value 17.
Create a text variable called routeName with initial value volume transmission.
Create a text variable called speedClass with initial value not a paraneuron.
If both hasSecretoryGranules and makesNeuralMessenger are true, then perform the following actions.
    If releasesOnStimulus is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set passesFujitaTest to true.
    End the conditional block.
End the conditional block.
If passesFujitaTest is true, then perform the following actions.
    If makesSynapseOnAfferent is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set routeName to wired transmission.
    End the conditional block.
    If totalDelay is less than 100, then perform the following actions.
        Set speedClass to reflex speed.
    Else if totalDelay is less than 60000, then perform the following actions.
        Set speedClass to behavioural speed.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set speedClass to endocrine speed.
    End the conditional block.
End the conditional block.
Display routeName to the console.
Display speedClass to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python with dataclasses is chosen because this type is a taxonomy rather than one cell, and a small typed catalogue with a classifier is the honest computational shape of a taxonomy.

from dataclasses import dataclass

# Paraneurons ranked by how long their message takes to reach the nervous
# system. Latencies are order of magnitude figures, in milliseconds.

@dataclass
class Paraneuron:
    name: str
    wired: bool
    release_ms: float
    transit_ms: float

CATALOGUE = [
    Paraneuron("neuropod enteroendocrine cell", True, 1.0, 60.0),
    Paraneuron("carotid body glomus cell", True, 5.0, 20.0),
    Paraneuron("Merkel cell", True, 2.0, 15.0),
    Paraneuron("taste receptor cell type II", True, 10.0, 10.0),
    Paraneuron("enteroendocrine L cell", False, 1000.0, 120000.0),
    Paraneuron("pinealocyte", False, 600000.0, 1800000.0),
]

def delay_ms(p):
    return p.release_ms + p.transit_ms

def speed_class(p):
    d = delay_ms(p)
    if d < 100.0:
        return "reflex speed"
    return "behavioural speed" if d < 60000.0 else "endocrine speed"

if __name__ == "__main__":
    for p in sorted(CATALOGUE, key=delay_ms):
        route = "wired" if p.wired else "volume"
        print(f"{p.name:30s} {delay_ms(p):9.0f} ms  {route:6s} {speed_class(p)}")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A paraneuron is recognised not by where it sits but by three tests: granules like a neuron's, messengers like a neuron's, and release triggered by a specific stimulus.

Under a microscope the signature is a small epithelial-looking cell staining for chromogranin A and synaptophysin, sitting in a sheet of cells that stain for neither.


PART NINETEEN: NEURONS NAMED FOR WHAT THEY ENCODE

CHAPTER 285. PLACE CELLS (COG)

In 1971 a rat crossed a small platform and one cell in its brain fired in exactly one corner and nowhere else, and the map inside the head stopped being a metaphor.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A place cell is an ordinary excitatory pyramidal cell of the hippocampus, the curled sheet buried in each temporal lobe, that fires only when the body sits in one small patch of a room, its place field.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It has a triangular body, one thick apical dendrite reaching up, shorter basal dendrites below, and about 30,000 spines, the knobs that receive excitatory contacts.

SIZE:

The body is 15 to 30 micrometres across in the human, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, with a dendritic tree of one to two millimetres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Superficial CA1 cells hold steadier fields than deep ones, CA3 cells carry giant thorny spines fed by mossy fibres, and granule, subicular and splitter cells qualify too.

WHERE IT LIVES:

CA1, CA2, CA3, the dentate gyrus and the subiculum, along the hippocampus from dorsal to ventral pole.

The key interface is stratum lacunosum-moleculare of CA1, where entorhinal layer III axons meet hippocampal dendrites directly.

NUMBER:

Zero additional neurons, because place cell is a functional label on hippocampal pyramidal and granule cells already counted in Part 12.

One human hippocampal formation holds roughly 40 million neurons, 14 million of them CA1 pyramidal cells, a stereological figure of moderate confidence.

Estimates of the fraction with a field in any one room run from 20 to over 90 percent, and the whole formation is 0.05 percent of the body's 86 billion neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Outside the field it fires below 0.1 spikes per second and inside reaches 5 to 20 in bursts at 150 to 200 hertz, precessing to ever earlier theta phases.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitation comes from CA3 Schaffer collaterals onto the middle dendrites and entorhinal layer III onto the tuft, while parvalbumin and somatostatin cells inhibit both.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon runs in the alveus to subiculum, entorhinal layer V, prefrontal cortex, septum and amygdala, while local collaterals conduct at 0.3 to 0.5 metres per second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, the main excitatory chemical messenger, acting on fast AMPA receptors and on NMDA receptors, which open only when the target is already active.

MYELINATION:

Myelin is a fatty insulating wrap that speeds conduction, and here the axon start is bare, collaterals patchy, the fornix output wrapped.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The hippocampus loses glucose uptake early in disease, yet each place cell is thrifty because it is almost always silent, a deliberate saving.

PLASTICITY:

A field can be built in one trial by behavioural timescale synaptic plasticity, a dendritic plateau of a few hundred milliseconds strengthening every input active within two seconds.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

One hippocampal astrocyte wraps some 100,000 synapses and supplies D-serine, a molecule the NMDA receptor needs before it opens, making these support cells gatekeepers of plasticity.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove place cells and the hippocampus becomes an associative network with no index, so memories would form but nothing would tag them with a where.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Dendrites compute locally, spikes travel backwards up them to report the output to the inputs, proteins are made inside the dendrite, and genes such as Arc switch on.

During rest, sequences replay inside 100 millisecond sharp wave ripples at up to twenty times real speed.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: entorhinal layer III drive 0 to 40 hertz; CA3 recurrence 0 to 30 hertz; septal theta 6 to 12 hertz; inhibition 0 to 100 hertz.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate spikes to subiculum, entorhinal layer V, prefrontal cortex and amygdala, 0 to 20 hertz.

• STATE: membrane voltage; dendritic calcium; plasticity eligibility trace; field centre and width; adaptation current.

• PARAMETERS: peak rate 5 to 20 hertz; field width 0.2 to 1 metre dorsally, 10 metres ventrally; background below 0.1 hertz.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 30 milliseconds; theta 125 milliseconds; plateau 100 to 300 milliseconds; eligibility 1 to 2 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: in Alzheimer's disease entorhinal input degrades and fields drift; in temporal lobe epilepsy synchronous discharge scrambles the code.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether a place cell fires now.
Create a number variable called field centre with initial value 1.2.
Create a number variable called field width with initial value 0.18.
Create a number variable called peak rate with initial value 12.
Create a list variable called spike times with initial value empty list.
For each time step in the walk, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the difference of current position and field centre.
    Set offset to the result.
    Calculate the product of peak rate and a bell curve of offset over field width.
    Set firing rate to the result.
    Set preferred phase to 360 degrees reduced in proportion to offset.
    Check if both theta phase is near preferred phase and a random number is below firing rate times step duration are true.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add the current time to the end of spike times.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display the total number of items in spike times to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python, the working language of computational neuroscience, reads like the English above.

import math, random
CENTRE, SIGMA, PEAK, THETA, BASE = 1.20, 0.18, 12.0, 8.0, 0.05   # m, m, Hz, Hz, Hz
rng, spikes, dt, speed = random.Random(7), [], 0.001, 0.30
for k in range(8000):                              # eight seconds of walking
    t = k * dt
    d = speed * t - CENTRE                         # offset from field centre
    rate = BASE + PEAK * math.exp(-(d * d) / (2.0 * SIGMA ** 2))
    frac = min(max((d + 2.0 * SIGMA) / (4.0 * SIGMA), 0.0), 1.0)
    pref = 360.0 * (1.0 - frac)                    # preferred phase precesses
    theta = (360.0 * THETA * t) % 360.0            # ongoing theta phase
    off = ((theta - pref + 180.0) % 360.0) - 180.0
    gate = math.exp(-(off * off) / (2.0 * 45.0 ** 2)) / 0.313
    if rng.random() < rate * gate * dt:            # fire near preferred phase
        spikes.append((t, speed * t, theta))
print("spikes fired:", len(spikes))
print("first: t=%.3f s pos=%.2f m phase=%.0f deg" % spikes[0])
print("last : t=%.3f s pos=%.2f m phase=%.0f deg" % spikes[-1])

It prints 21 spikes, the first at theta phase 346 degrees and the last at 6 degrees.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Nothing else is both almost silent and, in one small patch of the world, reliably loud.

CHAPTER 286. GRID CELLS (COG)

Lay a sheet of graph paper made of triangles over the whole floor of a room and imagine one neuron firing at every single vertex: that is a grid cell, the closest thing the brain has to a ruler.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A grid cell is a principal neuron of the medial entorhinal cortex, the gateway in front of the hippocampus, whose firing places form a triangular lattice, so it reports a phase in a repeating pattern rather than a place.

Several such lattices at different scales fix location exactly, which is path integration: tracking position by summing your own movements.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Most layer II grid cells are stellate cells, star-shaped neurons with many thick dendrites radiating outward, connected to each other through shared inhibition rather than direct excitation.

SIZE:

Stellate bodies are 20 to 25 micrometres across, larger than most cortical neurons, with dendrites spreading 400 to 600 micrometres inside entorhinal islands.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Pure grid cells sit in layer II and conjunctive grid by head direction cells in layers III, V and VI, all belonging to modules whose neighbouring scales differ by a ratio near 1.42.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The core population sits in medial entorhinal cortex, densest in layer II, with more in the para-subiculum.

The decisive interface is the entorhinal to hippocampal boundary, crossed by the perforant path from layer II and the temporoammonic path from layer III.

NUMBER:

Zero additional neurons, since grid cell labels entorhinal principal cells already counted in Part 12.

The human entorhinal cortex holds roughly 7 million neurons per hemisphere, 650,000 of them in layer II.

Since 10 to 30 percent of rodent layer II cells show clean grids, a human population near 100,000 per hemisphere is plausible but uncertain.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Stellate cells sag, an injected negative current making the voltage droop then rebound through HCN channels, giving a 4 to 10 hertz resonance.

In-field rates reach 5 to 20 spikes per second, and they phase precess.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input arrives from pre-subiculum and para-subiculum carrying head direction, the septum carrying theta and speed cells velocity, with parvalbumin interneurons inhibiting.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Layer II axons reach dentate gyrus and CA3, layer III reaches CA1 and subiculum, and layer V projects back to neocortex.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, modulated by acetylcholine from the medial septum, a chemical signalling novelty, and blocking it flattens the grid.

MYELINATION:

The perforant path is myelinated, because the entorhinal to hippocampal delay must stay short and constant for phase coding, while local collaterals are bare.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Entorhinal layer II neurons are the first in the brain to accumulate tangles of the protein tau, their demand driven by theta firing and a large HCN leak.

PLASTICITY:

The lattice is rigid, appearing days after a rat pup first explores and surviving a change of room, while its anchoring rotates to follow the walls.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes in layer II tile the same island and ocean pattern as the neurons, and microglia here react to tau earliest in the cortex.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete grid cells and the entorhinal cortex still passes on smells and objects but supplies no metric structure, so the map loses its ruler.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Grid firing comes from an attractor, a pattern of activity the connections hold in place and that movement signals push across the sheet.

Cells add resonance, dendritic nonlinearity and gap junctions.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: head direction 0 to 360 degrees; speed 0 to 1.5 metres per second; septal theta 6 to 12 hertz; boundary reset.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate spikes to dentate gyrus, CA3, CA1 and neocortex, 0 to 20 hertz.

• STATE: bump phase on the attractor sheet; membrane voltage; HCN activation; module orientation and offset.

• PARAMETERS: spacing 30 centimetres dorsally to past 3 metres ventrally; module ratio about 1.42; field width one third of spacing.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; HCN 20 to 200; theta 125; prospective bias 50 to 80 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: layer II neurons die by about 60 percent even in very mild Alzheimer's disease, and young APOE epsilon 4 carriers show weak grid-like signals.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the rate of one grid cell by adding three tilted waves.
Create a number variable called spacing with initial value 0.45.
Create a number variable called orientation with initial value 18.
Create a number variable called peak rate with initial value 9.
Create a number variable called wave sum with initial value 0.
Set wave number to four times pi divided by the product of the square root of three and spacing.
Repeat the following steps exactly 3 times.
    Set wave angle to orientation increased by a further 60 degrees on each repeat.
    Set projected distance to the position measured along wave angle.
    Calculate the product of wave number and projected distance.
    Set wave contribution to the cosine of the result.
    Calculate the sum of wave sum and wave contribution.
    Set wave sum to the result.
End the loop.
Calculate the sum of wave sum and 1.5.
Calculate the quotient of the result divided by 4.5.
Set normalised value to the result.
Calculate the product of peak rate and normalised value raised to the power 1.5.
Display the result to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python again, because the three-cosine formula is exact.

import math
SPACING, ORIENT, PEAK = 0.45, 18.0, 9.0              # m, deg, Hz

def grid_rate(x, y, spacing=SPACING, orient=ORIENT, peak=PEAK):
    """Firing rate of one grid cell at position (x, y), in metres."""
    k = 4.0 * math.pi / (math.sqrt(3.0) * spacing)   # wave number for this spacing
    total = 0.0
    for n in range(3):                               # three waves, 60 deg apart
        th = math.radians(orient) + n * math.pi / 3.0
        total += math.cos(k * (x * math.cos(th) + y * math.sin(th)))
    g = (total + 1.5) / 4.5                          # rescale into 0..1
    return peak * max(0.0, g) ** 1.5                 # sharpen the fields

print("origin: %.2f Hz" % grid_rate(0.0, 0.0))
for n in range(6):                                   # six neighbours
    phi = math.radians(ORIENT + 30.0 + 60.0 * n)
    print("neighbour %d at %5.1f deg: %.2f Hz" % (n + 1, math.degrees(phi) % 360.0,
          grid_rate(SPACING * math.cos(phi), SPACING * math.sin(phi))))   # all equal
mid = math.radians(ORIENT + 30.0)
print("halfway: %.2f Hz"
      % grid_rate(0.5 * SPACING * math.cos(mid), 0.5 * SPACING * math.sin(mid)))

It gives 9.00 hertz at the origin and at all six neighbours 45 centimetres off, 0.33 halfway between.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other cell fires on the vertices of a perfect triangular lattice across an unfamiliar floor.

CHAPTER 287. HEAD DIRECTION CELLS (COG)

Spin a person slowly in a dark room and something keeps count, a needle of activity swinging around a ring of cells like a compass that has never sensed a magnetic field.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A head direction cell fires whenever the head points one particular way in the room, whatever the body is doing, so it is a compass needle zeroed by landmarks rather than magnetism.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

They are small densely packed relay neurons in the anterodorsal thalamus, layer III pyramidal cells in the post-subiculum, and multipolar cells in the tegmental and mammillary nuclei.

SIZE:

Thalamic bodies are 10 to 20 micrometres across with dendrites of 200 to 400 micrometres, while post-subicular cells reach 25 and 500.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Classic cells report direction only, angular head velocity cells how fast the head turns, conjunctive cells add position, and anticipatory cells report the next heading.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The signal is generated in the loop between the dorsal tegmental nucleus of Gudden and the lateral mammillary nucleus, ascends through the anterodorsal thalamus, and reaches post-subiculum, retrosplenial cortex and medial entorhinal cortex.

The crucial interface is in the brainstem, where the medial vestibular nucleus feeds balance signals into that loop.

NUMBER:

Zero additional neurons, because head direction labels thalamic, brainstem and cortical cells already counted in Part 12.

In the rat anterodorsal nucleus half to sixty percent of recorded cells qualify.

Human counts do not exist, and the anterior thalamic complex is small, plausibly of order one million neurons, which is a bound.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The cell is nearly silent facing the wrong way and reaches 20 to 100 spikes per second at its preferred direction, with a tuning curve 90 degrees wide.

Thalamic cells anticipate the future heading by about 25 milliseconds, ranging from 0 to 50.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Glutamate runs from the lateral mammillary nucleus to the anterodorsal thalamus and on to cortex, GABA from the tegmental nucleus closes the loop, and pre-subicular cells need both inputs to coincide.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The mammillothalamic tract is compact, fast and visible to the naked eye in a human brain slice.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate ascending, GABA descending and local, with acetylcholine and noradrenaline setting the gain.

MYELINATION:

The mammillothalamic tract and thalamic projections to cortex are well myelinated, because a compass lagging behind the head is useless.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The population fires whenever the animal is awake, and since the same number of spikes leaves it whichever way the head points, demand is steady.

PLASTICITY:

A preferred direction is anchored to landmarks and rotates wholesale when one cue card is turned, so plasticity acts on the anchor, not the ring.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Nothing dramatically special has been described for the glia here, except that these nuclei are unusually sensitive to thiamine, a vitamin astrocytes help transport, so glial failure here causes amnesia quickly.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and grid cells lose the direction signal they integrate, so a person could recognise a place yet not know which way to set off.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This is the clearest mammalian example of a continuous attractor, a ring whose connections hold one bump of activity that a velocity signal pushes around.

The bump persists in darkness and sleep, so these cells maintain a state rather than report a stimulus.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: angular head velocity from vestibular nuclei, minus 500 to plus 500 degrees per second; landmark bearing; efference copy; optic flow.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate spikes to post-subiculum, retrosplenial cortex and medial entorhinal cortex, 0 to 100 hertz.

• STATE: angular position of the bump, 0 to 360 degrees; membrane voltage; bump width; landmark anchor offset.

• PARAMETERS: tuning width 60 to 100 degrees; peak rate 20 to 100 hertz; anticipation 0 to 50 milliseconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; synaptic 5 to 20; anticipation 25; re-anchoring 1 second.

• FAILURE MODE: retrosplenial stroke causes heading disorientation, the patient knowing every landmark yet unable to face the right way; Korsakoff syndrome adds amnesia.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to move an activity bump around a ring of head direction cells as the head turns.
Create a number variable called turning rate with initial value 90.
Create a number variable called bump centre with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step duration with initial value 0.001.
Create a list variable called activity with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called activity total with initial value 0.
For each cell in the ring, perform the following operations.
    Add a bell curve of its angle from bump centre to the end of activity.
End the loop.
Repeat the following steps exactly 1000 times.
    Calculate the product of turning rate and step duration.
    Calculate the sum of bump centre and the result.
    Set bump centre to the remainder of the result divided by 360.
    For each cell in activity, perform the following operations.
        Set this cell to neighbour excitation minus global inhibition plus a turning push.
    End the loop.
    Set activity total to 0.
    For each cell in activity, perform the following operations.
        Calculate the sum of activity total and this cell.
        Set activity total to the result.
    End the loop.
    For each cell in activity, perform the following operations.
        Calculate the quotient of this cell divided by activity total.
        Set this cell to the result.
    End the loop.
End the loop.
Set decoded direction to the angle of the activity weighted vector sum.
Display decoded direction to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python with NumPy, because a ring attractor is one matrix multiplication.

import numpy as np
N, DT, TAU = 60, 0.001, 0.020                    # cells, 1 ms step, 20 ms time constant
pref = 2 * np.pi * np.arange(N) / N              # preferred direction of each cell
d = (pref[:, None] - pref[None, :] + np.pi) % (2 * np.pi) - np.pi
bump = np.exp(-d * d / 0.5)                      # local excitation between neighbours

def run(omega, steps=1000):                      # omega is head turning, radians per second
    Wm = bump - 0.30 + 0.156 * omega * d * bump  # minus inhibition, plus velocity shift
    u = np.exp(-((pref + np.pi) % (2 * np.pi) - np.pi) ** 2 / 0.32)   # bump starts at 0 deg
    for _ in range(steps):
        drive = np.maximum(Wm @ u / N * 6.0, 0.0)
        u = u + (DT / TAU) * (drive - u)
        u = u * (10.0 / u.sum())                 # keep the bump one size
    return np.degrees(np.arctan2((u * np.sin(pref)).sum(), (u * np.cos(pref)).sum()))

for turn in (90.0, -90.0):                       # degrees per second
    print("turning %+6.1f deg/s -> heading %+6.1f deg after 1 s"
          % (turn, run(np.radians(turn))))

It prints plus 90.1 degrees for a rightward turn and minus 90.1 for a leftward one.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only cell whose firing depends on which way the head points and nothing else, and one cue card shifts every such cell in lockstep.

CHAPTER 288. BORDER CELLS AND BOUNDARY VECTOR CELLS (COG)

Walk along a wall with your eyes shut and your hand trailing the plaster, and somewhere in your brain a small set of cells is firing for exactly that, and only that.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A border cell fires when the animal is alongside a boundary such as a wall or a drop, and a boundary vector cell fires at a preferred distance in a preferred direction.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

They are stellate in entorhinal layer II and pyramidal deeper, and in the subiculum pyramidal cells that often burst, one stimulus drawing a packet of spikes.

SIZE:

Subicular bodies are 15 to 25 micrometres across with dendrites of 500 to 800, and entorhinal border cells match their grid cell neighbours.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Border cells proper hug a wall within 10 centimetres, boundary vector cells prefer distances up to half a metre with fields broadening as that distance grows.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Border cells occur in all layers of medial entorhinal cortex and para-subiculum, while boundary vector cells fill the dorsal subiculum.

The key interface is the subiculum to entorhinal boundary, crossed in both directions.

NUMBER:

Zero additional neurons, since both labels apply to cells already counted in Part 12.

Border cells are fewer than one in ten entorhinal cells, and the 2008 report called even that an upper limit.

The founding 2009 study identified 36 subicular boundary vector cells, and the human subiculum holds 4 to 5 million neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Peak rates are 5 to 15 spikes per second in the band along the wall against silence elsewhere, with bursts at 100 to 200 hertz.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input comes from visual and somatosensory cortex through the post-rhinal and peri-rhinal cortices, and whiskers alone drive them in darkness.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Subicular cells send long axons to entorhinal cortex, mammillary bodies and retrosplenial cortex, while border axons take the perforant path into the hippocampus.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, with local GABA interneurons sharpening the band of firing against the wall.

MYELINATION:

The subicular output runs in the fornix, one of the most heavily myelinated bundles in the human brain, while local collaterals are bare.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

These cells are quiet through the long stretches spent mid-room, so average demand is low.

PLASTICITY:

Firing follows the geometry rather than the memory of it, so a new wall grows a second band within minutes and without training.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

No specialised glia have been described here, and the astrocytes, oligodendrocytes and microglia around a border cell are ordinary residents.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and the map loses its pins, so the grid slides and place fields stop tracking the room's shape.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The computation is a true vector one, a distance and a direction in a frame fixed to the world, which needs head direction to rotate that frame first.

Subicular bursts arise from dendritic calcium spikes, and their reset of grid cells is tied to theta phase.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: bearing and distance to each boundary from vision, whiskers and self-motion, 0 to 360 degrees and 0 to 1 metre; head direction; theta.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate spikes to grid cells, place cells and retrosplenial cortex, 0 to 15 hertz.

• STATE: membrane voltage; dendritic calcium; burst state; distance and bearing to the nearest boundary.

• PARAMETERS: preferred distance 0 to 0.6 metres; distance tuning 5 to 20 centimetres; angular tuning 20 to 40 degrees; peak rate 5 to 15 hertz.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; burst 10 to 30; a new wall grows a field within minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: subicular damage and early Alzheimer's pathology weaken the anchor, so a patient loses track of which end of a corridor they entered.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the rate of one boundary vector cell by looking out in every direction and asking how far the nearest wall is.
Create a number variable called preferred distance with initial value 0.10.
Create a number variable called preferred bearing with initial value 180.
Create a number variable called total response with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called ray count with initial value 0.
While ray count is less than 360, repeat the following steps.
    Set wall distance to the distance from here to the first wall met along ray count degrees.
    Calculate the difference of wall distance and preferred distance.
    Set distance match to a bell curve of the result over 0.055.
    Calculate the difference of ray count and preferred bearing.
    Set bearing match to a bell curve of the result over 22 degrees.
    Calculate the product of distance match and bearing match.
    Calculate the sum of total response and the result.
    Set total response to the result.
    Calculate the sum of ray count and 1.
    Set ray count to the result.
End the loop.
Display total response to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python, because casting 360 rays is clearer than it is fast.

import math
W = H = 1.0                                   # one metre square arena
D_PREF, SIG_D = 0.10, 0.055                   # preferred distance and tolerance, metres
TH_PREF, SIG_TH = math.pi, math.radians(22.0) # this cell prefers a wall due west

def wall_distance(x, y, th):                  # first wall met along bearing th
    best = float("inf")
    for v, lo, hi in ((math.cos(th), x, W), (math.sin(th), y, H)):
        if abs(v) > 1e-12:
            t = ((hi - lo) if v > 0 else -lo) / v
            if 0.0 < t < best: best = t
    return best

def bvc(x, y, nray=360):                      # response summed over all bearings
    tot = 0.0
    for i in range(nray):
        th = 2.0 * math.pi * i / nray
        d = wall_distance(x, y, th)
        off = ((th - TH_PREF + math.pi) % (2.0 * math.pi)) - math.pi
        tot += math.exp(-((d - D_PREF) ** 2) / (2 * SIG_D ** 2) - off * off / (2 * SIG_TH ** 2))
    return tot
