f = [[bvc(ix * 0.05, iy * 0.05) for ix in range(21)] for iy in range(21)]
pk = max(max(r) for r in f)
for iy in range(20, -1, -1):                  # print the arena, north at the top
    print("".join("#" if f[iy][ix] > 0.4 * pk else "." for ix in range(21)))

The printed map is one vertical stripe hugging the western wall, exactly what a cell tuned to 10 centimetres due west should give.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Move a wall and the band moves with it at once, without learning, and in any room shape the cell hugs an edge at the same distance and bearing.

CHAPTER 289. SPEED CELLS (COG)

Behind your eyes is a speedometer made of nerve cells, and like any honest speedometer its needle rises in a straight line as you go faster, no matter what road you are on.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A speed cell is a medial entorhinal neuron whose rate rises in proportion to how fast the body moves, giving the same rate for the same speed in any room, lit or dark.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Some are principal stellate and pyramidal cells of layers II and III, but many of the strongest are fast-spiking interneurons, small inhibitory cells with smooth dendrites and almost no spines.

SIZE:

Fast-spiking interneurons have compact bodies of 10 to 18 micrometres with dendrites of 200 to 400, and principal speed cells match their grid cell neighbours.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Linear speed cells rise as a straight line, saturating ones flatten at high speed, negative ones fall, and grid and head direction cells are often speed modulated too.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The described population sits in medial entorhinal layers II, III and V, with speed firing also in hippocampus, septum and para-subiculum.

The critical interface is entorhinal cortex to hippocampus, because these fast-spiking cells project straight in, which inhibitory neurons rarely do.

NUMBER:

Zero additional neurons, since speed cell labels entorhinal principal cells and interneurons already counted in Part 12.

The 2015 report put them at roughly 15 percent of medial entorhinal neurons, which for 7 million human neurons per hemisphere is of order one million.

That rat to human extrapolation is an order of magnitude only.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Tuning takes two numbers, an intercept of 2 to 15 spikes per second at rest and a slope of 5 to 20 more per metre per second, and it leads actual speed by 50 to 80 milliseconds.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input comes from the medial septum, from vestibular and proprioceptive pathways, from optic flow and from efference copy, and these cells inhibit stellate and hippocampal neurons.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Local axons ramify within a few hundred micrometres, while the projecting subset reaches millimetres into the hippocampus.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA, the main inhibitory messenger, from the interneurons, most of which carry parvalbumin, a calcium-binding protein that lets them fire fast without tiring.

MYELINATION:

Parvalbumin axons are among the few inhibitory axons that are myelinated, in patches, because a speedometer reading that arrives late is a wrong reading.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Fast-spiking parvalbumin cells are the most expensive neurons in the cortex, which is why they fail early under oxidative stress.

PLASTICITY:

The code is deliberately stable, since a ruler that changed length with experience would be useless, though its slope shifts with arousal.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The perineuronal net around these cells, a lattice of sugar and protein built with astrocyte help, is genuinely specialised, and local astrocytes clear potassium unusually fast.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete speed cells and the entorhinal cortex keeps its map and compass and loses its odometer, so the grid bump stops tracking the animal.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The forward bias means the cell predicts rather than measures, so the circuit holds a body model.

Gap junctions, direct electrical pores between these interneurons, synchronise them and generate gamma rhythms at 30 to 100 hertz.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: vestibular and proprioceptive self-motion, 0 to 1.5 metres per second walking; optic flow; efference copy; septal drive.

• OUTPUTS: GABA onto entorhinal stellate cells and hippocampal neurons, or glutamate for the principal subtype, 0 to 100 hertz.

• STATE: membrane voltage; a buffer holding speed 50 to 80 milliseconds ahead; short-term depression at the output synapse.

• PARAMETERS: intercept 2 to 15 hertz; slope 5 to 20 hertz per metre per second; saturation constant near 0.5 metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 5 to 10 milliseconds, unusually fast; synaptic 2 to 5; prospective lead 50 to 80.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of parvalbumin function in schizophrenia and ageing degrades the speed code and gamma, and the grid loses its lock on movement.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn running speed into the firing rate of a speed cell, looking slightly into the future as the real cell does.
Create a number variable called intercept with initial value 5.
Create a number variable called slope with initial value 12.
Create a number variable called look ahead with initial value 14.
Create a list variable called rates with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called step index with initial value 0.
While step index is less than the total number of items in speed record, repeat the following steps.
    Calculate the sum of step index and look ahead.
    Set future index to the smaller of the result and the last position in speed record.
    Access the item at position future index in speed record.
    Calculate the product of slope and the result.
    Calculate the sum of intercept and the result.
    Set firing rate to the result.
    Add firing rate to the end of rates.
    Calculate the sum of step index and 1.
    Set step index to the result.
End the loop.
Display the total number of items in rates to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python with NumPy, because the point is the statistics that recover the slope and the lead.

import numpy as np
DT, INTERCEPT, SLOPE, LEAD = 0.005, 5.0, 12.0, 0.070   # s, Hz, Hz per m/s, forward bias
SAT = 0.50                                             # saturation constant, metres per second
t = np.arange(0.0, 120.0, DT)                          # two minutes of running
speed = 0.02 + 0.40 * (1 - np.cos(2 * np.pi * t / 5.0)) + 0.10 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * t / 1.3)
lag = int(round(LEAD / DT))
future = np.roll(speed, -lag)                          # the cell reports future speed
linear = INTERCEPT + SLOPE * future
saturating = INTERCEPT + SLOPE * SAT * (1 - np.exp(-future / SAT))
for name, rate in (("linear", linear), ("saturating", saturating)):
    slope, inter = np.polyfit(speed[:-lag], rate[:-lag], 1)
    print("%-11s fitted slope %5.2f Hz per m/s, intercept %.2f Hz" % (name, slope, inter))
n, m = len(linear), 40                                 # search for the best time shift
best = max(range(-m, m + 1),
           key=lambda L: np.corrcoef(speed[m:n - m], linear[m + L:n - m + L])[0, 1])
print("the rate leads the speed by %.0f ms" % (-best * DT * 1000.0))

It recovers a slope of 11.91 hertz per metre per second, an intercept of 5.04 hertz and a 70 millisecond lead, and shows the saturating variant reading only 5.63.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only cell here that says nothing about where you are and everything about how fast you are going, holding its line in a new room.

CHAPTER 290. TIME CELLS (COG)

Hold still for ten seconds and a relay race runs through your hippocampus, each runner carrying the baton for a fraction of a second, and the position in the race is the time elapsed.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A time cell is a hippocampal or entorhinal neuron that fires at one particular moment of a delay, and since different cells own different moments the population tiles the interval and gives a memory its when, as place cells give it its where.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Time cells are the very same pyramidal cells that act as place cells, with a triangular body, an apical dendrite in the entorhinal input layers and basal dendrites below.

SIZE:

The same as hippocampal pyramidal cells, a body of 15 to 30 micrometres in the human with a dendritic tree of one to two millimetres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Time cells proper have one narrow field, while ramping cells rise or fall steadily across the interval, and both have been recorded in humans.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Hippocampal fields CA1, CA2 and CA3 and the entorhinal cortex, in the same layers as place cells.

The essential interface is the CA2 to CA1 projection, since silencing CA2 disorganises the CA1 sequence and damages memory.

NUMBER:

Zero additional neurons, because time cell labels cells already counted in Part 12, often the very cells called place cells earlier.

In rodent CA1 during a fixed delay about a quarter of active pyramidal cells show a time field, published values running from ten percent to over half.

No human population count exists, so the honest bound is the 40 million neurons of one hippocampal formation.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

In-field rates are 5 to 15 spikes per second against near silence outside, and fields grow wider the later they occur, so the timeline is compressed and close to logarithmic.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input arrives from CA3 through the Schaffer collaterals, from CA2 and from entorhinal cortex, while interneurons impose the theta rhythm that carves the sequence into steps.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Identical to place cells, since they are the same axons: bare collaterals inside CA1 and long-range fibres in the fornix.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate on AMPA and NMDA receptors, with septal acetylcholine and local GABA setting the timing framework.

MYELINATION:

As for place cells, a bare axon start, patchy local myelin and a properly myelinated long-range output.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Each cell is active for only a slice of the interval, so the sequence spreads its load, though theta runs throughout the delay.

PLASTICITY:

Time fields form rapidly on a new interval and rescale rather than run out when a delay is stretched.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes supply D-serine as for place cells, but their own calcium signals run over seconds to tens of seconds, the span a sequence covers, so they plausibly feed the slow drift the code needs.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove time cells and the hippocampus says where an event happened but not when, so you recall the kitchen, the argument and the phone call but not their order.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The sequence is internally generated in an animal standing still in the dark, proof that the hippocampus makes its own trajectories rather than reporting the senses.

It rests on drifting entorhinal states, dendritic plateaus and short-term depression acting as a clock, and the same sequences replay in ripples at up to twenty times speed.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: cue onset; drifting context from lateral entorhinal cortex; CA3 and CA2 drive; theta 6 to 12 hertz.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate spikes to subiculum, entorhinal layer V and prefrontal cortex, 0 to 15 hertz.

• STATE: elapsed time since the cue; membrane voltage; short-term depression; which cell is active.

• PARAMETERS: peak time 0.1 to 30 seconds after the cue; field width proportional to peak time, Weber fraction 0.2 to 0.35.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 30 milliseconds; theta 125; field width 200 milliseconds early to seconds late; sequence 1 to 30 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: silencing CA2 scrambles the sequence, septal inactivation abolishes the code, and in Alzheimer's and Korsakoff syndrome the ordering of memories fails first.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to read elapsed time out of time cells whose fields widen the later they fire.
Create a number variable called log width with initial value 0.28.
Create a list variable called peak times with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called peak index with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called weighted total with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called rate total with initial value 0.
Set peak ratio to 40 raised to the power of one divided by 23.
While peak index is less than 24, repeat the following steps.
    Calculate the product of 0.25 and peak ratio raised to the power peak index.
    Add the result to the end of peak times.
    Calculate the sum of peak index and 1.
    Set peak index to the result.
End the loop.
For each peak in peak times, perform the following operations.
    Set log gap to the logarithm of elapsed time minus the logarithm of peak.
    Calculate the quotient of log gap divided by log width.
    Set this rate to a bell curve of the result.
    Calculate the product of this rate and the logarithm of peak.
    Calculate the sum of weighted total and the result.
    Set weighted total to the result.
    Calculate the sum of rate total and this rate.
    Set rate total to the result.
End the loop.
Calculate the quotient of weighted total divided by rate total.
Display the exponential of the result to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python, because the point is a population read-out.

import math
N, DELAY, T0 = 24, 10.0, 0.25            # cells, interval, first peak time
PEAK_HZ, SIG = 14.0, 0.28                # peak rate, log-time width (Weber fraction)
RATIO = (DELAY / T0) ** (1.0 / (N - 1))  # geometric tiling
PEAKS = [T0 * RATIO ** i for i in range(N)]

def rate(i, t):                          # Gaussian in log time, so fields widen
    if t <= 0.0: return 0.0
    z = (math.log(t) - math.log(PEAKS[i])) / SIG
    return PEAK_HZ * math.exp(-0.5 * z * z)

def width(i):                            # width at half maximum, seconds
    return PEAKS[i] * (math.exp(1.1774 * SIG) - math.exp(-1.1774 * SIG))

def decode(t):                           # population estimate of elapsed time
    num = den = 0.0
    for i in range(N):
        r = rate(i, t); num += r * math.log(PEAKS[i]); den += r
    return math.exp(num / den) if den else float("nan")

for i in (0, N - 1):
    print("cell %2d: peak %.2f s, width %.2f s" % (i + 1, PEAKS[i], width(i)))
for t in (0.5, 1.0, 2.0, 4.0, 8.0):
    print("elapsed %5.2f s -> estimate %5.2f s" % (t, decode(t)))

The first cell peaks at 0.25 seconds with a field 0.17 wide, the last at 10 seconds with a field 6.71 wide, and elapsed time decodes almost exactly mid-range.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only representation known to fire in a fixed order while the animal is still and nothing is happening, its fields blurring in proportion to how late they come.

CHAPTER 291. OBJECT VECTOR CELLS (COG)

Put a mug on the floor of an empty room and one cell fires every time you stand twenty-five centimetres north-east of it, and it does the same for a chair.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A vector is an arrow, having only a length and a direction.

An object vector cell fires whenever the animal stands at one particular arrow from any freestanding object, whatever that object is and wherever it sits.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A job description, not a shape: most are stellate cells of medial entorhinal layer two, star-shaped neurons whose thick input branches, called dendrites, radiate outward like a sea urchin.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are fifteen to twenty-five micrometres across, a quarter the width of a human hair, and the firing field they paint on the floor is ten to forty centimetres wide.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Near-tuned cells fire within ten centimetres of an object, far-tuned cells up to a metre away.

• Bearing subtypes tile the compass, each preferring one allocentric direction, meaning a direction fixed to the room rather than to the body.

• Relatives include border cells, boundary vector cells of the subiculum, and object-trace cells of the lateral entorhinal cortex.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The medial entorhinal cortex, a strip of ancient cortex on the inner face of the temporal lobe, in layers two, three and five, plus the neighbouring parasubiculum.

That region is the interface where the outer cortex speaks to the hippocampus, the memory organ, along the perforant path into the dentate gyrus and areas CA3 and CA1.

NUMBER:

Zero additional neurons, since this is a functional label on medial entorhinal cells already counted.

Measured fractions are 14.7 percent of cells in the founding 2019 mouse study by Hoydal and colleagues and 13.6 percent, 67 of 492 cells, in the 2021 follow-up.

The human entorhinal cortex holds about seven million neurons per hemisphere, so a similar fraction implies a few hundred thousand per side, a low-confidence bound because none has yet been recorded in a human, and against 86 billion neurons overall the addition is exactly zero percent.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Stellate cells sag, drooping slowly in voltage through pores called hyperpolarisation-activated cyclic-nucleotide-gated channels, or HCN1, which tunes them to ring at four to twelve cycles per second, and they fire under one spike per second outside the field and five to twenty inside it.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs bring objects and scenes from postrhinal and lateral entorhinal cortex, head direction from the presubiculum, and rhythm from the medial septum.

Strikingly, these cells barely contact one another, talking through inhibitory interneurons that use the calming transmitter gamma-aminobutyric acid, abbreviated GABA.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The output cable is thin, 0.5 to 1.5 micrometres, conducts at one to several metres per second and branches across millimetres of hippocampus, while layer five axons run back to the neocortex.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, the standard excitatory messenger, acting on fast AMPA receptors and learning-related NMDA receptors, with acetylcholine from the medial septum switching the region between encoding and retrieval.

MYELINATION:

Lightly and patchily myelinated, meaning the fatty insulating sheath comes in stretches with bare gaps, so perforant path transmission takes several milliseconds.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High and dangerously so, because entorhinal layer two is the first place neurons die in Alzheimer's disease, losing about 60 percent of its cells in the mildest dementia.

PLASTICITY:

Almost no learning is needed, since these fields appear on the first pass around a newly placed object, so the vector code is pre-built even though the objects change constantly.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia here are broadly ordinary and it is honest to say so.

Two things differ: these neurons secrete reelin, a protein instructing their own scaffold, and microglia, the brain's immune cells, turn abnormally active in this exact layer very early in Alzheimer's disease.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the map keeps its positions and walls but loses all its furniture, so every object becomes invisible to navigation and the lost flavour is "relative to that thing over there".

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

HCN1 channels make the membrane a band-pass filter, so spike timing against the theta rhythm carries meaning that rate alone does not, and regenerative NMDA spikes let one dendritic branch act as its own processor.

Vision alone suffices, since a flat patch of contrast on a wall drives the cell as well as a solid object, and the cell also raises local blood flow and makes new proteins inside the dendrite.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: allocentric object bearing 0 to 360 degrees and distance 0 to 150 centimetres from postrhinal and lateral entorhinal cortex.

◦ Head direction 0 to 360 degrees, theta phase 4 to 12 hertz, running speed 0 to 100 centimetres per second.

• OUTPUTS: spike train 0 to 25 hertz to dentate gyrus, CA3, CA1 and entorhinal layer five.

• STATE: membrane voltage, HCN1 activation, best current vector match, adaptation, theta phase.

• PARAMETERS: preferred distance 5 to 100 centimetres, preferred bearing 0 to 360 degrees, distance width 5 to 20 centimetres, bearing width 20 to 60 degrees.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 30 milliseconds, theta cycle 80 to 250 milliseconds, field formation under one second.

• FAILURE MODE: Alzheimer's disease kills about 60 percent of layer two neurons at the mildest stage of dementia, and patients lose the ability to orient by landmarks.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the firing rate of an object vector cell from its distance and bearing to each object.
Create a number variable called preferred distance with initial value 25.
Create a number variable called preferred bearing with initial value 45.
Create a number variable called best drive with initial value 0.
For each object in object list, perform the following operations.
    Set distance gap to the difference of the distance to object and preferred distance.
    If distance gap is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set distance gap to the product of distance gap and negative one.
    End the conditional block.
    Set bearing gap to the difference of the bearing to object and preferred bearing.
    If bearing gap is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set bearing gap to the product of bearing gap and negative one.
    End the conditional block.
    Set drive to the product of the difference of 1 and the quotient of distance gap divided by 8 and the difference of 1 and the quotient of bearing gap divided by 30.
    If drive is greater than best drive, then perform the following actions.
        Set best drive to drive.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Set firing rate to the sum of 0.8 and the product of best drive and 18.
Display firing rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the geometry reads at a glance and needs no library.

import math

PREF_DIST, PREF_BEAR = 25.0, 45.0   # centimetres, degrees anticlockwise from east
DIST_SIGMA, KAPPA = 8.0, 6.0        # widths of distance and bearing tuning
PEAK_HZ, BASE_HZ = 18.0, 0.8

def ov_rate(x, y, objects):
    """Spikes per second at position (x, y), in centimetres."""
    best = 0.0
    for (ox, oy) in objects:        # every object votes, the strongest wins
        dist = math.hypot(x - ox, y - oy)
        bearing = math.atan2(y - oy, x - ox)
        d = math.exp(-((dist - PREF_DIST) ** 2) / (2 * DIST_SIGMA ** 2))
        b = math.exp(KAPPA * (math.cos(bearing - math.radians(PREF_BEAR)) - 1.0))
        best = max(best, d * b)
    return BASE_HZ + (PEAK_HZ - BASE_HZ) * best

objs = [(0.0, 0.0), (100.0, 100.0)]                 # two identical objects
px, py = 25.0 * math.cos(math.pi / 4), 25.0 * math.sin(math.pi / 4)
print(round(ov_rate(px, py, objs), 2))              # 18.0, the tuned corner
print(round(ov_rate(px + 100, py + 100, objs), 2))  # 18.0, same vector, other object
print(round(ov_rate(-25.0, 0.0, objs), 2))          # 0.8, right distance, wrong bearing

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Move the object and the field moves with it, and take the object away and the field vanishes, unlike a grid cell's, which survives an empty room.

CHAPTER 292. CONCEPT CELLS, SOMETIMES POPULARLY CALLED "JENNIFER ANISTON NEURONS" (COG)

In 2005 a team recording from a hospital patient found one cell that fired for photographs of the actress Jennifer Aniston, for her name in letters, and for her name spoken aloud.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A concept cell responds to the meaning of a thing rather than its look, firing alike for a person seen from the front, in profile, as a cartoon or as a written name.

Show it a hundred other people and it stays silent, which gives each idea a tiny, stable tag that memory can link to others in an instant.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Again a job, not a body plan: these are pyramidal neurons of hippocampal areas CA1 and CA3, with a pyramid-shaped body and one thick apical dendrite climbing towards the brain surface, plus dentate granule cells.

SIZE:

Human hippocampal pyramidal cell bodies are fifteen to thirty micrometres across with dendritic trees spanning over a millimetre, while granule cells are about ten micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Single-concept cells respond to one person, place or object and nothing else in the test set.

• Multi-concept cells respond to two or three personally linked concepts, thought to be the physical trace of an association.

• Multimodal cells respond to picture, written name and spoken name alike, while relational cells, reported in 2021, signal abstract relations between concepts.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Four human structures: the hippocampus including dentate gyrus, CA3 and CA1; the amygdala; the entorhinal cortex; and the parahippocampal cortex.

The entorhinal cortex is the crucial interface, the doorway between the sensory cortex that recognises a face and the hippocampus that remembers meeting it.

NUMBER:

Zero additional neurons, since this is a functional label on medial temporal principal cells already counted.

The parent populations are about 40 million neurons per human hippocampus, including 15 million dentate granule cells and 16 million in CA1, plus 10 to 15 million in the amygdala and about 7 million in the entorhinal cortex.

Modelling by Waydo and colleagues in 2006 put the chance that any medial temporal neuron responds to any given familiar image at roughly 0.1 percent, about a million cells out of a billion and called an upper bound, and against 86 billion neurons overall the addition is exactly zero percent.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Baseline firing is often well under one spike per second, which is why the silence is so striking, and a response is a burst of ten to thirty spikes per second arriving late, about 204 milliseconds in the hippocampus and 220 in the amygdala.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Identity arrives from anterior temporal visual areas through perirhinal and entorhinal cortex already stripped of viewpoint, while names arrive through lateral temporal language cortex.

Area CA3 is heavily recurrent, meaning its cells contact each other, the classic architecture for rebuilding a whole memory from a fragment.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin, one micrometre or less, conducting at about one metre per second, and the granule cell axons called mossy fibres make only fifteen contacts each onto CA3 cells, every one so large it can fire its target single-handedly.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate for output, received by AMPA and NMDA receptors, while GABA from parvalbumin basket cells enforces sparseness by silencing every cell that is merely nearly right.

MYELINATION:

Sparse and patchy along hippocampal axons, though the fornix, the great cable leaving the hippocampus, is well insulated, so signals leave faster than they travel inside.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest at rest given the low firing rate, but area CA1 dies first after cardiac arrest, which shows how tight its oxygen margin really is.

PLASTICITY:

This is the plastic cell type par excellence, with recordings catching cells acquiring responses to newly learned associations within a single session, through the lasting strengthening of connections called long-term potentiation.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Hippocampal astrocytes, the star-shaped support cells, do what astrocytes elsewhere do not: they release D-serine, a molecule required as a partner for NMDA receptors, so local glia are part of the learning switch.

They also hand lactate to neurons during memory formation, and blocking that transfer blocks long-term memory in animals.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take them away and the medial temporal lobe still registers places and emotional tone, but nothing has a name, there is no compact handle for "my mother", and the flavour lost is meaning itself.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Dendritic branches fire local NMDA and calcium spikes that perform small logical operations before anything reaches the cell body, and back-propagating action potentials, spikes travelling backwards into the dendrites, report that the cell has just fired.

In rest and sleep these cells replay in compressed sequence inside sharp-wave ripples at 80 to 200 hertz, which is how memories consolidate.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: a high-level identity vector from anterior temporal and perirhinal cortex, typically 100 to 1000 dimensions normalised to unit length.

◦ Auditory and written-word vectors in the same space, emotional gain 0 to 1 from the amygdala, theta phase 4 to 8 hertz, ripples 80 to 200 hertz.

• OUTPUTS: a sparse burst 0 to 30 hertz to dentate gyrus, CA3, CA1, subiculum and back to entorhinal cortex.

• STATE: membrane voltage, adaptation current, stored prototype vector, association weights, plasticity tag.

• PARAMETERS: match threshold 0.5 to 0.8 cosine similarity, baseline 0.05 to 1 hertz, peak 10 to 30 hertz, sparseness 0.1 percent of the population per stimulus.

• TIME CONSTANTS: latency 200 to 400 milliseconds, burst 300 to 800 milliseconds, membrane 15 to 30 milliseconds, new concepts within hours.

• FAILURE MODE: hippocampal sclerosis in temporal lobe epilepsy destroys CA1 and CA3 cells and leaves patients unable to form new memories, while Alzheimer's disease severs the entorhinal input first.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether a concept cell fires for the stimulus now presented, no matter which sense it arrives through.
Create a number variable called match score with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called threshold with initial value 0.62.
Create a number variable called index with initial value 0.
While index is less than the total number of items in prototype list, repeat the following steps.
    Set feature weight to the item at position index in prototype list.
    Set feature strength to the item at position index in stimulus list.
    Set match score to the sum of match score and the product of feature weight and feature strength.
    Set index to the sum of index and 1.
End the loop.
If match score is less than threshold, then perform the following actions.
    Set firing rate to 0.15.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Set gain to the quotient of the difference of match score and threshold divided by the difference of 1 and threshold.
    Set firing rate to the sum of 0.15 and the product of gain and 22.
    Display 300 to the console.
End the conditional block.
Display firing rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because a dictionary makes one cell holding one concept across many senses immediately visible.

import math

# One cell, one prototype, in a meaning space shared by sight, reading and hearing.
PROTOTYPE = {"actress": 1.0, "friends_tv": 0.9, "blonde": 0.4, "eiffel": 0.0}
BASE_HZ, PEAK_HZ, THRESHOLD = 0.15, 22.0, 0.62   # a high threshold keeps it sparse

def cosine(a, b):
    dot = sum(a.get(k, 0.0) * b.get(k, 0.0) for k in set(a) | set(b))
    na = math.sqrt(sum(v * v for v in a.values()))
    nb = math.sqrt(sum(v * v for v in b.values()))
    return 0.0 if na == 0.0 or nb == 0.0 else dot / (na * nb)

def concept_cell(stimulus):
    """Return firing rate in spikes per second and latency in milliseconds."""
    m = cosine(stimulus, PROTOTYPE)
    if m < THRESHOLD:
        return BASE_HZ, None                  # silence, the usual outcome
    return BASE_HZ + PEAK_HZ * (m - THRESHOLD) / (1.0 - THRESHOLD), 300.0

for label, s in (("photo", {"actress": 1.0, "friends_tv": 0.8, "blonde": 0.5}),
                 ("written name", {"actress": 0.9, "friends_tv": 1.0}),
                 ("tower", {"eiffel": 1.0, "landmark": 1.0})):
    rate, lat = concept_cell(s)
    print(label, round(rate, 2), "Hz", lat)

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Total silence for ninety-nine pictures, a hard burst for the hundredth, and the same burst for the photograph, the written name and the spoken name alike.

CHAPTER 293. MIRROR NEURONS (COG)

In a Parma laboratory a monkey sat perfectly still while a researcher reached for a peanut, and the monkey's motor cortex crackled as though the monkey itself had reached.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A mirror neuron fires both when you perform a goal-directed action and when you watch someone else perform it.

The goal is what matters, since these cells ignore a hand that merely moves and care about grasping, tearing or holding a thing.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Ordinary pyramidal neurons of layers three and five, triangular-bodied, with one thick apical dendrite reaching the cortical surface, and some are corticospinal cells whose output cable runs to the spinal cord to command muscles directly.

SIZE:

Layer five pyramidal cell bodies in premotor cortex are twenty to fifty micrometres across, among the largest in the cortex, with an apical dendrite up to two millimetres long.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Strictly congruent cells fire for exactly the same action executed and observed, such as a precision grip in both cases.

• Broadly congruent cells fire for a related action, such as precision grip executed and any grasp observed, and outnumber the strict kind.

• Anti-mirror cells rise during execution and fall below baseline during observation, and the eleven found in humans may be what stops you copying what you watch.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the macaque, area F5 of ventral premotor cortex, area PFG of the inferior parietal lobule, and primary motor cortex; in humans, by direct recording, the supplementary motor area plus hippocampus, entorhinal cortex and parahippocampal gyrus.

Area F5 is the interface itself, sitting where the parietal cortex that computes what an object affords meets the motor system that acts on it, fed by the superior temporal sulcus, which analyses biological motion but has no motor cells of its own.

NUMBER:

Zero additional neurons, since this is a functional label on premotor, parietal, motor and medial temporal pyramidal cells already counted.

Gallese, Fadiga, Fogassi and Rizzolatti recorded 532 neurons in monkey area F5 and found 92 with mirror properties, about 17 percent.

Mukamel and colleagues recorded 1,177 human cells and found 68 matching ones, about 5.8 percent, of which 33 rose during both observation and execution, 21 fell during both and 14 moved oppositely, and against 86 billion neurons overall the addition is exactly zero percent.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These cells fire in bursts driven by calcium spikes in the apical dendrite, reaching fifty to one hundred spikes per second during a grasp and roughly half that during observation, and unusually they do not adapt to watching the same action again and again.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Descriptions of action arrive from the superior temporal sulcus by way of the inferior parietal lobule, and object graspability from the anterior intraparietal area.

Outputs run to primary motor cortex, to the spinal cord for corticospinal cells, and back to parietal cortex, each cell carrying ten to thirty thousand excitatory contacts.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Premotor corticospinal axons are among the thickest in the body, one to ten micrometres including the sheath, conducting at ten to seventy metres per second, with side branches spreading millimetres across the cortex.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate for output, while local GABA-releasing interneurons supply the inhibition that stops observation turning into imitation, and midbrain dopamine drives learning of new links between action and outcome.

MYELINATION:

Heavily myelinated on the corticospinal side, forming one of the most conspicuous white matter bundles in the human body, while local branches inside the cortex are insulated only in patches.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high, because a corticospinal cell must keep an axon up to a metre long supplied with proteins by continuous transport, day and night, on top of an enormous dendritic membrane.

PLASTICITY:

The associative account, argued by Heyes and Catmur, holds that mirroring is learned, because you see your own hand whenever you act, and brief training pairing an observed action with an incompatible executed one can reverse mirror responses, which a hard-wired system should not permit.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Nothing distinctive is known about glia around mirror neurons specifically, and that should be said plainly rather than invented.

What is distinctive belongs to the host cells, since oligodendrocytes maintain sheaths up to a metre from their own body and feed lactate straight into the axon, and motor myelination is activity-dependent, so practice changes the insulation on the very cable that carries the action.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Strip mirror properties from the premotor cortex and it still plans and executes movement, but it loses its resonance, so watching someone lift a cup no longer primes your own lifting machinery and "I see what you are doing" becomes slow inference instead of an instant.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Layer five cells are two-compartment computers, bursting only when top-down context reaching the apical tuft coincides with a back-propagating spike from the body, and they instruct their own myelination by maturing oligodendrocyte precursors when used.

They remodel dendritic spines within hours of motor learning, drive local blood flow, and are actively suppressed during observation, which is a mechanical solution to the problem of watching without moving.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: an observed action code from the superior temporal sulcus by way of the inferior parietal lobule, categorical, with confidence 0 to 1.

◦ Object affordance from the anterior intraparietal area, motor intention from prefrontal and supplementary motor areas, proprioceptive feedback from one's own limb.

• OUTPUTS: spike train 0 to 100 hertz to primary motor cortex, spinal motor circuits and parietal cortex.

• STATE: membrane voltage, apical calcium compartment, learned visual-to-motor weights, execution versus observation gain, inhibitory brake level.

• PARAMETERS: execution gain 1.0, observation gain 0.3 to 0.7, congruence breadth strict to broad, baseline 1 to 5 hertz, peak 40 to 100 hertz.

• TIME CONSTANTS: visual-to-motor latency 100 to 200 milliseconds, burst 50 to 200 milliseconds, membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds, retraining within tens of minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: the broken mirror hypothesis of autism is not supported in its pure form, since meta-analyses find no general mirror abnormality and one large analysis found the right inferior frontal gyrus more active during complex social tasks, whereas the well-established failures strike the parent cells, in corticospinal degeneration in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis and in premotor stroke causing apraxia, the loss of skilled purposeful movement.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the firing rate of a mirror neuron during both self-produced action and observed action.
Create a number variable called observation gain with initial value 0.55.
Create a number variable called goal match with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
If current goal is equal to preferred goal, then perform the following actions.
    Set goal match to 1.
Else if current goal is equal to related goal, then perform the following actions.
    Set goal match to 0.45.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Set goal match to 0.
End the conditional block.
If object is present is not true, then perform the following actions.
    Set goal match to 0.
End the conditional block.
If mode is equal to execute, then perform the following actions.
    Set drive to goal match.
Else if mode is equal to observe, then perform the following actions.
    Set drive to the product of observation gain and goal match.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Set drive to 0.
End the conditional block.
Set firing rate to the sum of 2 and the product of drive and 43.
Display firing rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the two-channel congruence test reads almost exactly like the original experiment.

GOAL_TUNING = "precision_grip"   # the goal this particular cell cares about
EXEC_GAIN, OBS_GAIN = 1.0, 0.55  # observing drives the cell less than doing
BASE_HZ, PEAK_HZ = 2.0, 45.0
# Broadly congruent: full match for its own goal, partial match for a cousin.
CONGRUENCE = {"precision_grip": 1.0, "whole_hand_grip": 0.45}

def mirror_rate(goal, mode, visible):
    """mode is 'execute' or 'observe'; visible is True if the act can be seen."""
    match = CONGRUENCE.get(goal, 0.0)     # a mime with no object scores zero
    if mode == "execute":
        drive = EXEC_GAIN * match
    elif mode == "observe":
        # F5 cells keep firing when a screen hides the final grasp, provided
        # the observer knows an object is behind it.
        drive = OBS_GAIN * match if (visible or goal == GOAL_TUNING) else 0.0
    else:
        drive = 0.0
    return BASE_HZ + (PEAK_HZ - BASE_HZ) * drive

for goal, mode, visible in [("precision_grip", "execute", True),
                            ("precision_grip", "observe", True),
                            ("precision_grip", "observe", False),  # grasp hidden
                            ("whole_hand_grip", "observe", True),
                            ("mime_no_object", "observe", True)]:  # no object
    print(goal, mode, visible, round(mirror_rate(goal, mode, visible), 1))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

One cell, two utterly different situations, the same burst, and it keeps firing when a screen hides the final moment of the grasp provided the observer knows something is behind it.

CHAPTER 294. FACE CELLS (COG)

There is a patch of cortex about the size of a lentil in which ninety-seven of every hundred cells you can find are interested in faces and in nothing else.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A face cell responds far more strongly to a face than to any other object, and these cells cluster into small, sharply bounded patches.

Each measures the face along one axis of a shared multi-dimensional face space, so a face's identity is simply the list of those measurements.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Pyramidal neurons of the inferior temporal cortex in layers two, three and five, and the patches are histo-architectonically distinct, meaning their layering and cell packing differ measurably from the cortex immediately around them.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are fifteen to thirty-five micrometres across, and the receptive field, the part of the visual world that can excite the cell, is huge at ten to thirty degrees, which is why a face cell fires wherever in view the face appears.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Posterior lateral and middle lateral cells are view-specific, treating a left profile, a right profile and a frontal view as three different things.

• Anterior lateral cells are mirror-symmetric, treating left and right profiles as the same, a halfway step towards identity.

• Anterior medial cells are view-invariant, responding to a person from any angle, and are the closest thing in the monkey brain to a concept cell.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the macaque, six patches along and inside the superior temporal sulcus, named posterior lateral, middle lateral, middle fundal, anterior lateral, anterior fundal and anterior medial, with more in prefrontal cortex.

In humans the equivalents are the occipital face area, the fusiform face area on the ventral temporal lobe and a region in the posterior superior temporal sulcus, with the right hemisphere dominant, and the fusiform gyrus is the interface where visual analysis behind it meets the memory structures in front of it.

NUMBER:

Zero additional neurons, since this is a functional label on inferior temporal and fusiform pyramidal neurons already counted.

Tsao and colleagues found 97 percent of visually responsive cells in the middle lateral and middle fundal patches responded at least twice as strongly to faces as to other objects.

About 205 cells, 106 from one patch and 99 from another, sufficed to reconstruct an arbitrary unseen face from neural activity alone, and against 86 billion neurons overall the addition is exactly zero percent.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Baseline firing is one to five spikes per second and a preferred face drives fifty to over a hundred, arriving in 80 to 130 milliseconds, and the response is a straight ramp, rising with the face's position along this cell's own axis and flat along every other.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from area V4 and posterior inferior temporal cortex carrying shape and colour fragments, and the patches are wired posterior to anterior in a hierarchy that converts view-specificity into identity.

Outputs go to perirhinal and entorhinal cortex and on to the amygdala, hippocampus and prefrontal cortex, the exact route by which a face becomes a memory and a feeling.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Long-range axons run several centimetres through temporal white matter at one to two micrometres thick, while a faster subcortical route through superior colliculus and pulvinar delivers crude face information in 30 to 50 milliseconds.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate for output and GABA for local inhibition from parvalbumin and somatostatin interneurons, with acetylcholine setting attentional gain and oxytocin modulating how strongly faces are attended.

MYELINATION:

The long links between patches and to the amygdala are well myelinated within the inferior longitudinal fasciculus, a major temporal highway, and damage to that bundle can cause face blindness even when the patches survive.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High during waking vision, since face patches are among the most reliably and strongly driven regions of the primate brain, firing in sustained rather than sparse bursts.

PLASTICITY:

Face patches must be built by experience, because monkeys raised without ever seeing a face do not develop them, one of the cleanest demonstrations anywhere that a cortical module is grown rather than delivered ready-made.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Nothing exotic is known about glia inside face patches specifically, and it should be said plainly.

What is distinctive is the vascular supply, since these patches are among the most reliably identifiable regions in brain imaging precisely because their neurovascular coupling, the local rise in blood flow when neurons work, is unusually strong and crisp.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take face cells out and the inferior temporal cortex still recognises cups, cars and trees, but every human being becomes a generic person rather than a person you know, a loss best weighed by imagining a family photograph in which nobody has an identity.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The face cell is linear algebra done by biology, computing a dot product between the incoming face vector and its own preferred axis, which is why a face can be rebuilt from about two hundred cells, and it divides its response by its neighbours' pooled activity to stay stable across contrast and clutter.

Stimulating the human fusiform face area distorts the face a patient is looking at, and stimulating the right anterior fusiform causes a transient inability to recognise faces, so this is causal machinery rather than a correlate.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: a shape and appearance vector from area V4 and posterior inferior temporal cortex, about 50 dimensions split into roughly 25 shape and 25 appearance axes, each about minus 3 to plus 3 standard deviations.

◦ Attention gain 0.8 to 1.5 from frontal eye fields, plus a fast crude face signal from superior colliculus and pulvinar at 30 to 50 milliseconds.

• OUTPUTS: spike train 0 to 120 hertz to other face patches, perirhinal cortex, amygdala, hippocampus and prefrontal cortex.

• STATE: membrane voltage, adaptation level, normalisation pool, current projection on the preferred axis.

• PARAMETERS: preferred axis as a unit vector in face space, baseline 1 to 5 hertz, gain 5 to 20 hertz per standard deviation, receptive field 10 to 30 degrees.

• TIME CONSTANTS: onset 80 to 130 milliseconds, response 100 to 300 milliseconds, adaptation 200 milliseconds to seconds, patch formation over the first months of life.

• FAILURE MODE: prosopagnosia, or face blindness, in which a patient sees a face perfectly well but cannot say whose it is, after damage to the right fusiform region or its white matter, developmentally without any lesion, or transiently under electrical stimulation.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the firing rate of a face cell as a projection of the seen face onto the single axis this cell measures.
Create a number variable called projection with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called index with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called face is present with initial value false.
If the total number of items in face feature list is greater than 0, then perform the following actions.
    Set face is present to true.
End the conditional block.
If face is present is not true, then perform the following actions.
    Set firing rate to 5.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    While index is less than the total number of items in face feature list, repeat the following steps.
        Set axis weight to the item at position index in preferred axis list.
        Set feature value to the item at position index in face feature list.
        Set projection to the sum of projection and the product of axis weight and feature value.
        Set index to the sum of index and 1.
    End the loop.
    Set firing rate to the sum of 5 and the product of 12 and projection.
    If firing rate is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
End the conditional block.
Display firing rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because plain lists show the axis projection and the linear read-out with no library hiding the mathematics.

import math

# A tiny four-dimensional face space; the real macaque code needs about 50 axes.
# These four axes are orthonormal, so decoding is simply the transpose.
AXES = [[0.5, 0.5, 0.5, 0.5],
        [0.5, -0.5, 0.5, -0.5],
        [0.5, 0.5, -0.5, -0.5],
        [0.5, -0.5, -0.5, 0.5]]
BASE_HZ, GAIN_HZ = 5.0, 12.0   # baseline high enough that no cell is driven silent

def dot(a, b):
    return sum(x * y for x, y in zip(a, b))

def face_cell_rates(face):
    """Each cell projects the face onto its own axis and ignores every other."""
    return [max(0.0, BASE_HZ + GAIN_HZ * dot(axis, face)) for axis in AXES]

def decode(rates):
    """Linear read-out: undo the gain, then add the axes weighted by the rates."""
    coeffs = [(r - BASE_HZ) / GAIN_HZ for r in rates]
    return [sum(c * AXES[i][j] for i, c in enumerate(coeffs)) for j in range(4)]

face = [1.2, -0.4, 0.9, 0.3]   # an arbitrary new face, never seen before
rates = face_cell_rates(face)
back = decode(rates)
print("rates:", [round(r, 2) for r in rates])
print("decoded:", [round(v, 2) for v in back])
print("error:", round(math.sqrt(sum((a - b) ** 2 for a, b in zip(face, back))), 4))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Its rate climbs along a straight line as a face slides along one axis of face space and does not budge along any other, and a couple of hundred such cells can rebuild a face nobody has seen before.

CHAPTER 295. ORIENTATION-SELECTIVE AND DIRECTION-SELECTIVE CELLS (COG)

In 1958 two researchers slid a glass slide into a projector, the edge of the slide swept across the screen, and a cell in a cat's brain that had been silent all day began to machine-gun.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An orientation-selective cell fires for an edge tilted one way and falls silent for the same edge turned ninety degrees, while a direction-selective cell fires for motion one way and not the other.

Together they convert a smear of brightness into the two facts that matter most: where the edges are and which way things are going.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

In the cortex these are compact spiny stellate cells of the input layer and pyramidal cells above and below, while in the retina they are direction-selective ganglion cells whose flat branches align precisely with the starburst amacrine cell, a neuron whose dendrites radiate like a firework and which does the actual computing.

SIZE:

Cortical spiny stellate bodies are ten to fifteen micrometres across, and near the fovea one cell may cover less than a tenth of a degree, about a full stop at arm's length.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Orientation subtypes tile all 180 degrees of possible edge angles and are arranged in the cortex as smooth pinwheels around singular points.

• Sharpness subtypes run from crisply tuned cells to cells with almost no preference, the broadest sitting in the input layer and sharpening above and below it.

• Retinal ON-OFF direction-selective ganglion cells come in four types preferring up, down, left and right, matched to the axes of the eye muscles, and an ON type has now been found in the primate retina.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Orientation selectivity first appears in the primary visual cortex, area V1, in the calcarine sulcus of the occipital lobe, across layers 4C alpha, 4C beta, 4B, 2/3, 5 and 6.

Direction selectivity begins earlier, in the inner plexiform layer of the retina where amacrine and ganglion dendrites meet, and returns in V1 layer 4B, where 66 percent of cells are directional and 59 percent of those almost perfectly unidirectional, and layer 4B is the interface, since it alone projects to motion area MT.

NUMBER:

Zero additional neurons, since these are functional labels on cells already counted: about 1 million retinal ganglion cells per human eye and about 140 million V1 neurons per hemisphere.

Most V1 cells show some orientation preference though sharpness varies enormously, so the defensible claim is that most of those 140 million are orientation-biased and a minority sharply tuned.

Direction-selective ganglion cells are a small minority in primates, only recently demonstrated, so their human count is unknown beyond the bound that they are far fewer than the midget and parasol cells, and against 86 billion neurons overall the addition is exactly zero percent.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

V1 cells fire under two spikes per second in the dark and twenty to eighty for an optimal edge, with orientation bandwidth at half height spread broadly from about twenty degrees to essentially untuned, and direction preference scored as an index running from 0 for none to 1 for absolute.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Classically a row of thalamic relay cells with centre-surround fields, aligned along a line, converge on one cortical cell, and that line is the orientation it prefers, though local inhibition and recurrent excitation are needed to sharpen it.

In the retina the whole computation is one asymmetric wiring trick, since a starburst amacrine cell inhibits the ganglion cell only on the side matching the null direction, so wrong-way motion arrives already vetoed.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Cortical axons are thin, 0.3 to 1.5 micrometres, and branch to distant patches sharing the same orientation, a pattern called like-to-like horizontal connectivity, while retinal ganglion axons form the optic nerve and conduct at up to twenty metres per second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate for excitation and GABA for cortical inhibition, and remarkably the starburst amacrine cell releases both GABA, which inhibits, and acetylcholine, which excites, from the same cell, the asymmetry of that GABA release being the physical basis of direction selectivity.

MYELINATION:

Cortical local axons are patchily myelinated while the layer 4B projection to area MT is well insulated because motion signals must arrive fast, and in the retina the whole computation happens in unmyelinated dendrites with no action potentials at all.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high, since V1 has the highest neuron density of any human cortical area and is so rich in cytochrome oxidase, an enzyme of energy metabolism, that the enzyme is used as a stain to reveal V1's internal structure.

PLASTICITY:

Orientation tuning exists roughly at birth and is refined during a critical period, a window in early life when the cortex is unusually mouldable, whereas retinal direction selectivity develops correctly even when GABA, acetylcholine and action potentials are all blocked, so it is essentially hard-wired.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

V1 astrocytes are packed at exceptionally high density to serve the exceptionally high neuron density and clear the transmitter glutamate fast enough to stop neighbouring orientation columns bleeding into one another.

In the retina the Muller cell, a giant glial cell spanning the whole retinal thickness, acts as a living optical fibre guiding light to the photoreceptors, a job no glial cell elsewhere performs.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove orientation selectivity and vision collapses into a fog of brightness with no contours, so you could tell light from dark but not see a shape.

Remove direction selectivity and the world keeps its shapes but stops flowing, objects appearing at a series of positions without ever seeming to travel, so the flavour lost is form in the first case and time in the second.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The retinal detector is the cleanest case of dendritic computation without spikes anywhere, since each branch of a starburst amacrine cell computes outward motion independently and releases transmitter locally without the cell ever firing.

In the cortex, tuning is sharpened by divisive normalisation, dividing the response by the neighbourhood's pooled activity, which gives contrast invariance, and the cells adapt over seconds, synchronise in the gamma band with like-tuned neighbours and control local blood flow directly.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: for cortical cells, a spatially arranged array of lateral geniculate nucleus centre-surround responses at 0 to 200 spikes per second each.

◦ For retinal cells, bipolar excitation plus asymmetric starburst inhibition as graded voltage, plus a local inhibitory pool for normalisation and feedback gain 0.8 to 1.5 from V2 and MT.

• OUTPUTS: spike train 0 to 100 hertz to V1 layers 2/3 and 5, to area V2, and from layer 4B to area MT.

• STATE: membrane voltage, adaptation level, normalisation pool, spatiotemporal filter history over the last 100 milliseconds.

• PARAMETERS: preferred orientation 0 to 180 degrees, bandwidth 15 to 90 degrees at half height, preferred direction 0 to 360 degrees, direction index 0 to 1, spatial frequency 0.5 to 8 cycles per degree.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds, V1 latency 40 to 70 milliseconds, integration 20 to 100 milliseconds, contrast adaptation 1 to 10 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: bilateral damage to area V5/MT causes akinetopsia, or motion blindness, first described in patient LM, who saw the world as still frames and could not judge liquid rising in a cup, while failure of orientation tuning during the critical period causes amblyopia, in which the eye is optically sound but the cortex never learns to see sharply through it.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the firing rate of an orientation-selective and direction-selective cell for a moving edge.
Create a number variable called preferred orientation with initial value 90.
Create a number variable called orientation bandwidth with initial value 25.
Create a number variable called direction index with initial value 0.8.
Set orientation gap to the difference of stimulus orientation and preferred orientation.
If orientation gap is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
    Set orientation gap to the product of orientation gap and negative one.
End the conditional block.
If orientation gap is greater than 90, then perform the following actions.
    Set orientation gap to the difference of 180 and orientation gap.
End the conditional block.
Set orientation factor to the difference of 1 and the quotient of orientation gap divided by orientation bandwidth.
If orientation factor is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
    Set orientation factor to 0.
End the conditional block.
If stimulus direction is equal to preferred direction, then perform the following actions.
    Set direction factor to 1.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Set direction factor to the difference of 1 and direction index.
End the conditional block.
Set firing rate to the sum of 1 and the product of 40 and the product of orientation factor and direction factor.
Display firing rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because a space-by-time loop makes visible the single idea that motion is orientation in the space-time plane.

import math

SF, TF = 0.05, 2.0      # spatial cycles per unit space, temporal cycles per unit time
SIG_X, SIG_T = 8.0, 0.25
XS = [i * 0.5 for i in range(-64, 65)]
TS = [j * 0.01 for j in range(-50, 51)]

def window(x, t):
    return math.exp(-x * x / (2 * SIG_X ** 2)) * math.exp(-t * t / (2 * SIG_T ** 2))

def filt(x, t, phase):
    # A filter tilted in space and time, so it prefers one direction of drift.
    return window(x, t) * math.cos(2 * math.pi * (SF * x - TF * t) + phase)

def energy(direction):
    """direction is +1 for a rightward drifting grating, -1 for leftward."""
    even = odd = 0.0
    for x in XS:
        for t in TS:
            s = math.cos(2 * math.pi * (SF * x - direction * TF * t))
            even += s * filt(x, t, 0.0)
            odd += s * filt(x, t, -math.pi / 2)   # the quadrature partner
    return even * even + odd * odd                # energy, so phase does not matter

pref, null = energy(+1), energy(-1)
print("preferred energy:", round(pref, 1))
print("null energy:", round(null, 1))
print("direction index:", round((pref - null) / (pref + null), 3))   # close to 1.0

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Sweep a bar one way and the cell shouts, sweep the identical bar back and it says nothing, even though every photon in the stimulus is the same.

CHAPTER 296. SIMPLE CELLS (Fixed-Edge Neuron) (COG)

This cell will tell you an edge is present only if the edge is in exactly the right place; move it half a stripe and the cell falls silent.

The word simple is a committee's shorthand from the 1960s and teaches nothing about what the cell in the primary visual cortex actually does.

Fixed names the defining property, that the position of the edge within the receptive field matters absolutely, because the on and off zones of that field never overlap.

Edge names the stimulus, an oriented border of light against dark, since the cell fires best for a bar or grating at one preferred angle and falls almost silent when that bar is turned ninety degrees.

Neuron marks it as a cortical cell, and the name is built to be read against the Roaming-Edge Neuron so the one dimension separating the pair, whether position matters, is visible in both names.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This cell has a receptive field made of neat parallel stripes, some switched on by light and some switched on by dark, and those stripes never overlap.

Because of that clean separation its response follows plain addition, so shifting the edge half a stripe turns the cell off.

The consequence is a measuring instrument: the cell reports not only that an edge of a given tilt is present, but exactly where it lies.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Typically a spiny stellate or small pyramidal cell of layers 4C and 6.

The correspondence is loose, however, and you cannot tell this cell from its roaming counterpart by looking at it, which is why the classification is functional rather than anatomical.

SIZE:

Layer 4C spiny stellate bodies are ten to fifteen micrometres across with dendritic branches spanning two to four hundred micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Even-symmetric cells, which prefer a bar centred on the field.

• Odd-symmetric cells, which prefer an edge, meaning a step from dark to light.

• Quadrature pairs, an even and an odd cell sharing orientation and spatial frequency but offset by a quarter cycle.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The primary visual cortex, area V1, in and around the calcarine sulcus of the occipital lobe, and continuing into area V2 and beyond.

Layer 4C is the interface at which thalamic input becomes cortical processing, and within it this cell and its roaming counterpart occur in roughly equal proportion, which corrects the tidy textbook story that layer 4 is purely simple.

NUMBER:

Zero additional neurons, since this is a functional label on V1 cells already counted, roughly 140 million per human hemisphere over a V1 surface that varies two- to threefold between healthy people.

This type is the minority overall, because the layers it dominates hold fewer neurons than those dominated by its counterpart.

Any precise split deserves caution, since the classifying measure shifts with contrast and surround.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The defining measurement is the modulation ratio.

Drift a sinusoidal grating across the field, take the mean firing rate and the amplitude of firing at the grating's own frequency, and divide the second by the first.

A ratio above one marks this cell, whose firing rises and falls with each passing stripe.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It gets its stripes from a row of aligned thalamic relay cells, with on-centre cells feeding the on-stripes and off-centre cells feeding the off-stripes.

Inhibition arrives in antiphase with excitation, stripe by stripe, which sharpens the separation between the zones.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Layer 4C axons climb vertically into layers 2/3, delivering the measured result to the cells that will generalise it.

Layer 6 axons feed back to the thalamus in a loop numerically larger than the forward path.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate for excitation and gamma aminobutyric acid (GABA) for inhibition, the latter being no mere brake here but an active shaping force arriving out of phase with excitation.

MYELINATION:

Layer 4C axons are largely bare over their short vertical course, while the layer 6 feedback to thalamus is fast and fully insulated.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Among the highest in the brain, since V1 is the most metabolically active cortical area in an awake seeing human and layer 4C is so rich in cytochrome oxidase that the layer can be picked out by eye in a stained section.

PLASTICITY:

Shaped during the critical period by patterned vision, so depriving one eye shifts cortical territory to the other.

In adults, perceptual learning sharpens tuning for trained orientations and adaptation reshapes tuning curves for minutes after prolonged viewing.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

V1 astrocytes sit at unusually high density, each tiling a domain about a hundred micrometres wide and controlling the blood vessels inside it, so the astrocyte network effectively maps orientation columns onto the blood supply.

That coupling is why functional brain imaging can read orientation-related activity out of V1 at all.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and V1 loses its ruler.

Nothing would measure where an edge is, and fine acuity and stereoscopic depth would go with it, since both depend on knowing position to a fraction of a receptive field.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

It obeys divisive normalisation, dividing its response by a pooled measure of local activity, which is what gives it contrast invariance and surround suppression.

Its dendrites fire local NMDA spikes, so a cluster of inputs on one branch counts for more than the same number scattered across the tree.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: an array of aligned on-centre and off-centre thalamic responses at 0 to 200 spikes per second each, plus antiphase inhibition and a normalisation pool summing local activity.

• OUTPUTS: spike train 0 to 100 hertz to V1 layers 2/3 and to the thalamic feedback loop.

• STATE: membrane voltage, per-stripe excitatory and inhibitory conductances, normalisation pool level, adaptation variable.

• PARAMETERS: preferred orientation, preferred spatial frequency, stripe phase, modulation ratio above 1, normalisation exponent about 2.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds, synaptic 2 to 10 milliseconds, adaptation seconds to minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of antiphase inhibition merges the on and off stripes, collapsing the modulation ratio and converting the measuring instrument into a detector that no longer reports position.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the response of a cell whose on and off zones do not overlap, so that the position of an edge determines whether it fires.
Create a number variable called response with initial value zero.
Create a list variable called on zone samples with initial value empty list.
Create a list variable called off zone samples with initial value empty list.
For each position in the receptive field, perform the following operations.
    If the position lies inside an on zone, then perform the following actions.
        Add the light level at that position to the end of on zone samples.
    Else if the position lies inside an off zone, then perform the following actions.
        Add the light level at that position to the end of off zone samples.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Set on total to the sum of all items in on zone samples.
Set off total to the sum of all items in off zone samples.
Set raw response to the difference of on total and off total.
If raw response is less than zero, then perform the following actions.
    Set raw response to zero.
End the conditional block.
Set response to the quotient of raw response divided by the sum of one and the normalisation pool level.
Display response to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because a Gabor receptive field and a dot product show the whole idea in a few lines.
import math

def gabor(x, phase, sf=0.1, sigma=8.0):
    return math.exp(-(x * x) / (2 * sigma * sigma)) * math.cos(2 * math.pi * sf * x + phase)

def fixed_edge_response(edge_pos, phase=0.0, width=40):
    xs = range(-width, width + 1)
    stim = [1.0 if x >= edge_pos else -1.0 for x in xs]      # a step edge
    r = sum(gabor(x, phase) * s for x, s in zip(xs, stim))
    return max(0.0, r)                                        # rectify

for pos in (-8, -4, 0, 4, 8):
    print("edge at", pos, "-> response", round(fixed_edge_response(pos), 2))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It beats in time with the stripes.

Drift a grating across its field and it fires one burst per stripe, so its spike train carries the rhythm of the moving pattern, and that single measurement has separated it from its counterpart for over thirty years.

CHAPTER 297. COMPLEX CELLS (Roaming-Edge Neuron) (COG)

This cell prefers exactly the same tilted edge as its neighbour, but it does not care in the least where that edge sits, which is the first step in the brain towards recognising a thing regardless of where it happens to be.

The word complex is a committee's shorthand and says only that the cell was harder to explain than the other one.

Roaming names the defining property, that the edge can slide anywhere within the receptive field and still drive the cell, because its on and off zones overlap.

Edge names the stimulus, the same oriented border of light against dark that its fixed counterpart prefers, since both fall almost silent when the bar is turned ninety degrees.

Neuron marks it as a cortical cell, and the shared word Edge keeps the pair legible as one family differing on one axis.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This cell prefers one tilt, like its neighbour, but its light and dark zones sit on top of each other rather than side by side.

The consequence is that it fires for that orientation anywhere within its window.

Between the two types, the cortex first measures precisely and then generalises, which is the beginning of recognising a shape independently of where it falls on the retina.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Typically a pyramidal cell of layers 2/3, 4B and 5.

As with its counterpart the correspondence is loose, and the two cannot be told apart by appearance alone.

SIZE:

Receptive fields are typically two to three times larger in area than the fixed fields feeding them, which is the direct consequence of pooling several of those fields together.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Standard complex cells, which pool across phase at one orientation.

• End-stopped complex cells, sometimes called special complex cells, which go quiet when a bar is made too long and so become detectors of corners and line endings.

• Direction-selective complex cells, which additionally prefer one direction of motion along the axis perpendicular to their preferred orientation.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The primary visual cortex, area V1, and onward into area V2 and beyond.

This type dominates layers 2/3, 4B and 5, though it is also present in layer 4C in roughly equal proportion with its counterpart, which corrects the tidy story that layer 4 is purely simple.

NUMBER:

Zero additional neurons, since this is a functional label on V1 cells already counted.

This type outnumbers its counterpart overall, because the layers it dominates hold more neurons than layer 4C alone.

Any precise split deserves caution, since the classifying measure shifts with contrast and surround.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The same modulation ratio that identifies its counterpart identifies this cell by falling below one, because its firing holds steady rather than rising and falling as each stripe passes.

Skottun and colleagues showed in 1991 that this ratio falls into two humps across V1, which is what makes it the standard test.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It pools several fixed-edge cells sharing one orientation but differing in stripe position.

It also takes direct thalamic drive, so the neat hierarchy from simple to complex is an approximation rather than a law.

Inhibition arrives as a sustained pooled signal rather than in antiphase.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Layer 2/3 axons run horizontally for millimetres, linking columns that share a preferred orientation, which is how the cortex stitches an extended contour together.

Layer 5 axons carry the result out of V1 towards subcortical targets.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate for excitation and gamma aminobutyric acid (GABA) for inhibition, the inhibition here being sustained and pooled rather than phase-locked.

MYELINATION:

Layer 2/3 horizontal axons are patchily myelinated, with insulated segments separated by bare stretches, an arrangement thought to tune conduction delay across the millimetres they span.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Among the highest in the brain, shared with the rest of V1, the most metabolically active cortical area in an awake seeing human.

PLASTICITY:

Shaped during the critical period by patterned vision, and in adults subject to perceptual learning and to adaptation that reshapes tuning curves for minutes after prolonged viewing.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Shares V1's unusually dense astrocyte population, each astrocyte tiling a domain about a hundred micrometres wide and controlling the blood vessels within it.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and V1 keeps its precision but loses its tolerance.

An edge would have to be exactly placed to be seen at all, and every small tremor of the eye would rewrite the image, so the flavour lost is stability.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This is the clearest nonlinear computation in the brain.

Squaring and summing a quadrature pair, meaning two fixed-edge cells offset by a quarter cycle, yields orientation energy that is blind to stripe position, and no linear device can do this.

Like its counterpart it obeys divisive normalisation and fires local NMDA spikes in its dendrites, so clustered inputs count for more than scattered ones.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: the rectified outputs of two or more fixed-edge cells at matched orientation and spatial frequency but differing phase, plus direct thalamic drive, a normalisation pool, and feedback gain 0.8 to 1.5.

• OUTPUTS: spike train 0 to 100 hertz to V1 layers 2/3 and 5, to area V2, and to subcortical targets.

• STATE: membrane voltage, squared subunit outputs, normalisation pool level, adaptation variable.

• PARAMETERS: preferred orientation, preferred spatial frequency, number of pooled subunits typically 2 to 8, modulation ratio below 1, normalisation exponent about 2.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds, synaptic 2 to 10 milliseconds, adaptation seconds to minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of pooling collapses the cell back to a position-sensitive detector, so the cortex loses translation tolerance and object recognition becomes dependent on precise fixation.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute a response to an oriented edge that does not depend on where within the receptive field that edge lies.
Create a list variable called subunit responses with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called energy with initial value zero.
For each subunit in the pooled set of fixed-edge cells, perform the following operations.
    Set subunit output to the response of that subunit to the current image.
    Set squared output to the product of subunit output and subunit output.
    Add squared output to the end of subunit responses.
End the loop.
For each item in subunit responses, perform the following operations.
    Set energy to the sum of energy and that item.
End the loop.
Set response to the quotient of energy divided by the sum of one and the normalisation pool level.
Display response to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because the energy model is literally square-and-sum, which is three lines here.
import math

def gabor(x, phase, sf=0.1, sigma=8.0):
    return math.exp(-(x * x) / (2 * sigma * sigma)) * math.cos(2 * math.pi * sf * x + phase)

def subunit(edge_pos, phase, width=40):
    xs = range(-width, width + 1)
    stim = [1.0 if x >= edge_pos else -1.0 for x in xs]
    return sum(gabor(x, phase) * s for x, s in zip(xs, stim))

def roaming_edge_response(edge_pos):
    even = subunit(edge_pos, 0.0)              # quadrature pair:
